Expositio in Cantica canticorum
by Gilbertus Foliot

## DEDICATORY EPISTLE.

*To the most reverend and most ample prelate, the lord WILLIAM JUXON, bishop of London and Lord High Treasurer of England, PATRICK YOUNG. Greeting.*

That oblivion has full often, *most Ample and [sic: Reverendissim] Prelate,* given occasion for a more renowned fame, and that many things have fallen in order that they might afterwards rise again more adorned, is a thing to which assurance is given and abundant witness borne not only by deserted ruined walls and by rubble long neglected, which, laid low by age, put on a new face and a more august form: but by the monuments of genius also, which are day by day corrected, are published in more emended form, and by the sedulous diligence of the studious are drawn forth, first out of mould and dust, into the light and into the day. Among these is this devout and elegant writing of thy predecessor, which until now has perhaps lain buried in oblivion, that in this learned age it might emerge from the darkness with a greater splendour, and might look again, under the auspices of thy most celebrated name, upon the light which it had first drunk in within thy walls. His aged infirmities, or rather those of the age in which he lived, which occur here and there in phrasing or in matter: if, out of your singular piety, *Your most Ample Lordship* be willing to cover them with but the hem of your own garment, and to deem him worthy of your patronage together with his *Acoluthus* (for to go forth into public alone, without a companion, was neither seemly, nor did the condition of so great a man allow it), and kindly to interpret this our small labour in publishing the two, you will make a step toward greater things, which may hereafter be dedicated to thy name, and will bind me, most devoted to *Thy Amplitude,* by a closer bond of observance for the time to come. Given the day before the Nones of November. In the year MDCXXXVIII.

## GILBERT'S PREFACE.

[1147A]To his lord and most dear friend Robert, by the grace of God bishop of Hereford, brother Gilbert, humble minister of the Church of London: to be exercised in those things which are God's, and in the same to be consummated with a most blessed end.

Lest the authority of thy command, my lord, should seem to me empty or cheap, I have straightway sent thee the book which thou didst ask for: the book, that is, which with the Lord's help I lately put forth upon the Song of Songs, and which I entrusted to no man before thee, either to be copied or to be looked into. For it was not written by me for praise, nor for the sake of a public commendation, but that I might hold in memory the things which, the Lord teaching, I had learned: and that I might show that these things are not, after the manner of the Jews, to be cheaply despised, but to be read humbly with the inmost understanding, and to be understood in the same spirit in which they [1148A] were spoken. And when it had been read through by thee with the utmost attention from beginning to end, thou didst receive it with much alacrity of mind, and, as I hear, didst commend with much praise that I had applied my zeal to this study: and, that nothing might be wanting to its perfection, thou didst ask that a Prologue be fitted to it by me, so that the completion of the work might be worthy, and to it a rendering of thanks be made; for it is written, « Love is strong as death [n: (Cap. VIII, 6)]. » For as there is nothing mortal that can break the bond of death, so there is nothing possible that the urgency of love cannot obtain. This it was that emptied the Lord, and bound him with human bonds; this it was that, after the cross and the sepulchre, led him down to the lowest seats, that he might snatch thence those who had believed that he would come, [1149A] and were diligently awaiting him: this it is that reforms into the liberty of grace those whom, at the dictate of justice, it had made sons of wrath, mercy tempering judgment. This changes my purpose, so that I set a prologue to a work which I undertook to expound without a prologue. For the author of the work was Solomon, who imitated the ancient writers, who do not make their readers attentive by prefixing a prologue, but by showing them the matter of which they are about to treat arouse their attention, and turn their zeal toward the recognition of the things that are said. Whence that saying of Moses: « In the beginning God created heaven and earth: but the earth was without form, » [var: Vulg. *inanis et vacua*, "void and empty"] etc. [n: (Gen. I, 1)]. And that of Exodus: « These are the names of the sons of Israel, who entered into Egypt with Jacob [n: (Exod. I, 1)]. » So, [1149B] running through the whole Pentateuch, thou wilt find the books of Judges and of Kings, and the others besides, written without a prologue; and if prologues have been added to them, thou wilt recognize that they were added long afterwards, after the coming of the Saviour. Imitating these, Solomon begins straightway from the very matter of which he is about to treat, knowing that the matter itself which is handled has in itself either a manifest usefulness, by which it may allure, or an evident reason, by which one may despise the things coveted, or theological considerations, by which one may cast these [1150A] from the mind, and cleave to him alone who is the founder and creator of them. Three books therefore he made, which he does not adorn or enlarge with prologues: one, of « Parables »; another, « Ecclesiastes »; this third, « Sirasirim ». In the « Parables » he allures men to the possessing of those things by which the present life is furnished, that a man's own necessity may be relieved, that in the things wherein there is need his neighbour may be succoured, and that to the indigent there be given as necessity requires. But in « Ecclesiastes » he warns that the mind be not entangled [sic: implieetur] in these things, that love be not bent toward the things which are transitory: but that it judge them as vain, and decide upon and desire the things that are higher than these. Whence, adding « Sirasirim » next in order (which is called the « Song of songs »), in which, flying past all things, the soul stands beside the divine majesty, [1150B] embracing him as a bridegroom, and presenting to him out of her affection all that love can be. The soul, therefore, or the Church, kindled with the fire of love, not seeking these visible things but sighing after the invisible with the whole affection of the heart and the whole intention of the mind, he thus brings in: as one seeking God, yet not yet apprehending him, and, that she may hold him as apprehended, saying these things.

## EXPOSITION ON THE SONG OF SONGS.



## CHAPTER ONE.

[1149C]

1. *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*

Who is this, so renowned? of form and grace so elegant? that, boldly seeking the kiss of the highest King, she says, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth? » Is this soul acting in a dream, feigning for herself what she wills, snatching herself away into some hope without substance, forming empty visions within herself, and even now hoping to apprehend what she could not obtain by laborious endeavours? For whither has this spirit ascended, so unmindful of gold and so forgetful of silver, accounting the precious stone as nothing, so spurning all that the world possesses and imparts to its own to be possessed, that, thrusting these away with the hand of holy perfection, she says: Stand apart, you; my love, withdrawn from you, [1149D] has been carried over into the things above, seeking him alone who alone truly is, and who furnishes to all things alike the cause and the effect of their being; sighing after whom, and impatient of holy desire, this soul communes with herself, and speaks, saying, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth: » for a soul so inspired speaks not vain things, but the highest. Long since has she drunk up the streams of Solomon's wisdom, and heard him disputing in « Ecclesiastes »: for once indeed, dainty and tender, when she had not yet come from outward forms to inward things, when she had not yet ascended from the creature to the Creator, [1150C] she clung to the world with all her embraces, and bound herself with all her affections to the very things that are seen: but having entered the school of spiritual doctrine, she was accounted worthy to hear no common master, but that Solomon supreme in wisdom, appointing for holy Church a certain introductory lesson, by which he might call away every elect soul from the world, and might join her, once called away and drawn apart, to him who is above the world, by the indissoluble bond of charity. For that supreme Solomon, seeing in the spirit that almost every soul was as it were digging in the earth, entangling herself in secular business, and no longer lifting her love upward but bending it lower; in order that he might raise up the bowed and wretched one, came down from the height of his own contemplation to the depths of human converse, calling back [1150D] the wanderer, instructing and reforming her with saving precepts, and so at length, by certain steps as it were of ascent, leading her through to the heights of holy contemplation and of love. First of all, therefore, he promises great things to the tender age, saying, « The parables of Solomon: to know wisdom and discipline, and to understand the words of prudence, and to receive the instruction of doctrine [n: (Prov. I, 1 seq.)]. » He suggests winning things, adding, « A wise man hearing shall be wiser, and he that understandeth shall possess governments. » Thus he does not at once preach to her contempt of the world and of the things that are in the world, [1151A] nor rehearse the hard and lofty matters of a straiter life; « for first that which is animal, then that which is spiritual [n: (I Cor. XV, 46)]. » Whence the best of masters, lest by urging weighty things at once he should blunt at the very beginnings the soul which he had taken up to be instructed, permits her, as though for the passing of her years of infancy, to use freely the world and the things that are of the world, and to take delight in them: yet appointing her a law, that she flow not away in sinning, nor turn to the use of malice and of lust what she has received by the great gift of a great author. He sets forth, therefore, to her who now listens attentively, the Parables; and with as many Parables as he instructs her, with so many laws does he inform and bind her to living well and honourably: which Parables indeed, if a prudent reader shall look into them more attentively with the eye of the mind, [1151B] he will understand that within them there is assigned most fully, for an honourable converse, a form of life for the possessors of this world and for those who pass their days in the common life. For because « without faith it is impossible to please God [n: (Heb. XI, 6)], » in the very opening of the treatise faith and the worship of the highest Godhead are inculcated, when it is said, « Hear, my son, the discipline of thy father, and forsake not the law of thy mother [n: (Prov. I, 8)]. » For the discipline of the highest Father, which the Church has most fully learned from him, is this: « To believe that he is, and that he is a rewarder to them that love him [n: (Heb. XI, 6)], » [var: Vulg. *inquirentibus se*, "to them that seek him"] and that in the end of the times he sent his Son in the flesh, and through him fulfilled his promises. But of the law of our mother the Church, the first is that natural law, engraved by the finger of God on the hearts of men; the second, that written upon tablets [1151C] of stone; the third, the law of the Gospel, ministered in the Holy Spirit and in grace. To forsake not the law of our mother, therefore, is this: not to withdraw from the unity of the Church, and from the worship of the highest Godhead, which she received inwardly by the inspiration of grace. But the remaining commandments of the Decalogue shine forth in distinct places throughout the whole volume of the Parables; and by these, and by very many other precepts besides, there is set forth as it were a certain secular holiness, and the life of secular men is ordered by a fit governance of reason; so that a man may so use the world as not to turn aside from God, and so possess the things permitted and made subject to him as unceasingly to abstain from the things forbidden. In the first instruction, since satisfaction has been made to human desires, and it has been permitted to man to use temporal things soberly, according to what was said to the first man [1151D] by the divine voice, « Increase and multiply, and fill the earth, and subdue it, and rule over the fishes of the sea, and the fowls of the air, and all things that move upon the earth [n: (Gen. I, 28)]; » there is now place for a second instruction, that concerning the things which man has long possessed he may now hear what judgment is to be made: whether he is to cleave to them with love, or whether love is to be raised from them to something higher and far better: whence Solomon, running through with the pen of truth each several thing that is under the sun, says in the second volume, « Vanity of vanities, and all is vanity [n: (Eccle. I, 2)]; » and mightily showing that this vanity is present in all transitory things, that the mind is by no means to be wrapped up in vain things, nor love to be entangled in vain things, [1152A] he sets apart from vanity the soul which he had taken up to be instructed, and applies her separately to truth, when at the end of that same work he adds, « Youth and pleasure are vain [n: (cap. XI, 10)]. » And a little after, « Remember thy Lord thy Creator in the days of thy youth, before the time of affliction come, and the years of which thou shalt say, they please me not [n: (Cap. XII, 1)]. » And a little after, in the very close of the discourse, « Fear God, and keep his commandments: this is every man [n: (ibid., 13)]. » By which compendious close of words, what else does he instil into the minds of men but the fear of that highest Godhead, and the reverence of the worship that is owed. For this is the way, this is the access to life, if the mind, seized with holy fear, always turn aside from unlawful things, and the mind, kindled within with the fire of love, [1152B] be unceasingly girded for good things: for man has been set midway in the order of the rational creature, having the good Angel above him, the evil one below, and, when this life has slipped away, he is to be brought to an equality with the one or the other according to the merits of his life. He therefore, as he is midway between these, so has something in common with each: love, namely, with him who is above, fear with him who is below: for the evil angels fear and tremble, but the good love perfectly; and as the evil angel does not rise up to love, so the good angels do not come down to fear, according to that saying, « Perfect charity casteth out fear [n: (I Joan. IV, 18)]. » Since, therefore, love alone wholly carries the good angel into itself, while fear alone presses the evil one downward, [1152C] there is not, apart from man, in the order of the whole creation, any in whom fear runs together with love, in whom these two meet joined to one another. To man, therefore, it is said, « Fear God, and keep his commandments: this is every man: » as if it were said, All this, that is, the whole of this, namely, « to fear, » and by loving « to keep the commandments, » belongs to man; that by fearing he may turn aside wrath, and by loving may attain to grace. Or if it be said, « This is every man, » that is, through fear and love a man is made perfect. Or, « This is every man, » that is, unto this every man is created, this does not differ from the same understanding, since this is the manner and the cause of his creation: that he should so escape wrath, and merit grace.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

This soul, therefore, receiving the admonitions of salvation not now drawn off [1152D] from afar, but from the fountain itself, that is, from the highest Solomon; taught already in the Proverbs in what manner she may possess worldly things, and in Ecclesiastes by what reasoning she may now forsake and despise in her mind the things she has possessed; hears now at last in the Songs how she may pass over from things visible to things invisible, and cleave to the author of all with the whole strength of her mind and the fullness of her affection. Thus, from the moral discipline by which she holily shares what she possesses, through the rational, by which she rightly judges of worldly things, having entered upon that holy and highest *theoria*, having now utterly forgotten the things that are behind, climbing that holy mountain and the summit of her own mind, she follows through the midst of the cloud and the darkness that holy Moses to the heights of contemplation; [1153A] where, abiding in a secret dwelling [n: (Exod. XIX, 20, et XX, 21)], she seeks not lofty panelled ceilings, nor the painted flanks of attendants, nor acres of fields stretched out beyond measure, but, that she may abound plentifully in all things, seeks him alone who is all things, who is the light and the rest of souls, the fount of good things and the origin of virtues, whom to apprehend is the highest thing, and whom to enjoy the most delightful: and being driven within by desire of him, she utters what she feels, and no longer restraining herself within the cloisters of silence, speaks, after the manner of a lover, either to herself or to some heavenly Virtue akin to her, saying, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » Love indeed errs rather often in judgment, and while it is borne headlong by its own desire, whithersoever it has turned itself it seems to itself to behold the thing it desires, and that any other person too [1153B] experiences in himself what it feels: whence, as though it were plain to others who it is that love draws and as it were absorbs into itself, when she utters outwardly what she does within, she designates no one by name, but with a certain discreet indiscretion so says, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » This indeed is the secret of a perfect mind, and the soliloquy of a soul now cleansed with the heavenly Bridegroom. Come then, turn aside the edge of thy mind a little, and for a short while come down from the highest things to the lower; consider a soul still bound fast by unbelief, one who has not yet been enlightened to receive the rays of faith, has not yet touched the thresholds of the Church, and knew nothing at all of the Bridegroom: wilt thou judge that the Song rightly belongs to her? that she should speak to the Bridegroom of holy souls, saying, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth? » Would not [1153C] this Parable be unseemly in her mouth? But if thou consider a soul enlightened indeed by faith, yet dead in works, grievously oppressed and overwhelmed by sins, making for herself a long rope of sin, and not running back to the good by that remedy of penance: can the Song rightly be fitted to her, that, misshapen of mouth, and of horrid face, and foul in all her body, she should nevertheless say to the Father of all, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » But a soul fortified by faith and imbued with the sacraments, now raising herself by the law of the mind to the good of virtue, now, in the lapse into sin, taking upon her the law that fights back in her members; subject to this alternation, mourning, and in her mourning crying aloud: « O who shall deliver me from the body of this death? » [1153D] [n: (Rom. VII, 24.)] Will she fitly sing the Song, that, overcast with the tears of sadness and drawing the sobs of penance, she should say, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. »

But if, advancing a little, thou consider a soul upon a certain higher step, governing herself by a kind of even moderation, so that she neither now admits the sins for which damnation is due, nor yet climbs to the heights of virtue, dwelling in a manner with Lot in Segor, not yet reaching the mountains: she assuredly, in proportion as she does not yet touch the summits of virtue, is the more delighted by outward things, and burns less strongly in the desire of the heavenly kiss; so that neither to her does that rightly belong which was said, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his [1154A] mouth. » Enter now at last the secret place of the spiritual soul; consider a soul now established in grace, whom the spirit has drawn out into the mountains, who has now determined to dwell on the heights, who, the world being now overcome, has trodden sin underfoot, who, running after the Lord with Peter, has now said, « Behold, we have left all things, and have followed thee [n: (Matth. XIX, 27)]; » she, though she be far from the darkness of unbelief, from the reign of sin and of death, from the assault of temptation, from the torpor of the mind, does not yet presume so far as to dare, where she awaits the kiss of the highest King, which perhaps she has not yet tasted, saying, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth: » but either she sits content, with Mary, with the kiss of the holy feet, or, according to John, « she stands with the friends of the Bridegroom, and rejoiceth with joy at his voice [n: (Joan. III, 29)], » [1154B] which voice, most full of sweetness, she has now been accounted worthy to hear within, and in a certain appearance and promise of things to come alike to be delighted and to rest. From hence ascend, if now thou canst, to the sixth step of perfection, by which a love now consecrated enters the heights of contemplation; where the Lord is transfigured upon the mountain for her, and that most holy Moses, hidden from human eyes, now privy to the heavenly secrets, rests most sweetly in the very hidden place of the Godhead: here the most blessed soul, contemplating in her heart the King whom she loves, and stretched out toward God as far as in this life is lawful, is refreshed by the sweetness of the eternal vision; and, turning back the eye of contemplation upon all things that have been created, while she wonders not so much at them as at [1154C] the wisdom of the highest Maker in them, and at his unspeakable power, disdaining all that is created, gathers herself wholly into that wisdom, and is bound fast to it by the bond of charity and by the light of contemplation; and while she sees that all that is comely in the creature is not in the least degree comparable to his comeliness, she takes fire and burns toward that invisible fire, and, experiencing the wound of charity inflicted upon her mind by the sweet sting of the Godhead, and demanding that there be foretasted for her certain pledges, as it were, both of the blessedness to come and of that most sacred conjunction of the created spirit with the uncreated, which shall be without end in an indissoluble bond of charity, with an inward; and crying out with a sound of the mind audible to the holy and supernal powers alone, she says, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » For love kindled toward God is charity; and [1154D] the beholding, with intent mind, of him who is loved is contemplation: and these so accompany one another that contemplation stirs up love, and love always advances contemplation toward the things before; so that whither contemplation penetrates by the ray of the supernal light, thither it draws love after it; so that to the soul thus advancing the Lord is both illumination through contemplation, and true salvation through charity; as it is said, « The Lord is my illumination, and my salvation; whom shall I fear? » [n: (Psal. XXVI, 1.)] The soul, therefore, turned away from the world and converted to Christ with her whole mind, forgetting the things that are behind [n: (Phil. III, 13)], and stretching herself mightily toward the things before, beholding the world now far down beneath her, snatching herself wholly into eternity, [1155A] beholding by contemplation the highest and eternal things of the Lord Jesus, and by urgent love desiring them, because she wholly succumbs to love and languishes with spiritual desire; yet not presuming upon the full embraces of the bridegroom, that her love may be satisfied at least in something, asks a kiss, and, speaking in mind either to herself or perhaps to a familiar angel, imparting spiritual things to the spiritual, opens her love, and, loving, so begins from love, saying, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » And since these things have been said in the manner of a little preface, we shall expound the rest in the following tome, the Lord breathing upon us, who lives and reigns for all ages of ages. Amen.

*Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*

By unremitting labour, by continual exercise of virtue, this faithful [1155B] soul has been brought so far that she aspires to the kiss of the highest King, saying, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » Hence it is that we by no means run on with a gliding pen, but stand as though clinging upon the heights, desiring earnestly to search out and to imitate the things at once hidden and perfect of this holy soul. In which matter this first occurs, that she seems most confidently to glory in herself together with the Apostle, so as to say, « Our glory is this, the testimony of our conscience [n: (II Cor. I, 12)]. » For she who seeks the kiss of the highest Godhead knows beyond doubt that she has a clean heart, knows that she has clean lips, an honourable face, and one not unworthy of the kiss of so great a King: so that there seems rightly to belong to her what was said, « For I have espoused you to one husband, that I may present you a chaste virgin [1155C] to a chaste one [n: (II Cor. XI, 2)]: » [var: Vulg. *Christo*, "to Christ"] and again elsewhere, « That he might present to himself a glorious Church, not having spot, nor wrinkle [n: (Ephes. V, 27)]: » for this soul has no « spot » from uncleanness, no « wrinkle » from duplicity, no depraved conscience from hatred of a neighbour; there proceed from her heart no murders, no adulteries, none of the other things that are wont to weigh down the conscience and are wont to defile a man: for with all watchfulness she keeps her own heart, hearing attentively through Solomon, « for from it life proceedeth. [n: (Prov. IV, 23)]. » At the doors of this heart stands the fear of God, provident and solicitous, lest on every side a way of approach to its secret places lie open to the vices: at the bulwarks of this heart stand the stern battalions of the virtues, which do not now, as of late, fight against sins; [1155D] but glory in the Lord Jesus, the victory over them being even now obtained: and by how much the bitterer the battle they long experienced, by so much the more joyful is the glory in which they now triumph. Above stands chastity girt up, which rejoices that she has crushed the uncleanness of the flesh upon the rock of abstinence. With placid countenance stands humility, the companion of chastity, minding nothing lofty; but repressing by the Lord's power the elation of mind that ever seeks high things, and bravely and prudently dashing the foul madness of its head against the rock, which is Christ. Thus faith despises unbelief, hope despairing misery, charity envy, simplicity guile, innocence malice, truth falsehood, and each several virtue despises the vices opposed to it, as [1156A] monstrous corpses now mightily dashed against the rock of truth. Here thou mayest see that ancient concupiscence of the flesh lamenting at the funerals of her extinguished offspring; carrying round in the golden cup of Babylon her various allurements, and all that can be contrived by the most exquisite art of temptation, plotting this by force, this by all her craft, to see whether the things grown cold may be warmed, and the things extinguished may be raised up to their ancient spirit of life. That sacred choir of the virtues mocks her as she casts her darts, knowing that the Egyptians, smitten by the hand of the Lord, are not to be called back to life any more: whence, exulting spiritually, it takes up psalm and timbrel, singing out that song of Moses with full gladness to the Lord, « Let us sing to the Lord; for he is gloriously magnified: the horse and the rider he hath cast into [1156B] the sea [n: (Exod. XV, 1)]. » The evil rider is every malignant spirit; the evil horses are the worst vices; and with what violence the evil Angels who sit upon them are borne along is manifest, when they drag down souls wretchedly tempted into the depth of hell, and subject them to eternal torments, the divine justice bringing it about.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

This soul glories that these are extinguished for her, and, sitting in the midst of the virtues, now entrusts herself wholly to them, and commits herself utterly to their services; and to her every virtue does service most humbly, each furnishing what is its own. Who could now run through with the pen, who could set forth in speech, what comeliness each several virtue confers upon this perfect soul, what admirable grace each several virtue supplies? Who could express her comeliness to the full? whom [1156C] continence renders chaste, and innocence simple, meekness gentle, mercy devout, circumspection provident, temperance sober, prudence discreet, patience brave; and whom charity, now perfected, directs with much reverence along the sacred paths of justice? For from the very beginning of holy fear, straightway planting her steps in humility, thence passing through the camps of unchastity, and so ascending through the remaining virtues as they are disposed in the mind by the Spirit of God, looking no longer upon vanities and false madnesses, she now entrusts herself wholly to love in a certain higher place, and commits herself to its most excellent fullness. Here, dwelling apart, she cannot but meditate upon what she loves; she cannot but contemplate [1156D] what she desires: with a thirst sent upon her from above, she takes fire toward that living fountain, and is daily refreshed with the sweet bread of tears; while she grieves that her desires are drawn out to such length, and, suffused with tears, makes her complaint. Here she frequently repeats and turns over with herself that saying, « To me to live is Christ, and to die is gain: I desire to be dissolved, and to be with Christ [n: (Phil. I, 21, 23)]. » And that other, « Woe is me! that my sojourning is prolonged [n: (Psal. CXIX, 5)]. » She is indeed forced to feel, and compelled to experience in herself, what she lacks of the highest, and how far off she is from eternity. A spirit released from the flesh has seen manifestly him whom it loves, enjoys the good it desires, is not tormented by the deferring of its desire, is touched by no grief at all; there love is wholly in the light, wholly rests in joy: [1157A] but the weariness of love in the present life is beautifully described, when it is said, « Laughter shall be mingled with sorrow, and mourning taketh hold of the ends of joy [n: (Prov. XIV, 13)]. »

In this life indeed the laughter of a faithful soul is this: that with a clean conscience, with a perfect heart, with a most full integrity of love, she knows that she follows the Lord Jesus into the heights, and that she reaches out the kiss of her mind toward his human things and his divine alike: yet this laughter is still mingled with sorrow, and mourning takes hold of this joy; since she knows that she has not yet apprehended him whom she follows with her heart, and considers that what she desires above all things is deferred from her. Hence the sorrow of the loving soul, hence the tears with which she washes herself through the nights, and waters the bed of her mind; that, being already cleansed, [1157B] she may be cleansed yet more, and that she who had been fair may shine forth far fairer. The soul, trusting in these virtues, conscious of this her comeliness, after vices long since stormed, after the gifts of the virtues obtained, after desires long drawn out, languishing with love, raising herself upward by contemplation, passing through heaven in her mind, asks a kiss for the solace of her grief, for a certain foretaste of the gladness she longs for, and thus begins the Song which is called of Songs, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » Thou mayest perhaps see an Angel indignant, and saying: What has been done? what is this so great and so new a change of things? why have the lowest been mingled with the highest? has heaven been stretched out above in vain? has a seat in the dunghill been appointed for man in vain? does the human mind penetrate [1157C] heaven? and does it, by no means content now with its own bounds, run through not only the regions of heaven, but the inmost parts of the heavens? But if in any way this has been permitted her, what is this, that the human mind should exalt itself to such a height, and should seek the kiss of the creating power, of the wisdom that works all things by a word, of a goodness subject to no constraint, that is, of the highest and inaccessible Godhead, saying, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth? » Has any Angel, or Archangel, or any spirit of the number of the Virtues, of the Cherubim or the Seraphim, ever so spoken with himself, silently or in mind, as either ever to ask or to hope for the kiss [1157D] of him beside whom we stand trembling, and whose embassies we have served by obeying for so many ages? When the Word was made flesh, and the Lord Jesus was passing through his human affairs upon earth, we called it a great thing, and accounted it a marvel in our eyes, that a poor woman was admitted to his feet, to fix a kiss upon those feet which she had nevertheless first wiped with her hairs and washed with the flood of her tears [n: (Luc. VII, 38)]. When the Lord transfigured himself upon the mountain, and revealed to human eyes his divine things, which he had always had, did that loving Peter, or the most beloved John, presume to seek the kiss of his majesty, who, not bearing the voice that came down from the magnificent glory, fell prone to the earth [1158A] [n: (Matth. XVII, 2)]. The Apostle too, caught up hence [n: (II Cor. XII, 4)], to whom the secrets of heaven were entrusted, and those holy words of silence revealed, did he, minding lofty things among us, so set his mouth in heaven that, not measuring himself, not considering his own human estate, he aspired to the kiss of the highest Godhead?

Moses too, going up into the mountain [n: (Exod. XIX)], hiding within the cloud, trembling at the sound of the trumpet, does he present himself to the delights of this kiss, who begs that that merely carnal law, soon to be abolished, be engraved not now upon the hearts of men, but only upon tablets of stone? Since, according to the Truth, « no one among those born of women is greater than John the Baptist [n: (Matth. XI, 11)], » and since, according to the same, « he that is least in the kingdom of heaven is far greater than he [1158B] [n: (ibid.)]; » it could have been enough for the human soul if even the least angel had received her with a kiss. This one's zeal perhaps, as proceeding not from anger or from malice, some supernal virtue tempers, answering him as he judges severely, that mortals are not to be resented if they desire to escape the snares of death, and strive with the whole effort of the mind to apprehend the liberty of the glory of the sons of God. For the condition of mortals is very wretched: set in the midst of the world, they behold hell beneath and heaven above; and for man not only is it easy to descend, but it is most difficult not to fall: to this the flesh is prone by its own weight, temptations press, malignant spirits drag him down with the ropes of sins, ever envying human salvation, as they have long envied our glory also. But as the descent [1158C] is easy, so is the ascent to the things above difficult: in this lies the life of men, in this the battle of souls, in this glow the endeavours of those who live holily in the world: that, escaping the snares of sins, they may pass over into our liberty, and conform themselves to us by purity of life, and join themselves with us in the love of the highest Godhead. For souls that have come forth out of the pit of misery and out of the mire of dregs, forsaking the vain things of the world, nay treading them underfoot in mind: with what devotion are they to be received, with how great a charity are they to be cherished by us, lest they ever look back and degenerate, but may unceasingly kindle their love toward the Father of lights, until they receive the gifts promised to the blessed? A soul, therefore, set in holy desires, spurning earthly things, not yet [1158D] apprehending the heavenly, let her be permitted to use the figures of her own words as a kind of incentive to her love; especially since, when she names a kiss, or an embrace, or a couch, or anything of the sort, she thinks nothing carnal, presumes nothing unworthy, meditates nothing at all save what is holy: [nt: ( For all that a wretched woman sins in a kiss, and carnal affection, is as far from her mind as she herself, purified by virtue, has withdrawn far from vice. )] For there is also a holy kiss, as the Apostle mentions, saying: « Salute one another with a holy kiss [n: (Rom. XVI, 16)]. » And, according to the Truth, the Father running to meet his returning son, « being moved with mercy, and running up, fell upon [1159A] his neck and kissed him [n: (Luc. XV, 20)]. » These things, therefore, which zeal had stirred up among the Supernal Ones, being lulled to rest by the working of modesty, we shall defer to the treatise following the things that are to be said, the Lord inspiring us.

*Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*

As has already been said in the tome above, there is a certain kiss of foul pleasure, which a mind now lifted up to the things above disdains even to remember: But there is a holy kiss, which the Apostle mentions, saying, « Salute one another with a holy kiss, » and the truth in the Gospel, « the Father, being moved with mercy, fell upon the neck of his son, and kissed him. » A kiss, therefore, is of one sort a kiss of reconciliation, of another a kiss of congratulation, of another a kiss of gladness and of a certain permitted and sanctified pleasure. It is a kiss of reconciliation [1159B] when minds before at discord are called back, through some mediator, into peace and the favour of friendship; and the kiss that is shown outwardly is the sign of that confederation and conjunction of minds which is made within. But it is a kiss of congratulation when we receive with congratulation kinsmen long absent, and friends returned from pilgrimage or from elsewhere, and show the certainty of our inward joy by the very kiss that we give outwardly. But the kiss of the Bridegroom to the Bride is a kiss of a certain sanctified pleasure. There is therefore a threefold kiss, for which the church sighs, and which every faithful soul desires to be given her: for the holy church longs for reconciliation, longs for congratulation, [1159C] and longs also for that holy gladness which shall be without end. For while the Word was not yet incarnate, there was no peace of the church with God, which, after this life had slipped away, was drawn wholly into hell.

For man made himself wholly liable to eternal death, that is, in his stock and in his offspring, when he scorned to obey his author, from whom he had lately received so many and so great things, him (I say) who not only willed and commanded, but even held out threats, in a matter most trifling. For although it be no great proof of love if a man obey in a small thing, yet it is a great sign of an irreverent mind, and a token of the utmost contempt, if a man scorn to obey in a small matter: for according to the Truth, « He that is not faithful in a little, how [1159D] shall he be faithful in a great matter? » [n: (Luc. XVI, 10.)] By how much the less, therefore, was the thing in which man scorned to obey, by so much the greater is the sin that he incurred, and the greater the punishment that he deserved. For he who offends against an eternal Judge, without doubt what he deserved was unto eternity; for that judicial power which lacks an end inflicts a punishment without end: and as man deserved good without end at the hand of the Lord, when he departed from the truth. For as the good angel was first light, and afterwards a firmament, when he did not consent to him who desired to set his seat toward the North [n: (Isa. XIV, 13)]: so man too, if he had resisted the temptation, would by that very fact have deserved to be confirmed against temptation and sin, so that thereafter he could neither have sinned nor have been tempted; but, the number of days appointed him being here innocently [1160A] fulfilled, and the tree of life tasted, he would have come to an equality with the angels, he who had created him working mightily in this also. As, therefore, the reward of obedience would have been eternal, so, by the even scale of justice, an eternal punishment was owed to disobedience; and to it man, after his sin, being wholly liable, fell wholly into hell.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

For hence the prophet says, « Mercy and truth have met each other [n: (Psal. LXXXIV, 11)], » which is the same as if it were said, mercy and justice began to come each against the other; for they that meet each other come from opposite sides, and tend as it were in contrary directions; so mercy and truth, straightway after the sin, began to tend in different ways: truth was exacting man for punishment, while mercy [1160B] was disposing something better for his repair. And this was a wonderful meeting, justice dragging man to the lower regions, and the other desiring to bring man back to the things he had lost. But as justice prevailed, man was given over to punishments, tasting miseries and hardships and death in the world, corruption in the sepulchre, and after all things, darkness in hell. And when satisfaction had now been made to justice in great part, the fault having been long punished, after many courses of years, place at length was given on high to mercy, which was ever knocking and pressing hard, in the hidden and secret places of that holy and highest Trinity itself; where it was disposed as wonderfully as devoutly, that in him in whom the Father had created all things, in him he should restore the things that were lost. This counsel [1160C] is that before [cj: *antequam*; read *antiquum*, "ancient"], of which the prophet says, « Let thy ancient counsel, O Lord, be made true. » This is that which, « hidden from the ages [n: (Col. I, 26)], » Paul calls a « sacrament. » The Son therefore obeyed the Father, and so much the more wonderfully and the more sacredly, in that it was not a younger obeying an elder, not a lesser a greater, but one almighty, great, himself great, immeasurable, almighty, equal in all things, who obeyed; as it is said in the prophet, « In the head of the book it is written of me, that I should do thy will [n: (Psal. XXXIX, 9)]. » For great is the book of the knowledge and wisdom of God, in which, though thou shouldst read for ever, no end of it ever comes: its inner pages he alone who wrote it has looked upon. In this both men and angels advance without end; and that treasure of God, however many [1160D] be for ever drawing from it, can never be drained: from end to end his wisdom alone and highest reaches. In this all men are written, and the days are formed, and the merits of each are comprehended, and the blessedness due to every one is contained expressly.

In the head of this book, as in its chief part, is written this sacrament of the Incarnation, in that among all the things which God has wonderfully wrought, in creating each thing, in conserving what he has created, and in disposing by a wonderful law the things he conserves, nothing is so astonishing or so wonderful as that, by his own self-emptying, at the command of commanding mercy, he seems to have been visited with injury to himself. But « the works of the Lord are great, sought out according to all his [1161A] wills [n: (Psal. CX, 2)], and his judgments are true, justified in themselves [n: (Psal. XVIII, 10)]. » God therefore, ascending the cross, and spreading out his innocent hands to the nails, as he consecrated the place of death unto life, so those who were held in death he called back to life. For as his blood dripped from the cross the rocks were cleft, that a bodily entrance might lie open by which that most sacred price, received by angelic hands, might be borne down to the lower world; that, the fruit of the tree of life being shared, they might run back into the liberty of life who were held in hell under the faith and at once the expectation of this grace. What is there, then, of which justice may now complain? In paradise a tree was set, « good to eat of, fair to the eyes, and delightful to behold; and the woman took of the fruit thereof, and did eat; and she gave to her [1161B] husband, who did eat [n: (Gen. III, 6)]: » and disobedience being thus consummated, the way to death was prepared: in the Church too the tree of the cross was set up, whose aspect was horrible and whose taste most bitter; its fruit was that temporal death by which the guilty were punished, who for divers crimes were condemned to the cross: to this tree that eternal wisdom stretched out its hands, and tasted this fruit, with no serpent suggesting it, nor woman persuading. Once indeed the serpent had done this: that the woman, coveting what she ought not, should be delighted, as the flesh is; that the man, as it were the rational sense, should acquiesce in the same; and so it had prepared for itself a way by which it might glide into the hearts of the immortal, and draw all things to itself: but [1161C] at the cross all that is his is made void; the serpent suggests nothing to Christ; here the flesh is not delighted; the rational sense itself, in the spirit, draws the flesh after it, to endure the hard things of the cross and to taste the bitter things of death. Virtue, therefore, resides in the cross; and, since he ascended it who is the virtue and wisdom of God, both virtue and wisdom are placed in it. This opens the way of virtue, which, although it be narrow, yet leads to life. The tree of paradise, then, put forth a fruit by which both the suggestion might be received, and concupiscence be aroused by certain enticements as it were: the cross, now set in the bosom of the Church, assuredly presents a fruit which, if thou shalt taste it in the mind, thou dost not succumb, neither with the enemy suggesting within, nor with the flesh enticing thee; but the enemy is conquered in his first encounter, and the flesh is at once called back to serve the spirit, [1161D] and the whole man is brought back into the love of consummated virtue. This, then, is the place in which mercy, which is true peace, kisses justice; for here satisfaction is made to both at once, since both man is redeemed, mercy so working, and to justice far more is paid than was owed; and, according to the Apostle, « The handwriting that was written against us is mightily nailed to the cross of Christ, and blotted out [n: (Col. II, 14)]. » For a handwriting is so drawn up that, after it has been cut and divided into parts, in each part of it the full record of the transaction is retained. Such is our condition with the malignant enemy: for when we sin, [1162A] the fault is with us, gravely engraved by the very pen of the perverse deed, and it is straightway taken up by the memory of the ancient enemy, and firmly retained as impressed there; so that, if there be none to redeem, none to save, there may always be at hand for him something to lay to our charge: which handwriting indeed has been blotted out by the death of Christ, through which both the fault is remitted to us, and his accusation against us is no longer feared.

But what is this, since man was held to an eternal punishment, but the Son of God bore only a temporal death for forty hours? how was satisfaction made, in his death, to the divine justice, which exacted eternal punishments from man? But [sic: *proculdio*] satisfaction was made. For let there be weighed in the balance the very death of the Son of God, momentary if you please, and the eternal death of all men, [1162B] however much it be owed; to whom is it doubtful that the death of the only-begotten, though temporal, outweighs the death, though eternal, of whatsoever mortals? For deservedly it is a greater thing, and by nature more wonderful, that God should die, who is liable to no sin nor to death, than that a multitude however great of mortals and sinners should die. For hence blessed Job says, « Would that my sins, whereby I have deserved wrath, and the calamity that I suffer, were weighed in a balance *(Job VI, 2)*. » That Job, which is « sorrowing, » designates the Lord Jesus, is now doubtful to none: he, pleading our cause before the Father, as set upon the cross, says, « Would that my sins were weighed; » naming as his own, indeed, our sins, whereby we have deserved wrath, in the unity [1162C] and sacrament of the body and the head, and saying: Would that thou wouldst consider, Father, the sins whereby man has deserved wrath, and the calamity, that is, the punishment which I suffer upon the cross, made man unto obedience to thee. For if thou consider both, as truly thou dost consider, my punishment, like the sand of the sea, will appear the heavier, that is, my punishment together with obedience will far outweigh the sins of men; and it will be enough for thee, for the expiation of his fault and for the discharge of the punishment owed by him. Holy Church, therefore, understanding by the spirit with which she was enlightened that this reconciliation was to be given through the hand of the mediator, the Lord Jesus, amid the soliloquies with which the spirit cherished her and kindled her holy love, within the delays of a hope long continued, repeated this Song of love in the hearts of the elect [1162D] with frequent sighs, so as to say, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » Understanding, indeed, in him who kisses, the Father, by whom the Son was sent for this grace; and designating, in the mouth itself, which after the usual manner is put forth for a kiss, the Son who was sent, who is the mouth and the wisdom of the Father; and expressing, in the kiss itself, which is the pleasing sweetness of those who kiss, the Holy Spirit, who is the love of both: she professed, as most truly as most devoutly, the faith of the holy and highest Trinity, and the incarnation of the Word that was to come, and, through the sacraments of the incarnate Word, the full reconciliation of man with God. For what is more devout than this holy supplication? What can be purer [1163A] than this prayer? In the very beginnings we are at once taught how prayer is to be made, and in what measure supplication is to be offered to the Most High: for « without faith it is impossible to please God [n: (Heb. XI, 6)]; » and the prayer that is without faith he cannot hear. Whence James says, « But let every one ask in faith, nothing wavering [n: (Jac. I, 6)]: » for it is faith that first opens the entrance for prayer into the inner sanctuary of the exalted majesty. Whence she who desires to be heard begins from the Sacraments of faith, so as to say, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth; » as if she said: In that I desire to be heard, I stand forward to ask in this faith; in the very substance of the deity I confess unity, in the same I confess and adore the Trinity of persons. It is a confession of unity, that which was said, « Let him kiss; » but of the Trinity, that one who kisses, and a mouth, and a kiss [1163B] are named. The one who kisses is the Father himself: the mouth itself which is put forth for kissing is the Son: and the kiss which is completed by the mouth of him who kisses is the Holy Spirit. Which truth indeed neither dissipates the very substance of the Trinity, nor tears asunder that of the Unity. He indeed who kisses is neither his own mouth by which he kisses, nor the kiss itself which is ministered by his mouth. So too the mouth itself is neither he who kisses, nor the kiss itself which is given. The kiss also itself is neither he who kisses, nor the mouth itself from which it is. Yet far be it that, when we see a kiss given by someone, we should therefore understand three substances: for the substance of him who kisses is one; and both the mouth itself and the kiss are not indeed in another, but only in that very substance of him who kisses. So [1163C] the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit, since they are most truly three Persons, of whom none is the other: this truth of the Trinity takes nothing away from the unity, or from the simplicity of the substance of God; even as that simplicity itself neither adds anything to, nor lessens, the Trinity of the Persons. But because it is not enough to believe divine things concerning God, unless his humble things also be most truly believed, she uses a most exquisite word, saying, « A kiss; » designating most fully by this both the Word that was to be incarnate, and the sacrament of human reconciliation that was to come. And since these things have been thus said, let us, the Lord helping, renew both our spirit and our pen for the treatise following.

*Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

[1163D]We observe that it was not any one just man from Adam down to Christ, but all men throughout the world far and wide, into whom the grace of the Holy Spirit had poured itself, who understood that from the seed of Adam one would at some time be born who, out of the trespass of one, would at some time destroy that same thing on behalf of all his members, and, the kingdom being handed over to the Father, would make men equal to the Angels. This faith, then, and this revelation in the spirit, kindled the desires of the Saints and drew their minds forward into the ages to come, so that they reckoned the delay of this life long, by which so great an expectation was deferred, and short as well, as though it were to be ended before they should behold this with bodily eyes. All these, therefore, whom one faith governed and hope urged on, that is, the whole multitude of those who believe devoutly, Solomon (in [1164A] whom wisdom had established a seat for herself), regarding them by that very light with which he was illumined, brings in, as a Bride, set in longings for the Bridegroom long since promised her, uttering the words of her desire in this manner: « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » By the threefold repetition, in which it is said, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth, » he marks a threefold kiss which she shows that she desires: the first of reconciliation, as has been said; the second of congratulation; the third, that of everlasting gladness. Reconciliation the Church desired, she who had learned that she had wholly sinned in Adam; and this, because she knew that it could be brought about by no other than by the Only-begotten of the Father himself, that is, by her Bridegroom. She desires that the Father kiss her, which is, that he remit his anger to her by the kiss of his mouth, that is, by the incarnation [1164B] of his Only-begotten, who is both the mouth and the Word of the Father. Whence it is also expressly said that the Father kisses, but with the kiss of the Son; because when the Father saves, he saves those whom he saves not indeed by his own incarnation, but by that of his Son. Yet since the Father also truly wrought that incarnation (seeing that the working of the whole Trinity is inseparable), therefore rightly, since it is a kiss of the mouth, the Father is thereupon said to « kiss », that is, to work in the Son that very assumption of flesh which he does not have in himself.

These things, then, having been said concerning the kiss of reconciliation, let us come, with the Lord's help, to the searching out of that which is called the kiss of congratulation. For usage has it so, and Scripture often calls it to mind. For Isaac says [1164C] to his son, « Come near to me, and give me a kiss, my son; and he came near, and kissed him [n: (Gen. XXVII, 26, 27)]. » It is read also of Jacob that, when he had seen Leah and had known that she was his cousin, « he removed the stone with which the well was closed, and, the flock being watered, he kissed her, and lifting up his voice he wept [n: (Gen. XXIX, 10, 11)]. » Laban also, « when he had heard that Jacob had come, ran to meet him, and embracing him and rushing into kisses, led him into his house [n: (ibid., 13)]. » Of Jacob too, when he had returned from Mesopotamia of Syria after that long sojourning, it is read that « Esau, running to meet his brother, embraced him, and clasping his neck and kissing him, wept [n: (Gen. XXXIII, 4)], » Joseph likewise, « when in his embrace he had fallen back upon the neck [1164D] of Benjamin his brother, wept, and kissed all his brethren, and lamented over each one [n: (Gen. XLV, 14, 15)]. » The prodigal son too, « when he was yet far off, his father saw him, and was moved with mercy, and running to him fell upon his neck, and kissed him [n: (Luc. XV, 20)]. » By all which, without doubt, is understood the kiss of congratulation, by which the parents and kinsfolk of their own congratulated them upon their successes and applauded them inwardly, showing outwardly by the kiss the joy which they cherished within. And this kiss of congratulation the Church hopes and expects to be given to her: which will be on that last day, when the merits of life shall be set forth, and the Lord Jesus shall render to each according as he has done in life, whether good or evil. For when the lost sheep has then been carried back to the folds on the shoulders [1165A] of the good shepherd, his friends and neighbours will be called together, the holy angels, to be sure, who keep his will with them in unbroken steadfastness, and for this reason are friends; and who enjoy his vision without ceasing, and for this reason are rightly named neighbours; to whom he will say, « Congratulate me, because I have found my sheep which was lost [n: (Luc. XV, 6)]. » which he is to say in like manner of the lost drachma as well.

This will be the highest congratulation, when not only shall that heavenly Church of blessed spirits applaud the good shepherd, but she also who shall have been carried back upon the shoulders of his humanity will offer her congratulation to the Bridegroom, who rejoices over her restoration, and to the multitude of the supernal virtues, which rejoices likewise. There will be fulfilled on that very day [1165B] what is contained in the history: for Joseph, making himself known to his brethren, « commanded that all should go forth out of doors, and that no stranger should be present at their mutual recognition. And he lifted up his voice with weeping, and said to his brethren, I am Joseph; and he kissed them [n: (Gen. XLV, 1)]. » Strangers will they be on that day to whom the Lord is to say, « Depart from me, ye cursed, into the everlasting fire, which is prepared for the devil and his Angels [n: (Matth. XXV, 41)]. » When these have been separated on that day, the Lord Jesus will make himself known to his brethren, saying, « I am Joseph; » as if he should plainly say: Call back to memory how, being sold and set in the prison of death, I came forth thence by rising again; and now behold openly that I both possess the sceptres of the kingdom and, [1165C] being thus endowed by the Father, hold the summit of judiciary power over every creature. Here the Bridegroom will congratulate the Bride, and the Bride the Bridegroom; and the indivisible joining of their minds will be made solid in a perpetual covenant. The Bride will congratulate the Bridegroom, because after the infirmities of the flesh, after the prison, after the scourges, after the buffets, after the spittle, after the nails, after death, after the sepulchre, after the great stone rolled to it, he has been so far exalted that in the flesh he took upon him he judges all flesh, and bends to his glory every knee of things heavenly, earthly, and infernal. But the Bridegroom's congratulation toward the Bride will be, that she has not run at uncertainty, that she has by no means fought in vain; that, death being triumphed over and Satan trodden down beneath his feet, he has gained the Church for himself the more gloriously by how much the more laboriously, [1165D] and has already endowed her, so gained, with unspeakable glory: of which indeed the good one will on that day applaud both all and each. For what is that giving of thanks by the Lord, in which he is to say on that day, « I was hungry, and ye gave me to eat [n: (Matth. XXV, 35)], » and the rest in this manner, if not a certain congratulation of the Judge himself and Bridegroom upon those inward goods of his Bride? For it is divided into six clauses, the very perfection of the number designating the whole fullness of all goods; by which in the present life every faithful soul pleases the Lord; by which also the Church wholly conforms herself to her Bridegroom. For the works are mercy: that the poor man be fed, and given drink, and the naked clothed, and the guest received under a roof, the sick visited, and [1166A] the imprisoned. But is God well pleased in these alone? Is not every several virtue pleasing to him? For what of that holy virginity? what of honourable chastity? what of the frugality of the Saints? what of silence? what of holy preaching? what of justice, fortitude, temperance, and prudence? what of the virtues beyond number, whose praise is in the Holy Scriptures, in the acquiring of which the continual and unremitting labour of the Saints sweats? But if the Lord is well pleased in these, what is it that in the judgment, when he shall congratulate the Church upon her goods, the Lord will call to mind these alone which were bodily performed? They are indeed goods, but not the only ones.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

It stands, therefore, that in the clause of words already spoken all the endowments of the soul, all the goods of the Church generally, [1166B] are comprehended. For the Lord hungers and thirsts: yet in saying this we subject the holy Godhead to no suffering; for the life of that supreme Godhead itself is incapable of suffering; and by as much as temporal life is subject and liable to suffering, by so much is that eternal and uncreated life utterly far from and a stranger to all suffering. Yet after the manner of the Scriptures he is said to hunger and to thirst; and not in one way only, but in several. For the Lord hungers in his members, and he hungers in himself. In his members he hungers, when any one of his own is brought to the necessity of bodily or spiritual food: which suffering charity relieves, to be sure, with the various works of mercy, refreshing with food and drink him who is wasted, and with spiritual things instructing [1166C] and building up him who desires the spiritual: for as it is a devout thing to relieve bodily hunger and thirst, so it is most devout to relieve the want of spiritual food as well: for as by the judgment of truth the soul excels the body, so it is far better, far holier, to relieve her than the body. Yet both are good, both are well pleasing to the Lord, even unto thanksgiving. Like is the consideration concerning nakedness also. The Lord is cold in body, and he is cold in spirit: in body, when the harshness of the cold is driven off by no covering of clothing; in spirit, when a man is clothed within by no garment of virtue. This cold is from the North wind, by which the hearts of many are not only chilled but hardened [n: (Job XXXVII, 9; Eccli. XLIII, 22)]; of which the prophet says, « Before the face of his cold who shall stand? » *(Psal. CXLVII, [1166D] 17.)* This nakedness of Christ in his members he covers, who forms and instructs by the examples of the virtues the man destitute of virtue. To whom the Lord, congratulating him, is to say at the end, « I was naked, and ye covered me. » But in what manner the Lord is a stranger and a guest in his members is plain; while in himself he is a stranger and a guest among those who have not yet received the faith which is in him: to such as these he himself also says, « The foxes have holes, and the birds of the air nests: but the Son of man hath not where to lay his head [n: (Matth. VIII, 30)]; » because unclean and deceitful spirits, possessing them, dwell in the darkened hearts of sinners; « but the Son of man hath not where to lay his head. » For the head of the Church is Christ; and the head of Christ is God; and with those [1167A] the Son of man lays his head who confess him equal to the Father, coeternal, and consubstantial: to whom the Son himself is neither a recent God nor an alien one (for to those he is recent who protest that he once began, and was not begotten of the Father from eternity; and to those he is alien who attempt to affirm that he is not the same in substance with the Father). Among these he is a guest and a stranger; to all of whom it is said by the prophet, « Let the deceitful lips be made dumb, which speak iniquity against the just, in pride and in abuse [n: (Psal. XXX, 19)]. » Among these he is a guest and a stranger, knocking by the prophets, by the apostles, by all the Scriptures, that they may open to him; to whom the Lord is to say on that day, « I was a guest, and ye took me not in *(Matth. XXV, [1167B] 43)*, » because the faith which is in me, which I preach, unto which I was sent, ye did not admit. Whence it is given us to understand who they are that receive the Saviour into their lodging: those, without doubt, who receive the teaching of sound faith with worthy veneration, utterly execrating heresy, and, simple as doves, gathering only the clean grains of faith; for whom indeed it is not enough to receive the Saviour under their roof by faith only, unless they receive him also with charity and virtue of every kind For he himself says, « Behold, I stand at the door knocking; if any man shall hear my voice, and open the gate, I will come in to him, and will sup with him, and he with me [n: (Apoc. III, 20)]. » The Lord enters in to us when we ourselves open the gate of the heart to believing: we are refreshed by a common supper when, love having been conceived from above, we are stretched out toward [1167C] every good work by the gift of spiritual grace. But to visit the sick, both brotherly charity and divine authority admonish us. For what is more fitting to humanity than that man should relieve man, brother brother, the strong the weak, both with the solace of a word and with the help of a work, with devout affection and full compassion of mind? And this is to be shown not only to him who is sick in body, but to him who is ailing spiritually. For in many the languor of the spirit so gathers strength that in them there is now neither the voice of confession nor the feeling of one repenting. Of these the Apostle says, « Who is weak, and I am not weak? Who is scandalized, and I am not burned? » [n: (II Cor. XI, 29.)] These are to be visited often, and admonished with the word of life, [1167D] until, gaping seven times, they may open their mouth unto confession by the gift of the sevenfold Spirit, and may be conformed to the very teachers of life, not only by growing warm within, but also by the full ordering of their outward man. For hence it is that when Elias raised up the dead man [n: (III Reg. XVII, 21)], he spread himself over him; and Elisha, when he raised up the dead man, « applies his own mouth to his mouth, and his eyes to his eyes, and his hands to his hands [n: (IV Reg. IV, 34)]. » So that, as the Apostle says, those who are raised from death, by imitating the holy teachers, may now compel all the members of their body which before had been « vessels of iniquity unto iniquity [var: Vulg. *arma iniquitatis*, "weapons of iniquity" (Rom. VI, 13)], » to serve virtue Of these the Truth itself says, [1168A] « The healthy have no need of a physician, but they that are ill [n: (Matth. IX, 12)]. » Some of these indeed are wearied by lighter sicknesses, others by heavier and long-lasting ones. And so these, whom the oldness of sin oppresses, a certain prison of souls, as it were, shuts in; whose person the prophet takes upon himself when he says, « Bring my soul out of prison [n: (Psal. CXLI, 8)]; » and that other word, « I am stuck fast in the mire of the deep [n: (Psal. LXVIII, 13)]; » and again, « The cords of sinners have wrapped me about [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 61)]: » and to visit these in a spirit of gentleness, to prick them with the word of life, and, drawing them away from vices, to restore them to virtue, is a far greater mercy than to bring out those shut up in body by devoutly loosing their chains.

Since therefore there are two commandments, one pertaining to the love [1168B] of God, the other to the love of one's neighbour, on which the whole law hangs and the prophets, and in which the perfection of human life consists, the clause spoken above embraces in itself the whole by which either commandment is fulfilled. For by those things which are shown to our neighbours with a regard to the Godhead are to be understood all things by which we relieve any necessity whatever of theirs, spiritual or bodily: for among sufferings there is nothing more bitter than hunger, thirst, nakedness, want of shelter, sickness, chains: whence by these things, which press and afflict any brother more vehemently, are to be understood absolutely all things by which, at the working of mercy, with a regard to the Godhead, we relieve any bodily or spiritual necessity that demands it. For in his members, in two ways, as [1168C] has been said, the Lord hungers and thirsts and is cold, desiring now a bodily, now a spiritual remedy; but in himself in one way only: for he does not require this alone of us, that we bestow on our brethren what is needful; he wills that our faith and charity be directed principally upon himself, and be derived from him secondarily upon our neighbour. Those things, then, which have regard to our neighbour, these the Lord hungers and thirsts for in his members, and demands; but those which are not toward our neighbour, but are kept for reverence of Christ, these are they which the Lord is said to hunger and thirst for in himself, since with him this is the same thing as to will. « And the Lord, coming to the fig tree, hungered [n: (Matth. XXI, 18)]; » and hanging on the cross he said, « I thirst [n: (Joan. XIX, 28)]. » What [1168D] did he hunger or thirst for? Our salvation, beyond doubt, our faith, and the charity which is toward him, long-suffering, and continence, and the other virtues which, although they do not reach toward our neighbour, are kept for God's sake: for the virtues which are toward our neighbour he hungers for both in himself and in his members; but these which are toward himself, in himself alone.

It stands, therefore, that in the clause of words already spoken all the endowments of the soul, all the goods of the Church generally, are comprehended. And this is the kiss of congratulation for which the Church sighs, which every soul asks in the Spirit, saying, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » Thus she speaks to the Father, his mouth, according to this [1169A] sense. Naming the Son, and understanding by the kiss both of the Father himself and of the Son the Holy Spirit. For she says, « Let him kiss me, » that is, let the Father, he who created me, make me worthy of the kiss; and this, « With the kiss of his mouth, » that is, through the Holy Spirit of his Son, who is his mouth. For that Spirit is himself the kiss which the Father gives, the kiss which the Son gives; because he is a vapour, and a sweetness proceeding from both: who, as he proceeds from both, so both the Father and the Son pour him forth into the hearts of the blessed. For of him the Lord says, « I will send you the Spirit [n: (Joan. XV, 26)]: » and elsewhere, « The Spirit whom the Father sends in my name [n: (Joan. XIV, 26)]. » She asks therefore of the Father that the Spirit be given her through the Son, so that, receiving from him the gifts of graces, she may pass over into a certain new comeliness, [1169B] that on that day she may hear from the Father through the Son the voice of congratulation, and receive the kiss of congratulation. For it is the voice of congratulation by which the Lord is to say on that day, « Well done, good servant [n: (Matth. XV, 6)]; » and that word to the heavenly Virtues, « Congratulate me over the lost sheep. Congratulate me over the drachma that is found [n: (Luc. XV, 6)]; » as if he should say: Consider what the comeliness of the Church is, and how great; understand with how many gifts of graces, with how great a glory of virtues she shines; rejoice together with me that I have laboured for one such and so great. What (I ask) will be the peace of the whole Church at this voice? what will be the tranquillity of holy souls in mind? when at this congratulation of the Bridegroom, now secure concerning her own glory, [1169C] there will be nothing more for her to fear, nothing at which for the rest she should shudder. And this peace indeed is that security for which the Church sighs, that on that day, on which the angels shall tremble and the archangels be greatly afraid, she may have her Bridegroom appeased toward her, and, sanctified by his Spirit, may be pleasing to herself in the comeliness of the virtues; and through the kiss of the Son, which is the Holy Spirit, may receive from the Bridegroom peace, congratulation, and the gifts promised: of which may the Lord Jesus make us partakers, who is unto the ages. Amen.

*Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

The kiss of reconciliation, therefore, and the kiss of congratulation having been set forth, it remains that we pursue, with the Lord's help, the kiss of pleasure. And lest this be perhaps troublesome to delicate ears, [1169D] if in treating of spiritual things we call to mind a certain bodily pleasure, let them have this before their eyes: that it is an epithalamium of the Bridegroom and the Bride that is being enacted, that it is a nuptial [sic: *carnem*] that is being handled: nor is it a wonder if in a marriage, in the Sacrament of spiritual things, mention be made of this kiss; since the carnal commingling itself of Bridegroom and Bride unfolds in the Holy Scriptures no small Sacrament among things spiritual. For that carnal commingling is a Sacrament of that joining which is in the Lord Jesus, namely of flesh and of the Word: so also that kiss of the Bridegroom and the Bride is a certain manifest showing forth of that gladness which, after that dreadful judgment and [1170A] the heavenly Bridegroom's congratulation upon the goods of the Bride, is to be forever. For pleasure is of one sort foul, and of another most holy: and that indeed is holy of which the Prophet makes mention, saying, « They shall be inebriated with the plenty of thy house; and thou shalt give them to drink of the torrent of thy pleasure [n: (Psal. XXXV, 9)]: » which pleasure indeed is called a « torrent », not because it passes quickly away, but because it pours itself in abundantly. For of this also « Isaias says, The eye hath not seen, besides thee, O God, what things thou hast prepared for them that love thee [var: Vulg. *expectantibus te*, "them that wait for thee"] [n: (Isa. LIV, 4)]. » According to which « Paul » says, « Neither hath the eye seen, nor hath it ascended into the heart of man, what things God hath prepared for them that love him [n: (I Cor. II, 9)]. » Whence the Prophet also fittingly says, « They shall be inebriated with the plenty of thy house: » [1170B] for drunkenness changes the state of the mind and in a manner estranges it from itself; so in that glory there will be no small change of the mind. In this life a certain affection of the flesh draws us toward parents and kinsfolk, acquaintances and well-wishers; in that life all this will be swallowed up; the whole of love will be stretched out toward God, the whole mind will gather itself into him; she will not know a father, she will be ignorant of a mother also; she will be touched with affection toward no one save him whom she has known to be conformed to Christ, whom she has perceived to be joined and united to his body: « for the Lord Jesus shall deliver up the kingdom to God and the Father, » and he himself shall be « all things in all [n: (I Cor. XV, 24, 28)]. » In this life indeed we are carried down by a certain propagation of the flesh through father and mother; and thus many of us are joined to one another by a certain kinship of blood and affection among ourselves: [1170C] but in the regeneration nothing is owed to flesh or to blood. For what father shall raise thee up out of the dust? What mother shall lead thee through from death to life? The order of things shall be led back to its own beginning. The first man was created out of the earth, and the woman formed out of his bone; and by a certain flowing forth of blood all men were propagated from them.

The first creation, therefore, of men was out of the earth; the second, out of a man; the third, out of a man and a woman; the fourth, in the Lord Jesus, not out of a man, but out of a woman: but in the regeneration that alone will be which went before in Adam. There will now be nothing in it which we shall owe either to a woman or to a man. He who formed Adam out of the earth will on that day raise up all men out of the dust; so that [1170D] we may now owe the whole to our creation, nothing to the flesh. And as Adam was debtor and bound to the Lord alone for the whole of his affection and love, and in every good, bodily as well as spiritual, so altogether on that day let us owe anything to none but the Lord. He will be to us father and mother and brother and sister, and in the truth of the flesh raised again the vigour of the spirit shall possess all things; there will now be nowhere a father according to the flesh, or a mother, nor will there be able to be any other degrees whatever of kinship according to the flesh. All these things having now been exchanged in a happy commerce, so that in place of those which we had in the flesh we may now have God as father even according to the flesh; that he himself may be for us [1171A] a sufficient pleasure, and « all things in all. » For if thou turn the mind's eye toward spiritual things, thou wilt find the same more abundantly. For here man is delighted with gold, he is delighted with silver and with precious stone, with the affection of his children, with the abundance of crops and fruits, with the subjection of birds and beasts and fishes, and with this, that the round world lies open and subject to him far and wide: this is a great pleasure, but to senses imperfect and as yet little enlightened. The glitter of gold dazzles the eye, but the eye not yet enlightened. If thou hold out gold to a glorified man, thou dost not move or delight him. The glorified eye gazes inwardly: in gazing upon the creature it beholds and contemplates him who presides over the creature.
