To me as I gaze, the body which is without is set in my way, and what is within is hidden: [1171B] such is not the vision of a glorified man: for him the creature does not hide the Creator, but opens the soul through the body; in the soul it opens the mind, in the mind the memory, the understanding, and the reason, and all the virtues also which grace the mother forms within: nor does he behold only the effect of the work, but he also gazes upon that hand which wondrously forms all things. He beholds that supernal wisdom working wondrously in bodies, and far more wondrously in spirits; and while he wonders at the work, he wonders far more at the workman, who mightily holds all things together. He wonders at that highest and superessential Spirit poured into every creature, immeasurable and simple; granting to some to be, not to live; to others life, but without sense; to others sense, but devoid of reason; to others [1171C] reason, but hindered by the admixture of the body; to others a substance wrapped in no commingling of body. And considering in all these a venerable loveliness and an unspeakable comeliness, he wonders nevertheless at the art itself more in the artificer than at the whole of this which he observes in the order of the creature: fixing the mind's eye upon whom, he flies away as into the deep of the sea, contemplating in the Godhead itself such things as neither the tongue can tell nor even a holy mind can conceive in the present life. The heaven is moved, the stars are borne in their circuit, the planets are moved in their circles, the air is moved by the blowing of the spirits appointed to it, the sea, binding the earth about, renders it unmoved: in these the living creatures walk about in varied forms and figures, the kinds of metals [1171D] gleam, the pleasing sweetness of fruits smiles upon us, and the colour, smell, and taste of things beyond number strike the senses of mortals with their great variety, and in all these there is a most beautiful order, praiseworthy by the merit of that wisdom which so disposes all things. But the glorified spirit, running through in mind all the forms in matter and the whole comeliness of the creature, enters that holy secret place of the highest Godhead; where it looks upon the causes of all things, and at last upon the whole creation, as it is in the mind and ever lives, far more fully than it would look upon it while it slipped away under time. And so, entering into the treasures of wisdom and knowledge, it finds in overflowing measure all that can adorn and beatify the mind; so that nothing is now lacking to it in any grace, but it says confidently [1172A] now to the Lord Jesus, « Thy grace is sufficient for me [var: Vulg. *sufficit tibi gratia mea*, "my grace is sufficient for thee"] [n: (II Cor. XII, 9)]. » For man, gathered within the wave of the Godhead, his mortal part having been swallowed up by that magnificent glory, overflows with peace and tranquillity and with an abundance of all things desirable. So, that superessential and uncreated Spirit pouring himself into each of the elect, that in all he himself may be all things, in that he is in himself and from himself the unfailing sufficiency of all things that can be desired, both for each and for all. This is the hope of the saints, this their expectation, this that end at which, when any soul has arrived, it will rejoice that it has now most blessedly completed its course. This is the kiss of pleasure which, out of the place of pleasure which the Lord had planted from the beginning, the holy Church desires to be given her, which every faithful soul desires to be given her, [1172B] saying, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » And so in a wondrous manner, at the very beginning of the work she makes mention at once of the end, saying, « Let him kiss me: » so that she may bear witness that he toward whom she burns with desire, who is the beginning and the end of all things, is beginning and end for her also; to whom may the Lord Jesus lead us, to whom with the Father and the Holy Spirit be glory, honour, and dominion unto the ages of ages. Amen.

*Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*

These are the words of the Bride, with which she addressed the Bridegroom above: the words which follow are the Bridegroom's, which he joins on to those at once, saying:

*For thy breasts are better than wine. 2. Fragrant with the best ointments.*



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Let us see how these things cohere with those that have been said. [1172C] The Bridegroom marks at once her who asks the kiss and burns with this zeal of spirit: and because hope that is deferred afflicts the soul, lest it afflict her and, by drawing out her hope at length, defer it, he answers her thus as she desires: what thou askest shall be done, « For thy breasts are better than wine. » Which is as if the Bridegroom should say within himself: Thou indeed, my beloved, thou who art joined to me in a heavenly wedlock, not uselessly, nor as one barren, but as one who art fit to bear sons and to nourish them that they may be brought to perfection, from this, that thou hast breasts made fruitful by the heavenly dew. These things are joined thus, lest the reading of the Holy Spirit should seem less coherent with itself, and as it were unstitched. Henceforth let us see what the things that have been said signify. Yet first [1172D] it must be asked: why does the praise of the Bride begin from the breasts? Why does the Bridegroom himself keep silence about the praise of the other things which stand out as worthy of praise? For why is the face adorned with tresses not praised, why the cheeks, why the lips, why the eyes, which he afterwards calls dove-like? and why is the whole praise of the Bride contained first of all about her breasts? This indeed is so because, whereas in the Bride, as has been said, many things please the Bridegroom, there is nothing in which he takes more delight than that she herself should become a mother, should nourish peoples, and, made fruitful by the heavenly dew, should minister with breasts full of the Holy Spirit holy teachings, as it were the nourishment of milk, to sons beyond number. This, then, is that in which the Bride pleases him more, in which she is more at the Bridegroom's heart, whence she is the more praised, and is extolled by him as with [sic: *praecontis*]. Shall we turn aside to the breasts, [1173A] and consider what the breasts of the Church are? The breasts (*ubera*), then, are so called from plenty (*ubertas*), because into them there flows a certain great store of milk, which we suck, and by which we are fed, and by which we are sustained while we pass through the years of infancy. As these are fixed in the breast, so to the mind of the Church, who is the Bride, two Testaments are set forth, that she may meditate upon them day and night, and gather in them an abundance of milk with which she may feed peoples beyond number, may nourish them, and lead them through even to perfection. For some milk is sweet, some sour; yet each stands good and full of savour. What is sweeter than to believe one God, and to love him with the whole heart, the whole soul, the whole mind, not to harm one's neighbour, to abstain from murder, adultery, and theft, and false witness, and from all those things [1173B] which are forbidden by the Law? But to those who sin willingly and maliciously the Law itself sets forth sour milk, adding this: « Let a man render soul for soul, eye for eye, tooth for tooth, hand for hand, foot for foot, burning for burning, wound for wound, bruise for bruise [n: (Exod. XXI, 23, 24, 25)]. » Which milk indeed seems sour to him who suffers, but to a good King, and to a Prince established according to the fear of God, it is a soothing milk and most sweet to the taste; because, « when the pestilent man is scourged, the fool will be the wiser [n: (Prov. XIX, 25)]. » And this is the will of the Lord, that one man in the people restrain many, and so punish those who sin maliciously that the Church of God may rejoice in much peace, and may shrink from having any of her sons made sorrowful by the sour taste of the milk. [1173C] But the substance of this milk of which we treat is that which is resolved into butter, and into curd, into cheese, and whey: these declare the degrees of the virtues, by which one must ascend from the least to the perfect. For the whey (*serum*), which is gathered late (*sero*) from the milk, is itself the fear of the Lord, of which it is said, « The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord [n: (Psal. CX, 10)]. » From this there is an ascent into repentance of heart, and confession of mouth, and satisfaction of work: so that thus, through the suffrages of prayers, through many fastings, through alms widely distributed, and through all the things by which sins are purged, one may come to the firmness of the cheese, as it were to a certain consolidating of the virtues: and thus, a certain curd of peace having been made toward all, an ascent may be made to the broad realms of charity, which is designated by the butter. [1173D] The page of the New Testament also overflows abundantly with the sweetness of this milk, which hints to us that the floodgates of heaven are opened, and shows that what was held sweeter among the delights of God has been sent forth to us from heaven unto salvation. It declares that the Word made flesh, that is, God made man, has mercifully come to the aid of man in himself as man, setting forth his sayings and deeds, which Christ wondrously performed in this life; promising also his kingdom, and the life which will be without end, to all the good. It gladdens every man coming into this world with the sweetness of this milk. To the rest, however, it gives sour milk to drink, saying, « Woe to you, Scribes and Pharisees, because you shut the kingdom of heaven before men [n: (Matth. XXIII, 13)]. » [1174A] In which place, threatening « Woe » seven times, he hints at that milk, sour indeed to the setting of the teeth on edge, which he is to give at the end to all the reprobate, saying, « Go, ye cursed, into the everlasting fire, which is prepared for the devil and his angels [var: Vulg. *Discedite a me*, "Depart from me"; and *paratus* for *praeparatus*] [n: (Matth. XV, 41)]. »

These things, therefore, concerning the breasts of the Bride having been tasted beforehand by way of a little preface, this also must be added: that as the breasts are so fixed in the human body that one holds the right side, the other the left, so all Scripture divinely inspired is so divided by its warnings and words that certain things are appointed to the left hand of the Church, certain things to her right. For the Church has a left hand, and she has a right also. Her left hand is this life, her right the life which is to come, which is also eternal. And so all the Scriptures which either [1174B] the first or the New Testament contains, which have been so published as to order this present life, teaching how a man ought to use this world and the things which are of the world, so that he may run through this life without offence to God, turning aside from the vices which it forbids and pursuing the virtues which it commands, all these are appointed to the left breast of the Church. But to the right breast are assigned those which persuade us to despise the world and the things which are of the world, to shrink from its delights, to love God alone, to long for him, to be dissolved hence and to be with Christ. Whence that saying, « Behold the elect of God: they tame the flesh, they strengthen the spirit, they command demons, they flash with miracles, they preach the heavenly fatherland by their manners and their life, and hasten to reach it even through torments; they can be slain, they cannot be bent: » [1174C] and things of this kind rightly belong to the right breast. Enriched, therefore, with these breasts, the Bride feeds peoples, and leads those nourished in the first virtues to the highest and perfect things; and rightly does the Bridegroom applaud her, saying, « Thy breasts are better than wine. » But the wine, in comparison with which the Bride's breasts are called better, what does it signify but the sciences beyond number in which the human mind entangles and wraps itself? so much so that it neglects the things which are God's, and scorns to seek out and search into those things which are God's. Who could number these? who could comprehend them under a number? The arts indeed are seven, which are for this reason called « liberal », because free men spent their studies upon them; learning first what are the elements of speech, [1174D] and expressing these both by their characters and by their names; joining these to one another, fitting syllables together, and forming words out of syllables, and speeches out of words: its own labour entangles this art, stretching the mind out into the infinite, and, unless it be greeted first upon the threshold, racking it grievously with its torments. But let logic be left to its own sweats; the more it advances with itself, the more it turns away from God those who give their labour to it. But the adorned labour of orators serves falsehood as much as it serves those things which are true; they reckon that excellently said, whether it be true or false, which is uttered ornately and (to speak after their manner) aptly. In so far as this yields obedience to falsehood, it is far from him who is simple truth, offering the holy knowledge of the Gospel and without paint. Why should I enumerate the numbers [1175A] of arithmetic, the consonances or dissonances of music, the figures of geometry? in which « there is labour, and affliction of spirit [n: (Eccle. I, 17)]: » since Wisdom says, « God created man upright, and he himself hath mingled himself with questions beyond number [n: (Eccle. VII, 30)]. » Furthermore astronomy, dividing the heaven by colures and parallels, giving names to the planets and to the stars, and assigning the motions of each in the circles which are given them, noting now their stations, now their progressions, now their retrogradations, and at length measuring out by its own rules the equalizations of them all in one philosophical year, which contains not less than fifteen thousand years. This, observing the elevations, the depressions, [1175B] and the middle stations of the sun in one hundred and eighty-two circles, and of the other planets in the circles appointed to them, distinguishes floods, tides, plenty and want, and the middle seasons, and presumes in a manner to foretell the things which God before the ages disposed to come in a fixed order: this inebriates minds with much wine, and while it studiously considers the works of God, it wanders away from God, and either is utterly ignorant of his humility, or loves it less.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

There are also the arts which are called « mechanical », which by the wise men of this world are indeed reckoned necessary for use, but not so far worthy; such as agriculture, architecture, the working of wool and weaving, the shoemaker's craft too, and the smith's and the founder's, and others beyond number, which necessity, or pleasantness, or at any rate pleasure has found out. This is the wine with which the world is given drink, or rather is made drunk; so that while it seeks what necessity, or what [1175C] pleasantness, or what pleasure, or what curiosity suggests, it passes over the things which are God's: and whether God was made flesh, whether he suffered, whether he rose again and ascended and promised eternal things to mortals, it is either utterly ignorant, or perhaps despises what it knows. But what does the Bridegroom say to the Bride concerning these things? « Thy breasts are better than wine; » declaring that it is far better to be nourished simply by the milk of the Church than to be proudly and uselessly made drunk with the things which have been named. Hence Isaias says, « Why do you spend your silver not upon bread, and your labour not unto fullness? » [n: (Isa. LV, 2.)] For to spend silver not upon bread is to consume eloquence and labour upon those things which do not give the knowledge and the vision of God: of which David says, « I shall be satisfied [1175D] when thy glory shall appear [n: (Psal. XVI, 15)]. » But why they ought to be reckoned better, he adds the cause, saying, Because they are « fragrant with the best ointments. » That ointments well compounded by the art of physicians should smell well is not to be doubted. Of these some are piercing, some soothing, some preserving. For some pierce, that they may heal; some soothe the things which are painful; some preserve, that they may remain untouched by rottenness. This is the effect of the spiritual virtues. For penitence grieves that one has committed that which it laments: after the wailing, and the confession, and the works of satisfaction, hope now soothes that which was painful: hence charity preserves unto eternal life the things which have been made solid. The « breasts » therefore of the Church « are better, » that is, those simple [1176A] teachings which are drawn out from the breasts of the Church are mightier and more effectual unto the soul's salvation than the « wine, » that is, than all the figments and inventions which secular wisdom has found out, that with them it may give drink to and inebriate those following it and giving their labour to it: and therefore « they are fragrant with the best ointments, » that is, fragrant in that manner in which those ointments are fragrant which are the best.

*Thy name is oil poured out.*

This is the Bride's friendly and honourable answer to the Bridegroom. The Bridegroom lately gratified her with his commendation, saying: « Thy breasts are better than wine; fragrant with the best ointments. » And she, as though giving him thanks, answered him with this commendation, saying, « Thy name is oil poured out, » as if she should say: if my breasts are to be praised, [1176B] this has come to them from thy proclaiming; for what is brought forth from my breasts is from thine, whose name is oil poured out. By which answer she doubtless hints that the faith which is in the name of Christ transcends every faith: and that, as oil, with whatever liquids it be mingled, without doubt transcends them all and floats above them, so the faith which is signed and founded in the name of Christ, and proclaimed by the teachings of the apostles, remains above and prevails over all the inventions [sic: *invensionibus*] against it which have already been named, or which perhaps may yet be found: whence it is also said, « Thy name is as oil, » that is, the faith which is in thy name is thus as oil. And that she may express this the more, there is added, « Poured out. » For this oil was not poured out during the whole time in which the Word was not [1176C] made flesh; in which, sin prevailing and mercy not obtaining, the whole race of men was made over to the darkness of hell and held under the dominion of the evil one: but after satisfaction had been made to wrath, and the time of fullness and of grace had come, that fruitful olive tree, namely God the Father, from whom is the origin of every good, sent forth the dove bearing as it were the branch of the olive, and appointed his Son to be made flesh through this branch; and the oil pressed out from this branch he poured forth as widely through the world as this incarnation was made known most widely to the whole world in many and various ways. This profited men beyond number by its anointing; « for virtue went out from him, and healed all [n: (Luc. VI, 19)]. » This, by enlightening them, turned men beyond number from the darkness of blindness to faith. [1176D] This restored sight to the blind, hearing to the deaf, speech to the dumb, put fever to flight, and restored the paralysed limbs, and raised the dead, restoring to them the breath of life. This made solid the waves of the sea beneath his steps. This multiplied twelve loaves to satisfy nine thousand men.

Why should I linger in the telling of these things? Let us hear John, saying, « There are also many other things which Jesus did; which if they were written every one, I think the world itself could not hold the books that should be written [n: (Joan. XXI, 25)], » that is, could not give credence to the multitude of the signs which were to be committed to books. For the pouring out of this oil enlightens the world. It gladdens the face of the Church, it fits and prepares her food; so that without the mingling of this, the spirit can scarcely prevail to be satisfied with its own food. [1177A] For as Paul says, « Without faith it is impossible to please God [n: (Heb. XI, 6)]. » For if without charity I should offer all the things which are of the world, and even myself, so that I burn, I am nothing [n: (I Cor. 13)]. For if, offering that without charity, I am nothing, then doing it without faith I am found to be utterly nothing. In Mark, the widow offering two mites is said to have offered more than the others who were offering great things [n: (Marc. XII, 44)]; here by the two mites understanding the love of God and of one's neighbour, in which the Church, when she offers, offers more than those who, giving their own without these, offer much and waste it. The faith of Christ, therefore, without which neither charity nor any good whatever can be had, is that which seasons, fits, and prepares the food of the soul, so that things offered in faith may please; and however small they be, they are nevertheless great, [1177B] and obtain from God a kingdom truly great, namely of the heavens. Rightly therefore has it been said that the « name » of Christ, that is, his faith, in which we must be saved, is « oil poured out, » that is, communicated to the world and approved by virtues; wherefore there follows:

*Therefore the maidens have loved thee.*

For hence was the love of the maidens stirred up, hence arose the beginning of their loving. For they saw the signs, they saw also the wonders which obeyed him at a nod; they saw a man set in weakness, holding all the things that are man's save sin and ignorance, and doing those things which are above man; and they were moved to believe him above man, [1177C] whom they saw sustaining fasts of forty days and nights, then changing water into wine, then transfiguring himself on the mountain, with his face shining in the manner of the Sun, showing also in his garments the whiteness of snow, holding converse with the dead, and the Father bearing witness out of the cloud, « This is my beloved Son, who is pleasing to me in all things; hear him [var: Vulg. *Hic est Filius meus electus, ipsum audite* (Luc. IX, 35); the wording *dilectus* and *qui mihi per omnia complacet* answers rather to Matt. XVII, 5, *in quo mihi bene complacui*] [n: (Luc. IX, 35)]; » and so at the last, having undergone a manifest death, triumphing in immortality over that death itself, and so returning to the heavens whence he had come; what could they do but believe him God, venerate and adore him, and love him above all things? Him therefore « the maidens have loved, » that is, the souls lately converted to the faith: and such a soul I judge Peter's to have been at the first, when he said, [1177D] « Depart from me, because I am a sinful man, O Lord [n: (Luc. V, 8)]. » Peter had fished the whole night, and had taken absolutely nothing; at his word he let down the net, and took a copious multitude. Peter had been a little youth, and gladly heard the Lord; but strengthened by this deed he grew up into a youth, afterwards into a most steadfast young man, and so came forth into a perfect man. And of Paul the persecutor what shall I relate? who at one moment a persecutor, at the next was made a little youth, the Lord enlightening him and saying, « Saul, Saul, why persecutest thou me? I am Jesus of Nazareth, whom thou persecutest [n: (Act. IX, 45)]. » At this voice he was straightway truly converted, [1178A] saying, « Lord, what wilt thou have me to do? » [n: (ibid., 6)] If [cj: *Si*; read *Sic*, "So"] made a little youth, he heard from Ananias the words by which he grew up, by which he was made a great man, and the wall and defender and propagator of the whole Church; so an unnumbered multitude of believers, coming to him at the first and hearing from him the word of life, drawn at first by a little, then enticed more by the word, from which they understood the signs and wonders which they will be able neither to resist nor to gainsay, protested, by word and by work, that this name alone is to be adored, and to be loved before all things which are under heaven: whence there is added, « Thy name is oil poured out; therefore the maidens have loved thee. » There follows:

3. *Draw me after thee.*



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

[1178B]The Bride therefore, marking the glory of the young maidens, in which they were received through love; according to which they who had been young maidens are now accounted queens; and considering that these could not have been raised into this glory unless they were drawn by the Father, opens her bosom, enlarges her prayer, praying for herself, that she in her least members may be drawn to the Father; wishing that we may all together meet with her unto a perfect man, and the measure of the age of the fulness of Christ [n: (Ephes. IV, 13)]; whence also she subjoins that which follows, « Draw me after thee. » For it must be known that all of us, as many as run, are drawn in two directions: the one which is called the right, the other which is called the left. He is drawn to the left who, always entangled in evil works, [1178C] consumes his life in them. But he is drawn to the right over whom a good angel presides, showing him the good things that he should do, and to the finishing of these let him always be intent: whence, speaking to God, she says, « After thee, » that is, in those things which are pleasing to thee, in which I may have thee as my leader before me, in those things keep me, and suffer me neither to stray nor to be turned aside elsewhere: for so it is written, « No man comes to me, unless the Father, who sent me, draw him [n: (Joan. VI, 44)]. » [var: Vulg. *Nemo potest venire ad me*, "No man can come to me"] That we may therefore approach to God, there is need of the Father drawing, that he himself may go before us, may so present himself as leader, and, when he has led, may himself receive us. Likewise also we read it written, « And I, if I be lifted up from the earth, will draw all things to myself [n: (Joan. XII, 32)]. » Christ indeed was lifted up from the earth when he was raised from the earth upon the cross: [1178D] likewise he was lifted up when, forsaking the earth, he was set in heaven at the right hand of the Father: and by the consideration of himself thus, whether set upon the cross or raised to the right hand of the Father, he draws every chosen soul to himself, while they both crucify themselves to their vices and, caring not for earthly things, hang themselves upon him who sits above on high. For he who is drawn seeks the heights, and wholly intends to lift himself above himself; he beholds before him the mountain of the virtues, and while he strives to lay hold of its steep places, he beholds himself at times in the depth, and wonders. And so he cries out, saying, « Draw me after thee, » because out of this valley I do not ascend save by thee. But if thou shalt have felt the hand of God stretched out to thee, and shalt have recognized thyself drawn out of the valley of miseries and out of [1179A] the mire of dregs, lay hold of the hand, hold it, kiss it, and say, « I will not let thee go, unless thou bless me [n: (Gen. XXXII, 26)]: » have thanks to him who will truly lead thee forth, that thou mayest run in the odor of his ointments: which thou wilt recognize by a like saying. For dogs run by the keenness of their nostrils, that they may take wild beasts, which, while they flee, infect the thickness of the air with their scent, and so show to those that follow the way by which they may be taken.

That comparison indeed is a figure of the demons: for they who are tempted flee, and there are malignant spirits, or evil men, that follow, in order to drag the fleeing into evils, and to slay them, and to glory in their deaths, and to sate themselves. So is that hateful hunt accomplished in the world, in which the spirit that is the author of malice [1179B] sounds the horn, admonishes with his voice, and, in order to suggest what he wills, admits evil men and perverse spirits, and slays innumerable men who do not resist temptation. But the Spirit of God works far otherwise: he gives the good will, gives to carry through in effect that which is good; gives to take delight in the things which are above, and to be sprinkled unceasingly with the odor of the things which are above. Wonderful indeed is the anointing of the divine Spirit, who renders the skin which he anoints supple and fit for the stitching, and makes it apt for the adornment of the supreme King. With the odor of this ointment is he drenched who assiduously meditates in the law of the Lord, fitting himself so that he may resist whatever things are hurtful, may give himself over to no sins, may apply himself to the manners of the saints, may sanctify himself by imitating them, and, drenched with the odor of spiritual [1179C] ointments, may always rejoice in them, may securely take his delight, and rest. He entreats therefore in this manner, « Draw me after thee, » that is, Thou, to whom none comes save by thee, knit for me the rope of thy love, whereby I may be drawn to thee, whereby it may not suffer me to turn aside elsewhere. For Solomon says that « a threefold cord is not easily broken [n: (Eccle. IV, 12)]; » and if thou shalt have suffered this to be knit to my members by the hand of the malignant one, so that by thinking evil things it drag my heart to itself, by speaking these things inflame my whole tongue out of itself, and by doing these things bend my whole body and turn it aside to itself; what is there for me, save that I say that word of the apostle? « Unhappy man that I am: who shall deliver me from the body of this death? » [n: (Rom. VII, 24.)] Do thou, O Lord, [1179D] answer me, « The grace of God by Jesus Christ our Lord [n: (ibid., 25)]. » Let this thy grace, O Lord, deliver me, because thou art strong and mighty: in this battle do thou, being the stronger, snatch his rope out of his mighty hand; do thou break it asunder; do thou knit thy whole threefold cord to my members; that I may inwardly savor thee in my heart, may proclaim thee and thy things with my tongue, and may devoutly fulfil in work those things only which are pleasing to thee. Do thou mightily snatch me, willing to follow thee, from the malignant one; make me to walk after thee, that when I have walked after thee I may not begin to run at uncertainty, but may lay hold of the crown which thou hast promised to them that love thee, being drawn hither [1180A] by thee. This indeed is that which follows:

*We will run in the odor of thy ointments.* [var: Vulg. *in odorem*, "to the odor"]

That is, being snatched from the malignant one by thee, stirred to the good by thee, brought to the highest by thy drawing, « we will run, » that is, we shall go forward our whole life long; « In the odor of thy ointments, » that is, in the fragrance of the spiritual virtues, wherewith thou touchest and anointest thine own. And so, wishing gifts to the good, and desiring that they be drawn to better things, she says, « Draw me after thee; » that thou mayest help me, who am willing, and running, and desiring thee with my whole heart, in this, that at length by a blessed end I may lay hold of thee, and, the good course being finished, may now rest secure and be delighted in thee. But because certain men neither run nor will to take up the course after thee, [1180B] let thy visitation deign to visit these, that thou mayest open to them the light of truth, and draw them, being enlightened, to thee, and, a rope of charity being given them, bring them through to thee. These that great Paul signifies, who before his calling ran wholly after Satan; but afterwards, being struck by the Lord, he was laid low and enlightened, and made a wonderful preacher, and, bound by this threefold cord, running after God, was consummated by the sword. Whether therefore thou drawest after thee one willing and able to run, that he may run the more, and may arrive there whither he could not arrive save by thee; or convertest one unwilling and unable to run, and dost so enlighten him by the grace given, that he too may lay hold of thee. In the good odor « of thy ointments, » that is, of the virtues breathing forth from thee, we will run, rendering thanks to thee who [1180C] hast laid hold of us, and rendering praises in the glory to which thou hast brought us by thy gift. There follows;

*The King brought me into his cellars: we will exult, and be glad in thee.*

A prayer has been set forth above, in which it was said, « Draw me after thee; we will run in the odor of thy ointments. Lest it should seem useless and uttered without fruit, there is consequently added, « The King brought me into his cellars. » For behold, she is now drawn, drawn indeed, and brought into the very cellars of the King. But who is more worthily called King than he who, having the rights of all kingdoms, distributes them to whom he wills, and when he wills, and as long as he wills; and, considering in these the manners of men, either crowns them according to their merits, [1180D] or, marking their insolence, afflicts them worthily for the things they have committed. This truly is God, who rules all things by one command, who contains the things which are above and the things which are below, giving to each of his creatures its own being, that they may be, and be well, and himself God ruling over all things. This is he whom this Bride desires, that is, the soul, or the Church, wholly purged of sins, whose kiss she has this long while demanded, seeking to lay hold of him whose desire she already feels within her. Which indeed, because it is near to the highest blessedness, is still deferred, nor is the kiss given, until the soul, or the Church, may understand from the creatures whether she has chosen one who is worthy. Hence it is that [1181A] either by an angel or by some virtue this Bride is brought into the cellars of the King, that she may either know his riches, and from his riches recognize who he is, and of what sort and how great is he whom she desires. Which this Bride observing, and giving thanks, says, « The King brought me into his cellars: we will exult, and be glad in thee. » Behold, she who is in the cellars exults in the cellars: and the cellars indeed are some part of the divine palace, containing within themselves the riches of the king, and his secret things. But the palace of the King is that of which the prophet makes mention, saying, « O Israel, how great is the house of God, and vast! great is the place of his possession, and it has no consummation, high and immeasurable [n: (Bar. III, 24, 25)]. » [var: Vulg. *finem*, "end"] Hence let us consider his palace, which is assuredly as great as [1181B] the creation reaches. Its length, its breadth and its height measure out the whole dimension of the creature. But if thou shouldst go further in mind, hear the Scripture; for it says, « It has no consummation, high and immeasurable: » for God is indeed in every creature. But is God consummated together with the creature, so that where the creature receives its end, there God also should obtain his end? Far be this, and let it be driven far from human understanding. For God, as he is immeasurable, so is he infinite and eternal: and if he is infinite, there is assuredly nothing whereby he should have an end; but where he appoints the boundary of the creature, there he receives himself in his own simplicity and purity: according to which the Truth testifies in the Gospel, saying, « Glorify me, Father, with thyself, with the glory which I had before the world [1181C] was made [n: (Joan. XVII, 5)]. » For then was the Father in the Son and the Holy Spirit, the Son was in the Father and the Holy Spirit, the Holy Spirit also was in the Father and the Son, filling up the blessed Trinity, and sanctifying one unity in substance.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

This therefore is the palace of the king; and its cellars are certain heavenly mansions, of which the Lord makes mention, saying, « In my Father's house there are many mansions [n: (Joan. XIV, 2)]. » Which mansions indeed are the diverse habitations of the heavenly virtues, and of diverse glory, which he disposes and orders in that secret manner of his own which the Lord knows, so that in them he may establish the place of his glory and of his holy habitation. Who discerns and separates these? [1181D] who divides and gives judgment upon their fellowships? High indeed, and unknown to our understanding, is that incomprehensible indwelling of all the virtues and angels, who, invisible to us, were found standing and stable in temptation; and, when those fell headlong who, lifted up in mind against the Lord, fell away, they were made stable by the merit of their own constancy, and were joined to God by perpetual charity. Whose names, as they are known to us, so are their powers unknown, holding together heaven and earth, disposing all the things that are done in them, moderating the life of the just, and not ignorant of the punishments which are due to the wicked: whose number God the Creator alone knows, who created them from the beginning, and enjoys and takes delight in their unceasing obedience: their nature the holy Trinity knows, their number the second person [1182A] in the Trinity knows, our Lord Jesus Christ, who, when he was being led to his passion, said, « Turn thy sword into its place. Dost thou think that I cannot ask my Father, and he will present me more than twelve legions of angels [n: (Matth. XXVI, 52, 53)]. » Which twelve legions are found to be seventy-nine thousand, and nine hundred, and ninety-two, that is, eighty thousand less eight. But the number of the others is unknown to us, who sound forth praises beyond estimation to God, and, being occupied in those praises, are not wearied, nor do they fail, but rather it is the more a delight to be delighted by the frequenting of these praises; so that either they may always go forward in the love and the knowledge of that majesty which they behold, or, being brought to perfection, may [1182B] persevere without end, stable in that which they have long since attained.

These mansions therefore are the cellars, into which this Bride says that she has been brought, showing that by an angel, or by some virtue, she is formed by their examples, so that, contemplating God, she may love him with all her strength, and may cherish his praise without intermission, and may show that she takes her delight not in the affluence of temporal and passing things, but rather in the continual frequenting of these; saying, « The king brought me into his cellars: we will exult, and be glad in thee. » For to exult is to rejoice in mind, and to be glad is to show by an outward sign the joy which is within. Therefore, that she may attribute wholly to God the joy which she announces that she has conceived, she adds, [1182C] « In thee: » for vain alike is exultation, and vain is gladness, save that which is founded in the Lord: whence the Psalmist says, « Be glad, ye just, in the Lord, and glory, all ye upright of heart [n: (Psal. XXXI, 11)]. » And likewise he begins a Psalm, saying: « Exult, ye just, in the Lord; praise becomes the upright [n: (Psal. XXXII, 1)]. » Behold what gladness and exultation, which praise assuredly and glorying follow after, is wholly from God, when God is gathered up with the whole heart, the whole soul, the whole mind, when thy heart is opened to loving thy neighbor, even as thyself: and the recompense of all these things is not sought in this life, but is most patiently awaited in the life to come: whence also there is subjoined;

*Mindful of thy breasts above wine.*

Here truly it must be attended to, that this exultation and [1182D] gladness proceeds wholly from the memory of the breasts, which the Bride confesses to be the breasts of the Bridegroom himself: and by what reason this is true must be considered. For the Bridegroom, in the first answer of his, answered the Bride above, Thy prayer is not to be repelled, because « thy breasts are better than wine: » whence, since here the Bridegroom says that the breasts are the Bride's, and in what follows the Bride professes that the breasts are the Bridegroom's, either these must of necessity be the same breasts, or diverse ones must indeed be assigned: but lest hindrances make delay, we testify that the breasts will be the same, both the Bridegroom's and the Bride's. For these breasts we testify to be the divine oracles; which indeed are God's, divinely inspired by him: and they are the Church's, granted to her out of the grace of God for the nourishing and instructing of the peoples. This [1183A] Bride therefore says: « We will exult, and be glad in thee; mindful of thy breasts, » that is, of the divine oracles, which have been inspired by thee, and granted to me by thy giving. « Above wine, » that is, above the understanding of all those oracles which, as wine turns men's minds away, so turn the soul away from thee, and detain it occupied in their hands. There follows:

*The upright love thee.*

Which addition indeed is the commendation of the things said above. For no one loves save he who is upright, nor can any man be upright and not love. For he is upright who raises his head to that for which he was created, who is stretched out with his whole body thither whence it has been granted him to be, to live, to be wise, and to understand. Whence is this to any man, save from God? For the Psalm says, [1183B] « Know ye that the Lord himself is God; he made us, and not we ourselves [n: (Psal. XCIX, 3)]. » He therefore who raises himself to God is upright, because without doubt he tends thither whence he acknowledges these things to be present to him: but he who is bent downward, who is inclined to the world, fixing his head downward and raising his feet upward, is likened to a monster, and is crooked; of whom the prophet says, « I am made wretched, and I am bowed down even to the end [n: (Psal. XXXVII, 7)], » bewailing without doubt the wretchedness of him who, thus departing from the good, ends his life in death. But the Lord, not willing that we should be bent down in this manner, healed and raised up the woman in the Gospel who for eighteen years was bowed down and unable to look upward, making her whole from being sick, and upright from being crooked; showing that it is he himself [1183C] whose love heals and raises up, makes upright and receives. There follows:

4. *I am black, but beautiful, O ye daughters of Jerusalem.*

Hitherto the Bride has addressed her Bridegroom, she who is either the Church of God, or any perfected soul, now loathing the world and contemplating God with a pure heart: but she, seeking the kiss, says, « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » Which, because it is not yet given, but is deferred to that time in which, after the resurrection of all, the souls, united together with the very bodies in which they rendered a service pleasing to God, shall be gladdened with a perpetual eternity, and shall together be endowed with immortality. But it was answered her by the Bridegroom: The kiss which thou askest I do not indeed [1183D] give now, but when God shall be all in all, and the number of your brethren shall be filled up, then I promise that I will give it: and for the filling up of that number of the brethren I judge thee indeed apt; and this for this reason, because « thy breasts are better than wine. » Cheered by which answer, she addresses him again, saying, « Thy name is oil poured out: » and likewise that other, « Draw me after thee, » that, helped by the strength of thy breasts, with such a multitude as befits thee, I may at length come through to thee. But now, being taught what is to be done, and that the peoples must be nourished by her breasts, so that, being enlarged unto salvation, she may run together with them, having before her eyes that word, « Obedience is better than victims, and to hearken, rather than to offer the fat of rams [n: (I Reg. XV, 22)]; » she obeys, and, [1184A] being humbled, turns herself to the peoples, saying, « I am black, O ye daughters of Jerusalem. » But who does not know Jerusalem to be that city which the Lord chose before all others, that in it he might teach, and in it bring forth the words of life, and in it and in its borders work the miracles at which the world should be astounded, and might wonderfully fulfil what had been begun by an angel in Nazareth, brought forth afterwards in Bethlehem, and in that city at the last by his passion, and by the glory of his resurrection, by his ascension also, and by the sending of the Holy Spirit. But addressing this city, she says, « I am black and beautiful, O ye daughters of Jerusalem. » [var: Vulg. *sed*, "but"] But she calls its daughters all the souls converted to him by the preaching of the Lord Jesus; which deserved to hear either him bodily, or his preaching, beforehand through the prophets and other just men, or afterwards through the apostles, and those [1184B] who had faith in them: these she names daughters, as being suppliant, as being obedient, doing nothing against the command of the Father, keeping the faith as it was committed to them, and, in the keeping of it even to the term of life, most constantly persevering, and despising death of whatever kind.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

But because, as Solomon says, « The just man is first the accuser of himself, taking the beginning from herself, she says, « I am black. » Which testimony indeed, despicable as concerning herself, is attended to more than what is most worthy of commendation. For what comeliness has the Bride, if she appear to the Bridegroom black, if dark, if horrible in the look? if she whom love flees should present herself to embraces; and she whom love follows, whom it joyfully receives, should plainly be found lacking these things. It must therefore be attended to, that this blackness [1184C] has in itself more of form, more of comeliness, more of honor and of seemliness, than all that which allures the sight, which snatches to itself and distracts the affections of mortals: for thence there is subjoined, « I am black, but beautiful. » This blackness spiritual affections work. For the Holy Spirit brings in this blackness: he pours the love of God into hearts, gives contempt of the world, gives to spurn the things that fall away, and to love the things which are eternal: to these things he rouses the affections, labors for the acquiring of them, prays in them, fasts in them, in them assiduously macerates the body, and prepares the world to be contemptible to itself, and itself to be contemptible to the world. Our Lord Jesus Christ declares that this blackness shall be present to him, saying, That « I must go to Jerusalem, and suffer many things from the elders, and [1184D] the Scribes, and the chief of the Priests, and be slain [n: (Matth. XVI, 21)]. » Which blackness of his the text of the Gospels announces, setting out the things which he sustained, which he willed to suffer for the redeeming of man; and which at the last, by wonderfully rising again, and by so ascending into the heavens, and by sending the Holy Spirit, he changed into a wondrous form of comeliness. This blackness laid hold of Peter also, his apostle, when Herod, delivering him over to four quaternions of soldiers, kept him taken, and, bound with two chains, held him fast in the prison; who, when in the morning he was to be brought forth to the sword, that very night being taught by an angel, passed through all the guards, and, the angel leading, escaping also the iron gate which was opened to him of its own accord, « Coming to himself, said: [1185A] Now I know truly that the Lord has sent forth his angel, and delivered me, etc. [n: (Act. XII, 11.)] » Paul also, asserting that he has part in these things, says: « In very many labors, in prisons more abundantly, in stripes above measure, in deaths often [n: (II Cor. XI, 23)]. » This blackness therefore we have assigned to Christ, who is the head of the whole Church, and to his chief members, Peter and Paul, for this reason, that from it it may be established that all who godly will to live in Christ must of necessity sustain bodily persecution, such as these, or such as all the martyrs far and wide through the world have unconquerably suffered for Christ; or at the least a spiritual one, such as the apostle bewails, saying, « Unhappy man that I am! Who shall deliver me from the body of this death? » [n: (Rom. VII, 24.)] For blackness [1185B] enfolds all men, whether such as is from the persecution of the body, or such as is from the assault of the mind that struggles against it.

This Bride therefore says to the daughters of Jerusalem, whom she has taken up to be instructed: ye daughters of Jerusalem, attend, because « I am black, » prompt indeed for every peril, for the taking up of every kind of death, prepared for the undergoing of every temptation; so that « neither death, nor life, nor Angels, nor Principalities, nor Powers [n: (Rom. VIII, 38, 39)] » [var: Vulg. *neque virtutes*, "nor virtues"], nor the rest which follow, which the apostle enumerates, « can separate her from the charity of God, which is in Christ Jesus. » Thou also, who desirest to be called a daughter of Jerusalem, by looking upon me, by setting me before thee as an example, stand fast, drawing near to the service of God, lest thou be [1185C] easily moved; because out of this blackness an unspeakable form of comeliness shall come to thee: for so it is written, « I am black, but beautiful. » Which form indeed is first from this, that man was created to the image and likeness of God; « to the image, » from reason; « to the likeness, » from the imitation of virtue. For if a man have reason, and cease to imitate virtue, he is indeed an image; but the likeness in which he might be able to please is wanting: of such men it is written, « But God, seeing that the malice of men was great upon the earth, and that all the thought of their heart was bent upon evil at all times, it repented him [n: (Gen. VI, 5, 6)], » and he blotted them out, not recognizing the image deformed, and the likeness not admitted into comeliness. [1185D] But in so great a multitude of men as was blotted out by the waters, how many faces there were which after the manner of man were called most fair, out of the fitting composition of God! how many lofty heads of hair! how deep the breasts! how open for work the shoulders! how many bodies framed for good uses, had they willed it! how many legs! how many hands! how many arms! in which, if nothing were sought save that which is outwardly fair, they would surely not have been enfolded in the waters, nor found in the lowest places. But all these, wrapped about with the blackness of sin, were plunged into a black house, namely, hell, allotted to the prince of blackness of this kind, therein experiencing what the difference is between those blackened by sin, and [1186A] those who for Christ are bound fast by sufferings, and by the things which accompany sufferings. These gnash in the darkness; the glory of the others is in the light: whence also it is here said, « I am black, but beautiful. » For the Church of God is black, whether by the suffering of the body, or by the maceration of the flesh, and the wearing down of the spirit, because it follows Christ, until it may reach through to him. There follows:

*As the tents of Cedar, as the skins of Solomon.*

Two things having been set before, two also are subjoined. For to that which was said, « I am black, » there is subjoined that which was said, « as the tents of Cedar: » but to that which was set down, « but beautiful, » there is placed beneath that which was said, « as the skins of Solomon. » [1186B] Which being so set down, let us so understand them. For Cedar is understood to have been the son of Ismael [n: (Gen. XXV, 13)], who, after he had grown up, disdaining to dwell among his brethren, chose for himself a solitude to be inhabited, raising for himself tents which should take upon them all the inclemency of the air, and should avoid no excess either of heat or of cold. In them he sustained the burning Crab, in them he did not fear the Goat stiff with cold, in them he sustained the assaults of whatsoever men came against him, and, breaking the arms of them that warred, he withstood them both by the strength of his body and by the solidity of his tents. The Church therefore, or any perfected soul, says that she is black as the tents of Cedar, because, as these could be beaten back by no onsets, so neither could these women be turned away from the love of God and from the worship of his justice [1186C] by any threats, by any terrors of adversaries, or by any scourges: according to that word of blessed Paul, « I know that no creature at all shall separate me from the charity of God, which is in Christ Jesus [n: (Rom. VIII, 39)]. » There follows that which has now been said, « as the skins of Solomon. » Which skin indeed is to be understood not of sheep, not of goats, not of any animals at all; but the very skin of Solomon; of which it is also said elsewhere, « Thou hast clothed me with skin and flesh, thou hast put me together with bones and sinews [n: (Job X, 11)]. »

This therefore is the skin which covers his face and his neck, fencing his throat, his breast and his arms, covering also the vital parts, and shutting in and closing within itself the rest of the body, so far as [1186D] it goes. But who does not know that Solomon was a most wealthy king, to whom the Lord gave a wise and understanding heart, so far that none before him was like to him, and who obtained all the kingdoms from the river of the land of the Philistines even to the border of Egypt, they offering gifts to him, and serving him all the days of his life. What could be lacking to him, which did not obey him in food and in the adornment of clothing? For the highest comeliness and the highest beauty was present to him; whom. whosoever is well in his wits understands to lack nothing whereby the life of men may be adorned. To this skin therefore holy Church, or any perfected soul, rightly compares herself, [1187A] saying, « I am beautiful as the skin of Solomon; » because as among those human things nothing is understood to be fairer than this skin, nothing more worthy; so among those things which are toward God nothing is found more adorned or more exact than holy Church, than the perfect soul. But if any man raise his contemplation higher, by this Solomon may be understood that great Solomon, our Lord Jesus Christ, who in his own blood made peace over all things, who made both one, confederating the heavenly things and the earthly, that in the heavens there may be glory to God, and on earth peace to men of good will. To this skin the Church conforms herself, and every faithful soul, hoping with a sure hope that she may be with him in blessed immortality, being about to be immortal, and to be endowed with eternal blessedness in incorruption. Whence also there follows:

[1187B]



## 5.

*Do not consider me, that I am dark, because the sun has discolored me.*

As if she should say, I am indeed black, discolored by innumerable sufferings, afflicted with horrible torments: but you who will to be daughters of Jerusalem, do not attend to this, do not set my sufferings before your eyes, and do not reckon them as intolerable. What I suffered at Rome in Peter by the cross, what through Paul by the sword, what through John in the cauldron, what through Andrew in Achaia, what through the several apostles in the diverse places of the world, then through Stephen, through James in Judea, what through Polycarp, what through Dionysius, what through Laurence, what through Vincent, what through the Theban Legion, what [1187C] through all the martyrs far and wide through the world, how in these things I was discolored, do not attend; but for what cause I endured these things; what fruit I may obtain through that cause; with what blessed glory I hold by most certain hope that I shall be endowed. Hither attend, hither look, that the Sun of justice [sic: *justistitiae*] himself wrought this discoloring in me. These things I suffered for him who is the Sun of justice, who shall reform our body, configured to the body of his brightness, that I may live with him in perpetual glory, and enjoy everlasting blessedness. There follows:

*The sons of my mother have fought against me, they have set me a keeper in the vineyards.*

The Church says that she was black; but whence this blackness first befell her she consequently shows, [1187D] saying, « The sons of my mother have fought against me. » These she calls the sons of her mother, begotten of the stock of Abraham, and Isaac, and Jacob; who, when all the sons of men in the whole round of the earth turned aside to the left part, to adore idols and to sacrifice to them, refused to do this, but rather remained stable and immovable in the faith of the one God, who had created all things, through whom all things subsist. These she testifies to have been in this the sons of her mother, that is, of divine grace; whom, when the world was wrongly turning aside, she called back from its errors, and kept stable to herself in this faith, as sons: to whom, when the time of grace had been manifoldly revealed through the prophets, and afterwards, the Lord being born from the heavens among them, [1188A] had evidently appeared by signs and mighty works, yet they refused to receive him, to believe him, to give faith to his preaching, though it was declared by innumerable mighty works: but decreeing to be stable in the law given through Moses, they willed, after the signs, and the mighty works, and the miracles, to accuse him before the judge, betrayed by the hand of his own disciple, though he was innocent, and to receive him when he was given to them, and, though he bore all things innocently, to fasten him to the cross. Resigning this life, they were given over into a reprobate sense, and they who received it received power to be the sons of God: according to that which is said in the prophet, « I will call her that is not my people, my people, and her that is not my beloved, beloved; and her that has not obtained mercy, one that has obtained mercy. And it shall be in this place, where it was said [1188B] to them, You are not my people, there they shall be called the sons of the living God [n: (Ose. I, 10, et Rom. IX, 25, 26)]. » But this Bride says that these, long since the sons of the mother, grace, while that state still remained, while the prophecies were as yet in suspense, when the signs were not yet, the mighty works not yet declared; but now by no means sons, but most bitter enemies and unclean murderers, assailed her; that, the head being broken, the members also might be broken, and, Christ being taken away, as they hoped, out of the midst, his Church might be utterly taken away.

This is plainly shown in the Acts of the Apostles, where « the Prince of the Priests rising up, and all who were with him (which is the heresy of the Sadducees) were filled with zeal; and they laid hands upon the Apostles, [1188C] and put them in the public ward [n: (Act. V, 17, 18)]; » and, when these had been mightily delivered by an angel, they afterwards gave charge to the blind, that they should by no means speak in the name of the Lord Jesus. But these, long since the sons of divine grace, now wholly reprobate as toward the grace that offered itself, made war against me. But they of the new people, receiving the faith, and confessing the Word of God, true God and made man, subjecting themselves to him with the heart, set me, who from the first Abel have stood forth, who with Henoch deserved to have God, and who have taken up into myself all the just to whom this grace was revealed, set me a keeper for themselves in the vineyards, which they established throughout the whole world far and wide. These vineyards the Church calls holy, which they who had received the faith of Christ planted in their own blood, and consecrated unto his glory. [1188D] Set over these, this Bride says that she was set a keeper in the vineyards: over which, although she applied as much keeping as she could, yet she confesses that she did not keep them. Which we understand to be said with regard to the opinion of men: for they who were consummated by the sword, who were consumed by fire, who were destroyed by such punishments as the world could devise, who shall understand these to have been worthily kept? According to this opinion, this Bride says:

*I have not kept my vineyard.*

That is, I did not forbid them from the taking up of torments for Christ: whom nevertheless I have already sent before into eternal life, where there are no perils, but only eternal joys. To this there is consequently annexed:

*[1189A]*



## 6.

*Show me, thou whom my soul loves, where thou feedest, where thou liest down in the midday, lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions.*

For this Bride has confessed to her Bridegroom that, although set as keeper in the vineyards, she « did not keep » them, or her, for the sake of the one love of faith and of charity; that is, she did not restrain them from the fervor of undergoing suffering; but, by exhorting and encouraging them, she sent them over with most certain hope to the joys of the heavens promised to her. Hence, that she may be made the more certain, and that no one may be held doubtful in himself concerning that hope, she sends forth a prayer, saying, « Show me where thou feedest, where thou liest down; » having nevertheless set this before, « Whom my soul loves. » For it is wont to be the custom with suppliants, that when they have besought that anything be given or done for them, they hold out [1189B] something, or offer something, whereby they may understand that the face of him to whom supplication is made will be at peace toward them: whence, because among the heavenly gifts nothing is found more eminent than charity, nothing that can be given dearer than love, she holds out that love of hers which she asks for, saying, « Show me, thou whom my soul loves; » that what she asks may not be denied to one who asks and at the same time loves. For what is dearer to God? what is found more acceptable than the love of our soul? Run through all the things which are in the world: nothing comparable to this, nothing so acceptable to God that it might be offered, will meet thee. For God created the world, and all the things which are of the world; but for what cause? that it might indeed be a habitation for man: but for what cause did he here establish man? that he might [1189C] obey him, and out of the love of his soul be obedient to him. The end therefore and the cause of the creation of things is this, that we may love God with the whole soul, may please him in all things, may cleave to him indivisibly: according to that which is said, « He who is joined to the Lord is one spirit [n: (I Cor. VI, 17)]. » [var: Vulg. *adhaeret*, "cleaves"]

This Bride therefore, joined to God, having all souls, both those which are and those which shall be, joined to God in that unity which Paul the Apostle sets forth to us, what can she ask of the Bridegroom which he should deny? concerning what can she intercede which he should not hear? She says therefore, « Thou whom my soul loves, show me where thou feedest. » Which short word, how widely it may be extended we understand, if we rightly acknowledge the Lord to be the shepherd of all the things which [1189D] are fed far and wide. For he himself says, « I am the good Shepherd; the Shepherd [n: (Joan. X, 11)], » I say, « good, » through whom not even the bird hungers, through whom not even all the things which are sustained by another's food hunger. But what is it to us to inquire concerning the feeding of those who, though they are fed by his giving, neither render thanks to the giver, nor, so as to know how to render them, have received the thanksgivings of the giver? Men therefore are they whom God feeds; to whom he subjects all the things which are in the world, sheep and oxen, all the beasts of the field also, the birds of the air, and the fishes of the sea. Of whom certain men receive these gifts of God with a worthy giving of thanks; but others, not attending to what they are, or whence they are, or for what cause they were given, grasp all these things [1190A] as by a chance occupation; nor do they by any reasoning know who is the author of them, or who the giver. Since therefore God feeds all these, it lies with him which of them he may worthily reward according to their giving of thanks, or which, being unworthy, he may cast away from his table by eternal damnation. But what is it to us to handle these matters, since these are bodily things, and this Bride of God spiritual? let her intend to inquire further, saying, « Show me, thou whom my soul loves, where thou feedest. »

A spiritual pasture therefore must be sought, wherewith it is worthy that the Bridegroom should feed, and being fed with which they may be made spiritual. For there is a certain bodily bread, wherewith the body is nourished; and there is a bread wherewith the spirit is fed. The bread wherewith the body is nourished is procured by the plow and the hoe: the bread wherewith the spirit is fed is ministered from above [1190B] out of heaven. For there is a bread of life, Holy Scripture, divinely inspired by the Father of lights; and there is a bread of life, every perfected virtue: there is a bread of life, the body of Christ, granted to us for a sacrament and a sanctification. These are the breads of which the prophet makes mention, saying to the daughters of the Bride, « Blessing I will bless her widow, I will satisfy her poor with breads [n: (Psal. CXXXI, 15)]. » For Sion, « a watchtower, » is holy Church, from the lofty watchtower of the virtues assiduously contemplating the Lord Jesus, and fearfully and anxiously awaiting his coming. A certain part of her, already glorified with the Lord, rejoices in the heavens; a certain part, still sustaining exile, is a pilgrim from the Lord upon the earth: according to [1190C] this part she is a widow, as though separated from her husband, the Lord, namely, Jesus, who went away into a far country when he pierced the heavens with the truth of the body he had assumed, and is to return in the day of the full moon, that is, of the glorification to come. This widow the Lord blessing blesses, namely, with a twofold blessing; giving her grace in the way, that she may not fail; giving her at length glory in the fatherland, that she may rest from her labors. But he refreshes with breads those poor of his who are poor in spirit; who, although for the solace of their pilgrimage they possess certain temporal things, yet do not incline their mind to these, but raise up their love and their affection to the giver of all things. These he refreshes with the breads already named, namely, with the bread of the scriptures, [1190D] the bread of the virtues, the bread of the sacraments, the bread, namely, most white, which is the bread of angels; of which it is said: « Man ate the bread of angels [n: (Psal. LXXVII, 25)]. » These are the breads of which the Gospel discourse makes mention, saying, « Which of you shall have a friend, and shall go to him at midnight, saying, Friend, lend me three loaves; because a friend of mine has come to me, and I have not what to set before him [n: (Luc. XI, 4, 5)]. »

Well does he receive a friend, who intends to refresh and to honor him with this threefold bread. The bread of the scriptures therefore is the first among these breads, for this reason, that in spiritual advancement the first thing is to know in whom thou believest and whom thou lovest; the second, to be conformed [1191A] to him whom thou lovest; the third, to be confirmed in this very thing, that thou look not back, that thou go not back. Through the scriptures therefore we come to know, through virtue we are conformed, but through the sacrament we are confirmed. By the bread of the scriptures we are enlightened unto faith, that afterwards we may be confirmed unto virtue; whence it is written, « The declaration of thy words gives light, and gives understanding to little ones [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 130)]. » For there is a word which is heard when uttered, and there is a word which abides as born: but from the declaration of the word which is heard uttered, there is conceived the understanding and knowledge of the Word which always abides born; this is Christ Jesus our Lord; whose faith is surely conceived when the word of God, Holy Scripture, is soberly and faithfully opened and expounded. For it is written, that [1191B] « they knew him in the breaking of bread [n: (Luc. XXIV, 35)]. » The Gospel history lies open: the Lord was walking with the disciples, and was not indeed known, who was received by them into their lodging, and at length was known in the breaking of bread. O blessed breaking, whereby Christ is known! whereby Christ is conceived in the heart of his own! The bread of the scriptures therefore is the first; which the Lord also reveals, when, working miracles, he deigned to refresh five thousand men with five loaves; but four thousand with seven loaves. For it is written, « When, having taken five loaves, looking up to heaven, he blessed, and broke, and gave the loaves to his disciples, but the disciples to the multitudes [n: (Matth. XIV, 19)]. » O wonderful blessing! which, while it breaks the loaves, does not diminish them, and increases them when it distributes them; [1191C] multiplies them when it consumes them in the refreshing of many; and what is against the nature of bodily bread, this it brings back into nature. He handed over the Pentateuch to the apostles, commanding that they break it, and distribute it to all, that all may be satisfied from it, and that many fragments of the expositors may remain: so also, blessing the Heptateuch, he commanded it to be broken for the peoples, and from this that very many baskets of the expositors be filled. For this bread has such a nature, that when it is broken, and distributed, and communicated in the correction of many, it is always increased; and whence it seems to be diminished, thence rather it receives enlargement and increase. But the Pentateuch, that is, the five books of Moses, and the Heptateuch, which are the four Gospels, the epistles of the apostles, and their Acts, [1191D] which Luke writes, « Paul also the apostle. » These things the Lord sets forth to his own, that they may be as seeds, and that many fruits may arise from them, whereby the whole multitude cleaving to God may be fed, and fully refreshed. But it is the bread of the virtues, when from the word we have heard we conceive virtue in the mind, and remain firm and stable in it; when, driving off the disgrace of the vices, we mortify them in work, turn aside from them in speech, and dread to touch them in thought. Thus is place made for virtue in the mind; so that what ambition widely embraces, that is despised for Christ; what incontinence suggests, « let it slay in chastity; » what is exalted through pride, that is broken by humility; [1192A] and every vice that is spread abroad is driven far off by the mighty command of charity. Of this the Psalmist makes mention, saying, « My tears have been bread to me day and night [n: (Psal. XLI, 4)]. » For when he said that the love of God was to be preferred to the world, it was answered him that this should be laid open by a manifest miracle; for they said, « Where is thy God? » [n: (Ibid.)] Which hardness of heart being heard, the prophet turns to tears, praying for them that they may be softened, that at length they may be converted; having these tears indeed for breads, and reckoning them his refreshment. He makes mention of the same, saying, « I am smitten [sic: *suum*] as hay, and my heart is withered; because I forgot to eat my bread [n: (Psal. CI, 5)]. » For unless this bread of the virtues be assiduously eaten, so that a man be strengthened by it, he is smitten by the sickle of sin, and his [1192B] greenness falls away, and straightway his flower withers. For the same prophet repeats, saying, « All the day my enemies reproached me [n: (ibid. 9-10)]; » and why? « Because I ate ashes as bread, » that is, I was delighted with sin as though with virtue. These are the breads which Habacuc had crumbled in the little trough [n: (Dan. XIV, 32)], that he might carry them to the reapers, whom the angel of the Lord carried by his hair, and in the vehemence of his spirit set him in Babylon, that he might carry them to Daniel. For that Daniel had crushed these breads of the virtues in the trough of his heart; and there he made trial what things, and of what sort, and how great, virtue could do. This bread is that which was set at the head of Elias fleeing from the face of Jezabel, in which it was said to him, « Arise, eat; for a great [1192C] way remains. He arose therefore, and ate, and walked in the strength of that food forty days, and forty nights, unto the mount of God, Horeb [n: (III Reg. XIX, 7, 8)]. » This therefore is that bread which we demand daily, saying, « Give us this day our daily bread [n: (Luc. XI, 3)]; » because without this bread of virtue and of grace it is unjust for any man to approach to, or to attain to, any bread of the sacrament. But this third is that bread of the sacrament, which the Lord makes of himself in a wonderful manner, which he grants to his Church to be communicated unto life, and with which if any man shall not have communicated by believing and equally by eating, there is no doubt that he will by no means have eternal life.



## 6. (cont.)

Let these things, then, that have been said concerning the pasturing of the Bridegroom suffice; and if we so understand him [1192D] that he either furnishes pasture or himself receives pasture, it makes no difference; when he distributes these loaves to them by preaching, or they receive the same loaves by believing and loving; let him be one and the same who is pastured and pastures, and in them, as in his faithful members, is delighted and takes his rest. For hence it is added, « Where thou feedest, and where thou liest down, » in that he has the couch of his own rest in those whom he either feeds with these loaves, or who are refreshed by the same loaves, a faithful teacher ministering the word. For this too is joined on, « At midday. » For midday is called the middle of the day, in which the sun shines more fervently, and the day dawns brighter than is its wont. Each one, therefore, has his own midday, when, [1193A] forgetful of temporal things, he now runs over spiritual things in his mind; and unmindful of bodily things, sets the things that are of the spirit before his eyes in his mind. Hence came forth the dogmas of the Stoics, of the Epicureans, of the Peripatetics, hence the many fictions of studious men, who, the way not yet laid open to them, laying down the highest good one in this fashion and another in that, and not having Christ for their leader, each erred in his own midday. But to Abraham, sitting in the valley of Mambre [n: (Gen. XVIII, 1)], in the door of his tabernacle, in the heat of the day, as it is written, the Lord appeared; and, plainly showing him the unity of substance, in which he truly is, and the trinity in persons, he made him a professor of the true faith. Of whom the Lord in the Gospel: « Abraham your father rejoiced that he might see my day: he saw it and [1193B] was glad [n: (Joan. VIII, 56)]. » For then he manifestly beheld the day of his trinity, when he understood him in the trinity to be the third person, and one God in that same trinity. For thus it is written, that « the Lord appeared to one sitting in the tabernacle [n: (Gen. XVIII, 1)], » in which the unity of substance is shown: and when he had opened his eyes, « three men appeared » [n: (ibid., 2)]; behold the mystery of the Trinity. And since it was afterward said to them, « Lord, if I have found grace in thy eyes, pass not by thy servant [n: (ibid., 3, etc.)]; » behold the mystery of Unity. « But I will fetch a little water, and wash ye your feet; and I will set a morsel of bread, and strengthen ye your heart: afterward ye shall pass on: for therefore have ye turned aside to your servant. Who said, Do as thou hast spoken; » behold the mystery [1193C] of the Trinity. And after these things, all things are comprehended under the mystery of the Trinity, as far as that word, « Returning I will come to thee at this time, life accompanying [n: (ibid., 10)]. » This is that noble midday in which the Lord lies down, in which he rests: as also that of Moses [n: (Exod., 12)], in which he understands that the people of God is to be led out of Egypt in the blood of the lamb: as also this most recent one, in which the Lord, prefiguring himself to the apostles, showed his face after the manner of the sun, and his garments in the whiteness of snow; and a voice also sounded from heaven, « This is my beloved son, in whom I am well pleased [n: (Matth. XVII, 5)]. » Of this the Bride says, « Show me, thou whom my soul loves, where thou liest down in the midday, » that is, what that midday may be, « Where thou liest down, » that is, where thou restest, and preparest for thyself a place of rest and of inmost repose; [1193D] « Lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions. » He is said to wander who is not held by one judgment of his own mind, now approving one thing, now thinking another about the same matter, and settling nothing certain for himself in which he may abide and establish for himself the foundations of his belief. That this may not befall her, she requires to be made certain by the Bridegroom what meditation may please him, what things she ought to think concerning him, or to preach to others, so that persevering in these she may not run about after the flocks of his companions, nor increase the number of those who set themselves up as the Lord's companions by thinking certain good things of him, while in other things they contradict him by lying most wickedly. There follows:

*[1194A]*



## 7.

*If thou know not thyself, O most beautiful among women, go forth.*

Taught by the Spirit of wisdom, this Bride prayed to the Lord, that he would teach her by his Spirit where he pastured, where he chose himself a couch at midday. But afterward, being for a little while lifted up in spirit, she subjoins what has been said, « Lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions. » Whence she seems to hold out threats, that unless what she asks be done, she will mingle herself with the flock of strangers.
