Marking this, the Lord is carried a little way, and for a little while, into anger against her who was lifted up, according to what is said, « With the holy thou wilt be holy, and with the innocent man thou wilt be innocent; and with the elect thou wilt be elect, and with the perverse thou wilt be perverted [n: (Psal. XVII, 26, 27)]. » For the Lord, as he is found holy to the holy, innocent [1194B] to the innocent, elect to the elect, so most justly is he found perverse to the perverse. Whence to her saying, « Lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions. » The Lord answers, « If thou know not thyself, O beautiful one among women [var: Vulg. *pulcherrima*, "most beautiful"], go forth. » But it is to be marked what is said, « O beautiful one among women! » For the Lord chose her for himself as Bride, that she might be with him, might abide with him, might bear herself in his company in good faith, and might never depart from him through any man's perversion: beautiful therefore is she who has been so lifted up, so beloved, that while the rest remain in the estate of women, she herself has been chosen unto the throne of a Queen, and appointed to hold princedom over them all. And these women, among whom she has been chosen (in comparison with whom she seems comely, nay to be wondered at [1194C]), are all the souls of the Heathen, of Publicans, of Jews not following the law and not confessing Christ, and of Christians also, who confess in words that they know God, but deny it by their deeds. That this therefore may be brought back to her memory, it is said, « O beautiful one among women, if thou know not thyself. »

It is indeed a great peril for a perfect soul, or for a chosen bride, to be ignorant of herself: to be ignorant, namely, of this, that she was created out of nothing by the highest Father, the God of all, and yet formed to his image and likeness. She is indeed « Image » (as we said above) by reason, knowing what is just, what is unjust, what is to be done, what to be shunned, and that what she does not wish to be done to herself is to be denied to her neighbor, [1194D] and many things of this kind: all which things the soul observing, fully expresses and keeps the image of him by whom it was created. But she is « Likeness » when, faith having been received, she proceeds further, choosing for herself virtues which she may love, which she may cherish, by which she may rightly please him by whom she was created, and, all these being gathered in mind and in deed and perfected by charity, may give and offer them. There is moreover this, that she is said to be created to the image and likeness of God, because she was so created that she never ceases to be, but abides and stands fast unto perpetual eternity, forever retaining the good which by virtue she has merited from the Lord's hand, or else the evil, if her deserts have required it. There is another thing also in which it does not become the Bride to be ignorant of herself, but it is just to keep perpetual [1195A] knowledge of it: that he who before the ages was ever God and not man, for the sake of redeeming her and leading her back and fitting her to himself in the dignity of a Bride, was for her sake made man, mortal from immortal, passible from impassible, corruptible from incorruptible, and, having suffered those things which the long series of the Gospels unfolds, bore all, that he might lead her back to the Father, and glorify her with himself in glory. Who shall tell out this glory? which neither eye hath seen, nor ear heard, nor hath it ascended into the heart of man, which God hath prepared for them that love him. If the soul or the beloved Bride is ignorant of these things, what remains but that it be said to her, « Go forth, » as if to say, withdraw from my company, « And go after the steps of the flocks, » the steps of those whom thou callest thy flocks, that is, [1195B] preach their sayings and their opinions, and proclaim them on every side: and feed thy kids? Behold what fruit will come to her, if she shall have gone out from the company of the good, if she shall have listened to those who walk ill, if she shall have proclaimed what they themselves preach and teach. What fruit is this? that she will feed « her kids, » that is, carnal motions, wanton and fetid; these she will feed by loosening the reins to luxury and to all the vices [n: (Matth. XXV, 41)], so that they may grow into those kids whom, set on the left hand, the Lord on the last day will send into eternal punishment. There follows:

*Beside the tabernacles of the shepherds.*

Some shepherds are good, who train their sheep unto life, and, if necessity require it, do not cease to die for them: the best exemplar of these is Jesus [1195C] Christ our Lord. There are others also, who by teaching perversely drag the flocks committed to them into death: and for this they fix tabernacles for themselves, as it were certain lecture-halls in which they may solemnly be heard. Being moved, therefore, the Bridegroom gives counsel [sic: *eonsilium*] to the Bride who was lifted up; that, if wearied of good counsel she tries to flee, she may try to pitch her tent for herself beside the tabernacles of these men, and to teach, so that those whom an equal guilt binds in fault an equal torment may also rack in punishment. There follows:

8. *To my company of horse in the chariots of Pharaoh have I likened thee, my friend.*

The Bridegroom does here what belongs to a lover. Her whom he had chosen for himself as Bride he commended with words, soothed with attentions, enlarged with promises. And she, receiving these promises, [1195D] while she is borne along by headlong desire, went a little too far in a word, saying, « Lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions. » A word indeed which she ought not even to have thought, and which, even if it had been presumed in thought, it was yet not lawful to open by speaking. The Bridegroom, moved for a little while at this word, marking that whence thanks ought to have been returned, thence threats were held out against him, answered with moderation, speaking a word of anger, « If thou know not thyself, O beautiful one, go forth, » and go, following the steps of those whom thou callest to thyself shepherds; and since I have no communion with them, thou wilt have to withdraw from my company. But the Bride, [1196A] understanding that her word was heavily taken by the Bridegroom, groans and grieves; and because she had offended by a word, she trembles to utter anything at all by a word. And when this became known to the Bridegroom, whether by an Angel intervening, or in that way by which he knows all things, forgetting the husband he put on the Bridegroom, he grieved with her that grieved, and to her that repented he at once forgave what she had committed, mindful of that which is said, « I will confess against myself my injustice to the Lord, and thou hast forgiven the impiety of my sin [n: (Psal. XXXI, 5)]. » Therefore, hatreds being wiped away and anger removed, that the things now said might be taken away from memory and this Bride recalled to her former state, he accosts her with a word of friendship, a word which she had not yet deserved to hear, saying, « My friend; » thus signifying that all that had been diminished from grace is now pleasing to him in thee. [1196B] He says therefore, « My friend, I have likened thee to my company of horse. » But here what company of horse of the Bridegroom is understood, save those who fight bravely everywhere for the Bridegroom, and spread his victories abroad in heaven and in air, by land and by sea? This is that multitude of Angels which, when battles were first waged, when the proud cohort said, « I will set my seat toward the North, and I will be like the Most High [var: Vulg. *exaltabo solium meum … sedebo in monte testamenti, in lateribus aquilonis*] [n: (Isa. XIV, 14)], » clung with one accord to its own prince, giving honor and glory to him who had created it, and resisting the disturber, unto all his confusion and ignominy. Him therefore, cast down from heaven, irrevocably estranged from that glory which he was lately able to hope for, he casts down into hell, and commits to him places of horror and of vast solitude to be inhabited, so that no going forth lies open to them save such as the divine [1196C] dispensation grants; so that hence a going forth, they may either prove the elect, or hurl headlong those foreknown unto damnation by a temptation fitted to them. Hence the Lord Jesus, and his chief members, Peter and Paul and all the apostles, and all the thousands throughout the world, at his instigation and by his sending it into the hearts of the wicked, were given over to the cross and to the sword and to other passions innumerable, that they might be proved by the temptations of these, and, not overcome even by death itself, might be received into eternal glory. For John says, « When the devil had now put it into the heart, that Judas the son of Simon Iscariot should betray him [n: (Joan. XIII, 2)]: » and the Lord in Job, « Behold he is in thy hand, but yet save his life [n: (Job II, 6)]. » Behold, that power was given him in the head, and in his best members [1196D]. For the power which was given him in Job, that same power was granted him in Peter, and in Paul, and in all the other elect throughout the world; that he might either tempt them with death, or press them down in the flesh, yet himself keep their souls unharmed. So the company of horse of God, obtaining victory in these, exercised the same also upon men, first drowning in the waters of the flood those defiled by their deceit, afterward destroying with brimstone and with fire the cities given over to sin; but later, when the Lord was plaguing with hardest strokes the Pharaoh raging against the sons of Abraham, and he still did not come to his senses, upon him, who had entered the sea with chariots and horsemen, that he might seize the sons of Israel walking through the dry ground in [1197A] the midst of the sea, the heavenly company of horse being sent in, it executed vengeance for the Lord, and swallowed up both the king himself and the princes, the chariots and the charioteers and the horsemen, the waters being brought back to their former place by their ministry; and so it freed his people by a glorious victory.



## 7. (cont.)

This therefore is what the Bridegroom says, « My friend, I have likened thee to my company of horse, in the chariots of Pharaoh, » that is, while the angelic company was occupied in overthrowing the chariots of Pharaoh, I appointed thee like to that company of mine, so acting for thee, so fighting for thee, so rescuing thee, as one willing always to have thee a partaker of my company of horse. According to what was said by the Lord to the Jews, « You err, not knowing the scriptures, nor the power of God. For in the resurrection [1197B] they shall neither marry nor be given in marriage, but they are as the angels of God in heaven [var: Vulg. *erunt*, "shall be"] [n: (Matth. XXII, 29, 30)]. » But how great a thing it is to be likened to those whose virtue is that virtue, whose power is that power, which was shown at Lachish, in the army of Sennacherib [n: (IV Reg. XIX, 39)], when, as he held out threats against Jerusalem, it came to pass that in that night the Angel of the Lord came and struck of the camp of the Assyrians a hundred and eighty-five thousand men, for the delivering of Jerusalem. But if by one Angel striking so many thousands of men were laid low, what would happen if the whole company of horse of God rose up against the earth? it would not come about that even one of the reprobate should be left. For the Lord says, when the profane nation was seizing him, and Peter, to defend him, [1197C] struck with the sword the servant of the Prince: « Put up thy sword into the scabbard [var: Vulg. at this place *converte gladium tuum in locum suum*; the words printed are those of Joan. XVIII, 11]. Dost thou think that I cannot ask my Father, and he will give me more than twelve legions of angels? [n: (Matth. XXVI, 52, 53.)] » Which number indeed contains eighty thousand, eight less: and for the defence of Eliseus also many thousands indeed were sent, which, when Eliseus prayed that the Lord would open the eyes of his servant, were shown to the servant himself: for thus it was said, « Behold the mountain full of horses and of fiery chariots round about Eliseus [n: (IV Reg. VI, 17)]. » This therefore is the company of horse of God, in whom he fights, by whom he works his mysteries, to whom he likens the Bride whom he chose out of men, and conforms her to himself in comeliness, in power, and in virtue. He says therefore, « My friend, I have likened thee, » that is, in my predestination [1197D] I have already made thee like « to the company of horse » of mine, that is, to my Angels, whom I send forth to execute my vengeances, to my wars, to those things which seem to men exceedingly grievous, as was lately shown in the chariots of Pharaoh. There follows:

9. *Beautiful are thy cheeks, as the turtledove's: thy neck as necklaces.*

The Bridegroom says to the Bride, that he may soothe her and render her at peace with him in all things, and may not allow her in any degree to spring back to the feeding of her kids, « I have likened thee, my friend, to my company of horse, » that is, in predestination I have appointed thee like in all things to my company. But because what has been predestined [1198A] must needs be fulfilled in act, in order that thou mayest be made worthy of the heavenly company, it behoves that they who hold the place of cheeks in thy body should be as it were the cheeks of a turtledove, and should present in themselves as it were the likeness of that bird. For the nature of this bird is this: it is joined to one mate, and when he is slain it is not coupled to another: it sits upon the branches of trees now dead, lacking leaves and fruit: there it moans, and sends forth a moan instead of a song. Those therefore whom this Bride is understood to have in her body as cheeks, these she is bidden to have like to the cheeks of the turtledove. For the cheeks are those parts which chew the food, and champ it and lessen it, and make it fit to be passed into the body. These are the holy writers, who, taught by the word of God, conceive Holy Scripture in their mind, and refresh themselves with the same, [1198B] and by imparting it to the uses of holy Church render her fit for the nuptials, and dear to God, and worthy of the eternal wedlock. These understand themselves to be joined as to a mate to Jesus Christ our Lord, « who, being in the form of God, thought it not robbery to be equal with God: but emptied himself, taking the form of a servant, being made in the likeness of men, and in habit found as a man [n: (Phil. II, 6, 7)]. » To him as to a mate they join themselves, to him they cleave, him they love; and since he has now been taken up from them into heaven, they affirm and maintain « that there is no other name under heaven in which men must be saved [n: (Act. IV, 12)]. » These, sitting upon dead branches, because those whom they consider dead to the world but living to God, [1198C] in them they take delight and rest, those whom neither the leaves of worldly wisdom clothe, nor worldly fruits delight, which quickly wither and perish. But these send forth a moan instead of a song, moaning that they lack that which they long for, and do not lay hold of the Lord Jesus whom they desire. Of these gathered into one it is said, « Blessed is he whose will is in the law of the Lord, and in his law he shall meditate day and night. And he shall be like the tree which is planted beside the running waters, which shall bring forth its fruit in its season. And its leaf shall not fall off, and all things whatsoever he shall do shall prosper [n: (Psal. I, 1, 2, 3)]. » These things are assigned to the writers of the divine law; as those which follow are attributed to its teachers and preachers.

[1198D] *Thy neck as necklaces.*

For *collum*, the neck, is so called from *colligando*, binding together, in that it binds the body to the head, and turns these two into one. By which name preachers and holy teachers are aptly named, who, receiving those things which are given by the Holy Spirit and expounding them by his gift, set them before the whole Church, and make her cleave to her head; who assuredly ought to be as necklaces fixed upon the breast, admonishing of the good those to whom they are attached. For a necklace fixed upon the breast speaks thus, says thus: Whom I have undertaken to guard, be thou chaste, be modest, be a lover of all honesty; let no adulterous hand know thee, nor let anything touch [1199A] thee whereby the faith owed to thy Bridegroom may be injured, or love in any point diminished. Which voice assuredly well befits the preacher and the teacher, that he be chaste and honest, and, like a round necklace, perfect on every side by his roundness, and circumspect. Let him keep these things in himself, let him teach everywhere that they are to be observed. Whence that golden necklace rings out well, saying: The loins of the faithful are girded, when evil concupiscence is repressed, when wicked thought is restrained, when the mind resists lust, when effect is denied to wantonness, when luxury is trodden down, when avarice and rapine are contemned, when injury of a neighbor is avoided, when pride is conquered, when envy is overcome. And that these necklaces may fitly hang from the neck, and remain firmly fixed upon the breast, the Bridegroom says:

*[1199B]*



## 10.

*We will make thee golden murenulae.*

The *murena* is a fish, comely in its length, and sprinkled over with a variety of color. Whence chains wrought of gold are called *murenulae*, which, set about the neck, are stretched out at length, and, joined to the necklace, bind it immovably to the breast-band. These therefore he sets forth that he will make for the Church his Bride; because as the understanding of those who give the law, or who give the first principles of letters, out of which either the Old or the New Testament arises, is from the divine wisdom, so also the understanding, the talents, and the whole invention of those who are the expositors of the same Scriptures are from him: and the things by which they enlarge, or magnify, or strengthen the Church proceed from the overflowing of the divine gift. The Bridegroom speaks of them [1199C] because he himself will make them, namely those sentences, but « vermiculated with silver, » that is, adorned with the divine eloquence. Whence he signifies that he gives them not only the understanding which the expositors put forth, but that he also ministers the fragrance of eloquence, whereby they please, whereby they are received into the fellowship of their hearers. He says therefore, « We will make thee golden *murenulae*, » that is, we will confer upon thee divine sentences, « vermiculated, » that is, adorned after the manner of worms, and set off « with silver, » that is, with the divine eloquence, which rings more sweetly than all metals. But the very change of speech is to be marked, that whereas hitherto the Bridegroom has used the singular in his speech, now, framing his words to the same Bride, he speaks in the plural number, « We will make golden *murenulae* » for thee; thus [1199D] showing the secret of the Trinity, which established the whole of both laws, and administered to the expositors of both the grace whereby they might do this and be pleasing. Whence the restoring of the highest salvation is now to be hoped for by her, whom, lest she ever give heed to the feeding of her kids, he both names above « friend, » and here, promising his own gifts, now reveals to her the mystery of the highest and adorable Trinity.

11. *While the King was in his repose, my spikenard gave forth its odor.*

Now when the Bride had heard those things which were set before her by the Bridegroom, namely, that she had been likened to the heavenly company of horse; and that they who in her body hold the place of cheeks [1200A] ought to have the likeness of the turtledove; and that they who hold the place of the neck should be likened to necklaces; having heard nonetheless the promise, that *murenulae* made by the hand of the highest Trinity are to be given her; the Bride opens her mouth and draws in the spirit, having yet nothing that she may offer, nothing that she may worthily render in return, save only that charity with which she was endowed from the beginning, before the incarnation of Christ, and that with which, after his advent and his ascension into the heavens, she was drawn to him by a marvellous gift. Hoping this to be precious, reckoning this unworthy of repulse, she makes mention of it, and promises that it will abide with her in eternal stability, saying, « When the King was in his repose. » And by the King's repose she names that purpose of the divine mind by which the Father disposed to send his Son into the lands, [1200B] to take flesh of the kindly virgin Mary, and that he, born of her, should teach the world, and that they who did not bear his holy doctrine should affix him to the cross, and that he should die, and after death rise again by his own power, and afterward ascend mightily into heaven, charity marvellously working these things, and the Son obeying herein, and the Holy Spirit herein consenting. And although this was disposed by eternal law, yet it had delay, and from Eve unto Adam, and on account of Adam's offence, it was deferred unto this fullness of time. But now through Christ grace and truth has been given [var: Vulg. *gratia et veritas per Jesum Christum facta est*, "grace and truth was made by Jesus Christ"] [n: (Joan. I, 17)]; and what had been disposed by the Father was completed by the working of grace.

He says therefore, « When he was in his repose, » that is, while he whom king above all things by judgment, the Son, [1200C] namely, who with the Father and the Holy Spirit rules all things; while he « was in his repose, » that is, was resting without labor in this purpose of his mind, « My spikenard gave forth its odor. » The spikenard is a lowly and odorous tree, by which the Bride designates those to whom, before his advent, God himself conferred knowledge of the mystery and of the Sacrament which he was to work; who preached and loved him that was to come, and who burned with the inmost desire of the mind for this, that as they saw him in the spirit, so they might be made worthy to see him with bodily sight. This burning is charity, and the odor of holy desire, by which the holy Fathers kindled one another toward him. It is that odor which the Bride declares that the spikenard gave, while the king was in his repose. [1200D] This odor went forth first from the Father into the world and into the hearts of mortals, who, being indignant at the serpent because he had deceived the woman, said: « Because thou hast done these things, thou art cursed among all living things and beasts of the earth. Upon thy breast shalt thou go, and earth shalt thou eat all the days of thy life [n: (Gen. III, 14)]. » Which, as it was said, was so fulfilled in him by the divine sentence. For what goes upon its breast is never lifted from the earth: which is true of him, because the hearts of those whom he inhabits, while he lords it in them, he never permits of his own will to be lifted from earthly pleasures and thoughts, never to rise up into the light of the virtues which is from heaven; but « thou shalt eat » it all the days [1201A], that is, he compels them to be delighted with earthly pleasures, to sleep and rest in them, by a persuasion as false as he himself is false. But at length mercy looked down from heaven; for God says, « I will put enmities between thee and the woman, and thy seed and her seed [n: (Gen. III, 15)]; » as if he should say: This woman by believing thee has slipped away, I will create one woman who shall crush thy whole head, who by the opposing of virtue shall wear away the beginnings of all thy suggestions which thou fixest in wretched mortals, and will in no wise permit them to reach the effect thou urgest.

This was that blessed Mary, who was sanctified from the womb, who was straightway so walled about by the Holy Spirit that no place was left to the serpent of tempting her, or of suggesting anything sinister: if [cj: *si*; read *sic*, "so"] all that [1201B] is wont to move the hearts of mortals, offered to her, presented to her, was straightway crushed by the heel of virtue. Whence between her and the serpent enmities have been set and made firm, so that what pleases the one displeases the other, what the one party urges the other utterly abhors, nor can wills wholly contrary agree upon one point. She possesses the glory of heaven, and rejoices to have with her perpetually all who commit themselves to her devotion; and those who tarry here, and have their heart toward her, she guards with her prayers, nor are they to be turned aside by his suggestions, or indeed to be wholly made subject. But the serpent goes about the lurking-places of hell, and there disposes places for his own; here, for those who stray from the faith, who do not fear to fulfil in act the things which he suggests in a thousand ways, he prepares punishments beyond reckoning, [1201C] and deadly griefs. These then are the enmities set between this woman and the serpent, which are also firmly established between the seed of the woman and the seed of the serpent. For the seed of this woman is Jesus Christ himself; the seed of the serpent, all who are not Christ's. The first seed of her was Abel, who confirmed his testimony to the Son of the Virgin by faith, by sacrifice, and by death. Her seed was Henoch, who bore testimony to the same son by true faith and by his ascension. Her seed was Noe, who, after the pattern of the same son, saved the Church in the flood of many waters in the wood. Her seed were Sem and Japheth, who did not look upon their father's shame, but covered it with faces turned away, veiling their own faces. For here God the Father, after the pattern of Noe, is said to have fallen asleep, when he consented that his son should be seized, [1201D] and, shamefully affixed to the cross, should be exposed to reproach; whom Cham saw and mocked, the accursed people of the Jews: but the two brothers, with faces turned away, did not see these things, nor mock them; two peoples, namely, the one of Jews only, penitent and converted, the other of the Gentiles. These, venerating the things done upon the cross, reckon nothing base, but covering them with the cloak of the scriptures, they venerate and adore them all. And to this seed Abraham is worthily ascribed, to whom, sitting in the door of his tabernacle in the heat of the day, the Lord is read to have appeared, and to have opened at once the signal mystery of the highest Trinity and of the Unity: to whom afterward, in his son Isaac, the mystery of the Lord's [1202A] passion was revealed, when, Isaac being bound for the passion, the father's sword, the angel forbidding, is drawn back from his neck, and the ram caught by the horns is offered. In which the will is truly to be held for the deed, which the angel alone hindered, and to which the father consented, the sword being now brandished, the neck stretched out, the son. With these two patriarchs that third, Jacob, is worthily numbered, whom we know as sleeping upon the stone, which is Christ, and beholding the ladder raised into heaven, by which certain angels were descending to visit the elect, while others were carrying up to the heights those already elect and now long well-deserving; and whom, worn down by the long squalor of toil, we know to have earned the two daughters of Laban, that is, the two Churches, the one given to action, the other to contemplation, [1202B] and, his father Laban being unwilling, to have returned with them to his own place; and, as he was at his last and prophesying by a prophetic spirit true things concerning Christ in his son Judah, we do not doubt that he said these words. « Judah, thee shall thy brethren praise [n: (Gen. XLIX, 8)]. » In which, when he had foretold many things concerning his passion and resurrection, he adds this also, « The sceptre shall not be taken away from Judah, nor a ruler from his thigh, until he come that is to be sent, and he shall be the expectation of the nations [n: (ibid., 10)]. » Which indeed is true of Christ; for from David their first King they had Kings in succession from the tribe of Judah, or at least Leaders, until, seizing Christ, they said, « We have no king but Caesar » [n: (Joan. XIX, 15)]. « If thou release this man, thou art not Caesar's friend [1202C] [n: (ibid., 12)]: » and adding they said also, « His blood be upon us, and upon our children [n: (Matth. XXVII, 25)]. » By which blood they were consequently overwhelmed; and when the Roman army came they were so destroyed by sword, by famine, and by pestilence, that countless thousands of that people died; and all the remnants, given over to exile and scattered under divers kings, serve foreign rulers, and guard their life by performing what they are commanded.



## 10. (cont.)

This they obtained from the death of Christ, of whom it is said, « and he shall be the expectation of the Gentiles: who his robe [n: (Gen. XLIX, 11)], » that is, his inner garment, « shall wash with wine, » white, namely, and pure and clarified, most clear, that is, he shall purify it by a certain interior and spiritual [1202D] understanding: « and his cloak, » namely the outer garment, this he shall cleanse « with the blood of the grape; » that is, with the passion of Christ. For those who serve Christ inwardly, discussing his hidden things in mind, and searching out more closely the several things which are said of his Trinity and Unity, of his divinity and his assumed humanity, and of the several sacraments of the Church, must needs excel by a certain higher and supereminent understanding. But for the rest it is enough if they confess and believe his death and blood and their own deliverance; concerning whom this was set down before, « Tying his cloak [var: Vulg. *pullum suum*, "his colt"] to the vineyard, and his she-ass, O my son, to the vine [n: (ibid.)]. » For Christ is the vine to which the she-ass is tied, [1203A] that is, by whose preaching the primitive Church of the Jews, which established both laws, was converted. But « to the vineyard » of his, that is, to the apostles, the Church of the Jews and of the Gentiles has been tied, when, acquiescing in their words and admonitions, it believed and adored Christ, the power and wisdom of God, bearing our infirmities, redeeming by his own death the death to which it was liable. But who would doubt that Moses belonged to this seed? who first wrote, « I will put enmities between thee and the woman, and thy seed and her seed. » And who could recount David also, and Job, Isaiah, and all the prophets? whose testimonies concerning the advent of Christ, if any man should undertake to write them, would lay upon himself the care of making a great volume. All these indeed are recorded to be the seed of the blessed virgin; [1203B] whom she bore, testifying by word, desiring in heart, and earnestly ministering the good odor and the desire of him to one another and to others. And these indeed are the things which this holy Bride recounts to her Beloved: that these holy fathers believed Christ before he came into flesh, foretold him, and desired him with a marvellous expectation. Now, proceeding further, she shows (after he has now come into what is ours, and, the mystery for which he had come being fulfilled in our flesh, has ascended into the heavens) how the Church, in whose number she reckons herself, follows him thither, saying:

12. *A little bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me, he shall abide between my breasts.*

[1203C]For this is as if she should say: My Beloved has been removed far from me in body, but has not departed from me in spirit: for he will make a stay with me; but what stay? that of which the Lord says, « If any man love me, he will keep my word, and my Father loveth him [var: Vulg. *diliget*, "will love"], and we will come to him, and will make our abode with him [n: (Joan. XIV, 23)]. » Behold that this stay is the abiding of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, with the Bride: in which she knows the Father. For she has the power of treading upon serpents and scorpions, and of resisting every spirit that is not good, drawing her into evils, nay urging the worst things; she has also wisdom, whereby she has the savor to do what she can; she has also in the Holy Spirit the will always to do that which is good: so that, while she can always, is always wise, [1203D] always wills that which is of honor, that which is of virtue, thou mayest hence acknowledge in her the abode of the highest Trinity. In the Apocalypse also John says, « Behold I stand at the door and knock: if any man shall hear my voice, and open the gate, I will come in to him, and will sup with him, and he with me [n: (Apoc. III, 20)]. » Who would doubt that this Bride heard the voice of her beloved? who would question that she opened the door to him? who would not understand that she is made glad with him at the supper? who would not judge that at this supper the Father and the Holy Spirit sit together with the Son? A rich supper, at which it is not doubtful that the heavenly Spirits minister; over which he presides who is their Lord. What banquets, what dishes, [1204A] what gifts are set out at it, understand from this which the apostle says, « comparing spiritual things with spiritual [n: (I Cor. II, 13)]: » for spiritual are the things which the Church sets before her Bridegroom with those assessors.

Here is set first the humility that is pleasing to God, with those virtues which accompany it, that is, the fear of God, penitence, confession pleasing to God, and the holy satisfaction of work. In a certain second order are set ordered chastity, moderate sobriety, commendable abstinence, and the things which are about these. The third place is held by peace, patience, brotherly love, and that which transcends all, charity toward God, and holy contemplation. This the Lord in John names a « supper, » because as no other meal follows supper, so he desires that the end of our life be consummated [1204B] in these things; that we may lay hold of him by fearing, by repenting, by making satisfaction, by living chastely, by having love toward all, and by loving God above all things, and may enjoy him without end. The Bride says therefore, « My Beloved to me, » that is, with me, « shall abide; » as if she should say, he will make a stay. And that she may signify the place where he will make his stay, she adds, « Between my breasts. » The breasts contain within themselves the chest, the principal part of our body, in which the heart is contained, and the liver, and the other parts whence very many powers arise, by which the human body is in great part ruled and quickened. That therefore she may designate by name the place where the Beloved may abide with her, she says, « Between my breasts, » that is, In the midst of my breast, that [1204C] he himself may hold my heart, and kindle it with love of himself, and that whatever shall seek to turn me to another thing, this he may weaken and turn away. For with the heart we love God, with the heart we hold charity; and if we retain charity, we assuredly have justice and prudence, fortitude and temperance; for charity retains in itself the effect of all these. For justice renders to each what is his own: it renders to God what is his, which is all honor, all glory, and the humble and holy devotion of every creature: it renders to a neighbor this, that one love him as much as oneself, that one watch over his advantages as much as over one's own, and be saddened and grieve at his disadvantages as much as at one's own: and this, resisting lust and the other impulses of the mind which are not right, what does it bear but the act of fortitude, [1204D] and strive to fulfil that act in itself? But temperance fulfils it, while in all these things it considers and provides that nothing be too much, that nothing be less than right. Let us mark what Paul asserts concerning it, saying, « Charity is patient, is kind: Charity envieth not, dealeth not perversely, is not puffed up, is not ambitious, seeketh not her own, etc. [n: (I Cor. XIII, 4, 5)]. »

And if any man should diligently seek to pursue these things, he will find this the more excellent way, that is, the way to which no effect of virtue is lacking, from which it is established that nothing is taken away whereby one might be able to be brought near to God. Whence the apostle says, « You are bought with a great price. Glorify and bear God in your body [n: (I Cor. VI 20)]. » The Bride says [1205A] therefore that her Beloved will abide with her, and will be to her as a bundle of myrrh. For myrrh is an ointment which flows from a tree, which is named by the same name, myrrh; for they are equal in name because they are equal also in quality: for myrrh is a tree, and also the bitter ointment which runs down from it. Whence the bodies of the dead are sprinkled with this very ointment, lest either rottenness corrupt them, or a worm come to destroy them: so the Lord's body is read to have been anointed with myrrh and aloes. This Bride says therefore, « My Beloved to me shall abide between my breasts [n: (Joan. XIX, 39)], » and he will be to me as a bundle of myrrh. And if thou refer this to the ointment, it is as if it were said, He will be to me as some little store of myrrh, which [1205B] may defend me both from rottenness, lest I be corrupted, and from the biting of worms, lest I be destroyed: if to the tree, it is plain that a bundle is made from a tree, and that she has this laid up within her breast for the same defence. For this « bundle of myrrh is the great memory of the passion of Christ; by which each man sees himself bound unceasingly to every neighbor, and understands how great a debtor he is to God, who suffered such things for him: and if thou hold it, if thou behold God, whithersoever thou turn thyself, suffering upon the cross, thou wilt free thyself by this very contemplation from the rottenness of vices and from the biting of worms, that is, from the temptation of unclean spirits. There follows:

13. *A cluster of Cyprus is my Beloved to me.*

Because this Bride, speaking of the Bridegroom, says, « Between [1205C] my breasts my beloved shall abide with me: and he will be to me as a bundle of myrrh, » which prepares bitterness only, it could be seen from this that the passion of Christ furnished cause for grief alone, but conferred no gladness upon the mind: whence, the bitterness being set first which is owed to Christ truly suffering, she adds the delight which from his death is shown to all who venerate it. For as it is just that grief and compassion be shown to one who grieves, so to him who frees and redeems the world joy and exultation are truly owed. Whence this Bride says, « A cluster of Cyprus is my beloved to me. » For Cyprus is an island abounding in vines, having clusters incomparable to the rest in their greatness, giving a wine which gladdens those who taste it [1205D] by the sweetness inborn in it, and soothes and mitigates grief, if there be any from suffering. For Christ is the bread which strengthens, he himself the wine which gladdens, and he himself is the oil which maketh glad the face of man, This Bride says therefore that her Beloved has been made a cluster of Cyprus; because as a cluster is nothing else than a gathering of many like grains firmly joined together into one, yielding wine abundantly when it is pressed, and gladdening on every side those who taste it: so has Christ been made, binding many to himself by his death, and reconciling them to himself in the faith of his passion; who, when they are pressed out, that is, when they are questioned with tortures and with other means most wickedly devised, send forth the wine of holy confession, by confessing and glorifying the Lord Jesus, and even, if need be, by undergoing death; [1206A] they confess and acknowledge that he alone is the living and true God: and if they survive, they exult with joy as confessors; if they are consumed, glad in death they rejoice as victors with Christ conquering.

Paul expresses both, saying, « Christ was made obedient for us unto death, even the death of the cross [var: Vulg. *humiliavit semetipsum, factus obediens usque ad mortem*, without *pro nobis*] [n: (Phil. II, 8)]. » Behold the myrrh, to which we owe myrrh, and bitterness, and compassion of every kind. « For which cause God also hath exalted him, and hath given him a name which is above every name: That in the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth; and that every tongue should confess that the Lord Jesus Christ is in the glory of God the Father [n: (ibid., 9, 10, 11)]. » Exulting indeed in this joy, this Bride says, « A cluster [1206B] of Cyprus is my Beloved to me. » For cleaving to the wood, he moved the earth, he darkened heaven; crucified, he crucified the world. So he drew all things to himself, and as victor he gladdened me, who suffer with his death, and overflowed me with joy. There follows:

*In the vineyards of Engaddi.*



## 10. (cont.)

Which is as much as if it were said: with the Lord Jesus suffering I have suffered together, and with him conquering I too gladly rejoice. For the world resounds far and wide with his victory, proclaims it, and by proclaiming it echoes it back, as appears in the vineyards of Engaddi. Engaddi is a plain beside Jericho, in which stand trees planted after the manner of a vineyard; from which, when they are wounded, there flows down balsam, useful indeed and precious: [1206C] which plain indeed signifies the breadth of the Church, which is known to spread itself now into the four parts of the world. This is named « Engaddi, » which is, « the fountain of the kid; » and as it signifies the Church, so the fountain which was in it signifies baptism, which is the fountain given to the Church, in which, while kids are cleansed, out of kids lambs are made. The power of Christ appears in these things, where from the wounded tree flows balsam, just as from the wounded Christ flowed the oil of blessing, when he said, « Father, pardon them, for they know not what they do [n: (Luc. XXIII, 34)]. » [var: Vulg. *dimitte illis: non enim sciunt*, "forgive them: for they know not"] Which balsam indeed is mingled with oil, that a most holy chrism may be made. With this chrism indeed the fountain of Engaddi is consecrated, which is called the fountain of baptism; with this the kids are anointed, thus passing over into the people of God; with this they are again confirmed, [1206D] lest Satan tempt them, and they be turned away from the truth; with this churches and altars are sanctified to God; with this the chalice is anointed, wherein God, delivering himself wholly to man, is made propitious; with this again kings are consecrated, bishops anointed, priests ordained, offering to Christ his own gifts on behalf of the people, and rendering to him what is his. This therefore is what the Bride says, « A cluster of Cyprus is my Beloved to me, » which appears in « the vineyards of Engaddi, » that is, in the churches spread abroad through the whole globe of lands, which all proclaim his glory, and announce his victory with great praise. There follows:

14. *Behold thou art beautiful, my friend, behold thou art beautiful. Thine eyes are those of doves.*

In which place this is to be observed, that the Bridegroom has already magnified his Bride with a threefold commendation. saying, [1207A] « I have likened thee to my company of horsemen: » and that other, « Thy cheeks as a turtledove's: » adding also a third, « Thy neck as jewels. » To whom the Bride also answers with a threefold praise, saying, « While the king was at his repose: » and that other, « A bundle of myrrh is my Beloved to me: » and that which follows, « A cluster of Cyprus is my Beloved to me, in the vineyards of Engaddi. » Whence, because there are now discourses of peace mingled between them, the Bridegroom, applauding the integrity of the Bride, whom he knows that he himself has loved, and now loves, and will in time to come always love, and knows that all this increases her beauty, commending her confidently, says, « Behold thou art beautiful, my friend; behold thou art beautiful. » But hearing this beauty commended, let us not attend to that beauty by which little women are wont to allure their [1207B] lovers, by which they are wont to lead those who have put their trust in them from the camp of chastity to the camp of uncleanness: for we have learned that God detests this, through the prophet saying, « The day of the Lord of hosts upon everyone that is proud and lofty, and upon everything that is beautiful to the sight [n: (Isa. II, 12, 16)] » : and pursuing it further, he says, « The Lord will take away in that day the ornament of shoes, the little moons, and the chains, and the necklaces, and the bracelets, and the bonnets [n: (Cap. III, 18, 19)], » and the rest that follow; all which without doubt are things that minister to this corruptible beauty, and to that which leads to corruption, and that turn the mind away from what is honourable and pleasing to God. But that beauty is to be attended to which true humility makes in the mind, the fear of God, holy [1207C] penance, humble confession, satisfaction for sins, peace toward all, and true concord, temperate chastity, and that which is above all, holy charity toward God. Of this the prophet makes mention, saying, « All the glory of the King's daughter is from within [n: (Psal. XLIV, 14)], » that is, from the beauty which is within is all the glory of the faithful soul, which is the daughter of God; whose glory is in « golden borders, » that is, in the consummation of the virtues. For the border is the finishing of the garment, which is well recorded as « clothed round about with variety, » that is, adorned with the varied diversity of all the virtues.

This indeed is to be attended to, in this is God well pleased, of which Scripture makes mention, saying, « I will recount [1207D] to thee all my years in the bitterness of my soul [n: (Isa. XXXVIII, 15)]. » For this omits no time, but that it calls to mind both the time itself and the sins committed in it: this bewails what was committed in infancy, what in boyhood, what in adolescence: this most bitterly accuses the excesses of youth; this powerfully convicts the enervating of manly strength, and grievously blushes that it has failed in something in the sluggishness of old age. Which blushing indeed confounds the demons, puts hell to flight, opens heaven, wins over the angels, renders God propitious; and when, falling down, it says, « Father, I am not worthy to be called thy son: make me as one of thy hired servants [n: (Luc. XV, 21)], » it earns the first robe, and the ring, and it glories that the shoes [1208A] of glory are set upon its feet. So, so does he name her beautiful, saying, « Behold thou art beautiful, » and he repeats that she is beautiful, adding, « Behold thou art beautiful. » Which repetition without doubt denotes that there is to be another for the soul itself or for the Bride, which he thus makes plain to the understanding. For the soul or the Bride, however perfect, here indeed rejoicing in beauty, feels a threefold misfortune; one from the flesh, which the Apostle laments, saying, « Unhappy man that I am, who shall deliver me from the body of this death? » [n: (Rom. VII, 24.)] another from a neighbour; a third from any unclean spirit. This is made manifest in Job, whom the flesh solicits, whom his wife counsels not well, whom his friends, at the prompting of the malignant spirit, compel to go astray. But the prophet counsels him, saying, « His truth shall compass [1208B] thee with a shield: thou shalt not be afraid of the terror of the night, of the arrow that flieth in the day, of the business that walketh about in the dark, of invasion, and of the noonday devil [n: (Psal. XC, 5, 6)]. » Where indeed some temptation is shown to be manifest, some hidden, some grievous, some light. For « the arrow » « that flieth in the day, » is manifest and light; « the noonday devil, » manifest and grievous; « the business that walketh about in the dark, » hidden and grievous; but « invasion » is understood to be hidden and light. But all these temptations will not be present in that life which is to come. For the glorified flesh will not be able to be tempted, nor will there be in it anything that resists the spirit. No spirit therein shall then be hurt by a neighbour; because then charity will be complete, so that each may love his neighbour as himself. But from [1208C] the malignant spirit what vexation shall there be able to be? who will neither be present, nor will there be able to remain to him any license at all of tempting the good, even for their testing. From then on his dwelling will be in hell, where he will be tormented by that very fire with which burn the wretched whom God has cast away, who by consenting to temptations have been sold under sin. There follows:

*Thine eyes are those of doves.*

The beauty of the Bride having been shown, he now subjoins what the cause of this beauty is: for this is its cause, that « her eyes are those of doves, » that is, that her whole regard is dove-like. For the dove, walking in her pasture, gathers only clean grains, feeds on these, nourishes herself with them: so also [1208D] this soul or Bride of God, while, disdaining no one for her own feeding, but hearing many, she considers each single thing, and what is said purely, holily, and truly, these things she keeps in mind, the rest she spurns and shudders at. The dove too is without gall, she neither hurts another bird with her beak, nor tears it with her claw, denoting the simplicity of this Bride, who is not angered unreasonably, nor anyone by word, nor by deed, but however greatly offended, keeps herself within the camp of her own innocence. She has groaning too instead of song, because, desiring to be present at the presence of her Lord, while this is denied her, bewailing that all things are wanting to her, she groans withal, and having this groaning instead of song, in groaning she says, « My soul [1209A] hath thirsted for God the living fountain; when shall I come and appear before the face of God [n: (Psal. XLI, 3)]. » [var: Vulg. *ad Deum fortem vivum*, "unto the strong living God"] She flies in flocks, because in all that she flies, in that she desires to be aloft, she chooses by no means to be separated from the Lord's flock, but to be taken up with them, to be glorified and made blessed with them. This dove-like eye would indeed have been necessary to Eve, when « she saw the tree that it was good to eat of, and beautiful to the eyes, and delightful to behold, and she took of the fruit thereof and did eat, and gave to her husband, and the eyes of them both were opened [n: (Gen. III, 6, 7)]. » Then they knew themselves stripped of obedience, ensnared in disobedience, and girt about with all manner of evils and liable to them. From her there proceeded in Cain an eye by no means dove-like, and thence it wandered wickedly through the world, and was opened to the concupiscence [1209B] of women. So that now there are few who say, « I was an eye to the blind, and a foot to the lame. I was the father of the poor: and the cause which I knew not, I searched out most diligently [n: (Job XXIX, 15, 16)]. » There follows:

15. *Behold thou art beautiful, my Beloved, and comely.*



## 10. (cont.)

Thus the lover answers the lover with an equal commendation: being called beautiful, she declares that he too is beautiful, saying, « Behold thou art beautiful, my beloved. » Hearing which, let us not attend to the beauty which is in creatures corporeal or incorporeal. For a certain beauty is present in these corporeal things, arising from the fitting disposition of their parts, when part is fitly joined to part, and so one comely form is produced from their fitting conjunction. [1209C] The greatest indeed among creeping things is the dragon, among swimming things the whale, among flying things the griffin, the elephant among four-footed beasts, the giant among men: in all of which, if the disposition of the parts itself be attended to, and the fitting conjunction of part to part, a form beyond doubt most beautiful, and the highest wisdom of the Creator, is found in them all. So there are some middling, and some are understood to be least; which, unless they have contracted sin, or the divine power has reserved them to itself to be healed, are known to display in themselves a wondrous beauty. If therefore thou attend to that great Adam, from whom the whole race of men is begotten, if thou attend to him, who and of what sort he was created, who he was before sin, who he was when the Lord [1209D] brought to him all the living things of the earth, and taught him what he should call them; it will be clear that he shone with a certain wondrous beauty: and Abraham, knowing that Adam would afterward obtain mercy through the incarnation of Christ and his death, passed through all places, and chose for his own burial that place alone in which Adam was buried, that double cave which he bought from Ephron the Hethite [n: (Gen. XXIII, 19)]; where with their wives rest Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and the great Adam. But is the beloved of this one called beautiful with this beauty? Far from it; for there follows, « And comely. » Whence it is given to be understood that he is beautiful with that beauty to which nothing is fitting, nothing is joined, or can be joined, save what is [1210A] comely. This beauty was not Adam's, in whom, if for a time sin was not present, yet it could be present, whence also he sinned.

But if thou attend to the heavenly bodies, the sun, the moon, and the stars, these indeed are beautiful, which with their own light illumine the darkness of the world, so that the things which lack light of their own, being illumined by these, may shine again: but of them it is written, « They shall perish, but thou remainest: and these shall grow old as a garment [n: (Psal. CI, 27)]. » [var: Vulg. *omnes sicut vestimentum*, "all of them as a garment"] But looking upon the angels, and the orders even up to the Cherubim and Seraphim, and stretching thy gaze above them, the holy Trinity alone will meet thee, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, whose beauty is that which neither eye has seen [n: (I Cor. II, 9)], nor ear heard, nor has it ascended into the heart of man, which God will make manifest [1210B] to those who love him. [var: Vulg. *quae praeparavit Deus*, "which God hath prepared"] Of which it was said to Moses, « Man shall not see me and live [n: (Exod. XXXIII, 20)]. » For here it is not granted to the living to see what is promised to be seen by the man already glorified and taken up into the true life, the Lord saying, « This is life eternal, that they may know thee the true God, and Jesus Christ whom thou hast sent [n: (Joan. XVII, 3)]. » [var: Vulg. *solum Deum verum*, "the only true God"] This is the form, this the fairness, this the beauty, into which the angels also desire to look [n: (I Pet. I, 12)]. This is that beauty of which David says, « I shall be satisfied when thy glory shall appear [n: (Psal. XVI, 15)]: » which appearing to us and showing itself visibly, there is nothing that we should seek further, there is nothing wherewith we should desire to be further refreshed; with this beauty therefore it was said, « Behold thou art beautiful, my beloved: » [1210C] to this beauty also, because there is nothing that cleaves to it, nothing that can be mingled with it, save what is honourable, and what is believed worthy of all glory, there is rightly joined, « And comely; » so that he who seeks to stand by this beauty may always hold himself upon the summit of virtue and in comeliness. There follows:

*Our little bed is flowery.*

Behold, she who praises comeliness in the Bridegroom seems, pierced through by love of him, to call him to the little bed, that she may fully enjoy his presence, that she may be gladdened by delight in him. To which she seems to entice him both by the pleasantness of the bed and by the delightfulness of the place, saying, « Our little bed is flowery. The beams of our houses are of cedar, our panelled ceilings of cypress. » For it is pleasant [1210D] to rest on a flowery bed, and safe indeed to lie down beneath beams of cedar and panelled ceilings of cypress: and these things indeed are true, but let us attend to them more deeply, if we take care to understand them spiritually. For this little bed is not indeed a place of softness, not of obscene buffoonery, but the little bed to be attended to is that of delightful rest in the Lord, and of perfect contemplation in him. For contemplation is when a man first considers himself, who he is, and whence he has come, and to what he has come, and searches out, and is not ignorant, that God created for him a rational soul, and sent this into his body, and that he, being so born, received the grace of baptism, and so grew up in faith; and being taught by faith, he does not doubt, the truth of faith teaching it, that the Son of God came into flesh, underwent death [1211A] in it, and rose on the third day for the redeeming of man, and thus, that man might ascend to life, ascended into heaven, and thus drew all things to himself. Attending to these things, he knows not whither to turn himself; he despises all that he has done hitherto; he bewails with tears the sins which infancy, or youth, or another age committed; he casts himself down; he purposes to transform himself into some other man; and thus he himself makes ready a bed for God with the hand of devout and diligent contemplation. Which « little bed » nevertheless is still understood to be « flowery. » For these are the beginnings of contemplation, which give hope of good advancement and of ripe fruit, which will then be present, when the world shall now be forsaken and wholly trodden under the foot of the mind, when now all things [1211B] that pass away shall be despised, and those alone loved which always abide and stand unchanged. Knowing therefore, this soul or Bride, that this flower of good imitation has come forth by the gift of God, and that he himself has sent these things before as the first beginnings of his coming, she calls him to the flowery little bed, that he may rest in it, and bring this flower through to the ripeness of holy perfection. She asserts also that this little bed is common to the Bridegroom and the Bride, while she herself receives this contemplation within her, and knows that it comes forth from the gift of God and from his presence. There follows:

16. *The beams of our houses are of cedar, our panelled ceilings of cypress.*

This is a persuasion drawn from safety; for as it is pleasant to rest on a flowery bed, so also it is safe and secure to rest beneath a roof [1211C] that is covered with beams of cedar. For the cedar and the cypress are of an incorruptible nature, they know not how to be corrupted, they preserve alike their greenness and their scent: by their scent too they put serpents to flight and kill them, and thus provide safety for those resting beneath them. Thus therefore is this house roofed, into which the Bridegroom is invited by the Bride. No rain comes down here, from which the overshadowings of cedar and cypress should protect it. For these cedars are indeed the angels, to whom the keeping of this house is committed: for of them it was said, « Scandalize not these little ones: for I say to you, that their angels always see the face of my Father, who is in heaven » [n: (Matth. XVIII, 10)]. [var: Vulg. *Videte ne contemnatis unum ex his pusillis*, "See that you despise not one of these little ones"] But « the panelled ceilings » [1211D] which furnish the beams with defence and strength, which preserve their own greenness, and preserve a most excellent scent, by which serpents are killed, these are the holy archangels, furnishing strength to the angels, ever green with that greenness indeed in which they were created, and with that very greenness with which they were endowed when they withstood him who said, « I will set my seat toward the north, and I will be like the Most High [n: (Isa. XIV, 14)]. » And the scent of their virtues kills the malignant spirits, lest they disturb the hearts of the good, lest they rise from good beginnings to greater things. But it must be observed that when the Bride says, « The beams of our houses are of cedar, the panelled ceilings of cypress, » she uses that manner of speaking by which the whole is understood through a part: for in « the bed, » to which the better part is ascribed, the foundation of the house itself and the walls are understood: but in « the beams » [1212A] and « the panelled ceilings, » which are the chief part of the roof, all that belongs to the roof is ascribed. There follows:



## CHAPTER II.

1. *I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys.*

The Bridegroom, called to rest, answers thus: Thou callest me to rest, my friend, my Bride; and that thou mayest allure me the more, thou addest that « our little bed is flowery: » but this answer I give thee, that « I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys. » In which this is to be observed, that there are two fields, one heavenly, the other earthly. The heavenly field extends as far as heaven reaches, because, [1212B] having no ending, it is great and measureless. This field has flowers: angels, archangels, virtues, dominations, thrones, principalities, and powers, cherubim, and seraphim: of each of these orders there are many thousands: with these flowers heaven is filled. All the saints desire to be among these flowers; with groans and sighs they pant to be in their company. Of all whom indeed « I am the flower: » from me indeed they receive what they are; from me they have this of fragrance, that they are good; all these stand unceasingly before my majesty; they sing together a hymn of glory to me, saying, « Holy, holy, holy, the Lord God almighty » [n: (Apoc. IV, 8)]. Me therefore, who refresh all these with good fragrance, and supply fragrance sufficient for their feeding, [1212C] what is it that thou demandest that I be present at thy flowery little bed, and that thou redoublest thy voice, as though about to add something to my glory? Which indeed was said thus, not because the Bridegroom disdains to be present at the good beginnings of the faithful; but that the Bride, hearing these things, may be the more inflamed with her own desire, and may the more choose by her own prayer that he be present, who is the flower of the whole world, and its comeliness, and its illumination. Which the Bridegroom too indeed intimates, saying, « Of the field, » the heavenly one, « I am the flower, » yet I am « also the lily of the valleys. » The earthly field, therefore, is this world, which that higher flower, visiting it, showed himself to the faithful as a lily, and gave and showed himself humble to the humble. In the lily indeed there is a flower most sweet in scent, most pleasing to the sight, and there is also [1212D] a stem thick with leaves; feeding not indeed by scent, nor having sweetness of taste, yet having great efficacy in medicines. These things indeed befit Christ; who, sent by the Father, truly appeared on earth as a lily of most sweet scent; of whom Paul says: « But thanks be to God, who always leads us in triumph in Christ Jesus, and manifests the odour of his knowledge by us in every place: For we are a good odour to Christ unto God in them that are saved, and in them that perish [n: (II Cor. II, 14, 15)]. » [var: Vulg. *Christi bonus odor*, "the good odour of Christ"] But he was most pleasing to the sight of those who received him. Whence Peter says: « Lord, to whom shall we go? thou hast the words of eternal life. And we have believed, and have known, that thou art the Christ the Son of the living God » [n: (Joan. VI, 71, 72)]. But the stem thick with leaves is his humanity, [1213A] which indeed, in the words by which he reproved the world, displeased many, to whom he took care to offer the help of a saving medicine. But he is « the lily of the valleys, » because « God resists the proud, but to the humble he gives grace [n: (Jac. IV, 6)]. » From above too it was said, « Let the proud be confounded, because they have done iniquity unjustly against me [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 78)]. » But of the humble it was said, « that the valleys shall abound with corn: they shall cry out, yea they shall sing a hymn [n: (Psal. LXIV, 14)]. »

2. *As the lily among thorns, so is my friend among the daughters.*

The flower of the world has spoken of himself, saying, « I am the flower of the field. » who also declared to the world that he, true God and man, would profit the humble, saying, [1213B] « And the lily of the valleys. » But now he goes on concerning the Bride, who is joined to him by the love of the heart, saying: As I, a lily among thorns, have lived in the world; so my friend shall dwell among the same in the world. But he names as « thorns » the detractions, upbraidings, and mockeries by which the world despised him with wicked derision: he names as « thorns » also their deeds and handlings, by which, seizing him with swords and clubs, they led him as a robber and a murderer to the chief men, Annas and Caiaphas: and thereafter, before the Judge, spat upon, dishonoured with blows of the fist and with buffets, and crowned with thorns, they demanded that he be condemned and fastened through with nails. To these thorns, to this thorny bed, he calls the bride, who was preparing for herself a flowery bed [1213C] beneath beams of cedar and panelled ceilings of cypress, who, humbled, did not refuse this bed, but endured one like it in Peter, and in Paul too, and the rest of the apostles, in Stephen, and in the other many thousands of martyrs throughout the world, and received it gladly. But from whom she received it he adds, saying, « Among the daughters, » that is, generations depraved and perverse, to whom the Lord showed himself. Yet these did not know him; for so it is written, « He came unto his own, and his own received him not [n: (Joan. I, 11)]: whom he names « his own, » not by affection, but by creation alone; since indeed, laying hands upon his own, they afflicted them with torments and tortures: he now names the transgressing daughters by the first creation, and now [1213D] signifies that they have a filial affection along with his own. There follows:

3. *As the apple tree among the trees of the woods, so is my beloved among the sons.*

The Bridegroom and the Bride answer one another with reciprocal praise, and, refreshing themselves with mutual praises, the more they praise, the more they desire to praise. For this Bride says, « As the apple tree among the trees of the woods, so is my beloved among the sons. » For the apple tree is a tree that puts forth branches widely, brings forth leaves, swells with blossom, and out of the blossoms brings forth the fruit of apples even to ripeness: which fruit, whether it be called *pomum* or *malum*, is of surpassing colour, of pleasant taste, and of a fragrance excellent beyond what can be or could ever be told. Laden with this, the tree itself, which is called « the apple tree, » not only takes on comeliness in itself, [1214A] but also clothes the surrounding woods with comeliness, and renders the whole place about it most pleasant, and most worthy of a holy habitation. This Bride says therefore, « As the apple tree among the trees of the woods, so is my beloved among the sons; » wishing by the tree of surpassing fruit that the only-begotten Son of God, beloved of her, be understood, who so clothed and arrayed with glory and honour the flesh which he assumed, that deservedly, before all the saints, it is held most worthy of honour and reverence, and is most excellently venerated. Whence it was said, « The Lord hath reigned, he is clothed with comeliness, the Lord is clothed with strength, and hath girded himself [n: (Psal. XCII, 1)]. » He adds also this which is said, « Among the trees of the woods; » for as Christ is a heavenly tree, so all the saints are heavenly trees, who, living in the flesh, [1214B] brought forth fruits pleasing to Christ. Among them the apostles, and the holy martyrs, and all the Saints together are numbered, who, knowing nothing save Christ, following him by a glorious death, bearing witness to the truth, have long since received glorious seats in heaven. Of these it is said in the Psalms, « Then shall all the trees of the woods rejoice before the face of the Lord, because he cometh; for he cometh to judge the earth [n: (Psal. XCV, 13)]. » For the Lord is to judge this world; who in a body once condemned and now wondrously glorified will show himself visible to all the saints in their own raised bodies; and there will then be rejoicing for the trees of the heavenly woods, when by a sure apprehension [1214C] and glorification of themselves, not only of the spirit but of the body also, they have known that they did not run at uncertainty, nor labour in vain. But the trees of that wood through which the beasts and the whelps of lions pass to snatch food for themselves will then be assigned to their own night, and to everlasting darkness. This therefore is what the Church says: « as the apple tree, » laden with its own fruits, shines out « among the trees of the woods » with a certain wondrous beauty; « so my beloved, » the only-begotten Son of God, upon whom my love has bestowed itself wholly, stands far more eminent with a certain unspeakable glory among all the saints, whom he has adopted to himself as sons not by creation alone, but by grace also. There follows:

*Under the shadow of him whom I had desired, I sat.*



## CHAPTER II. (cont.)

Here it must be observed whence a shadow comes. [1214D] A shadow comes to these bodies from a light-bearing body, and from a shadow-casting body, and from the place upon which the shadow is thrown: if any of these be lacking, there is no shadow. This tree of the apple therefore, since it stands among the trees of the woods, since it is spread wide with branches and rich with leaves, must needs cast a shadow against the sun: beneath which it follows that this Bride sits and rests. The « shadow, » therefore, is the frequent meditation upon the divine incarnation and passion, upon the resurrection also and the ascension, and upon his acts and words; into which she protests that she panted with the whole desire of her heart, that there might be fulfilled in deed what had been promised by the Father, and foretold by the prophetic promise of the holy Fathers. Whence Isaias, « Let thy ancient counsel, O Lord, be made true [n: (Isai. XXV, 1)]. » [var: Vulg. *cogitationes antiquas fideles*, "ancient faithful designs"] Jeremias [1215A] too, desiring this in the Lamentations, says, « The breath of our mouth, Christ the Lord, is taken in our sins: to whom we said, Under thy shadow we shall live among the nations [n: (Thren. IV, 20)]. » And there stand the other oracles of the holy Fathers, which a diligent reader will easily find for himself. This therefore is the shadow, in which this present Bride bears witness that she has sat. For whereas the postures of bodies are four, to stand, to sit, to recline, and to lie, sitting is that which is more fit for ordering and disposing affairs, and more acceptable at once for giving counsels and for forming judgments. Whence Holy Scripture too often makes mention of it; for David says, « The Lord shall sit as king for ever [n: (Psal. XXVIII, 10)]. » And that other, « Thy throne, O Lord, is for ever and ever [n: (Psal. XLIV, 7)]. » [var: Vulg. *Sedes tua, Deus*, "Thy throne, O God"] And that which follows; « The Lord said [1215B] to my Lord: Sit thou at my right hand [n: (Psal. CIX, 1)]. » In the book of Kings too it was said by the prophet Micheas: « I saw the Lord sitting upon a high throne, and all the host of heaven standing by him on the right hand and on the left [n: (III Reg. XXII, 19)]. » [var: Vulg. *super solium suum*, "upon his throne"] Isaias too says: « I saw the Lord sitting upon a throne high and lifted up: and the things that were beneath him filled the temple [n: (Isai. VI, 1)]. » And after all these the Lord himself says, « When the son of man shall sit upon the seat of his majesty, then shall all nations be gathered together before him [n: (Matth. XXV, 31, 32)]. » Girt about with these authorities also, the Church says of herself, « Under the shadow of him whom I had desired, I sat, » that he might come, or, which shadow I had desired, that it might be fulfilled in affection, I sat deliberating and desiring, [1215C] wholly bent in this affection, that he might fulfil to me, being his Bride, the things promised, and might not put off my prayers to an immeasurable while. In this his shadow « I sat, » because I applied my whole mind to this, that I might devoutly receive him, whether about to be incarnate or perhaps already incarnate, might faithfully adore him born, might behold him teaching the fullness of faith, might not despair of him dying, but might rise up with him rising again in great certainty of faith, and might follow him ascending thither whence he had come, with the whole intent of my mind, and might cleave to those who render him service. There follows:

*And his fruit is sweet to my throat.*

As a shadow stands apart from its own body; so the fruit of that apple stands apart from its own image. For this apple tree is a heavenly [1215D] tree, the heavenly wisdom indeed, in the heavens for this reason, that it is rooted in the heart of the Father; which not in the heavens only, but, when it pleased the Father, made a root in the earth: in the earth indeed, when the whole flood of divinity betook itself into the heart of the immaculate Virgin, and the heavenly shower with a gentle gliding-down utterly drenched the virgin fleece: which, since it is rooted in heaven and in earth, brings forth indeed a fruit that ministers sweetness to those who are in the heavens, and to those who are on earth. But what is this fruit? The Flesh indeed which from clean bread upon the holy altar, by the office of the minister, by the power of the word, has been made, and granted in a most sacred tasting for the feeding of the Church, lest perchance it faint by the way. Of which the Church says, to whom this too has been given, « And his fruit is sweet to my throat. » [1216A] O blessed throat, to which this sweetness has been granted! that, abiding in man, it may snatch itself above man, and being on earth, joining itself to him who presides in heaven, may mingle itself, from this sweetness, with that which is in life eternal. And because this has been granted only to the good and the holy, the Church says in her elect, « And his fruit is sweet to my throat. » For nothing in this life is sweeter than to pass from this tasting into that life which lacks end and all malice, and to come from this image of the fruit to that fruit which may always satisfy us, and make us joyful in eternity. There follows:

4. *The king hath brought me into the wine cellar; he hath set charity in order in me.* [var: Vulg. *Introduxit me in cellam vinariam*, without the king]

Between the Bridegroom and the Bride mutual discourses have now long been held: [1216B] she interposed, he answered, she commended him with many praises, and was herself much commended with praise by him. Whence, because words have been joined to words, and sayings like to sayings set down in return, lest likeness beget weariness, the Bride refrains from this manner of speaking, no longer speaking to the Bridegroom, but directing her words to her friends, and reporting these things, « The king hath brought me into the wine cellar. » But it must be observed in this place that some wine in Holy Scripture is commendable, and some reprobate. Reprobate, as is that of which Moses makes mention, saying: « Their vine is of the vineyard of Sodom, and of the suburbs of Gomorrha: their grape is a grape of gall, and their clusters most bitter. Their wine is the gall of dragons, and the incurable venom of asps [n: (Deut. XXXII, 32, 33)]. » [1216C] For the wine which is pressed out from this vineyard, or from those clusters, or from those grapes, is that most bitter wine. For this is the sense; of the reprobate, who, understanding the Holy Scriptures perversely, draw them into an alien meaning, and, fixing their eyes upon the sun without discretion, are themselves darkened by the light, and drag others with them into condemned error. There are also those who, though they do not raise their friends up to the Holy Scriptures, yet by their daily deeds and words corrupt the simpler sort, and they themselves compel them, bound with a threefold knot, to have foremost dwellings in hell. Of these the Prophet: « There is none that doeth good, there is not so much as one. Their throat is an open sepulchre, with their tongues they have dealt deceitfully; judge thereby, O God: the venom [1216D] of asps is under their lips [n: (Psal. V, 11, et Psal. XIII, 3)]. » [var: Vulg. *judica illos*, "judge them"] Commendable is that of which Solomon says, « Wisdom hath built herself a house, she hath hewn out seven pillars. She hath slain her victims, she hath mingled her wine, and set forth her table. She hath sent her handmaids to call to the tower, and to the walls of the city [n: (Prov. IX, 1, 2, 3)]. » For the heavenly wisdom, for herself, « constructed a house, » when God wholly appeared in man: « she hewed out » in it « seven pillars, » because the sevenfold spirit is in it. She administered grace to the world: « she slew her victims, » because by her own passion and by that of her own she prepared that same house: « she mingled wine, » because to those not grasping the secrets of the divinity she laid open the sacraments of the assumed humanity: « She set forth her table, » because she prepared for us the pastures of Scripture [1217A] by opening them: « She sent her handmaids, to call to the tower, and to the walls of the city because, as the Apostle says, Not many are wise according to the flesh, not many mighty, not many noble: but the foolish things of the world hath God chosen, that he may confound the wise: and the weak things of the world hath God chosen, that he may confound the strong: and the base things of the world, and the things that are contemptible, hath God chosen, and the things that are not, that he might bring to nought the things that are. » [n: (I Cor. I, 26, 27, 28.)]



## CHAPTER II. (cont.)

These indeed are handmaids on account of their humility and lowliness, through whom the Lord admonished us to possess « the tower, » that is, the eminence of eternal life, and the walls of the heavenly Jerusalem. This is the wine which was pressed out from that vine and its branches, who says, « I am the vine, you the branches *(Joan.
