XV, [1217B] 5)*. » These the heavenly Father presses out, and they yield the best wine; that wine of which David says, « They shall be inebriated with the plenty of thy house [n: (Psal. XXXV, 9)]. » With this wine were Peter and Paul inebriated, when the one by the cross, the other by the sword, bore witness to God at Rome. With this wine all the apostles and martyrs were made drunk, when, despising all things that are of the world, and reckoning them as dung, they cast them away, and following Christ with a pure confession, they resolved rather to choose death for themselves with him and for him, than to lead a life together with those things. The Church says therefore, « The king hath brought me into the wine cellar, » that is, into a certain storehouse of the divine Scriptures, by which he drew me away from the world, by which he now permits me to have my dwelling among the things that are above: whence there follows, [1217C] « he hath set charity in order in me. » Behold that God sets charity in order in the Church, without which there is no ascending into heaven, without which there is no coming to God by any reasoning. For « God » is « charity [n: (Joan. I, 4, 8)], loving beyond doubt every creature; which unless he loved, it would not be at all, nor would there appear any cause or manner of its existence among creatures. He endowed with heavenly glory the good angels, and all the heavenly powers whatsoever, persisting in the goodness in which they were created, and to the spirit of deceit (who says, « I will set my seat toward the north, and I will be like the Most High) [n: (Isai. XIV, 14)] » in no way consenting, but firmly cleaving to the most high goodness by which they were created: so that they who, though they could sin, did not sin, [1217D] may now lack all possibility of sinning, and, being truly called « the firmament, » may cleave to him firmly and indissolubly, and love him, and obey him. Having charity toward these, he is assuredly loved by them; and this is found to be the first progression of charity. He also loves even the malignant spirits, not approving their malignity, by which they are turned away from him; but approving what is from himself, that they are knowing, that they are trembling, that they are strong, that they are unceasingly in possession of their own existence, and stand unceasingly obedient to the command of the highest King: this he approves, and it is acceptable to him, and he retains what is his own for himself, and confirms these same in all that he himself wills. Hence it is that Job is not tempted [n: (Job II, 8)] save by license granted from the highest Father: and when, sitting on the dunghill, he scraped the corrupt matter with a potsherd, [1218A] it was forbidden to Satan that he should tempt him in his soul: and Satan, fulfilling his commands, having fully tempted him in the body, did not strike him in the soul. So too, when Ahab was to be tempted, the Lord is read to have said to the adversary, « Who shall deceive Ahab king of Israel, that he may go up and fall at Ramoth Galaad. And a spirit went forth, and stood before the Lord, and said, I will go forth, and I will be a lying spirit in the mouth of all his prophets. And the Lord said, Thou shalt deceive, and thou shalt prevail [n: (III Reg. XXII, 20, 21, 22)]: » and thus, license being received, Ahab, tempted by the malignant one, perished. So too God loves even evil men, wholly reproving indeed their malice, but that they are, that they are men, that they are capable of discipline, that they are capable of reason: these things indeed [1218B] he approves and loves, because they too are from him: but that they use all these things for evil, that they degenerate from that for which they were made, these things he neither approves nor loves, because they are not from him. Hence there is no discrepancy of the Scriptures; hence it is said to God, « Thou hast hated none of the things which thou hast made, O Lord [n: (Sap. XI, 25)]. » Hence also the Lord says, « I will be an enemy to thy enemies, and I will be adverse to thy adversaries [n: (Exod. XXIII, 22)]. » [var: Vulg. *affligam affligentes te*, "I will afflict them that afflict thee"]

Thus in the Scriptures thou wilt find many things that seem contrary to one another: but when this is understood, that in the same creature God loves certain things, while there are in that same creature certain things which he hates, thou wilt find nothing contrary. For Judas the traitor [n: (Joan. XIII, 2)], when the devil had already put it into his heart that he should betray him, did God [1218C] pursue him with hatred? [n: (ibid., V, 27.)] For he handed him the dipped bread, saying, « What thou dost, do quickly; » and very many other words besides, by which he might have been rebuked and corrected, had he not willingly given himself wholly over to Satan. So Saul [n: (Act. IX, 1)], when, breathing out threatenings and slaughter against the disciples of the Lord, he was going to Damascus with letters received, that he might persecute Jesus with the utmost hatred, the Lord indeed looked upon, when a light from heaven shone round about him, and falling to the earth he could see none of those things which he had been wont to see until then; to whom counsel was given from heaven, that he should enter the city, and be baptized, and consent to those things which should be said to him by Ananias; to which he indeed consented, and being baptized, he preached Jesus [1218D] the more loftily in proportion as he was made, not earthly from the earth, but wholly heavenly, being taught from heaven: and if it had befallen him to die before he was called, God would have found out for him a cause in which he might justly be saved.

God therefore loves all, both good and evil; the good, because they persist in goodness and advance; the evil, because, falling away from good, they present themselves as a matter for the justice of God, and, unwilling as they are to be tormented for ever, making themselves worthy of it, they prove and demonstrate that God concludes all things with a just end. Thus he himself has fulfilled what he commanded to be done: for he says, « Love your enemies; do good to them that hate you [n: (Matth. V, 44)]. » For how great are the good things which God shows to his enemies, and to those who hate him, there is no one who does not easily comprehend. Thus therefore God [1219A] sets charity in order in himself, teaching the Church how she ought to have charity set in order in herself [n: (Joan. I, 4, 19)]. For since God first loved us, this is the order, that we love him first and chiefly. And « let us love » him « with the whole heart, and the whole soul, and the whole mind [n: (Matth. XXII, 37)]: » we love with the whole heart, if we covet nothing in our heart, revolve nothing in our mind, which we believe displeases him: we love « with the whole soul, » if we apply the soul, which moves the body, to the fulfilling in act of nothing that displeases him: we love with the whole mind, when we do anything good, and there exists nothing of our devotion which draws us to anything other than to God. This is the first order of charity [n: (ibid., V, 39)]: the second, that a man love his neighbour as himself. But whom [1219B] do we judge to be our neighbour? This question is ancient, and was put to the Lord himself, and was resolved by the parable put forth concerning him who fell among robbers. For the Lord says: « It happened that a certain priest went up by the same way, and having seen him passed by. In like manner also a Levite, when he was near the place, and saw him, passed on. But a certain Samaritan, being on his journey, came near him, and seeing him, was moved with mercy. And drawing near he bound up his wounds, pouring in oil and wine. And setting him upon his own beast, he brought him to an inn, and took care of him. And the next day he took out two pence, and gave them to the host, and said, Take care of him, and whatsoever thou shalt spend over and above, I, when I return, will repay thee [n: (Luc. X, 31-36)]. » [var: Vulg. *descenderet*, "went down"]

This good Samaritan is said to have been his neighbour, [1219C] who seeing him was moved with mercy, and took care of him. O how many priests are we! O how many Levites! who passing by see the afflicted, see the wretched, and do not bind up their wounds, pouring in the oil of mercy and the wine of justice! O how many are we who, having not two pence only, but many weights of silver and gold, do not bestow even one mite upon the host, that he may take charge of him; and that the Lord himself may answer for us when he shall return! Whom therefore we see moved with mercy toward one afflicted, whom thou seest bearing care of him, and grieving with and suffering with his pain as with his own, him do thou, who hast entrusted thyself wholly to God with heart and soul and mind, reckon thy neighbour, and love as [1219D] thyself; which is, in this, in which « love also thyself. » For with this Scripture, in which it is said, « Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with thy whole heart, » that agrees which the Lord says in the Gospel: « Blessed are the clean of heart, for they shall see God [n: (Matth. V, 8)]; » and likewise: « From the heart come forth evil thoughts, murders, adulteries, fornications, thefts, false testimonies, blasphemies: these are the things that defile a man [n: (Matth. XV, 19, 20)]. » Whence Solomon too says, « With all watchfulness keep thy heart, because life proceeds from it [n: (Prov. IV, 23)]. » The same also subjoins in the Proverbs: « There are six things which God hates, and the seventh his soul detests: [1220A] Haughty eyes, a lying tongue, hands that shed innocent blood, a heart that devises most wicked thoughts, feet swift to run into evil, a deceitful witness that uttereth lies, and him that soweth discords among brethren [n: (Prov. VI, 16-20)]. » Whom therefore dost thou love as thyself? him for whom thou desirest that to befall which thou desirest for thyself, to whom thou standest as a helper in this, whom thou callest back from the evil which is everlasting, and from this, so far as thou canst, thou turnest and separatest him. What shall I say to thee of a father, or of a mother? who indeed are to be loved in that in which they are loved by God; but in the rest to be spurned. The Lord loved the Egyptians, imparting to them the good things which he made common to good and evil alike, showing signs and wonders by which they might have been converted, if they had had minds capable of being turned [1220B] to good. And profiting nothing in them, he gave them over to destruction, that they might go into the punishment which they had themselves deserved. God therefore teaches in what a father and a mother ought to be loved, or kinsfolk, and the rest of one's relations; namely, in that in which each is loved by God also: but in certain other things, in which one is turned away from God, he is indeed not to be loved, yet nevertheless to be honoured; according to that saying, « Honour thy father and thy mother, that thou mayest be long-lived upon the earth [n: (Exod. XX, 12)]. » For honour is paid to many, and far from them love is entwined with something else. There follows:

5. *Stay me up with flowers, compass me about with apples, for I languish with love.*



## CHAPTER II. (cont.)

This Bride of Christ, that is, the Church, or any perfected soul, having been brought into the wine-cellar of the King, [1220C] and given the Lord's wine to drink, shows charity set in order within her, saying to the Angels, or to the heavenly Virtues, or to the very ordainers of the Church, « Stay me up with flowers, compass me about with apples; for I languish with love. » This languor is good, this languor is to be desired; it seeks that no medicine be applied to it save that which is of flowers, or assuredly of apples. This languor is from the ordering of charity, when a man loves God with his whole heart, with his whole soul, with all his strength, and grasps him with his whole mind [n: (I Joan. II, 15, 16)]. Here the concupiscence of the flesh, here the concupiscence of the eyes, is not; here there is not the pride of life, which is of the world. She, for this charity, is truly dead, and in her lives the spirit which truly quickens, nor does the flesh bear rule, which [1220D] truly profits nothing: she seeks to be succoured, so that she may be stayed up with flowers, and compassed about with apples. What flowers shall we give? what apples are to be sought, that we may succour her that languishes? Fragrant flowers indeed, refreshing by their odour, and feeding souls, and strengthening them, that she may abide in that very thing wherein she is, and advance, and not grow lukewarm until she lay hold of it. Here, if thou bring together the flower of the violet, the whiteness of the lily, the reddening roses, and the unwithered flowers of every herb, and the flowers of all trees that are fruitful, thou shalt be welcome, and shalt work together, on the other part, toward the staying up of her, lest perchance she slip. These are the words of the saints, wherewith, being questioned concerning the Lord, [1221A] they answered most readily; those in which thou shalt be able to find faith, humility, patience, constancy, and fortitude, peace and hope, and that charity of which we treat. These, if thou begin from Abel, and rightly run through the line of the just as far as Abraham; if from his sacrifice thou come as far as Moses; if from Moses, the Spirit leading, thou reach unto Christ Jesus God; if from the Lord Jesus thou rightly fix the mind's edge upon those who were sent by him into the world: thou shalt find nothing in the words of these save what may teach faith, strengthen hope, and sow charity far and wide. But because fruits come forth from the flower, it is rightly subjoined, « Compass me about with apples, » that is, strengthen me with their fruits. For their fruits are their works, by which they lent credence to their words, by which [1221B] they in no wise blushed to give their sufferings to Christ, nay rather consecrated by a most valiant death the truth which they preached. Here thou shalt rightly give the cross to Peter, the blessed prince of the apostles, the sword to Paul, the cauldron of boiling oil to John, the stake to Andrew; to the one James thou shalt assign the sword of Herod, to the other the fuller's pole. And running through the rest of the apostles, and showing all the holy martyrs crowned for Christ by a most valiant death, thou shalt most strongly compass her about with these fruits as she abides in her languor, that she waver not, that she doubt not, but may come from this life which must needs be ended, to the life which is always and without end. There follows:

6. *His left hand is under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me.*

[1221C]This Bride, having addressed her friends, embraced this in her prayer, that through them she might be stayed up with flowers, and compassed about with apples, that her intent might never be turned away from the love of the Godhead, wherewith she had already begun to languish. And so she gladdens them, as men heard on her behalf, saying: « His left hand is under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me: » as if she should say, From this I understand that your prayers for me have been heard, in that his left hand is now under my head, and now assuredly I conceive that his right hand shall embrace me. But here it is to be noted that the Lord has alike a left hand and a right; according to what Solomon says of wisdom in the Proverbs: « Length of life is in her right hand, and on the left riches and glory [n: (Prov. III, 16)]. » [var: Vulg. *longitudo dierum*, "length of days"] But that Jesus Christ is [1221D] the head of the Church no one now doubts. For as all the members depend from the head, and that member is without office which does not serve it: so indeed all the faithful have from Christ that they are, that they are faithful, that they are working well in faith, and in good work conceiving hope of eternal life, to which they are already joined by charity. These indeed are the gifts of God: temporal blessedness, and that which is already the angels', and shall be eternal for us: whence the Lord says: « All power is given to me in heaven and in earth. Going therefore, teach ye all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son and of the Holy Ghost [n: (Matth. XXVIII, 18, 19)]. »

The senses of the body also are strong chiefly from the head, by which all the members of the body are governed, namely [1222A] sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch: which indeed are acknowledged to be strong in Christ beyond all mortals. For he sees every work that we do, he hears what we speak; what we think, he assuredly smells, the good which we conceive in the mind; and this he tastes which we fulfil in act. But what shall I recount of touch? which indeed is wonderful; for whom he touches, him he straightway changes into himself, changes into another man, so that he himself becomes a stranger to himself. He therefore understands the left hand to be under the head, who knows that riches and all this temporal glory are from him [n: (I Reg. 2, 7)]. For the Lord makes poor and makes rich, he humbles and lifts up, that a man may sit with princes, and hold the throne of glory. For of himself the Lord says: « All power is given to me in heaven and in earth. Going therefore [1222B] teach ye all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost. Amen. » But if not only in heaven, nay rather in earth also, all power has been given to him, it is certain indeed that all riches, and all glory and temporal blessedness, which is known to have an end, is from him; and that also which in heaven has long since been granted to the angels, and is promised to be granted without end to all his own. Whence rightly is the left hand of God declared to be under the head of the Bride, in that it is certain that none obtains this temporal prosperity and glory save by his gift. For in his figure it is said, « By me kings reign, and the founders of laws decree just things: by me princes rule, and the mighty decree justice [n: (Prov. VIII, 15, 16)]. »

[1222C]Because therefore the kings of the world are now in great part subject to Christ (since to him is bowed every knee of kings, of prelates, of the mighty, and of princes), it is rightly said by the Church that the left hand of God rests under « her » head, that is, under the head of the Church; and it is certain that he dispenses all these things to his members as he wills. Yet these things he gives to some who receive the benefits ungratefully, who exalt themselves in what is bestowed, who, having no thanks to God, heap up for themselves wrath in the things that are given, against the day of eternal retribution. But God confers these things on his own faithful, that they may dispense all good things to the good, that they fail not on the way, being shamefully in want; that they may show themselves sons of charity, and may reckon that this grace is not given them for merit; but may rather seek that [1222D] which is to be shown hereafter to his own. Whence also it is subjoined: « And his right hand shall embrace me. » For what is the right hand of God reckoned to be, save the very eternity of God? which indeed embraces the whole Church, glorifying her first of all in the patriarchs and the prophets, crowning her in the apostles and the martyrs, and worthily rewarding also her other members; so that there is no part at all of the Church which may complain that it is untouched by his grace. There follows:

7. *I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes, and the harts of the fields, that you stir not up, nor make the beloved to awake, until she herself will.*

Thus far the Bride has drawn out very many words, speaking indeed of the Bridegroom, yet not to him, which is a sign of exceeding love. For love so stirs the mind [1223A] that, if at any time either shame or fear does not permit her to speak to the Bridegroom, yet, kindled by the goads of love, she does not omit to make words concerning him, to herself or to any other whatsoever. Which the beloved himself considering, shows a like token of love, no longer speaking to her, but making words to others concerning her, thus: « I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields, that you stir her not up. » He sees her, now in great part of herself languid with love, desiring to rest in his bosom away from the world's disturbances, loathing even necessary conversations, glad in the delight of the Bridegroom alone; and considering also that other part of herself which knows nothing of this languor, bound in great part of itself to the world, which, reckoning her as one dead, desires her to condescend to its own necessity [1223B] and to take up a part of its labour also: pitying her that languishes, he thus says, « I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, that you stir her not up, » that is, that you call her not away from her peaceful rest.

But the adjuration must be attended to, wherein he says, « by the roes and the harts of the fields. » For this adjuration is not without cause. These indeed are beasts estranged from the world, set apart from the converse of men, refreshing themselves with herbs, clean animals indeed, which chew the cud and divide the hoof. All these things suit the saints, who, set apart in mind from carnal things, are fed with green herb, that is, with the unfading doctrine of the saints, which they chew, that is, once it is understood they often call it back to memory; and they divide the hoof, because, these things being understood, they separate eternal things from temporal [1223C]. Separately also it belongs to roebucks that they seek the heights, and, when wounded, touch dittany: which is worthily fitted to the saints, namely to seek heavenly things, and, if there be any hurt, to heal it with Christ as physician. This too is the nature of the hart: when he is a hundred years old, or perchance of greater number, and now inclines to old age, he marks for himself in whatever place the serpent's dwelling, and, as nature teaches, straightway draws it forth with his breath, eats it, and consumes it; and having drunk down this supper with a draught from a spring of purer water, he straightway lays aside his horns, and changes his hide and his hair, and recovers his sight, and, reforming his whole self, made young again and now to live long, he passes over into another life. And this is fitted to the saints in this [1223D] way: for the saints live, and, their virtue long kept and now ripened, when they already incline to old age, they mark for themselves in whatever place the dread serpent's dwelling, namely where he reigns, where he dwells, where by his great craft he scatters the hearts of those that dwell there, and severs them from charity: hoping therefore that the consummation of their virtues will be good if they wear down these commotions, if they restore peace, and teach men to keep it when restored; they approach this place of the serpent, and, taught by the breath of the Holy Spirit, they draw forth this ancient serpent, they scatter what he has sown, and, grinding him as it were with their teeth, they eat and consume him, [1224A] and bring his deeds to nothing: and afterwards, by the draught of a clear spring, that is, taught by the graces and the doctrines of our Lord Jesus Christ, they pass over into eternal life: in which, changed into the new man, they live a blessed life, to which nothing is lacking, or nothing can be lacking, that it desires. But such is this adjuration: « I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields, that you stir not up, » that is, so may it be granted you to attain the virtues which we signify by the roes and the harts, that you stir not up the beloved, until she herself will.



## CHAPTER II. (cont.)

Here the times of action and of contemplation are fixed. The times of contemplation indeed are pleasing and acceptable, so that they to whom this grace is given can scarcely be torn away from it. For they love, and [1224B] by loving they understand how many things they lack which they behold in contemplating; these they strive to attain; and they reckon those troublesome to them through whom it comes about that this calling is hindered. But it is the voice of the beloved that is added, « Until she herself will. » Whence it is given to understand that there are certain times in which the Bride may wish to be torn away from this calling, and to be conformed to lower things: as for instance when faith is weakened, when heresy is sown abroad, when it already clings after the manner of a canker, and, unless remedies be applied, it already takes hold of the whole body, and hopes now to possess the whole for itself. Whence Arius says, « There was, when the Son was not, » reckoning that the whole Trinity was not co-eternal with itself: but supposing that divine generation (of which it is said, « Who shall declare his generation?) [n: (Isai. LIII, 8)], » to be like to this [1224C] human generation of ours; in which he who is father goes before, and from him consequently is he who is son. Which heresy assuredly ranged over the whole world, so that scarcely four bishops could be found in the Church who thought rightly concerning the faith of the eternal Trinity. For, to be silent of the other parts of the world, I will disclose how great evils came of this in Africa alone. Huneric the son of Genseric, who on his father's death had succeeded to the kingdom, moved by the Arian perfidy, thrust three hundred and thirty-four catholic bishops into exile, closed their churches, and afflicted the people with divers punishments. And Thrasamund, succeeding him in the kingdom, when the churches of the Catholics were closed, banished two hundred and twenty bishops into exile in Sardinia [1224D]; to whom Pope Symmachus ministered daily supplies. In other parts of the world also this heresy so ranged abroad that it was needful to call together the whole Church, and to come together into one, and to entreat God with poured-out prayers, that, this pest being removed, he would declare the truth of the matter that was in question: and he, inclined by the devout prayers of his beloved and of the other faithful, compelled Arius, when he had come to Constantinople to defend the parts of his heresy and had of necessity turned aside to the public privies, to undergo death, his inmost vitals being poured out; nor did any defender of his heresy appear [1225A] thereafter. So too, when contemplation is penetrating the secret things of heaven and busying itself in them so far as it can, if, teaching having now ceased, either heresy or perchance other sins reign among those in subjection, or if perchance it is desired that succour be brought to the need of neighbours in their distresses: it is the time in which contemplatives should wish to come down, and to succour those who are awaiting help from them. The order of the reading, therefore, is this: This is the voice of the beloved [sic: *moi*], « I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields, that you stir not up, nor make the beloved to awake, until she will. » There follows:

8. *Behold, that one cometh leaping upon the mountains, skipping over the hills.* [var: Vulg. *iste*, "this one"]

This is the Bride's assertion. For she had said that that voice by which it was said, « I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, [1225B] etc. » was the voice of her beloved: this she now asserts. Seeing her beloved coming, and not gainsaying those things whereof the Bride testified that he was the author, she thus proclaims his coming beforehand, « Behold, that one cometh, leaping upon the mountains, skipping over the hills. » But here a broad reading is opened up, where in Holy Scripture there is treatment of mountains and of hills. For this Scripture reckons some mountains evil, and affirms some worthy of praise: it reckons those evil, as the mountains of Gelboe, where they fell who seemed strong in Israel; which David cursed, saying: « Gelboe, let neither dew nor rain descend upon you [n: (II Reg. I, 21)]. » [var: Vulg. *veniant*, "let there come"] For Gelboe is interpreted « descent, » signifying the mountains of pride and of boasting, whereon, the more thou ascendest, so much the [1225C] more thou fallest and descendest. Whence it is said, « Every mountain and hill shall be brought low [n: (Luc. III, 5)]. » On this indeed there fought the children of Israel, already reprobate before God, Saul and Jonathan, who, being lifted up and reprobated by God, led forth against the enemy the people that followed them, and fell together with those that fell. Whence David says, « Mountains of Gelboe, » that is, of vices, on which the more one ascends, the more one descends; « let neither dew nor rain, » that is, heavenly doctrine, « descend upon you, » where they fell who, hoping themselves strong in Israel, fell conscious to themselves of their weakness. These are evil mountains; David himself recalls that they are to be turned away from, saying, « The mountains, like wax, melted at the presence of the Lord [n: (Psal. XCXVI, 5)]. » [1225D] For they who, being powerful and exalted in the world, prefer themselves to others, and look down on them as though unequal to themselves, these do not behold the Lord coming in wrath and in fire; but as wax before the face of fire, so they melt away and fail. But there is a good mountain of which the same David says, « Selmon, the mountain of God, a fat mountain [n: (Psal. LXVII, 15)]. » For Selmon is interpreted « shadow, » signifying the Lord Jesus Christ, whom the Godhead wholly overshadowed; whence it is subjoined: « The mountain of God, a fat mountain; a curdled mountain, a fat mountain. » For there is none among the sons of men on whom the flood of the Godhead has so wholly bestowed itself, whom the whole fulness of the Godhead has inhabited bodily: whence also it is subjoined, « Why suspect ye curdled mountains? » [n: (Psal. V, 17.)] For there is none among the sons of men, [1226A] in whom the holy Godhead is so curdled together with the humanity that one and the same person is both true God and true man: for this is the mountain in which it hath pleased God to dwell therein: for in it the Lord shall dwell unto the end, which is given to be understood as without end. For is the Godhead to be separated from the humanity? on whom is bestowed all power in heaven and in earth of judging, of taking up, and of taking away. He, as « King of kings, and, Lord of lords [n: (Apoc. XIX, 16)]; » so indeed may be called the mountain of mountains: whence also Isaias says, « In the last days the mountain of the house of the Lord shall be prepared [n: (Isai. II, 2)]. » For this house of the Lord, which God inhabits bodily, is the Lord Jesus Christ; and the mountain of this house is the very loftiness of the Godhead; into which the Lord Jesus himself [1226B] is lifted up above the top of all mountains, to whom all nations shall flow together, and all peoples; whence all the saints receive it that they are called mountains. Whence is that saying, « I have lifted up my eyes to the mountains [n: (Psal. CXX, 1)]: » and elsewhere, « Thou enlightening wonderfully from the everlasting mountains [n: (Psal. LXXV, 5)]. » He indeed by his own fulness enlightens the mountains, and, establishing them in eternity, grants them to others throughout the world, that these may be illumined by their light: seeing therefore her Bridegroom coming, this Bride says, « Behold, he cometh, leaping upon the mountains. » From what mountain does he leap? from the heart of the Father. Into what mountain does he leap? into the heart of the holy Virgin (in whom he put on humanity, fitting to himself a body and a soul, and taking to himself this mortal life), from which he makes a leap onto the cross, whereby he redeemed us: [1226C] his leap from thence is next into the sepulchre; wherein tarrying forty hours, and rising thence, he makes a leap into immortal life; into which, tarrying forty days with the apostles and the rest of his own, he ascended to the place whence he came forth, that he might teach us to ascend thither by the virtues, and to reckon this the end of our course.

The Lord also, in coming, is said to leap upon the mountains. For the mountains are the doctors of the Church, illumined from the everlasting mountains, who by reason of the eminence of their knowledge and understanding are rightly named mountains; upon whom he makes his leaps, when he lights them up with the fire of his flashing. For he made a leap into the apostle Peter, when, the Lord asking, « But whom [1226D] do you say that I am? » [n: (Matth. XVI, 15.)] and the others being silent, he answered, « Thou art Christ, the Son of the living God. » Whence he received the keys of the Church, and the power also of binding and of loosing, and obtained the primacy of the whole Church after Christ. But that he made leaps into him and into the others, when he assured them of his resurrection, when on the day of Pentecost he made them bright with the Holy Ghost, when he made them steadfast and strong under every questioning of them, when at the beautiful gate Peter and John healed by a word alone the man lame from his mother's womb, when by his preaching Peter converted and baptized unto the Lord Jesus now three thousand, and at another time five thousand, is left in doubt to none; since it is certain that these works came about thus not by the power of man alone, but by the true [1227A] presence of God. Since therefore it is certain that he knew the fraud of Ananias and Sapphira, none but God revealing it, and that they also expired at his rebuke; who would not understand that God leapt forth into him, and that he accomplished these things at the command of him who willed them? And when at Joppa in the upper room at the sixth hour Peter was praying, and there fell upon him an ecstasy of mind, so that with heaven opened he saw let down to him from heaven a great vessel, after the manner of a linen sheet, by four corners, in which were all four-footed beasts, and creeping things of the earth, and fowls of the air, and he heard a voice saying to him, « Peter, kill and eat [n: (Act. XI, 6)]; and he said, « Far be it from me, Lord, because I have never eaten anything common and unclean! » and the Lord said to him, « That which God hath purified, call not thou [1227B] common. » Who would not say that the Lord leapt into him, when, the messengers of Cornelius being heard, he came to Caesarea, and finding that God is not a respecter of persons, baptized him and all his house, upon which the Holy Ghost had descended.



## CHAPTER II. (cont.)

Since therefore it is certain that the Lord made his leaps into Peter, the first of the apostles, in these ways and in very many others, it remains that in those same ways we understand it of all the rest, who, being sent into the world, both preached him and converted the nations to him, as is most well known by the wondrous showing forth of their works. For into the last of the apostles, namely Paul, he gave a leap, when, a light being sent forth from heaven, he thus called him, cast down upon the earth and seeing nothing, « Saul, Saul, [1227C] why persecutest thou me? it is hard for thee to kick against the goad [n: (Act. IX, 4-15)]: » who, being sent to Ananias, received his sight, and was baptized, and, the Lord leaping into him, openly confessed before all that he was his disciple. How many leaps God made into him, how wondrously he raised him up, how choice he made him by a wondrous doctrine and conspicuous to all, his epistles teach; and those which are called « The Acts of the Apostles » plainly declare it. And so the Bride says of him, « Behold, this one cometh, leaping upon the mountains: » she joins also what follows, « Skipping over the hills. Why this is so, let us see. For what we have said of the mountains, the same must be said of the hills. For some hills are good; whence it is said, « Bless the Lord, ye mountains and hills [1227D] [n: (Dan. III, 75)]; » and that of Isaias, « The Lord shall be exalted above the hills [n: (Isa. II, 2)]. » [var: Vulg. *et elevabitur super colles*, of the mountain, not of the Lord] But some are evil; whence in this place it is said that the Lord leaps upon the mountains, but skips over the hills: for where he leaps, there he is; but with those over whom he skips he makes no stay, but forsakes and abandons them. Who then are these? they who make themselves great by inventing heresies; in these they exalt themselves, they desire to be made equal to the height of the mountains, they turn peoples after them and draw them; they make themselves great when they are not, boasting that they have God, when they are far from him. Among these is Arius, among these Eutyches, among these all the heresiarchs whom the holy Church marks out, shuns, and condemns: over these indeed the Lord skips. [1228A] He skips over the hypocrites also, who in words confess that they have known the Lord, but in deeds deny him. So indeed he skips over all whom neither faith fortifies, nor the holiness of life makes honourable. The cause of this thing is this; because,

9. *The beloved is like to a roe, and to a young hart.* [var: Vulg. *dilectus meus*, "my beloved"]

To these animals the coming Bridegroom is rightly likened, both because God beholds all things clearly, and because he tarries on the lofty height of the virtues, is fed among them, measures out his leaps among them, and discerns also in whom the ancient serpent lies coiled at rest, or uncoiled draws out his folds; and these indeed he turns aside from, even as there is no fellowship of Christ with Belial. There follows:

[1228B] *Behold he standeth behind our wall.*

This indeed is promised of the beloved, because he is like to a roe. For he is keen of sight, as one who sees the Father, and, living the Father, beholds all things in the Father; not only the acts of works, but also the affections of thoughts. Whence she now admonishes all, that we all fortify ourselves for his gaze, by thinking what things are good, what are useful, what may seem worthy of the sight of God; and that we fulfil these same things by a consequent act. For he says, « Behold he standeth behind our wall. » Him also she said to be like to a young hart, that is, a son of the ancient fathers, having from them a body taken from a virgin, yet liable to no sin nor weighed down by the mass of sin; inasmuch as [1228C] the force of his Father's nature has grown up in him, so that hidden things are not concealed from him, that serpents flee him, that poisons do not harm him. To him therefore, to whom our hidden things must be laid open, to whom the serpent is wholly displeasing, who spurns everything that is infected with his poison, let us fit ourselves, that he may see what things are worthy of God, and may behold nothing in us infected with the serpent's poison, whereby it might please him to turn aside from us: « For behold he standeth behind our wall, looking down through the windows. » [var: Vulg. *respiciens*, "looking in"] Who is said indeed to stand, that is, by the most excellent disposition of his state to hold himself unmovably for the beholding of all things which he looks upon; to whom neither do the good things which we do add anything, nor do the evils perpetrated by us diminish anything of his glory; but all things subject themselves to the eternal retribution which they have deserved. Who indeed [1228D] « standeth behind the wall; » for the wall is that outward part of our condition (wherewith we are clothed, wherewith we are covered, whereby we are subjected to numberless temptation), namely our body, which, as a wall is built of stones and mortar, so is fitted to us by a fit disposition of its parts; which assuredly, because she sees that her beloved is about to take it up, or perhaps has already taken it up, she names indeed « our, » that she may show him a partaker of our condition. But here the Beloved « standeth behind the wall, » that he may freely behold it, as though set before him, and freely mark through the windows and the lattices what is done within. For the windows of our body are the five bodily senses [1229A], namely sight, hearing, taste, smell and touch. Some indeed use these unto good things, and make themselves good; others, drawing them by most abandoned habits toward evil things, consequently make themselves most wicked: of whom Jeremias says, « Death is come up through our windows [n: (Jer. IX, 21)]: » which indeed is then, when the eyes of the sinner see a woman to lust after her; for he has committed adultery with her in his heart: and thus death has entered in to the soul through the windows of the eyes. But also when a man receives vain hearing, and especially the false sciences of perverse teachings, then death enters in to the soul through the windows of the ears. And so of the rest, of smell, of taste, of speech and of touch: when any soul abuses these, and applies itself to evil, death now enters through these windows [1229B], and gains the primacy. But if the soul, beholding the adornment of the world, and from the beauty of creatures understanding God the founder of all, wonders at his works, and praises the creator of the works, unto this soul life enters through the windows of the eyes. But also when it inclines its hearing to the word of God, and is delighted with the reasonings of his knowledge and of his wisdom, to this soul the light of wisdom enters through the windows of the ears; of whom Isaias says, « Who are these that fly as clouds, and as doves to their windows? » [n: (Isai. LX 8.)] For seeing in spirit the loftiness of the apostles, and wondering at their swift course through the world, he says, « Who are those, » etc.: but wondering at their simplicity and innocence, he subjoins, saying, « And as [1229C] doves to their windows, » that is, simple and innocent, and in all their sight, and hearing, taste, smell and touch imitating that dove which appeared above the Lord on the day whereon he was baptized

So then, standing behind the wall, this beloved contemplates through the windows by which gate each man seeks to enter, as the Lord says: « For wide is the gate, and broad the way that leadeth to death, and many there are who go in thereat: but narrow is the gate, and strait the way that leadeth to life, and few there are that find it [n: (Matth. VII, 13, 14)]. » [var: Vulg. *ad perditionem*, "to destruction"] These therefore he looks down upon, as labouring below; he looks in upon them, that he may help them in their beginnings, and that those labouring in their own strait, [1229D] grace being now freely given, he may lead further on, and, beginning the narrow way, may bring them, set at length in the breadth of charity, to blessed glory. But those entering the wide gate, and holding the broad way which leadeth to destruction, he exhorts, admonishes, and calls back, first by the natural law, then by Scripture, then by the patriarchs, by the prophets, hence by himself, then by the apostles, and by all the doctors, that they may return, that they may forsake the way begun; lest, glorying in the breadth of the way, and at length entering through the broad gate, they enter into darkness, and the dreadful places of perdition, which are even now truly destined for those running by this way. These things the beloved contemplates through the windows, contemplating [1230A] namely the manifestly good and the manifestly evil; and not content with these alone, he looks forth through the lattices; for he says: « Looking down through the windows, looking forth through the lattices [n: (I Tim. I, 15)]. » For lattices are narrow openings, not lying open after the manner of a window, but drawn out narrowly lengthwise by the masons under the vault; through which the beholder sees whom he wills, nor yet can he be seen by them, because neither does he will it.

Who then are they whom he thus beholds? they who either do evil things not evilly, or perform good things not well. They do evil things not evilly, as Saul, and they that are like him. For these dragged the men of this sect bound to the synagogues and to the tribunals: which indeed was evil, but was not done evilly. For in this, deeming that they were rendering [1230B] obedience to the creator, serving the law, and repressing transgression, they burned with a zeal indeed good, yet not according to knowledge, and believed that they were worthily avenging an injury brought upon the supreme Godhead. Whence Paul himself says: « I persecuted the Church of God: but yet I obtained mercy, because I did it ignorantly in unbelief [n: (I Tim. I, 13)]. » For he was ignorant that God the Father had a Son, and that he had sent him into the world for a remedy: he did not know that this was Christ Jesus. He knew that the circumcision of Abraham and the law of Moses had been given by the Lord, but he did not know that this had lasted in its integrity until the time of John the Baptist, and that then the things promised in it had expired: and therefore these things were revealed to him, and the things done [1230C] in ignorance were forgiven. Him therefore the Lord looked upon through the lattices, seeing him and pitying him, when indeed he hoped nothing of mercy, but breathed wholly « threatenings and slaughter » unto evil. There are also those who perform good things not well, who assuredly, having faith and exercising the works of faith, pray much in the streets, distribute their goods to the poor and the needy; but while they do these things, not having the charity which is the virtue of works, and not seeking to attain God by these works, but, because the whole world is subject to God, desiring to be honoured and to be set over honours, they lose all that they do, and that which has been distributed without charity, that they forfeit. For Paul says, « If I shall distribute all my goods to the poor, and myself also [1230D] so that I burn, but have not charity, I am nothing [n: (I Cor. XIII, 3)]. » [var: Vulg. *nihil mihi prodest*, "it profiteth me nothing"] The same also says, « The word of God is living, and effectual, and more piercing than any two-edged sword: and reaching unto the division of the soul and the spirit, of the joints also and the marrow, and a discerner of the thoughts and intents of the heart; nor is there any creature invisible in his sight *(Hebr IV, 12)*. » Whence whatsoever they do, they are before God, they do not escape his eyes; nor is he slow to conceive in mind not only what they do, but what they intend thereby: seeing the manifestly good and evil through the window; but [d: those who hide themselves, beholding through the window; but] those who hide themselves, beholding through the lattices. There follows:

[1231A]



## 10.

*Behold my beloved speaketh to me.*

This Bride has admonished all to beware of her beloved, who cometh leaping upon the mountains, skipping over the hills, who cometh like to a roe and to a young hart, who standeth behind the wall, beholding through the windows and the lattices what is done within, what pleases him, or what displeases him. But now she intimates that the Bridegroom speaks to her, saying, « Behold my beloved speaketh to me. Arise, make haste, my friend, my dove, my beautiful one. » He had seen her sitting in the house as it were on high at a window, cherishing her love toward the Bridegroom by holy contemplation, and pondering the ways in which her love might burn unceasingly toward the Beloved: and that he may stir her up, as one languid and by that very languor as it were dead to the world [1231B], and, once raised up, may kindle her anew with the fires of a new love, he says:

*Arise, make haste, my friend, my dove, my beautiful one, and come.*

Whom he exhorts with three words, saying: « Arise, Make haste, Come; » and whom he designates with three names, saying, « My friend, My dove, My beautiful one; » that the friendly setting of the names may lighten all that the threefold command of the words urges to be done. For he says, « Friend; » for from this I knew thee loving me, that thou hast left father and mother, brothers and sisters, house and fields, and hast followed me. Thee also have I heard saying in Paul, « Who shall separate us from the charity of God, which is in Christ Jesus? For I know that neither death, nor life, nor angels, nor principalities, [1231C] nor powers, nor any other creature shall be able to separate us from the charity of God [n: (Rom. VIII, 38, 39)]. » Thee also have I heard protesting in Peter, « Lord, thou knowest all things; thou knowest that I love thee [n: (Joan. XXI, 17)]. » He adds also, « My dove, » as a most innocent bird, intent upon the propagating of its young, fed on the cleanness of grains, having a moan instead of a song, flying in flocks, reckoning nothing good save what is in common, tearing at a bird unlike itself neither with beak nor with claw: thee indeed, conforming thyself as far as thou canst to the likeness of that dove which bore witness to Christ in his baptism, and pleasing me in such a form; thou whom by these reasons I justly approve as beautiful, Arise. But in saying, « Arise, » [1231D] he did not utter it in that manner in which it is said to a sinner, Arise. For to a sinner it is said, « Arise, thou that sleepest, and rise up from the dead, and Christ shall enlighten thee [n: (Ephes. V, 14)]. » Which indeed is said to a man wrapped in grievous sins, in which he is said to sleep, that is, to rest in a grievous delight; to whom it is said with a holy voice that he arise, that is, that he rest from sins; and that he rise up from the dead, that is, that he set not for himself a place of abiding in hell, among the dead: and when thou shalt have ceased to sin, and hast begun now to abhor these things which pleased thee, then « Christ shall enlighten thee, » that is, he will show the way by which he wills thee to go. Nor indeed is it in this [1232A] way said here to the Bride that she arise, who long since had left father and mother, house and brothers, and the rest of the things that are had in the world, and, having followed Christ, had said, « Draw me after thee; we will run in the odour of thy ointments. » [var: Vulg. *Trahe me: post te curremus*, "Draw me: we will run after thee"]

What then is it to say to the Bride that she arise? He had known that she was wholly given to this, that she meditated assiduously upon his love, which had drawn him from the Father into the holy Virgin; and that him whom, born of her, it was certain to have run through the evils of the world, and, crucified, to have risen again with death despised, and thereafter to have ascended into the heavens, and to have promised heavenly goods to those that follow him, she embraced with her whole desire, longed for with a vow, and delighted in with soliloquy: to her therefore the Beloved says, « Arise, » which is, Rouse thyself from this [1232B] rest of thine, from this peace of thine: I indeed am he who, adjuring the daughters of Jerusalem, said, « That you stir not up, nor make the Beloved to awake, until she herself will. » Now thou art one spirit with me; I do not doubt that thou willest what I will. Beg not to die, while thou canst profit many, lest this delay draw peril to itself; make haste, that by hastening thou mayest come to me. I am he whom thou lovest; already thou hast without the one whom thou desirest; wherefore I say to thee, that thou come, that thou mayest see with me, and understand thy works, and, if they are good, rejoice together in them.

11. *For now the winter is past.*

Winter is that part of the year in which the light is shorter for us, but the darkness of the night deeper; in which all things are bound fast with frost, in which all things are stiff with wintry ice, and moisture has dominion over bodies: all these things [1232C] mark the inclemency of the season, and he testifies that it has now passed away, and that therefore the time of his coming is opportune. Which indeed was present then, when unbelief had oppressed the world on every side, when the light of faith shone upon the hearts of few, when evil had come forth from the north wind, which held in the hearts of all, chilled with the frost of malice; and hardened impiety permitted the ice of hearts to be melted by no warmth of the Holy Spirit. Moisture had dominion in bodies, in which no love of chastity or of honour restrained the reins of luxury: but luxury, and the importunity that accompanies luxury, commanded that all things be mingled in foul intercourse. This the disobedience of Adam the first father bears witness to; the malice of Cain also bears witness; and Lamech « the murderer » [1232D] bears witness; this also the impurity of the ages bears witness to, blotted out first by water, afterwards by fire, eight excepted. There follows:

*The rain is gone and departed.*

Not every rain is called *imber*, but only the wintry; joining almost day and night together, which not even roofs keep out; by which rivers are swollen, by which bridges fall down, and the rising fords deny the way to travellers, and, snatching all things away by their onrush, bear them down into the deep. Which indeed agrees with the time of unbelief: for where it happened that faith was lacking, there it came about that all spiritual goods were lacking [1233A]: there was no hope there, no charity, no fear of God, no obedience to God, no reverence, and almost all that mankind owed to God, it paid to Satan. This rain was unceasing: the unspeakable teaching of the masters, magnifying Jupiter, offering him sacrifices, venerating Apollo, Mercury, and Bacchus, adoring Juno, Diana, Venus, and Pallas, and the rest, which shame blushes even to have named in a sacred treatise, and modesty forbids: but the necessity alone of expounding the sacred reading sets them forth. For this rain overthrew the foundation of faith, the walls of hope, and the roofs of charity: for when the rain descended upon this house, the floods came also together with it; for this unbelief being received, the flooded minds of men swelled beyond the channels appointed for them; [1233B] and they to whom the faith, worship, and reverence of the one God was given by God the Father, and by the fathers who gave him credence, attributing this to many gods, and confessing that there are many Gods, went forth from the channel and the banks appointed them, and drew with them into the deep of hell countless thousands of men. For the winds blew, because the blasts of the malignant spirit were not wanting to them. The bridges were overthrown, because Holy Scripture, which makes for us a bridge to God, became cheap. No passage lay open to the traveller, because for him going forth from this life there was no access to God. For there was made, as the Lord says, « a great fall of that house; » and truly great, inasmuch as for it there is no return to life, no communion with the good to come for ever. Thee therefore, whom I perceive to be my friend, because thou willest [1233C] what I will; thee, whom I understand to excel in dove-like manners, whom I judge conformed to me, and therefore beautiful, thee I call forth, saying to thee, « Arise, » that is, Come forth from this contemplation of peace and quiet wherein thou art held; make haste, that the compliance of thy obedience may follow at a word upon the command of my precept, « and come, » that, made a partaker of one spirit with me, thou mayest rejoice together with me, who helped thee by my spirit that thou shouldest do these things, that thou shouldest remove these scandals of the world: for the winter being removed, and the rain removed which had troubled all things, a sweeter breath has now blown over the face of the earth, and the breeze of the Holy Spirit, sent forth from above, has adorned it, when faith was received, with the flower of its own comeliness: this is what is said:

12. *The flowers have appeared in our land.* [1233D]

Where unbelief commanded, there faith now reigns; where there was despairing misery, there good hope hopes for itself, and rejoices to attain eternal things; where the mind was cold and grew numb, kindled by no fire of charity, there charity now orders all things by its own command, raising upward the things that were below, and by good works presenting all things to the good creator. He added also:

*The time of pruning is come.*

Which pruning assuredly shows forth the time of holy confession. For trees are pruned, and from them the useless shoots are taken away, which bear no fruits, and which, if they grow out, hinder the useful branches from bearing: so vines, [1234A] so other trees are cut free of useless branches, that those which are useful may become more useful, and may bring forth richer fruits for the husbandman. Which indeed is rightly fitted to the time of fulness and of grace. For some trees are dry and dead, bringing forth neither leaves nor fruits; some indeed have leaves, but not fruits; others indeed fruits, but bad ones: and others there are to which the leaves lend beauty, and the best fruits their worth. In these the whole race of men is comprehended: of these indeed the axe and the fire await three kinds. But those whom neither the leaves of words nor the fruits of works clothe, or who, if they bring forth any fruits, yet bring forth bad ones, these the judicial axe of the Lord, discerning well, cuts off, and by a just judgment [1234B] fits them to the fire that is appointed for them. But those to which the leaves lend beauty, and the best fruits their worth, are the trees of Paradise, in which God dwells, among which he walks, unto which he calls forth his Bride, and which he cleanses with the pruning-hook, that they may bring forth fruits; which fruits may remain for them unto life eternal. Which pruning-hook indeed is holy confession, wherein a man considers himself, calls to mind all that he has done in his life, opens and condemns himself to himself; and, confessing this very thing with his mouth to him to whom he is appointed, repenting with his heart, washing and cleansing it with tears, taking up and rendering a worthy burden of penance, he is made a fruitful tree, which is planted beside the running waters, and, bringing forth the fruit of grace, is most joyfully received by the Lord. The time of pruning also is most pleasing to the Lord, [1234C] wherein he sees the glory of the apostles, the constancy of all the martyrs throughout the world, who indeed were cut off by the heavy pruning-hook of martyrdom, and cast down as useless, and, received gloriously by him in heaven as members of the most high body of Christ, do now judge the nations, and reign for ever. There follows:

*The voice of the turtle has been heard in our land.*



## 10. (cont.)

That is, The voice of the preacher has been heard on every side throughout the world in the land now ours. For anyone is preached [cj: *Praedicatur*; read *Praedicator*, "a preacher"], not seeking out the prudence which is of the earth, nor the little fables which are utterly empty, announcing by his song the departure of winter and the coming of spring; he moans here, and, loving chastity, he dwells on high, [1234D] he flees the converse of men, remaining in body in the world, in mind in heaven: whence the Baptist says, « Offspring of vipers, who hath shewed you to flee from the wrath to come? Bring forth therefore fruits worthy of penance: and begin not to say, We have Abraham for our father [n: (Luc. III, 7, 8)]. » But Peter says: « Do penance, and be every one of you baptized in the name of Jesus Christ unto the remission of sins; and you shall receive the gift of the Holy Ghost [n: (Act. II, 38)]. » Paul also says: « Not in rioting and drunkenness, not in chambering and impurities, not in contention and envy. But put ye on the Lord Jesus Christ [n: (Rom. XIII, 13, 14)]. » But these [1235A] voices of the turtle, who could number them? He alone knows them, God, who forms the voice and the hearts. There follows:

13. *The fig tree hath put forth her green figs.*

The fig is a most well-known tree, which abounds in leaves, and fills with fruit him who tends and possesses it: it produces its fruits in divers ways. For when spring is begun, and now a little advanced, it produces its first fruits in small measure, which are called green figs, and are bitter, and by reason of their bitterness are spat out by the taste, and are cast aside among bitter and worthless things: but the same tree, with some little advance of time, bears fruit, giving a most delightful fruit, and most pleasant to the taste by its inborn sweetness. This is the fruit for the sake of which it is tended, for the sake of which it is dug about, and for the cultivating of which labour is manifoldly bestowed. But when its first fruit is born abundantly, [1235B] because then almost all the bitterness that is in the fig is sent forth, there is good hope of that which follows, that the more bitter this one is, the sweeter that which follows may be sent forth. Of this fig therefore it is said, « The fig tree hath put forth her green figs. » For this is the fig tree of which it is said in the Gospel: A certain man had a fig tree planted in his vineyard, to which the lord coming for three years, and finding no fruit on it, said to the dresser: « Cut it down: » of which the dresser said: « Lord, let it alone this year, until I dig about it, and dung it [n: (Luc. XIII, 7, 8)]. » This indeed is said to be the Church, to which the Lord coming for three years (in one year, namely, that time which was before the law; in the second, namely, that which was under the law; in the third [1235C] indeed, that which began under grace) found no fruit; whence, being angered, he uttered these words, « Cut it down. » For although from the very beginning of creation the Church has indeed always existed, yet through all this time it existed in so few that its fewness, compared with the copious multitude, would be held as nothing, and reckoned for nothing. For in all this time, if thou find any just men, and compare them with the rest of the multitude, the comparison will be as if thou shouldst compare some small thing with an infinite multitude; whence our Lord and Saviour says: « The foxes have holes, and the birds of the air nests: but the Son of man hath not where to lay his head [n: (Matth. VIII, 20)]. » But this tree, now dug about, fortified by the admonitions of the holy apostles, taught by the holy examples of the martyrs, [1235D] the bitterness of martyrdom being now despised, now brings forth sweet fruits throughout the world: now it fills with the sweetness of its figs those whom it bewailed in the bitterness of its passion. Whence it is subjoined

*The vines in flower have yielded their sweet smell.*

But of the vineyards of the Lord there is one, and there are many: for the Church is one, and many. One indeed is the congregation of all the faithful who from the beginning loved God, and who love him still, and who shall love him: this indeed is one, abiding in the unity of faith, and of hope, and of charity: which indeed shall then appear, when the Lord shall judge the world, they who shall receive the kingdom from the Lord, the Lord saying: « Come, [1236A] ye blessed of my Father, possess the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world [n: (Matth. XXV, 34)], » who then shall already be free from the « snare of the hunters, and from the sharp word [n: (Psal. XC, 3)]. » For the snare of the hunters is the temptation of malignant spirits, which they stretch out for wretched mortals, that they may ensnare them in sins, and lead the ensnared to death: but the sharp word (than which nothing in the world can be sharper) is that which they shall hear, « Depart from me, ye cursed, into everlasting fire, which was prepared for the devil and his angels [n: (Matth. XXV, 41)]. » Which indeed the good also shall hear, but, with the hand of the Lord protecting them, they shall not fear, because he who freed them from the snare of the hunters, he also freed them from the sharp word, so that they may not dread it either, [1236B] but may rest secure in the glory already received. This indeed is what shall be there hereafter, which here is divided into many parts: for here there is a certain Church of Jerusalem, a certain Church of Antioch, a certain Church of Rome, and others innumerable throughout the world. For every congregation of faithful men, in the places which the Lord has chosen for himself, has there established a solemn house of prayer, in which they may pray, in which they may lament, in which they may render thanks for benefit: and these indeed are the vines which in flower have yielded their sweet smell. For the good that is done here is wholly in flower, sending forth from itself a good report; but it shall bring forth fruit when for the good rendered in time it shall receive an everlasting reward. [1236C] There follows:

*Arise, my friend, my fair one, and come, 14. my dove in the clefts of the rock, in the hollows of the wall.* [var: Vulg. *in caverna maceriae*, "in the hollow of the wall," singular]

The Beloved has now called forth the Bride, that she might come to him: he has invited her also by these seven things set forth, saying, « The winter is past; gone is the rain, and departed; the flowers have appeared in our land; the time of pruning is come; the voice of the turtle has been heard in our land; the fig tree hath put forth her green figs; the vines in flower have yielded their sweet smell. » By all which the opportuneness of the time was shown, that she should come. And still persevering in the same calling of her to himself, he repeats, saying, « Arise, my friend, my bride, and come, my dove. » He adds also, « In the clefts [1236D] of the rock, in the hollows of the wall. » Which words indeed add the memory of the Lord's passion. For who is ignorant that the apostle said of Christ, « And the rock was Christ [n: (I Cor. X, 4)]. » But that there are clefts in this rock, who would not know, who doubts not that Christ was fastened to the cross with nails? That there is also a hollow in this wall, who would not know, who doubts not that what he reads is true? For it is written, « One of the soldiers with a spear opened his side, and immediately there came out blood and water [n: (Joan. XIX, 34)]. » Which side assuredly is rightly named a wall: for a wall is a joining of stones without mortar; and this side indeed was joined without the mortar of human seed, [1237A] and was composed and formed by the hand of the Holy Spirit. Let the beloved Bride therefore come to her bridegroom who calls her forth, safe in the protection of the passion, fortified with the fortification of faith; let her come out of the world to him who overcame the world; let her come from the place of grief and of suffering to the place where there is no grief or suffering, but perennial peace, and continual exultation. There follows:

*Shew me thy face.*

The Bride obeys her Beloved; she knows no delay when she is called. For thus it was said to her, « Arise, make haste, come to me; » and she, being thus called, arises straightway, comes hastening, and stands by with no delay intervening. But she comes with her head covered, lest the sun burn her face, lest her whiteness be made base by the whiteness of the sun. But to the Bride standing by and covering herself, the Beloved says, « Shew [1237B] me thy face. » For it is just that the Bride be presented to her Beloved with unveiled face. For if Rebecca, at the well whose name is « of the living » and « of the seeing [n: (Gen. XXIV, 65)], » on seeing Isaac came down from the camel and covered herself with a cloak (which indeed was done thus because she was not yet a bride, and he had not yet received her, but brought her, destined for himself, into his mother's house, and took her to wife, and loved her so greatly that he tempered the grief which had befallen him from his mother's death): for this one, long since beloved of her Beloved, long since betrothed to the Bridegroom, and now known by spiritual love and by divine soliloquies, there is no need that she should appear veiled before the Bridegroom, but with unveiled face let her contemplate and love him. « For the word of God is living and effectual, and more piercing than any two-edged [1237C] sword, reaching unto the division of the soul and the spirit, of the joints also and the marrow, and a discerner of the thoughts and intents of the heart; and there is no creature invisible in his sight, but all things are naked and open to his eyes [n: (Hebr. IV, 12, 13)]. » What need therefore has he of a covering, to whom neither flowers, nor leaves, nor trees, nor garment, nor any covering are a hindrance, but that he may look upon and consider a thing from the end of its beginning even to the end of its issue; and rightly declare to himself whatever belongs to thought, and to speech, and to ancient habit and to work? Whence in the psalm David himself says three times: « O God, convert us [n: (Psal. LXXIX, 4)]: » and again: « O God of hosts, convert [1237D] us [n: (ibid., 15)]: » and a third time he subjoins: « O Lord God of hosts, convert us [n: (ibid., 20)]. » For it is needful for a man that he be converted from those things wherein he has offended in thought; that he be converted from those wherein he has offended in word; and from those wherein he has offended in deed and in habit; whence after this threefold invocation he subjoins, « And shew thy face, and we shall be saved [n: (ibid.)]; » for we shall be able to see the face of God securely, when God shall have shaken these offences from us, and he himself, being worthily invoked, shall have as it were let them go from his own memory, mercy bringing this to pass. So also God desires to see us with unveiled face, that nothing in us may appear in thought unworthy of him, nothing at all in word that may offend, nothing in act and deed that may displease. Whence he says, « Shew me thy face, » which thou hast made [1238A] worthy to appear to me, now after long pursuits of the virtues, after long desires of my kiss, that I may see whether thou hast done anything that is worthy of my kiss, that is worthy to be rewarded with the prize of my kiss. There follows:

*Let thy voice sound in mine ears.*



## 10. (cont.)

Where the face is now pleasing, where nothing intervenes that might be reprehended, licence is given of advancing further; that as the sight is delighted by the face of her Beloved, so the hearing may be delighted by the sound of the voice of the same: whence it is said, « Let thy voice sound in mine ears. » Ask what thou wilt; for thou wilt ask nothing, except what is fair and just, and what is worthy to be heard. Wherein it is to be noted that our voices sound in the ear of the Lord in divers ways. For sometimes we sound with a sound audible to God [1238B] alone, when we speak to him with the devotion of the heart only; as it is written of Anna, that « she spoke in her heart only, and her lips only were moved, and her voice was not heard at all [n: (I Reg. I, 13)]: » for her whole devotion was toward God; whence she deserved to be heard and to give thanks, and stood forth worthy to bring forth to the world the great prophet Samuel. God gives heed also to voices uttered with audible sound, if yet the intention of the heart be present, and the devotion which renders these audible; that the hearing may not be soothed only after the manner of birds, but rather after the manner of men, that the groanings of the inner heart may be heard. Hence in the Church the psalms resound, hence the hymns, hence the Alleluia resounds its sweet melody, hence other voices divers and innumerable, which human [1238C] invention puts forth unto the praise of God; which, assuredly, if due devotion accompany them, there is no doubt that they sound in the ears of God and obtain what is asked. Hence also the prophet says: « Sing joyfully to God, all the earth, sing ye a psalm to his name, give glory to his praise [n: (Psal. LXV, 1)]. » And at the end of the work, he concludes with the last psalm: « Praise ye the Lord in his holy places: praise ye him in the firmament of his power: praise ye him in his mighty acts: praise ye him according to the multitude of his greatness: praise ye him with the sound of trumpet: praise ye him with psaltery and harp: praise ye him with timbrel and choir: praise ye him with strings and organs: praise ye him on well-sounding cymbals: praise ye him on cymbals of joy [n: (Psal. 150)]. » Where, admonishing unto praise ten times over, he invites [1238D] to praise the nine orders of the heavenly ones, and man joined to them and made equal to them through grace, saying, « Let every spirit praise the Lord. » There follows:

*For thy voice is sweet, and thy face comely.*

« Thy voice is sweet, » which confesses and prays to me alone, magnifies and glorifies, judges that supplication is to be made to me for the things she has committed, and, after pardon granted, longs that grace and glory be given to her. « And thy face comely, » a face without spot, a face without wrinkle, a face that knows no repulse, and most worthy to be received and numbered among friends.

15. *Catch us the little foxes that destroy the vineyards: for our vineyard hath flourished.*

This Bride knew no delay: called forth by her Beloved, [1239A] who urged that she should come to him, she straightway arose, straightway stood before his majesty; and because she came covered, lest she should be overwhelmed by the glory, she deserved to hear what was said, « Shew me thy face, » which is, With face unveiled, contemplate me; « And let thy voice sound in mine ears, » that is, Ask what thou wilt, thou of whom I know that she will ask nothing but what is just. Received by the Bridegroom, she deserves to understand and to see what was promised, namely, that « The winter is past, the rain has gone away and departed; the flowers have appeared in the land; the time of pruning is come: the voice of the turtle has been heard in the land: the fig tree hath put forth her green figs: the vines in flower have yielded their sweet smell. » For these things the Bride gives thanks to the Bridegroom, knowing that all this is his work; and she herself receives praise and grace from the Bridegroom, because [1239B] his grace in her was not void, but she herself laboured excellently together with the grace given her. But now it is time that he instruct her concerning heavenly things, that he teach her concerning those things which are above her, that, called forth from common action into contemplation, she may understand the things she was contemplating, lest, when she has possessed them, she should stand forth wholly untaught. And because this is to be done by him, and he must in a manner be at leisure for the honour of his Beloved, he admonishes his companions that meanwhile they guard the vineyards which have now flowered, and « in flower have yielded their sweet smell, » lest the foxes destroy them, and the labours bestowed on them be brought to nothing: and he exhorts thus, « Catch us the little foxes. » But he speaks to his companions, that is, to the ministering spirits; of whom Paul says, « Are they not all ministering [1239C] spirits, sent to minister for them who shall receive the inheritance of salvation? » [n: (Hebr. I, 14.)] These are the angels, to whom the life of men is committed to be guarded; whom Jacob saw as he slept upon the stone. He saw a ladder standing upon the earth, and the top thereof touching heaven; the angels also of God ascending and descending by it; and the Lord leaning upon the ladder [n: (Gen. XXVIII, 12, 13)]; to whom here God speaks, commanding that they catch the foxes, who desire to harm this flowering vineyard, and to destroy it, and to bring its fruits to nothing: but these are the adverse powers, made by God at the same time and reprobate, hardened unto the harm of the Church; in this they stand, in this they abide, in this they reckon their successes, if it be able in anything to hinder the Church, [1239D] her that tends toward God, and desires him, and by the flower of good report now sends forth from herself a holy odour, or in some measure to disturb her. Which is plain in Job, is plain in Joseph, which was declared in Tobias, whom all these, as they drew them downward through adversities, were hindered by the good angels from accomplishing what they had begun; but, seeing that all these things wrought together unto good for them, they might perceive that their own power was nothing, save in so far as they were permitted by God. To the good angels therefore that is commanded by the Lord which was said, « Catch us the little foxes, » that is, Those whom you behold wandering like foxes, and disturbing all men by guile, [1240A] catch these, hinder these, and bring it to pass that they can do nothing except what is pleasing to my will. But the Lord makes mention of these foxes in the Gospel, saying: « The foxes have holes, and the birds of the air nests: but the Son of man hath not where to lay his head [n: (Matth. VIII, 20)]. » For to him who said to him, « Master, I will follow thee whithersoever thou shalt go, » the Saviour says, « The foxes have holes; » as if he should say, I receive thee not; I understand thee not to be predestined to me, but given to the foxes, that the foxes may tear thee, and the birds of the air, who are the malignant spirits, may rend thee. The same also he says of Herod threatening him: « Go, and tell that fox; behold, I cast out devils, and do cures to day and to morrow, and the third day I am consummated [n: (Luc. XIII, 32)], as if he should say, Tell this to him who has been given over to the fox for food, [1240B] He shall not slay me; but I, to day, and in the time that is to be until my death, perfect health in all who are weighed down by disease; and then on the third day I shall be consummated, that is, then I shall die, and all things shall be consummated which the enemy man is able to do against me. Of these foxes also David makes mention, saying to the Lord: « But they have sought my soul in vain [n: (Psal. LXII, 10)]. » For they who slew the Lord sought to destroy his soul, but in vain, because he lives: behold the Lord is King for ever. « They shall go into the lower parts of the earth [n: (Psal. XXVIII, 10)]; » for by the love of an earthly kingdom they shall be swallowed up, and blinded, so that they shall seem to have entered into the lower parts of the earth. For grieving to be made subject to the Romans, they were crushed by the edge [1240C] of the sword, and the greatest part of them was given to the foxes, that is, they were delivered to the malignant spirits, that these might feed upon them, and that whatsoever is done might be pleasing to their will. It is read also that Samson took three hundred foxes from the Philistines, and bound their tails together, and set the fire of lamps between the tails, and so sent them into the labours of the Philistines, the standing corn, and the vineyards, and the oliveyards, and consumed all things [n: (Jud. 15)].
