Which indeed is, as often as some Catholic doctor of the Church gathers together at once all the heresies of the heretics, and places in the midst of them the lamp of heavenly fire, that is, the fire of eternal malediction; and so, sending it into their labours, consumes what is theirs, and brings it to nothing. Wherein indeed it is to be noted [1240D] that these foxes are bidden to be caught while they are little; for they are little in the very first beginnings of temptations; and they become great when, as the temptation grows, they dash down the minds of men, and lead them into acts wickedly persuaded. For thus is chastity dashed down. First a woman is seen; and by frequent seeing she is coveted; after the coveting, there is a shaking by converse; after the seeing, and the coveting, and the converse, as the evil titillation grows, the act evilly desired is fulfilled, and chastity long assailed is cast down. So also covetousness is fed and nourished by sight, while a man measures not himself, « what he owes, what he does, » what it behoves to be justly done; but stretches himself, even unjustly, to the doing of those things which [1241A] are beyond him. So, possessing one thing, he seeks a second unit, that he may be multiplied tenfold: then, seeking a third, he does this, he seeks this, that he may be multiplied a hundredfold: to which, unless a thousandfold be added, he reckons that nothing has been done, since he is not ignorant that something is wanting to him. So this boundless disgrace of malice strives with the multiplication of number, never setting a measure to itself, so long as it has found some larger number into which it may grow. Whence the Saviour says: « That a rich man shall hardly enter into the kingdom of heaven. » And again: « It is easier for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle, than for a rich man to enter into the kingdom of heaven [n: (Matth. XIX, 23, 24)]. » The camel is the greatest rope that is had in ships, by which, when the tempest grows, ships [1241B] are compelled to stand fast with anchors cast; for this to pass through the eye of a needle is easier, that is, all things being despised, to depart through the hard suffering of death, than, this being avoided, to enter into the kingdom of heaven: which indeed, if it is difficult for man, is easy and possible for God. For we have known many rich men to have left all things for his sake, and to have entered to him through the eye of a needle; which is of the power of God alone, and of his ineffable mercy, who snatches them from the mouth and the lowest jaws of Satan, and leads them through to the heavenly kingdoms. So are the other virtues softened, so do they perish, so does vice possess what has been seized by the craft of the malignant one. Whence it is rightly said, « Catch us the little foxes, » which is, Resist these foxes while they are [1241C] little, shake their first motions, lest they be able to grow, dash them against me, who am the most firm rock. « For our vineyard hath flourished, » that is, it has sent forth from itself the hope of good working, and if it be defended by you, it has fulfilled this your hope with the fruit of good work. There follows:

16. *My Beloved to me, and I to him.*



## 10. (cont.)

This voice is the voice of the Bride, who, being called forth, has now come to the Bridegroom; with whom the Lord mingles conversations, mingles (that I may so speak) soliloquies; for to the Father alone, and to the Son, and to the Holy Ghost, are these things known which are spoken, which are revealed to this Bride: whence she herself also says, « My Beloved to me » as though he speaks to me; « and I to him » as though I answer words, but to you I reveal not what these secrets are. God keeps each of his secrets, [1241D] lest we attempt to enter in, nor let us presume to open what he reveals to the Bride alone; these things we pass over; to him alone to whom they have been committed we commit them, since he has not willed that they be opened to us. There follows:

*Who feedeth among the lilies. 17. Until the day breathe, and the shadows decline.*

These things being passed over, these being committed to silence, which God speaks to the Bride alone, the Bride adds what the Bridegroom does in the heights, wherein he is gladdened, wherein he is delighted, wherein he is refreshed, what pasture is held more pleasing to him, saying, « Who feedeth among the lilies. » But these lilies, sprung up in the heavenly gardens and courts, are so much the mightier in comeliness, in odour, and in sweetness, as he by whom they were planted is the more delighted to be present among them [1242A] and to be refreshed by the sight and the odour of them. These indeed are the heavenly virtues; their comeliness and heavenly fragrance is wonderful; so that he is said to be fed by this, who alone creates, alone orders, alone feeds all things which he creates, and sustains them. The Beloved is said to be fed among these, that those who, living on earth, already desire heavenly things may study to be conformed to them, that by the keeping of integrity they may bear up the palm of chastity even unto the converse of angels; and that the rest also may stand forth strangers to vices, claiming nothing of this world for themselves, that lust may not inflame them, that avarice may not goad them, that wantonness may not unman them, that ambition may not lay them low, that envy may not waste them, that the secular care of business may not make them anxious, « Until the day breathe, and the shadows decline. » It has been said that the Beloved is fed [1242B] among the lilies; but now it must be said how long he will abide among the same, others not being joined to him for the feeding; for this is what is said, « He is fed among the lilies; » and among these same, others not being joined to him, he shall be fed until that day of judgment breathe, in which God shall manifestly shew himself, rendering to every one according to his works; to the good, good things without end, to the evil, evil things indeed that shall abide without end. Two days indeed of the Lord are rightly named: one, in which he was judged, and of him it was said, « Not this man, but Barabbas [n: (Joan. XVIII, 40)]: » and likewise of him it was said, that « he be crucified; his blood be upon us, and upon our children [n: (Matth. XXVII, 23, 25)]. » The other, in which he shall judge the whole world raised up from death, « And he shall judge all men in equity, and the peoples [1242C] in his truth; » the day in which « the light of the moon shall be as the light of the sun, and the light of the sun shall be sevenfold, as the light of seven days [n: (Isa. XXX, 26)]. » This also is rightly called the day of the Lord, in which he is to shew himself the true Lord, and to render to them all things which they have deserved. When therefore this day shall have breathed, that is, shall have begun to appear, then shall he join the lilies gathered from the earth to those which have sprung up in the heavenly gardens; then shall there be made one Church of men and of angels, when men shall now not marry, nor give in marriage [var: Vulg. *neque nubentur*, "nor be given in marriage," passive], but shall be as the angels of God [n: (Matth. XXII, 30)]: then shall he be fed at once among the heavenly lilies, and among those which have been taken from the earth, and made equal to the heavenly, and, by virtue working through grace, made [1242D] conformable to their glory: then shall the shadows decline. The whole time which we here pass we run through under a certain alternation, now measuring the day and the hours of the day, now reckoning up the night and its hours as many: but this night the upraised shadows of the earth, and of the bodies which are in it, bring to pass: for when the sun moves below, the upper parts of the world are darkened, and the earth; and as the multitude of bodies which are in it casts its shadow upward, they are drawn over with the cloak of the night of the earth. But when this day shall have breathed, the sun abiding perpetually in it shall illuminate it, and the saints shall shine with clear light in the sight of the Most High, nor shall they feel this alternation of the oncoming night; but all those shadows which make this night, declining into the lower parts of the world, shall descend, and [1243A] shall make that dark night, now to abide perpetually, meet indeed for the damned and wretched. There follows:

*Return: be like, my beloved, to a roe, and to a young hart upon the mountains of Bether.*

The Bride, long since called forth, stood by the Bridegroom who called her, saw him feeding among the heavenly lilies, and, longing for that very thing for herself and for hers, deserved to hear that she should wait, « until the day breathe and the shadows decline: » then shall the lilies sprung from the earth be gathered together, and, joined and made like to the lilies above, shall be endowed with this gift, that the Bridegroom may be fed among them, and be delighted for ever, and that they themselves may sing that word of the apostle, « I have fought a good fight, I have finished my course, I have kept the faith [n: (II Tim. IV, 7)]; there is laid up for us [1243B] this crown of justice, which behold the just judge shall render to us. Whence she suppliantly prays that the Bridegroom may return, and visit, and cherish the Church which labours on the earth; that he himself may take up her labours, himself advance them, and himself lead them through even unto perfection, saying: « Return, my beloved. » There follows, « Be like to a roe, » that is, thy incarnation, whereby thou art like to a roe, that is, seeing all things clearly; and whereby thou art likened to a young hart, that is, art reckoned to have been brought forth to the world wondrously from the ancient fathers as a young hart: this present to her, this call back into her memory, that she may praise thee, adore thee, glorify thee above all the mountains of Bether, reckoning none equal to thee in the Church, but understanding thee to be far more sublime and far more holy than all the mountains which are in Bether, which is, « The house of God, » or, « The house [1243C] rising up, » or, « The house of the watch, » all which is the Church. There follows:



## CHAPTER III.

1. *In my little bed through the nights I sought him whom my soul loves; I sought him, and found him not.*

This Bride, or any faithful soul whatsoever, has entreated. Her Beloved, that, not deserting heaven, he may nevertheless look upon the Church labouring on the earth, and, taking pity, condescend to her labour; now already, that mercy may the more easily move him, and make him to be inclined to the things which are asked, she adds the words of the same wherewith she supplicates: « In my little bed through the nights I sought him whom [1243D] my soul loves. » But it is to be noted in this place, that Solomon, a festive king, and opulent in all things, given also to delights (as is the custom with most kings), makes known this his opulence to the world, and writing says: « There are sixty queens, and eighty concubines, and of young maidens there is no number [n: (cap. VI, 7)]. » But where there are so many queens, so many royal concubines, and the number of the young maidens who are nourished for the Bridegroom is not known, there [sic: *perfecto*] luxury abounds, wherewith one may gladden a taste that eats daintily and disdains common things; there are stipends, whereby their adornment may not displease the King at his coming, and all other things which may draw and allure him at his coming [1244A] to love. Which things, whosoever thou art that shalt hear them, it is not good that thou be broken in mind, that thou relax thy mind to soft things, nor that thou take delight in base things, nor that thou entangle thy mind in these things which are said, but rather that through these things which are said bodily thou understand spiritual things, as is the custom of the Scriptures, and that thou rather raise up and kindle thy love in those things which are spiritual. For this festive king Solomon is that great King, who is the Father, who is the Son, who is also the Holy Ghost, who, while the ages run their course, joins to himself one Church in three orders; of which he designates that part, « the first and chief, » by the name of queens, the second by that of concubines, the third by that of young maidens. And indeed they are designated by the name of queens who have long since run together, who [1244B] have ascended after him that draws, and have deserved to lay hold of him that draws; so that with the Apostle they may say: « For we know that neither death, nor life, nor any other creature shall be able to separate us from the charity of God, which is in Christ Jesus, our Lord [n: (Rom. VIII, 38)]. » [var: Vulg. *Certus sum enim*, "For I am sure"] Consider in this order Elias, consider Eliseus also; of whom the one was taken up to God in a fiery chariot and with fiery horses; the other, going up from Jericho to Bethel, having received the mockery of the boys of that place saying [n: (III Reg. II, 11)], « Go up, thou bald head, go up, thou bald head, cursed them in the name of the Lord. And there came forth immediately two bears out of the forest, and tore of them two and forty. But he went up from thence to mount Carmel [n: (IV Reg. II, 23, 24)]. » These two therefore, and very many others of the Old Testament, [1244C] thou wilt worthily attribute to this order, who, knowing nothing but God, and foreknowing that he should be born of a virgin, truly ran after him, and assuredly laid hold of him, and, being made heirs of his kingdom, glory with him for ever. But what is to be thought of those whom the page of the Gospel commemorates, who saw God present in the flesh, knew him from his words and deeds, and, following him alone, said: « Behold we have left all things, and have followed thee: what therefore shall we have? » [n: (Matth. XIX, 27.)] What of Peter? who, when the Lord said, « But whom do you say that I am? » [n: (Matth. XVI, 15, 16)] confidently answered: « Thou art Christ, the Son of the living God; » whom indeed the Lord declares blessed, because he answered that this was revealed to him not by [1244D] flesh and blood, but by his Father who is in heaven; whom he distinguishes as blessed in six clauses, saying: « Thou art Peter [n: (ibid., 18)], » that is, in the faith of me firm as a rock; and so named by me Peter, as it were a rock. « And upon this rock, » which I understand to be myself, and appoint to be in thee, « I will build my Church, » that is, I am about to give strength to the Church, and I will build so firmly that « the gates of hell shall not prevail against it, » that is, the malignant spirits, and the evil rich men who follow them, shall not hurt it. « And I will give to thee the keys of the kingdom of heaven [n: (ibid., 19)], that is, that none may enter into the kingdom of heaven, save through the faith and the sacraments [1245A] which have been committed to thee from me. And he shall be accursed, that is, the faith or the sacraments received shall not profit him; whom thou shalt have cursed; as those things shall be safe for him whom thou shalt have blessed.

What is to be said of John the evangelist? who says, « The Word was made flesh, and dwelt among us: and we saw his glory, the glory as it were of the Only-begotten of the Father, full of grace and truth? » [n: (Joan. I, 14.)] It is indeed to be said, that both these, and all the apostles, and all who are made like to these not indeed in dignity, but in charity and in works, these are they whose souls, received into heaven and honoured, are of such dignity that they deserve to be called queens by the King himself; in whom he so glories that he says, « There are sixty queens; » whom assuredly, how many soever [1245B] they be, he comprehends under the number of sixty, calling them sixty. The number sixty indeed consists of two perfect numbers multiplied into each other; for whether thou say six times ten, or ten times six, thou wilt make one number, namely sixty, and thou wilt assign these two numbers on either side, the ten of the law, the six of the Gospel. These therefore who are perfect in both, by fulfilling the law and the Gospel, are rightly applied to the number sixty.

These souls therefore are rightly called queens, who, joined to Christ by the right law of matrimony, have clung to him with the inmost affection of charity, and have brought forth sons by their example, justly heirs of the eternal kingdom; of whom it is said, « A chosen generation, a holy nation, a purchased people [n: (I Petr. II, 9)]. » Truly blessed are they who [1245C] have deserved to be made partakers of the heavenly virtues, and to enjoy that glory of charity which knows no failing. But in the second place are numbered those souls which are called concubines; for, as the Apostle says, « God indeed hath set some in the Church, first apostles, secondly prophets, thirdly doctors, after that miracles, then the graces of healings, helps, governments, kinds of tongues, interpretations of speeches [n: (I Cor. XII, 28)]. » In these perhaps thou wilt find a place for the concubines, which indeed are those souls which, though they be joined to God by some fellowship, have not cleaved to him with the whole heart, not with the whole mind, not with all their strength, nor have been made equal to the apostles; for, as the Apostle says, not all are appointed apostles, not all prophets. For to some [1245D] perfect grace is given, and the Lord knows who they are; to some imperfect, or perfect in one thing, not perfect in others; and these indeed beget sons from themselves, not as though begotten in matrimony, or heirs of the kingdom; but such as the Lord succours through his mercy, that they may be received in his kingdom and nourished, instructed, and saved. « But of young maidens there is no number; » for the young maidens are souls lately converted to the faith, or at least brought to conversion after very many sins committed: concerning whom it is not known what their consummation shall be; whom assuredly he regards, and, as they are the gifts of his mercy, in the manner which he determines, draws to himself. These therefore are the distinctions of the Church, which [1246A] Solomon distinguishes in what follows, saying, « There are sixty queens, » etc., which we have touched upon for the present for this cause, that we might teach that this Bride, called forth by the Bridegroom, and addressed with heavenly words, and taught by soliloquies, is that one who was endowed by Solomon with the name of queen, when it was said, « There are sixty queens: » but that she who remains on the earth, and is designated by the name both of concubines and of young maidens, is indeed that one for whom the Church above prays, saying, « Return, my beloved [n: (cap. II, 17)]. » To whose prayer, that the majesty of the beloved may the more easily be inclined, and may hear her voice, the words which she pours forth weeping are joined: « In my little bed through the nights I sought him whom my soul loves. » Our little bed indeed is our conscience, [1246B] in which we rest, if we present good things to ourselves; if evil things and the worst meet us, we are indeed straitened and afflicted. For him whose own heart reproves him, and who confesses that he has sinned grievously against the Lord, and has borne himself ill toward his neighbours, for him there is no rest, but labour, and affliction of spirit, and a broad confusion of grief: whence the Prophet says, « I have laboured in my groaning, I will wash my bed through each several night [n: (Psal. VI, 7)]. » He understands indeed the labour of conscience, which he shews both to groan, and to labour, and to wash the bed through each several night; which indeed then washes the bed, when in repenting it grieves and groans that it has offended God: and it washes it through each several night, while through each several sin it bewails that it has incurred the wrath of God: poured out in tears, when [1246C] it redoubles that it has watered with tears its own body, whereon it laid itself low by sinning, saying, « I have watered my couch with my tears; » because the eye of my mind is troubled with fury, that is, with the manifest wrath of the Lord, by the presence of mine enemies, that is, of my sins, among which, from the very first beginning of sinning, I have grown inveterate and been made old, and have been well-nigh reprobated from the very grace of the Lord; for Isaias says, « The bed is straitened, so that one must fall out; and a short cloak cannot cover both [n: (Isa. XXVIII, 20)]. » For the human mind is narrow and strait, so that if an evil spirit occupy it, the Spirit of God forsakes it; and when the Spirit of God has occupied it, this working, and free will judging better, [1246D] that malignant spirit shall justly and utterly depart, because the « cloak, » that is, the measure and brevity of our creation, « both, » the Spirit of God and the malignant one, is not able to « cover, » that is, to comprehend. Since therefore she seeks God through each several night, and it has been said to her by the Lord, « Ask, and you shall receive [var: Vulg. *et dabitur vobis*, "and it shall be given to you"]; seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened to you [n: (Matth. VII, 7)]; » what is it that she says, « I sought, and found him not? » This indeed is what we have said, that she has not yet ascended to the higher things, has not yet attained that dignity wherewith the queens were endowed, to whose petition nothing is denied, to whose seeking nothing is closed, to whose knocking it is opened the more readily: as yet she acts among the lowest things; whence she says:

2. *I will rise, and will go about the city: by the streets and the broad ways [1247A] I will seek him whom my soul loves: I sought him, and found him not.*



## CHAPTER III. (cont.)

« I will rise, » that is, from this burden of my sin, and at the same time I will shake off my filth; « And I will go about the city, » this great Church indeed, which now is common to the good and to the evil, that if I shall see in it any counsel, and any help that may profit, I may follow it out. And that she may shew more closely what she is about to do, she adds, « By the streets and the broad ways, » that is, by those bound to an order, and the married, or the free who remain without a spouse: through these « I will seek him whom my soul loves. » But although she is querulous, and zealous in seeking, yet she adds, « I sought him, and found him not. » What then?

3. *The watchmen who keep the city found me.*

[1247B]Who are these watchmen? Angels indeed, the guardians of the Church, to whom this guardianship has been committed, that they may know and understand what good, what evil any one has done, and what he has deserved to have rendered to him: of whom the Lord says in the Gospel, « See that you despise not one of these little ones; for I say to you, that their angels in heaven always see the face of my Father who is in heaven [n: (Matth. X, 18)]; » whom she addresses, saying:

*Have you seen him whom my soul loves?*

These indeed she addresses, from whom she does not openly receive an answer. For when she has twice surveyed the whole Church, and in both places has said that she has not found her Beloved, what the cause may be why she has not found him, [1247C] she is to hear secretly from the watchmen. For she said, « In my little bed through the nights I sought him whom my soul loves: I sought him, and found him not; » which is that she has brought back to memory in her conscience the « nights, » that is, the dark deeds wherewith she had offended God, and has said nothing further, nor done anything: which indeed is not enough with God for the remission of sin, to turn over in conscience alone that very thing which thou hast done, unless thou study humbly to amend by heart, by mouth and by work that which thou hast done. Thou amendest by heart, while thou repentest; by mouth, while thou confessest to him to whom thou art assigned; but by work, while by fasting, discipline, prayer, alms, watchings and teaching thou desirest to restore what was ill done. For blessed James says, « Confess therefore your sins one to another, and [1247D] pray one for another, that you may be saved [n: (Jac. V, 16)]. » This law of confession the blessed watchmen teach her, whom she both obeys, and she receives the said law of confession, repenting in heart, confessing with mouth, desiring to fulfil in work what is enjoined. There follows:

4. *When I had a little passed by them, I found him whom my soul loves; I held him, and I will not let him go, till I bring him into my mother's house, and into the chamber of her that bore me*

Behold how much this threefold confession profits: for she, having confessed her excesses by heart, and by mouth, and by work, when she had now passed by the « watchmen, » that is, the angels who guard the Church, in the Spirit, found him whom her soul loves, God, namely, whom she acknowledged [1248A] as her propitiator, by whom she understands that her sins have been remitted to her, and all things cast out from the eternal memory. Whence she says, « I held him, » that is, I clave to him with the firm root of charity, not about to let him go, « till I lead him into my mother's house, and into the chamber of her that bore me; » that is, till I convert to him the Synagogue, which is my mother, and the gentile world, which is she that bore me. For this holy Church, gathered from the whole world, had Judaea for her mother in faith, from whom first she had the worship of the one God, rejected idols, offered to him alone the victims of sacrifice and of holocaust, and obtained very many precepts of the law: but she had the gentile world for her that bore her unto the faith of Christ, since Judaea rejected it, and by her teaching she received fully his faith and all his sacraments. Him therefore she will not [1248B] let go, because she will not be silent, she will not cease from preaching, not from the testifying of the things which Christ taught, until both the gentile world and all Judaea receive him; and, both she, and that one who has been called forth on high and honoured as much by the name as by the dignity of a queen, bringing this to pass, the truth of Christ may be fully made known to the world. There follows.

5. *I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields, that you stir not up, nor make the beloved to awake, until she herself will.*

Where that which was set down is repeated, there is rightly a calling to mind of that which was said. The Bridegroom said above, « His left hand is under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me [n: (cap. II, 6)]. » Which indeed stood forth pleasing to the Bridegroom; for it proves the faith whereby [1248C] she subjects all temporal goods to the power of the Lord Jesus Christ: it proves also the hope whereby she sets forth that she is to be embraced in eternity by the right hand of God. Here also, that it is said that she is not to let him go, until by the preaching of both he be perfectly brought into the world, is gratefully received by him; and therefore it is enjoined to the daughters of Jerusalem, under the same adjuration, that they presume not to hinder it: « I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, that you stir not up, nor make the beloved to awake, until she herself will. » Of which, since what the Lord has given has been said, we pass on to the rest.

6. *Who is she that goeth up by the desert?*

This Bride, magnified by the presence of God, binds herself by promises that she will not rest, that is, that she will not desist from the preaching of Christ, until the whole [1248D] world acknowledge him, and be made subject to him. But the daughters of Sion, discerning her fixed in the fulfilling of this promise, and converting now these, now others to the faith, and ascending with them to things above, marvelling at her providence and her perseverance, say, « Who is she? » that is, of what sort and how great is she! and how most worthy of wonder, « who thus goeth up by the desert? » The desert is a place which all judge worthy to be deserted, which by this judgment none inhabit; the briars and thorns which it produces they abhor and fear; the venomous things which it brings forth, these they vehemently flee. By which name indeed this world is understood, which brings forth the thorns of sins, receives the venoms of unclean spirits, and sends those who receive [1249A] these things to the caverns of hell. « Who » therefore « is she! » that is, of what sort, and how great, and how most worthy of wonder, who through this world, subject to infinite temptations, and subjecting to itself with innumerable temptations those who give it their labour, and turning them back, sets for herself the steps of her ascent, and seeks to ascend from thence whence we know that so many thousands have hitherto descended; and seeks to acquire rewards for herself from thence, whence we have seen so many thousands of men incur for themselves the punishments of hell. « Who » therefore « is this she, » who treads down the glory of kings, and dreads not the threats of judges, fears not reproaches, despises flatteries, laughs at torments if they be applied, joyfully receives the penalty of death, and joyfully, accompanied by the virtues, transcends this world? Great indeed is this she, [1249B] who, if she is pressed by adverse things, is not broken; if she receives joyful things, is by no means lifted up; but shewing herself always the same in either case, purposes through this desert to flee, to ascend, and to draw near to God, whence also it is subjoined:

*As a little rod of smoke of the aromatical spices of myrrh, and of frankincense, and of all the powder of the perfumer.*

Smoke at the same time partly rises, and partly, that which had risen before, is wont to disappear in the higher regions: so the Church in certain of her members is ever begotten anew, in certain of them, who had been born before, is ever gathered to heavenly things. For smoke born of fire, mounting on high, withdraws itself little by little from human sight: so the Church, kindled by fire, namely of the Holy Spirit, unto the love of God, [1249C] ceases not to ascend, until, drawn away from earthly things, she be caught up to the invisible things of heaven: who is rightly called not a rod, but a « little rod, » because this is the manner of the ascent, that the ardour of compunction is sometimes so subtle that neither does he himself comprehend it who has deserved to have it, nor does he himself discern it to whom it has been given. Whence here not a rod, but a « little rod, » is rightly said, because there is understood a compunction of heart straight and attenuated, and nimble also, inasmuch as it is known to have arisen from the aromatical spices of myrrh and frankincense. Myrrh indeed defends bodies from putrefaction; but frankincense, when kindled, gives forth fragrance: whence by myrrh is understood the mortification of the flesh; by frankincense, the cleanness of prayer. The Church indeed mortifies the flesh, while she denies pleasures, and while she sends forth to God the fervour of prayer, she draws out as it were the smoke [1249D] of spices from the censer, that is, from her own heart, that she may smell sweetly to God, and kindle her neighbour. And lest she should present mortification only, and prayer, and draw near destitute of the other virtues, it is added, « And of all the powder of the perfumer, » which is: She herself is the little rod of smoke, arisen not only from myrrh and frankincense, but rather one which takes its substance from the powder which is pounded and mingled by the perfumer for the making of good perfumes, and is presented to God for glory. But in commemorating these spices, she sets first myrrh, then frankincense, afterwards all the powder of the perfumer, because it is first to mortify the concupiscence of the flesh, afterwards to offer to God the acceptable vows of the heart, [1250A] and so with humility to grow in every kind of virtue, whereby she may be able to please God. There follows:

7. *Behold sixty valiant ones of the most valiant of Israel surround the little bed of Solomon.*

The daughters of Jerusalem have already declared this Church to be wonderful in comeliness and in the marvellous exaltation of her work, whom they now proceed to set forth, shewing and magnifying the beatitude to which she tends, to which she ascends, whither she is drawn, whither, reduced into the powder of the perfumer, she shall now presently be seen worthy to be set, to be raised on high, and to be exalted: which beatitude assuredly he shews, designating the little bed of Solomon, and his litter, and the diadem of glory, wherewith on the day of his espousal he was held worthy to be exalted above all [1250B]. And of the little bed indeed it is thus said, « Behold sixty valiant ones surround the little bed of Solomon. »



## CHAPTER III. (cont.)

But it is certain that by Solomon, who is interpreted *peaceable*, Christ is signified, who pacified all things by his own blood, and brought back into one commonwealth the things which are in heaven, and those which he redeemed from the earth. But his little bed is the beatitude in which the King rests with the saints, to which the Church daily ascends through the desert; with how great adornments it is spread, with how great ornament it shines, flowing with how great abundance of delights, and overflowing with how great abundance of charity, they know whom that same little bed already receives, whom the Lord has already endowed with that same beatitude. This indeed « sixty valiant ones surround; » for that the chastity of this little bed may be kept inviolate, [1250C] sixty valiant ones « surround » it, that is, guard it. But in the number sixty are contained the preachers of the word, who, taught by the law and by the Gospel, instruct the good also with knowledge, and lead them through even unto perfection. For the number six is attributed to the Gospel; for by this number the Lord measures out the good things of the good, and the evil things of the evil, saying: « I was hungry, and you gave me to eat: I was thirsty, and you gave me to drink: I was a stranger, and you took me in: naked, and you covered me; sick, and you visited me; I was in prison, and you came to me [n: (Matth. XXV, 35, 36)]. » This therefore is rightly attributed to the Gospel, whereby, if ten be multiplied six times, sixty will be produced. These guardians of the little bed are therefore rightly reckoned sixty, who, [1250D] whithersoever they be carried in word, pass not beyond the Gospel, and depart not from the law. But these are named « valiant, » for fortitude is that virtue which alone defends the virtues, keeps judgments, unconquered in labours, valiant in battles, neglects money, flees the avarice which unmans virtue (for nothing is so contrary to fortitude as to be overcome by gain), is not tempted by covetousness, is not broken by fear, because virtue is consistent with itself, so that it pursues vices as the poisons of virtue; which, that he may not express it in a middling way, he subjoins, « Of the most valiant of Israel; » for they are most valiant who without weakness of desire contemplate eternal things, conquer the flesh, gainsay pleasures, bear the hard things of the world, despise blandishments for the sake of eternal things, overcome adversities, look upon their end, [1251A] labour without, that they may rejoice within. There follows:

8. *All holding swords.*

It has been said who they are that guard; but now it must be said in what manner, being fortified, they guard this little bed, « holding » indeed « swords. » Here the armour of the doctors is expressed, which is more fully shewn by the Apostle: for blessed Paul says: « Stand therefore, having your loins girt about with truth, and having on the breastplate of justice, and your feet shod with the preparation of the gospel of peace: in all things taking the shield of faith . . . and take unto you the helmet of salvation, and the sword of the Spirit, which is the word of God [n: (Ephes. VI, 14-17)]. This indeed is the armour of the doctors, which is designated in this place by the sword, which is the principal part [1251B] of the same armour. For the sword cutting on both sides is the word of God, dividing bodily things and spiritual, shaking the shadow away from the truth, « For the word of God is living, and more piercing than any two-edged sword, reaching unto the division of the soul and the spirit, of the joints also and the marrow, a discerner of the thoughts and intents of the heart. » This the valiant hold in their hands, practising in work that which they see in meditation. For they reckon it not enough to know and to understand, unless they fulfil in work that which they have understood: for we discern many who admonish well and live ill, who fear not to do that which they detest in word, and are understood to practise with their hands that which they « weave together » in word. So did the Scribes and Pharisees teach, [1251C] to whose words the Lord bids us give assent, but to turn aside from their works, and he charges his own to teach otherwise, saying, « So let your light shine before men, that they may see your good works, and glorify your Father who is in heaven [n: (Matth. V, 16)]. » By these therefore are swords held in their hands, who confirm their doctrine by pious works, and confidently teach what they themselves fulfil in work. There follows:

*And most learned in wars.*

For he is learned in wars who resists sin by work; more learned in wars, who resists it both by work and by the word of teaching; but most learned in wars, who resists it by word and by work, and by the zeal of discipline applied. For he delivers the innocent to destruction who frees from punishment him in whom the desire of robbing still breathes; and [1251D] he plots the destruction of many who spares the evil by granting pardon; for easiness of pardon is an incitement to fault. There follows:

*Every man's sword upon his thigh.*

The very impossibility of warring restrains many from warring, as if they have a sword, and hold it in their hands, and cannot draw it, or can do so only with difficulty: the enemy therefore is not slain, and he escapes unpunished from him at whose hands he has deserved punishment. Therefore it is subjoined, « Every man's sword upon his thigh, » that both the ease of drawing it and the opportunity of quickly striking the enemy may help him. For the sword is upon the thigh, when by preaching one's own flesh is tamed, lest, when he has preached to others, he himself be made a castaway. There follows:

[1252A] *Because of fears in the night.*

The life which is lived here is passed through days and nights: of the works which are done here, some are attributed to the day, some to the night; to the day, those which are of the light, to the night, those which are of the darkness. But the life which we await shall be wholly in the day, wholly in the light: there shall then be no participation of justice with iniquity, no fellowship of light with darkness, no agreement of Christ with Belial. Belial with his own shall wholly bristle with darkness, and thence no going forth, in whatsoever manner, to tempt the faithful shall now lie open to him by any reason; who, because he is not yet bound by this strictness of judgment, now goes forth; now, while there is time, he busies himself and strives to bring in by all means the work of the night and of the darkness. But these valiant ones drive him back, lest perchance the snares of the tempter lay these low, [1252B] secure and unarmed, and evilly snatch away anything from the guardianship committed to them. There follows:

9. *King Solomon made himself a litter of the wood of Lebanon. 10. He made the pillars thereof of silver, the reclining-place of gold, the going up of purple: the middle parts he paved with charity because of the daughters of Jerusalem.*

After the description of the little bed, the description of the litter is rightly subjoined. For the King, rising from the bed, takes himself to the litter, that he may be borne from place to place, according as he shall see the business of each to require. He says therefore, « King Solomon made a litter of the wood of Lebanon. » But Lebanon is interpreted *a whitening*; and the woods of Lebanon are cedars and cypresses; which indeed are straight and upright, putting forth their foliage on high, unfading, [1252C] incorruptible, sending forth also from themselves a most excellent odour: of these the King made himself a litter, wherein he might be carried about, whereto business should require him to be carried about. These woods indeed designate the ranks of the saints, which are straight, tending straight to God in meditation and in action; and incorruptible, because no corruption touches these when they are received into eternity, no suffering of death, or of grief, or of any trouble whatsoever affects them. Among these number the blessed apostles, Peter and Paul, and the rest raised on high with apostolic dignity, who bear the Lord through the world, who testify and announce to the world his deeds, his words and his promises. The same also he reckons pillars of silver, whom God gives as preachers to the Church, strengthened with the rectitude of justice, that they may sustain the Church by their examples, [1252D] and adorn her with the brightness of eloquence, as with the splendour of silver. He made also in this a reclining-place of gold, which indeed are the illustrious minds of the faithful hearers, in whom, while he shewed beforehand the heavenly joy, he composed as it were a reclining-place out of the gold of wisdom; for Wisdom is « more precious than all riches, and all the things that are desired are not to be compared with her [n: (Prov. III, 15)]. » But because to this one comes by much tribulation, and ascends by the shedding of blood, it is added: « He made the going up of purple; » for thither each one is borne up by martyrdom, which is designated by the purple; or assuredly by the passion of Christ, which is more precious than purple, through which there is an entrance into the Church and into heaven; the entry [1253A] to glory is opened by the sacrament of the passion. The litter being demonstrated, the pillars shewn, and the reclining-place, and the going up of purple also, it remains that the floor of the litter also be demonstrated, which he shews thus: « The middle parts, » that is, the floor which is in the middle of the litter; this he « paved with charity: » well does the prerogative of charity shine forth; for if thou art an apostle, thou belongest to the litter; if thou art a martyr, thou belongest to the same; if thou art a virgin, thou shinest forth in it by the merit of virginity: but if thou art not equalled to these merits, and yet thou lovest, thou art rightly received in this litter; for the middle parts have been paved with charity, that whosoever believes and loves may sit in the midst of it. Which indeed was added « because of the daughters of Jerusalem, » that is, because of those who are younger in time, and stand forth inferior in merit; who, if they are not [1253B] equalled to the highest, yet are not strangers to this litter, being kept in charity. There follows:

*Go forth, and see, ye daughters of Sion, king Solomon.*



## CHAPTER III. (cont.)

It has been said concerning the little bed, and concerning the keeping of it; and it has been said concerning the litter, and the adorning of it; it remains that it be said concerning the kingdom, and the glory thereof; which he pursues, saying: « Go forth, and see, ye daughters of Sion, king Solomon in the diadem wherewith his mother crowned him. » But by king Solomon, Christ (as has already been said) is understood, who bore the cross upon his shoulders, and through it has now subjected to himself all the kingdoms of the earth. To see him, therefore, the present Scripture stirs up all, as many as desire to be « daughters of Sion, » that is, daughters of the heavenly [1253C] watch-tower, who with the whole devotion of the heart long that a place be prepared for them in the heavens, that they may go forth, and after their going forth may see king Solomon in the diadem wherewith he was crowned [n: (Gen. IV, 8)]. But it does not exhort them to go forth as Cain went forth abroad, and rising up against his brother plotted against him. O wretched Cain, and to be pitied by none, who having overstepped the bound of innocence, who having forgotten brotherly affection, opened to the world the broad way of parricide, and slaying the innocent, declared himself guilty to the world [n: (Gen. XXXIV, 1)]; nor indeed as Dina went forth from her father's tent, that she might see the women of that region; whom, as she was busying herself curiously, and lifting up her eyes to those things which it was not lawful to imitate, Sichem the son of [1253D] Hemor saw, and loved her, and slept with her, oppressing the virgin by force; and the vengeance of this the very death of Sichem, and of his father Hemor, and of all the males who were found in the city, and the laying waste of the city itself, bears witness. For these goings forth are evil; nor are they written to this end, that it should be lawful to imitate them, but that, being written, they may be guarded against, and shunned alike in thought and in deed. But good goings forth are very many; as that of which the Lord said to Abraham, « Go forth out of thy land, and from thy kindred, and out of thy father's house, and come into the land which I shall shew thee: and I will make of thee a great nation, and I will bless thee, and I will magnify thy name, and thou shalt be blessed. I will bless [1254A] them that bless thee, and I will curse them that curse thee; and in thee shall all the kindreds of the earth be blessed [n: (Gen. XII, 1, 2, 3)]. » Which indeed was fulfilled by Abraham in obeying, and even now by the Lord, in truly fulfilling the things that were promised. Of Jacob also it is said, that going forth from Bersabe he was journeying to Haram [n: (Gen. XXVIII, 10)], and there, putting a great stone under his head, he saw a vision, and heard the Lord saying to him: « And in thee and in thy seed shall all the tribes of the earth be blessed: » and that this going forth was doubtless a good one, the very issue of the work that followed shews.

Of blessed Peter also it is read, that when he had gone forth from the hall in which Christ was being judged, the cock crew, and he wept bitterly: which going forth [1254B] doubtless signifies that he was even now estranged from the denial by which he had thrice denied the Lord, and that the tears poured forth from the bitterness of his heart, pardon being granted from above, were now received in heaven. Since therefore there is a certain going forth that is wretched, and departs far from God, and a certain one that is happy, and draws near to God, so far as may be, with the steps of penitence and of love; by what « Aaron » is it counselled to the daughters of Sion to go forth, that they may be able to contemplate and behold the King of glory in his diadem? Not surely by that whereby Cain went forth, whereby he slew his brother, and deserved to be slain himself: nor by that whereby Dina, following curiosity, wished to see those of another race, and grieved that she herself had been mingled with one of another race, and had incurred the loss of her virginity: nor by that whereby [1254C] the spirit going forth from the face of the Lord said: « I will go forth, and I will be a lying spirit in the mouth of all his prophets [n: (III Reg. XXII, 22)], » and I will slay Achab: but by that which Abraham and Jacob, Peter and Paul followed, and were made familiars of God, and his friends. We must go forth therefore from the camp of unbelief and of ignominy, from the camp of vain fear and of impenitence, from the camp of envy and of malice, from the camp of unchastity and of all uncleanness, that having gone forth from these, we may enter the camp of all the virtues, in which we may deserve to see the highest King, and to be pleasing to him. There follows:

*In the diadem wherewith his mother crowned him in the day of his espousals, and in the day of the joy of his heart.*

The holy mother of this highest King is that blessed virgin [1254D] Mary, whom the angel saluted, whom the Lord chose before all, whom the Holy Ghost overshadowed, whose praise surpasses all speech, of whom, whatsoever be said, that which is said is less than her praise; to her all utterances succumb, they ask pardon, and, however worthily the praises be uttered, they confess that nothing can be said whereby her merits may be increased or equalled. She crowned this King with a diadem, because this King took flesh in her and of her, in which he gloriously triumphed over the devil, and over the death by which the world was pressed down. For it is her flesh wherewith the woman compassed him, whom, born of her, the virgin most holily wrapped in swaddling bands, and laid in the manger. [1255A] This is he whom the star from heaven shewed forth, which drew kings from the East to adore him and to honour him with gifts; this is he who advances in wisdom, and age, and grace with God and men; this is he who in baptism purged our crimes; this is he who shone forth to the world by miracles; this is he whom the holy cross bore in its arms; this is he who rose from the sepulchre, and on the fortieth day, while the peoples looked on, ascended the heavens, and on the last day shall openly judge all; to see him therefore they who desire to be daughters of Sion are invited, that, having gone forth from the camp of the vices and entered the camp of the virtues, they may say with the apostle Thomas, « My Lord, my God [n: (Joan. XX, 28)]; » [var: Vulg. couples the two titles, "my Lord and my God"] and may see him and attend upon him in the last day, because, « this is life everlasting, that they may see thee the true God, and [1255B] Jesus Christ whom thou hast sent [n: (Joan. XVII, 3)]. » [var: Vulg. reads that they may know thee the only true God] With this diadem indeed his mother crowned him in the day of his espousals: for to this God, Father, and Son, and Holy Ghost, the blessed Virgin was truly espoused, when, acknowledging her the Mother of the Son of God, they joined her to themselves in true charity, and espoused her to themselves with the whole fulness of love. Hence indeed joy enters into the heart of the Virgin, hence she declared herself blessed, saying: « My soul doth magnify the Lord, and my spirit hath rejoiced in God my Saviour [n: (Luc. I, 46, 47)]. » This Bride, being summoned to the end of life, lest she be entangled and ensnared in these passing things, and admonished not to hope that what is spoken of is even now about to come, so that the Bridegroom should now be seen, should be bodily handled, the Bridegroom repeats what [1255C] was said above, saying:



## CHAPTER IV.

1. *How beautiful art thou, my friend, how beautiful art thou! thine eyes are those of doves.*

Concerning which, because by the Lord's gift we have already said what came to hand, we are now silent, wishing to attempt that which has been added:

*Besides that which within.*

He has indeed called his beloved like in her eyes to a dove; and lest this be understood too perversely, he added, « Besides that which lieth hid within; » for the dove, if she feeds on clean things, if she is without gall, if she tears not with beak or claw, if she has groaning instead of song, if she flies in flocks, is led to these things by a certain natural motion; to these things she is bound by no fear of God, [1255D] but is confined within them by a certain inborn law of nature: but the soul beloved of God, if in these things she likens herself to the dove, has within her in the mind that whereby she is found unlike her. For the dove, if she feeds on clean things, seeks food, not God: the soul, if she feeds on clean things, seeks God, not food. If the dove lacks gall, that is of nature; if the soul governs herself without anger, that is of grace. If she has groaning instead of song, she does not indeed grieve, but modulates her song as it has been given her: if the soul groans, she prays that the very presence of God may be with her, and that she may glory in it. « For great » (as blessed Gregory testifies) « is the glory of the open mouth; but far incomparable is the glory of the hidden recompense: » beautiful is this which is discerned without; but far and incomparably [1256A] more beautiful is that to which one comes invisibly: beautiful is the outward converse; but more beautiful is the unspotted desire of the heart, whereby blessedness is sought, and the conscience is cleansed. This therefore lies hid in the soul beloved of God, and within the dove is utterly unknown. There follows:

*Thy hairs are as flocks of goats.*

But it is to be noted that this commendation is comprised in the number seven, that thou mayest understand that the things which are said of her are said spiritually, and ought to be spiritually understood: for as it has been said of the eyes, so also it is to be understood of the hairs. For the hairs are of the body, and afford adornment to the face; sometimes they are cut off, and the very cutting confers adornment: if it should happen that they are wanting, a deformity of the face will appear. These [1256B] therefore are the faithful peoples, by whose throng the Church is adorned; who, by recalling to memory the heavenly things which they have heard, ruminate upon them, thus seeking their pasture on high, and contemplating things eternal in the cleanness of faith: who doubtless are cut off, when they confess that they have in any way sinned either in action or in contemplation; and this cutting affords adornment to the face, because true confession is the restoring and reforming of the spirit. Or if the subtlety of meditations be understood in the hairs, this is rightly compared to roes, which both tends to lofty things, and, if it is bound by temporal necessity, yet does not withdraw from the intention of things eternal. There follows:

*Which came up from mount Galaad.*

[1256C]Galaad is interpreted *a heap of testimony*, that is, the multitude of martyrs, who, having died for the faith of Christ, are witnesses of the truth. Or, « Galaad » is Christ himself, in whom the multitude of martyrs is one. From this mount the roes have come up, because the saints, imitating his death, have ascended to things on high There follows:

2. *Thy teeth as flocks of shorn sheep, which came up from the washing, all with twin young, and there is not one barren among them.*

« The teeth » are the doctors and expounders of the divine word: these grind the food for those whom they teach, and incorporate it, who, faithfully understanding and teaching the sayings of the Lord, pass these over into the substance of their souls, and keep [1256D] and break small the life already received by faith. These, compared to a flock of shorn sheep, are said to come up from the washing, who in baptism, the fleece of sin being laid aside, seize upon the way of virtue, draw the peoples after them, and out of Gentiles and Jews constitute one Christian people: or, rendering them perfect on this side by faith, on that by work, they enrich and adorn them with twin fruits. Whence none among them is found barren, by whom the preaching of the word received is diligently fulfilled in work. There follows:

3. *As a scarlet fillet, thy lips; and thy speech is sweet.*

The lips cover the teeth, they utter words, and the things which are formed within by the tongue and the teeth, being brought forth without, they render audible. They therefore who are the teeth are also the lips, which bring forth into the light without the things they form within; [1257A] and, that all the things they have said may be heard more attentively, they colour these with the Lord's passion, and labour together, with the sweetness of honey-flowing humility, that they may render them savoury to the taste of spirits. Whence here also there follows, « Thy speech is sweet. » There follows:

*As a piece of a pomegranate, so are thy cheeks, besides that which lieth hid within.*

The pomegranate is the Church, which holds as many grains under one rind as it contains faithful. The cheeks of this are the divers Churches established in divers places by the devotion of the faithful. For to whom his holy humanity is pleasing, by these a church is consecrated to it: to others the holy Trinity is at heart, to whom the place of a most devout man is sanctified. These therefore are the cheeks, running together into one head, namely the Lord Jesus Christ, [1257B] who is God threefold in persons, and one in humanity, the head and beginning of all things, which are, and which have been, and which are to be. Yet these cheeks are thus. « as a piece of a pomegranate: » for as in the pieces of a pomegranate there are grains, differing from the grains of the other part; so also in these places of the faithful there are divers devotions, the one there adoring and following the majesty of God, the other here most devoutly pursuing his humanity and humility, yet none of them fully comprehending the hidden things of God which as yet remain unknown. Whence here also it is subjoined, « Besides that which lieth hid within; » for that which neither eye hath seen, nor ear hath heard, nor hath ascended into the heart of man, this God keeps with himself, about to reveal it when he will. There follows:

[1257C]



## 4.

*Thy neck is as the tower of David, which is built with bulwarks: a thousand bucklers hang upon it, all the armour of valiant men.*

These parts having been shewn, the eyes, and the hairs, the teeth, the lips, and the cheeks, there follows the construction of the neck, whose framing is continuous with those things which have been mentioned. He says therefore that this neck is as the tower of David: for a tower was built by David in Jerusalem, repelling the darts of foes, fearing no engines of enemies; this is the preaching of the doctors, lofty and impregnable; which, while it preaches true things, is repelled by no falsehood of them that oppose it; while it flashes with miracles, it is proved to be true by the very demonstration of the thing. These therefore are the bulwarks [1257D] with which this tower is built, the holy Scriptures by which it truly builds up its own truth: and « a thousand bucklers, » that is, a thousand protections, by which it repels and represses the depravity of those who argue against it: these protections indeed hang from the holy preaching, because that which is holy in the patriarchs, that which is noble in the prophets, that which is notable in the miracles, all bears witness to the Gospel, and approves its truth, together with that armour of valiant men which remains. But who could express this armour of valiant men? Who could worthily comprehend in a word that which transcends all speech? [1258A] For who would suffice worthily to utter the constancy of Peter, who the fortitude of Paul, who the love of John, who the virtue of all the saints? by whom this armour goes forth, and affirms that these things which are preached are true. There follows:

5. *Thy two breasts are as two young roes that are twins, which feed among the lilies. 6. Till the day breathe, and the shadows decline.*

These are the breasts which we suck, from which we receive our increase, that we may please Christ the King, the two Testaments, of which the one is preparatory, the other consummating. That is preparatory which was given by Moses, in which it promises concerning Christ those things which we now see fulfilled concerning him in the New Testament; for this was the first prophecy, wherein the Lord [1258B] says to the serpent: « I will put enmities between thee and the woman, and thy seed and her seed: she shall crush thy head, and thou shalt lie in wait for her heel [n: (Gen. III, 15)]. » Setting forth therefore the blessed virgin, at whose heel, that is, at her outermost part, whereby the serpent lying in wait « saw » the earth, the serpent, that he might not prevail in his bite, was crushed by that very heel of hers. Her first seed, by Adam's teaching, appeared as Abel the just, who, offering a lamb, foreshewed the passion of Christ, and confessing it, fell therein innocent and a martyr. In this seed Seth « the numerable » and Henoch, Noe also saving the world by wood, and Abraham not sparing his own son, and Moses himself saying thus: « A prophet [1258C] shall God raise up to you of the midst of your brethren, like unto me, and he shall put his words in his mouth, and he shall speak to them all that he shall command him. But he that will not hear his words, which he shall speak in the name of God, he himself shall be the avenger [n: (Deut. XXVIII, 18, 19)]. » [var: Vulg. has God himself speaking in the first person throughout, "I will raise them up a prophet … I will be the avenger," and "like to thee"] And Isaias, bearing witness to this, says, « Behold a virgin shall conceive, and bear a Son [n: (Isa. VII, 14)]. » Jeremias says thereto, « The Lord shall make a new thing upon the earth, and a woman shall compass a man [n: (Jer. XXXI, 22)]. » [var: Vulg. puts the verb in the perfect, "the Lord hath created a new thing upon the earth"] Ezechiel affirming this says, « This gate shall be shut, and it shall not be opened, and a man shall not pass through it, and it shall be shut to the Prince [n: (Ezech. XLIV, 2)]. » And Daniel says, « when the Holy of holies shall have come, the anointing shall cease among you [n: (Dan. IX, 24)]. » [var: Vulg. at this place says rather that the Holy of holies may be anointed, and knows nothing of an anointing that ceases] Thus running through the whole Old Testament, thou wilt be able to find in it, in its promises, in its holocausts, [1258D] in its sacrifices, a certain preparation for the New Testament. But the New Testament is that which we hear, which we read, which we attest with Christ as its author, and, this now having its standing, the old perished, save that it bore faithful witness to the truth of this one. These are the breasts, « as two young roes, » which, when thou wouldst grasp them, will straightway flee from thee, and thou seest them climb up on high: for the understanding of man does not take in the lofty mysteries of the just, nor has faith any merit, for which human reason affords proof. Faith therefore is to be followed in these things, and let human reason wholly hold its peace in the confession [1259A] of the Trinity and in the truth of the Unity, in the mystery of the Incarnation and the sacrament of the Passion, in that gift of the altar which we offer, and let it venerate and love that which faith preaches (for these « feed among the lilies, » that is, they receive faith from those assemblies of the saints who, like lilies, stand before God, and receive from him the splendour of everlasting glory) until we stand with them, when the day of judgment shall have breathed, and these shadows of *igorantiae*, which for the present hinder us, shall have been wholly done away. There follows:

*I will go to the mountain of myrrh, to the hill of frankincense.*

The present seven having been run through in praise of the Church, lest he make delay in pursuing this through the remaining members, he does that which belongs to a lover: he desires to see [1259B] what he loves, and to be present with her whom he magnifies with so great praise. Whence he says, « I will go to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of frankincense, » that is, I myself will visit the Church in person, which is the mountain of myrrh and the hill of frankincense. But the mountain of myrrh is the height of mortification; of which the Apostle says, « Mortify your members which are upon the earth: fornication, uncleanness, lust, evil concupiscence, and covetousness, which is the service of idols [n: (Col. III, 5)]. » And the Church mortifies these things by considering the things that are above, where there is nothing, nor can be, of those things which have been named. But the hill of frankincense is the humility of prayer, whereby the Church raises herself on high, and, as from the censer of the heart, ascends like the smoke of frankincense. For by these [1259C] virtues the Church, or every faithful soul, is cleansed in the mortification of vice, striving in prayer, washing herself with tears, that, her filth being washed away, she may appear beautiful to the Bridegroom; whose endeavour he himself assists, and, praising his own work in her, he says:

7. *Thou art all beautiful, my friend, and there is not a spot in thee.*

And this indeed is the cause why I should go, because thou art « all beautiful, and there is not a spot in thee, » whose fulness he fully describes, setting last that which is first: for unto this spiritual beauty the first thing is that in the washing of baptism, or in the shedding of blood, the spots be blotted out: then that the spiritual virtues which please God be taken on, so that the Bridegroom, piously desiring her adorned in her head and in her members, [1259D] may come to her, and that a holy dwelling in her may please him. Whence he says:

8. *Come from Libanus, my bride, come from Libanus, come: thou shalt be crowned from the head of Amana, from the top of Sanir and Hermon, from the dens of lions, from the mountains of leopards.*

For Libanus is interpreted « a whitening; » whence she also, flooded with the light of the virtues, is called forth to come to God, inasmuch as to her it is thrice said, « Come, » that, perfected in faith and gladdened in hope, she may draw near to him, and, refreshed with the eternal fulness of brightness, may rejoice together with him: which joy indeed is noted, when it is said, Thou shalt be crowned from the head of Amana; » which [1260A] crown indeed is proved to be made from the summit of Amana, from the top of Sanir and Hermon. For « Amana » is a mountain of Cilicia, which is called « Restless, » or, « Turbulent, » designating the men of the world, who, out of avarice and desire of passing things, are always found restless and troubled. « Sanir » is a mountain in Judea, which is interpreted « Stench, » signifying those whose bodies are stirred by the burnings of luxuries, and are defiled. But « Hermon » is a mountain of Judea, which is also interpreted « Anathema, » and signifies those who, lifted up through pride, are so carried away that they have no communion with the Lord, who says, « God resisteth the proud, and giveth grace to the humble [n: (Jac. IV, 6)]. » What then is it, that the crown of his beloved is made out of these summits, unless that they who [1260B] had been troubled and overthrown by the filthy desire of things are fully converted to Christ; and that they whose bodies innumerable luxuries were tearing asunder embrace and love Christ alone; that they also who preferred themselves to all others are humbled even unto death for Christ, and say, « For me to live is Christ, and to die is gain [n: (Phil. I, 21)]. » By these therefore the Church is crowned and exalted; and embracing them in one word, he subjoins and says: « From the dens of lions, and from the mountains of leopards. » For these mountains are dens, that is, places in which lions take their rest. For malignant spirits are called « lions, » which lie down in those in whom the aforesaid vices now reign. But the « mountains » of leopards are those [1260C] in which virtue is mingled with vice, and that which is called hypocrisy rejoices to be honoured by all, the vice being covered with the cloak of virtue. And so, these who had been mountains of leopards and dens of lions being converted to God with a pure mind, the leopards, that is, embracing purity, and the lions loving simplicity and humility, a crown is prepared for the bride, which is now shewn to be for her a sign of victory, in that she has overcome the world. There follows:

9. *Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, my bride, thou hast wounded my heart.*



## 4. (cont.)

The Church, which is rightly called the bride of this highest King, purchased by his blood, consists in a division of two parts; one which is already perfect, the other not yet perfect. That one is perfect which has long since entered [1260D] upon the ways of life, which long ago went in the counsel of the good, and stood in the way of the same, and established for itself a seat in the chair of prudence. This one long ago left its darkness behind in Egypt, escaped the persecution of Pharao, and saw him, as he pursued, extinguished in the midst of the waves, because it knew that his power was made void by the waters of baptism. This one, following Moses in the desert, beheld the great works of God, and deserved to hear the words of his law brought forth from his mouth, which indeed it held so long, until it heard the Lord saying to Moses and to the people: « I will raise you up a prophet of the midst of your brethren, like to thee; and I will put my words in his mouth, [1261A] and he shall speak to them all that I shall command him. But he that will not hear his words, which he shall speak in my name, I will be the avenger [n: (Deut. XXVIII, 18, 19)]. » In this I would rightly count together all the fathers of the Old Testament, the patriarchs and the prophets; in the New Testament the apostles, the martyrs, and the confessors: these indeed are all joined and united to God, so that they are one spirit with him, that is, of one spirit with him; for they will that which he wills, and will not that which he wills not: so that there is no discord in them, whom he has himself taken with him into glory. To these I would rightly number the elect of God, living in the flesh, yet knowing nothing of the flesh, so far as is lawful, and long since « fortified » for God by the Spirit of God, to whom « to live is Christ, and to die is gain » ; saying continually to God: [1261B] Draw us after thee, that in the odour of thy ointments we may come to thee; this one indeed is perfect. But that one is imperfect which has lately received the faith, but has not yet fulfilled the works of God: it has indeed known the Lord, that he is good, but, not yet having attained the works of his goodness, it says with the Prophet, « For what have I in heaven? and besides thee what have I desired upon earth? » [n: (Psal. LXXII, 25.)] It has not yet left house, nor father, nor mother; not yet has this poor one followed the poor Christ. Others, having left father and mother, kinsfolk and children, houses and fields, do indeed follow Christ, yet do not deserve to attain him fully: the flesh presses hard, which stirs up and invites the one abiding in the flesh [1261C] to the allurements of the flesh and to its innumerable works: the adversary too presses hard, who assists this persuasion of the flesh, promising long times for penitence, and that, after the desires have been fulfilled, God, if he has been offended, being a merciful and appeasable Judge, can easily be recalled to pardon by tears. Whence also it laments, saying: « Oh! who shall deliver me from the body of this death? » [n: (Rom. VII, 24.)] And so it runs back over the times that are past, considering infancy, boyhood, and adolescence, judging youth and manhood and old age, condemning each single thing wherein it offended God, cleansing and purging itself also by penitence of heart, and confession of mouth, and satisfaction of work, by abstinence and discipline, by prayer and alms, by vigils and teaching, calling down the heavenly mercy upon itself; its sins [1261D] being forgiven, the more deeply it sinned, the more abundantly it now burns, saying, « Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, my bride; » as if it should say: O thou worthily espoused to God by works, who art justly called the bride of God, yet thou art my sister, since to us God is one Father by creation, and the Church one mother by generation; with the Bridegroom thou canst do much: shew for a sister what thou canst; the wound of love which I have borne in imitating thee, I beseech thee to enlarge, to spread charity wide in me, and by thy holy intercession to bring me through to that perfection wherein thou rejoicest. There follows:

*In one of thine eyes.*

The eyes of the Church are two, one of action, the other [1262A] of contemplation; one wherewith it looks upon its neighbour, the other wherewith it sees God; one wherewith it looks upon the needs of its neighbour, the other wherewith it searches out God and the heavenly secrets. In that eye therefore which is toward its neighbour, which is directed to his needs, it does not ask that regard be had to itself; but in that wherewith it sees God, wherewith eternity contemplates [cj: *aeternitas*; read *aeternitatem*, "wherewith eternity is contemplated"], wherewith it understands the secrets which lie hidden from a man living in the flesh, what is the breadth, what the length, what the height, and what the depth. For because God is charity, and the length of charity is the same as that which is God's; and as God is from eternity, so from eternity charity has existed, and as God is unto eternity, so unto eternity charity shall abide toward his creatures: this indeed is the length of God. But because there is no greater love than that [1262B] a man lay down his soul for his friends: his breadth stood forth in this, that he laid down his soul for his friends, that those whom he had had as enemies he might convert into the unity of faith, of charity, and of peace. But the height of God is in this, that everything which is, is from God; corporeal things indeed, God knitting together the parts of which they consist; spiritual things, God conferring upon them the life whereby they live, whereby they have understanding, whereby by a wonderful opening of the mind they understand the existence of all things: yet these are finite, beyond which thou wouldst understand nothing save God. And this is the height of God; from which he came down into the Virgin, from which he came down upon the cross, that thus at least, being himself man, he might restore by the power of his death all who should confess and believe him to be man. [1262C] But the depth is that of hell, into which he descended, that he might snatch thence those in whom he might recognize the seed of his mother: as was said by the Lord in the chief and first prophecy: « I will put enmities between thee and the woman, and thy seed and her seed; » understanding all to be her seed who should believe that he was to be born, or was born, of her seed, who, being without part in sin, should snatch the world from sin. There follows:

*And in one hair of thy neck.*

That is, In the unity of the hairs of thy neck; which hairs are as one hair. For the hairs are those which come down from the head upon the body, adorning the face and covering the neck; which are all so bound and joined in unity that they seem to be one hair. These are the several [1262D] persons of the saints, who, since they are all from God, strain toward him in such great charity and contemplation that they all seem as one; according to that saying, « And the multitude of believers had but one heart and one soul [n: (Act. IV, 32)]: » this part therefore, desiring to have part in this multitude, says, « Thou hast wounded my heart; » not indeed with the wound of death, which is declared to be deadly, but with that wound whereby life is conceived unto God, is nourished, and is perfected. Desiring therefore to be more wounded with this wound, and desiring to be more enlightened with this light wherewith God is seen, it says twice, « Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, my bride, thou hast wounded my heart, » desiring that a third wound [1263A] be laid upon it, wherein it may not hang in hope, but may rejoice in the apprehension of that thing which it desires. There follows:

10. *How beautiful are thy breasts, my sister, my bride! thy breasts are more beautiful than wine, and the odour of thy ointments above all spices.*

This is a worthy commendation of a sister by a sister; for it says: O thou who hast deserved to be worthily called the bride of God, who yet hast not denied that thou art a sister to me, « how beautiful are thy breasts! » that is, how beautiful is the teaching of thy breasts for the consolation of the weak, and the sustaining of little ones? And it makes these breasts manifest by a repetition of praise, saying, « Thy breasts are more beautiful than wine, and the odour of thy ointments above all spices. » Concerning which, [1263B] because we have shewn that which the Lord gave, we forbear for the time to repeat these things. There follows:

11. *A dropping honeycomb, thy lips, O bride.*

The sister still perseveres in the praise of the bride, saying: « A dropping honeycomb, thy lips, O bride » of Christ. Nothing is sweeter than a honeycomb; and of the divine words it is said: « How sweet are thy words to my palate! more than honey to my mouth [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 103)]. » For a honeycomb is honey in wax, that is, the spiritual sense of the words in the letter, dropping from the wax, which is as the letter, the spiritual understanding, allegorical, or moral, or anagogical. Or because all the divine words preach the incarnation of our Lord Jesus Christ, in which divers natures are joined, as honey is hidden in the wax which lies open, so the divinity in the flesh. [1263C] Therefore the lips of this bride are compared to a dropping honeycomb, which are proved not to keep silence concerning the testimonies of the divine incarnation.

*Honey and milk are under thy tongue.*

For they who preach his incarnation have honey and milk under the tongue, proclaiming God in the sweetness of honey, and testifying to the innocent man in the milk which is gathered from the earth. For honey is from heaven, because the virgin bee receives it from the heavenly dew, and gathers what she has conceived into the wax; so also the virgin mother conceived from heaven the conceived Son of God, clothed him conceived with flesh, refreshed him clothed with the nourishment of milk, and thus presented life to the world out of herself. Deservedly therefore are they said to have these things under the tongue, who, [1263D] meditating on them in the heart and testifying to them with the tongue, hold them by memory, and in preaching announce them.

*And the odour of thy garments is as the odour of frankincense.*

Garments cover the body, defend it from cold, and if they are more elegant, afford adornment, and confer grace upon the possessor. This the virtues confer upon souls, without which they are naked, and exposed to the colds which come from the north wind, where he has set his seat who has taken care to chill all things: but from this cold those souls are safe which have now left the world, which, following Christ more closely, have deserved to hear: « Come to me, you that labour and are burdened, and I will refresh you [n: (Matth. XI, 28.)]: » whom [1264A] indeed he well refreshes, while he commands them to spurn the world, and to seek God, and to cleave to these things with the whole mind.
