The soul indeed clothed with right faith, clothed with humility and penitence, clothed with confession and with satisfaction of every kind, when it now hopes well of pardon, and clothes itself with charity of every kind, and, exercising itself in this solicitude as much as it can, now uses garments in which it is pleasing to God, whose odour is best, and the fragrance sweet, « as the odour of frankincense. » Frankincense indeed, set upon the fire, gives forth from itself a most sweet odour: so the virtues, mingled with the fire of charity, bestow here a good example upon the peoples, and give pleasing honour to the Lord, and a most sweet odour. There follows:

*[1264B]*



## 12.

*A garden enclosed, my sister, my bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed up.*

With beautiful praise she has extolled her sister; she has praised her eyes and the unity of her hairs; she has commended the comeliness of her breasts, and the odour of her ointments, her lips and her tongue, together with the odour of her garments; now she designates the place where such things are laid up: they are laid up indeed in a garden enclosed; and not merely enclosed, but enclosed again. Which garden, twice enclosed, is understood to be she herself, to whom it is thus said: « A garden enclosed, my sister, my bride. » For a garden is called a pleasant place, where flowers give forth their scent, where little beds of divers kinds grow green, where pigments and incenses answer to the balsam, where the shade of any fruit-bearing trees affords a sweet seat and dwelling to one who wishes to rest. A garden of this kind [1264C] is the Church, which has now been received by God, or is being prepared to be received in the near future, which does not delay to present to God the virtue of good works, the appearance also of holiness (which is designated by the balsam or by the other spices), and the shade wherein the Lord may shew himself, in which he may rest and take delight, and to present her very self. This indeed is a garden enclosed. By what enclosing? By the bar of faith indeed; for true faith shuts her in and fortifies her on every side, so that in no part of her does the enemy find a door or an opening whereby he may come to her, by tempting or by in any degree assaulting her: who is again called « a garden enclosed; » but by what enclosing? she shews it, saying, « A fountain sealed up; » as if she should say: Thou art shut in by faith, and not by faith only, but also [1264D] by baptism; for the Church has received baptism, which is the fountain sealed up, in the name of the Father, of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost. And wheresoever the enemy finds this worthily kept, he flees, he vanishes, and, as wax before the face of the fire, so indeed he melts away. There follows:

13. *Thy sendings forth are a paradise of pomegranates, with the fruits of apples.*

« The sendings forth » are good words and works, whereby the Church begets and nourishes martyrs, and sends them over to heaven; others, living holily, she sets forth as an example to their neighbours, and thus sends forth the fruits of pomegranates. The pomegranate shuts up under a red rind a multitude of grains, and its fruit sweetly refreshes: [1265A] so they who pour out their blood for Christ gather to themselves a multitude of virtues: others who, living in peace, work good things, instruct their neighbours and refresh them; and both send forth the odour of the virtues, as it were a paradise of sweetness: for « Paradise » in Greek is in Latin called « a Garden, » or « an irrigation. » But there is a paradise in us, when the soul is animated and quickened by the virtues, and by the infusion of the Holy Ghost, bearing fruit in the nurseries of the virtues, in which God has chiefly set the tree of life, that is, the root of piety; for that is the substance of our life, if we render to God the worship that is due; let the just man therefore abide in paradise, that against the heats of the body and the burning of the flesh he may be cooled by the shade of the virtues, and be guarded by their helps; which is, « with the fruits of [1265B] apples. » For pomegranates are they who depart hence crowned with their own blood: but those fruits which are called only apples are they who, although they are not called martyrs, yet follow after God with honesty of life and with the confession of the true faith. There follows:

*Cyprus with spikenard. 14. Spikenard and saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon, with all the trees of Libanus, myrrh and aloes, with all the chief ointments.*

This is drawn back to that which was said, « Thy sendings forth are a paradise of pomegranates: » not pomegranates only are sent forth by thee; nay rather « cyprus with spikenard, » and the rest that follow, are thy sendings forth. The cyprus is an aromatic tree, with a white seed [1265C] and of good odour, which is boiled in oil, and thence is made an ointment which is called cyprus, wherewith princes also are anointed, that they may lift themselves above others in lofty virtues; and but if with the spikenard [cj: *sin*; read *sit*, "and let it be with the spikenard"], which is lowly, and a herb fragrant and warming, and signifying charity: intimating this, that a prince, however much he excel others in virtue, should always cleave to them in the charity which is designated by the spikenard. « Spikenard and saffron. » Saffron is of a golden colour, designating the divine wisdom, which it is needful that a prince observe, having that before his eyes: « The greater thou art, humble thyself in all things *(Sirac III, 20)*; » and that other: « He that humbleth himself shall be exalted, and he that exalteth himself shall be humbled [n: (Luc. XIV, 31)]. » -- « Sweet cane and cinnamon. » The sweet cane indeed, which is also called cassia, is an aromatic tree, [1265D] but small, of a sturdy and purple bark, which avails for the curing of the troubles of the bowels, and which, by reason of its shortness, is reckoned by some among the odoriferous herbs: it signifies the humble in spirit, who, mindful of the Lord's passion, are prepared to suffer for the Lord. But cinnamon is a short tree, yet odoriferous and sweet, of an ashen colour, availing for the use of medicine, surpassing the sweet cane twofold; signifying those who reckon themselves weak, whose humility has praise and sweetness with God. And well is the cinnamon of ashen colour set after the purple sweet cane, because through the remembrance of the Lord's passion there arises in us a despising of our own virtue. [1266A] « With all the trees of Libanus. » For as the sweet cane and the cinnamon shew the humble thoughts of the saints, so also the trees of Libanus, which are tall and sturdy, shew the lofty actions and prayers of the saints. « Myrrh and aloes with all the trees of Libanus. » Myrrh and aloes are the continence of the flesh, and chastity, which mortify the rottenness of luxury. But myrrh renders the body incorruptible: so bitterness and patience fortify and defend souls against carnal concupiscences. For the kinds of spices are the honour and the advancement of the divers virtues which are in the saints. Out of these kinds are made the first and chief ointments, which heal bodies; well therefore do they designate the virtues of souls, which being gathered together and compounded, a good odour goes forth, and health [1266B] is restored. There follows:

15. *The fountain of gardens, the well of living waters which flow with force from Libanus.*

The praise of a sister by a sister is thus repeated, « The fountain of gardens, the well of living waters. » For she it is in whom is the fountain of all the gardens, that is, the Holy Ghost, who, being God, waters the gardens, and brings them forth into buds, and makes them to have flowers, and fruits, and seeds, whereby they please, and for the sake of which they are cultivated. For from him is Scripture, which, begetting the water of wisdom, refreshes, and does not cease to flow; which is well called « of the gardens, » because it belongs especially to those in whom the virtues arise; which is a fountain as far as the surface, a well in the deep. For Scripture in certain [1266C] places is open, and is easily taken in by the understanding: but in others it is obscure, and unless it be expounded it is not understood. Yet the waters which glide down are living, that is, they assuredly lead to life the men for whom they were given, if they be understood: whence it is said, « The stream of the river maketh the city of God joyful: the most High hath sanctified his own tabernacle. « Which flow with force from Libanus [n: (Psal. XLV, 5)]: » which flow out from the heavenly Libanus, that is, from the whiteness of spiritual and heavenly virtues; « with force, » that is, they run down forcibly. For with great force they run down upon Moses, who described the first beginnings of the world, and spoke with God at a time when no man had withstood; with great force upon the prophets, who foretold that God would be man, when he [1266D] was not yet man; with great force upon David, who so ran through all the mysteries that nothing in them is left untouched; with great force upon the apostles, who, renewing the world afresh, preach life to us, and foretell life eternal. There follows:

16. *Arise, O north wind, and come, O south wind, blow through my garden, and let the aromatical spices thereof flow.*

Thus far the Bridegroom has heard the praise of his Bride, distinguished by six clauses, which he now confirms that he approves, saying, « Arise, O north wind. » For God commands every heavenly power, both that which was created in the heavens and was made reprobate by him through the elation of its mind, and those which stood with him, [1267A] and are joined to him by the firm root of charity. And so the Lord, about to come into his garden, that nothing sad may meet him, and that he may find nothing whereby he may be offended, powerfully commands the north wind to arise, to take itself far from that place, to go to foreign nations, to chill them, and to render them like unto itself. But he commands the south wind to come to his garden, and with its soft breath to blow through this garden, so that its aromatical spices may flow, and that a holy habitation may be made and may please God. He says therefore, « Arise, O north wind, » which is, Depart; I have no fellowship with thee, no participation of light with darkness. Let that Spirit of mine come, who may soften the necks of my own, who may turn the rock into water; who may appoint Samuel, an innocent youth, judge of the whole people; who may make David, the boy harper, a king, singing psalms [1267B] in the spirit of prophecy; who may fit Daniel, a youth, to be the judge of elders, the deliverer of the innocent, and Amos also, a countryman distinguishing the mulberries of brambles, to be among the prophets by that same spirit of prophecy; let him come who (that I may come back to modern times) may appoint Peter the fisherman and Paul the persecutor doctors of the whole Church, who willed to have Matthew the publican for an evangelist, and Mary Magdalen for a herald of grace and of the resurrection; who (that I may make mention of her from whom all these things were begun) consecrated Mary, a virgin pure, holy, and untouched, into θεοτόκον, that is, the Mother of God. Therefore, when he blows through my garden, « Let its aromatical spices flow, » that is, The spices which had been bound fast by the blowing of the north wind, that is, the virtues and gifts of God, shall flow, [1267C] shall drop down, and shall be made manifest to all, which hitherto lay hid. There follows:

*Let my beloved come into his garden, and eat the fruits of his apples.* [var: Vulg. has the singular, "the fruit of his apples"]

The word being heard whereby all things were made, which it is not lawful to gainsay, straightway the north wind departs, the holy Spirit is present, and at his coming its aromatical spices flow, and send forth a most sweet odour; and this Bride, hoping that it will please God, says: « Let my beloved come into his garden. » There is nothing in the garden that may hinder, nothing that can now displease by sight, by smell, or by taste; let him come therefore into his garden, « that he may eat the fruit of his apples, » that is, that he may « eat » the apples which are his fruits, that is, incorporate them into himself, and pass them over into his own body. This therefore [1267D] is the general prayer of the whole Church, that the Bridegroom may come by a certain spiritual grace into his garden, that is, into the Church herself, and may visit her trees and her herbs and her spice-bearing beds, and pluck of them, and taste apples of divers kinds, which are indeed as many as there are divers observances, constitutions, and divers customs in the Church of God: for this diversity of apples denotes the virtues of the divers Churches throughout the world, differing indeed from one another in something, yet without doubt running together into the service of the Bridegroom. For apples are whatsoever fruits are held to be of a tender rind, whereon the Bride desires her Bridegroom to feed, that is, to be delighted with every kind of virtue whereby the Churches stand and minister. [1268A] As this is the general prayer of the Church, so it can be the particular prayer of every faithful soul, that he may come into her, who is discerned to be his garden, and refresh himself with the variety of those virtues which are in her. There follows:



## CHAPTER V.

1. *I am come into my garden, my sister, my bride; I have reaped my myrrh with my spices.*

The Bridegroom answers those who are held back by desire, and testifies that he has already done the things which she was asking, saying: « I am come into my garden. » And because a sister, addressing a sister, has long used this name, « Sister, » that he may conform himself to her, he answers her with the same word, saying, « My sister, my bride, » as though joined and allied to me by my own blood. « Sister, » because we have one [1268B] Father, God, and one mother, the Church, which refreshes us with her sacraments. He says therefore, « My sister, my bride, » I long since came into my garden, and I have applied this sign of my coming, « I have reaped my myrrh, that is, With a certain sickle of death I have cut them off in this life, and have led them into the storehouses of eternal blessedness: the martyrs who suffered bitter things for me, or even all the others, who have crucified the flesh with its vices and concupiscences: « With my spices, » that is, with all those who are marked out by the report of good works, and therefore worthy of much veneration and renowned. This myrrh indeed he reaped, when he received Abel the first martyr from the hand of Cain: this also he reaped, when he snatched Noe, and Abraham, and all whom he knew to be the seed of his mother, from the curse. [1268C] There follows:

*I have eaten the honeycomb with my honey; I have drunk my wine with my milk.*

The honeycomb holds honey within wax, and when it is taken, it fills him who takes it with great sweetness: so when the preacher of an obscure word expresses the sweetness which is contained in it, he pleases God indeed, and in so far as God is delighted in him, he is taken up and incorporated. « I have drunk my wine with my milk. » The preacher of an obscure word pleases, when its sweetness has been expressed; so also the preacher of a word which is not obscure pleases by its gladness and by the sweetness mingled with it, and is taken up into his body. There follows:

*Eat, friends, and drink, and be inebriated most dear ones.*

[1268D]The Bridegroom, invited to the garden of delights, has used the delights in it, the honey wherewith he was fed, and the wine which he drank: now therefore, lest he seem to keep his banquet to himself alone, lest he be thought to enjoy these things alone, he performs the work of charity, and inviting his friends, that is, the heavenly virtues, to the same, he says, « Eat friends, » that is, be refreshed with the same delights as I, you whom from of old I have perceived to be friends; and « be inebriated most dear ones, » you whom I held dear, when I created you; and most dear, when I confirmed you in the grace of my charity: « Be inebriated, » that is, forgetful of all anxiety, be made glad with the wine of joyfulness. There follows:

2. *I sleep, and my heart watcheth.*

[1269A]The Lord came, being invited, to his garden; he was fed with honey, and drank wine; which refreshment indeed is an incitement to sleep. Whence also, that he may show that same place to be one of rest, and not of laborious working, the Bridegroom says, « I sleep. » Which sleep indeed was fulfilled in Abraham; to whom, sleeping after the sacrifice, the Lord thus said, « Know beforehand that thy seed shall be a stranger in a land not their own, and they shall bring them under bondage, and afflict them four hundred years. Nevertheless the nation which they shall serve, I will judge: and after this they shall come out with great substance. But thou shalt go to thy fathers in peace, and be buried in a good old age. And in the fourth generation they shall return hither: for as yet the iniquities of the Amorrhites are not complete, [1269B] even to the present time [n: (Gen. XV, 13-16)]. » Behold with how watchful a heart the Lord spoke to the sleeping old man, to whom he plainly foretold things that should come hereafter, after four hundred years [n: (Gen. XXVIII, 12)]. The Lord slept also in Jacob, under whose head a stone was set, and he saw a ladder standing upon the earth, touching heaven with its top, and the angels of God ascending and descending by it. Behold with how watchful a heart the Lord spoke to this man, showing him angels ascending, who were presenting to the Lord, leaning upon the ladder from above, the good, who had served him by virtue and had given him their labour through grace; and others descending, who should snatch out from them by grace such as were wrapped in sins, and raise them up, and, when they had been presented [1269C] to the Lord, should make them one body with the rest. The Bridegroom slept also in Joseph, and watched most fully in the heart, that is, in the soul wherein is the seat of the heart [n: (Gen. XXXVII, 7)]: for he saw himself and his brethren binding sheaves in the field, and his own sheaf standing upright, and his brethren's sheaves standing round about and adoring his sheaf. And likewise he saw the sun, and the moon, and eleven stars adoring him [n: (ibid., 11)]. The understanding of these things the Bridegroom opened to his father and to his brethren; for the brethren said: « Shalt thou be our king? or shall we be subject to thy dominion? » [n: (Ibid., 8.)] In like manner the father also said, « Shall I and thy mother and thy brethren adore thee upon the earth? » [n: (Ibid., 10.)] The Lord sleeps also in certain men, and in others he watches in heart, when for some he preserves the memory of the vision, and to others [1269D] he affords the understanding of it [n: (Gen. XL)]. For the ministers of Pharao saw dreams, and telling them to Joseph, both of them received the understanding of their own dream. For the one was restored to his place [n: (Gen. XLI)]; the other, being hanged, perished. Pharao also slept, and, watchful in heart, he beheld seven fat oxen feeding in the marsh, and seven others, foul and wasted with leanness, devouring them, whose appearance and habit of body was wonderful. He testified also that he had seen thin ears, and stricken with blight, devour the beauty of seven other ears. For whom, when he watched in heart unto understanding, who declared to him the abundance of seven years to come; and the famine of seven [1270A] years. The Bridegroom slept also in Nabuchodonosor, who, when he saw the vision, it was revealed to him neither unto memory nor unto understanding; which indeed was laid open to Daniel out of a watchful heart, when he said: « Thou, O king, sawest, and behold as it were a great statue. The head thereof was of the finest gold, but the breast and the arms of silver [n: (Dan. II, 31, 32)]. » Thus indeed he revealed the dream to him, and consequently showed him its mystery and the understanding of it. Such then is that which is said, « I sleep, » that is, whom I will I make to sleep; but while the outward sense is lulled, the inward mind freely contemplates those things which God provides with a watchful heart. But if thou refer it to the passion, such is that which is said, « I sleep, » that is, enduring on the cross the labour of death, thereafter I rest from [1270B] death. « The heart » however « that is mine, » that is, the wisdom of God, to which my heart is the seat, this same « watcheth, » that is, it watchfully preserves, orders, and disposes all things. There follows:

*The voice of my beloved knocking, open, my sister, friend, my dove, my undefiled.* [var: Vulg. *Aperi mihi, soror mea, amica mea*, "Open to me, my sister, my friend"]

While the Bridegroom sleeps in the garden, after a long delay, lest she herself should seem shamelessly to search into his secrets, this bride betakes herself into her own dwelling, the bridegroom being left in that same place under the guard of his friends. Yet meditating upon him in her heart, seeking him, and preparing herself, that she may be able worthily to meet him when he comes to her, and to make ready things pleasing to him when he comes. But the Bridegroom, roused from sleep after the delay, knowing that she had gone away and was anxious for him, does that [1270C] which belongs to a lover: he follows her, and finding the door shut, he knocks at it, saying: « Open to me, my sister; » as if he should say, Receive me coming to thee; reject me not, who seek a dwelling in thee; but be earnest to open to me the door of thy mouth, the dwelling of thy heart within thee, I who, seeking thee alone, desire to find thee, who art « my sister, my friend, my undefiled, my dove. » There is no need to linger in setting forth these names, since from what has already been said they may easily be understood. Yet there is need that we consider how much he wills to be heard, who willed her to be called to him by so many names of affection. There follows:

*For my head is full of dew, and my locks of the drops of the nights.*

[1270D]The cause why the Bridegroom asks that it be opened to him, he shows by these words, saying: « For my head is full of dew. » For thus he shows that he lay down and fell asleep not under a roof, but in the garden; for the garden, as has been said, has no roof; it receives none under a roof, but to him who rests in it the holy shades of the holy trees afford a remedy. Whence he says, here I, falling asleep by night, bring back my head full of dew, and my locks with the drops of the nights: *For dew cometh by night, by night it stealeth beneath*
*the grasses, and with what continuance that one is stretched out*
*it followeth her, and, sitting upon the topmost crown, a drop*
*it maketh, and thus maketh a certain shadow of rain*.


[1271A]This is the dew whereby the grasses grow green again, whereby the plantings sprout, whereby the fruitfulness of the earth is opened, and gladness is plenteously poured abroad upon all in common. The Bridegroom therefore, willing that his Bride be steeped in this dew, willing that she herself be filled with these drops, which are formed by night, that is, apart from the knowledge of men, asks that it be opened to him, saying: « Open to me; » and adding the cause, he says: « For my head is full of dew. For the head of Christ is God, in whom all the fulness of wisdom and knowledge is known to dwell, whom they that receive Christ are known to have in themselves indeed, and his gifts also; and « with the drops of the nights, » that is, with the heavenly virtues, which were conferred [1271B] upon them by night, that is, when man did not yet exist, nor had any knowledge of them. There follows:

3. *I have put off my garment, how shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them?*

The Bride, hearing the voice of the Bridegroom knocking and desiring to come in to her, the dew which the Bridegroom desires to bring in, and the drops of the nights, being not yet understood, is affrighted, and, not answering the Bridegroom, speaks to herself, and makes her words to herself, saying, « I have put off my garment, how shall I put it on? » For thinking that she is called back to her former and ancient manner of life, and fearing to be entangled in these worldly affairs for her neighbour's necessity, and complaining, yet not answering the Bridegroom, she says to herself, « I have put off my garment, » that is, I have been stripped of my ancient manner of life, and have been converted to Christ in baptism, and have been knit to him by the [1271C] preaching of good men: in what manner shall I now put that on? in what manner shall I embrace what I know not? whence she says, « I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? » Her feet indeed are her affections, which she washed by believing, by acquiescing in the exhortations of the saints, by hearing Paul, who says, « Not in rioting and drunkenness, not in chambering and impurities, not in contention and envy. But put ye on the Lord Jesus Christ [n: (Rom. XIII, 13, 14)]. » In what manner shall I defile these? how, by departing from them, shall I bend myself to the manners of the world which I had cast away? It is indeed to be feared to return to the action of the world, which cannot long be carried on without sin. There follows:

[1271D]



## 4.

*My beloved put his hand through the hole, and my belly trembled at his touch.*

Her excuse having been reverently made beforehand within herself, yet no answer having been made to the Bridegroom, she next shows what her beloved had done for her, saying: « My beloved put his hand through the hole, and my belly trembled at his touch. » Whence blessed Gregory thus says, « Bountiful is the mercy of God, which alike opens the gate of life to all who are converted after their sins, that they may know the mysteries of the heavenly country, and by knowing may thirst, by thirsting may run, by running may arrive. » These secrets of the heavenly visitation he had felt [1272A] who says, « My soul hath thirsted after the living God [n: (Psal. XLI, 3)]: » and blessed Paul, saying, « I desire to be dissolved, and to be with Christ [n: (Phil. I, 23)]. » Blessed Ambrose also says hereupon, our hole, that is, that whereby we see the works of Christ, namely, the eye of the mind, through which Christ enters, whereby we truly know him to be God, whereby we ourselves most joyfully sing with the Prophet, « Wonderful are thy testimonies, O Lord, therefore hath my soul sought them [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 119)]. » And likewise that, « Thy testimonies are become exceedingly credible [n: (Psal. XCII, 5)]. » Which testimonies indeed are his wonderful works, which, while all marvelled, he wrought in the flesh. Hence restoring paralytics, cleansing lepers, raising the dead, and finally healing all sicknesses, [1272B] by his word alone, whereby all things were created and abide for ever. Which things the world seeing, and marvelling at the testimonies, declared them to be true; and if they were false, they would receive from falsehood no effect of power. Whence she says, « My belly, » that is, I, made wholly belly, trembled and was afraid. For the belly lacks bone, it has no share in firmness, what is let into it, this it receives, and renders it digested: so also our humanity, not withstanding the divine works, trembles wholly, and is affrighted that God has been made man; yet the things which have been given from above, these it acknowledges with the mouth, loves with the heart, and, being touched by the gifts of the Holy Spirit, trembling, it says, « My belly trembled at his touch. But being touched, she shows what she did, saying:

*[1272C]*



## 5.

*I arose up to open to my beloved: my hands dropped with myrrh.*

« I arose up, » that is, I shook off from me the burden of sins wherewith I was weighed down. Whence it is said, « Rise, thou that sleepest, and arise from the dead, and Christ shall enlighten thee [n: (Ephes. V, 14)]. » Rising, I also admonished others, that they should rise: to these I showed in myself the pattern of a good work, and, opening the door whereby they might enter in to the beloved, I dropped upon them with my hands the first myrrh, and with my fingers the most approved myrrh. But myrrh, wherewith the bodies of the dead are anointed, and are guarded from rottenness and corruption, signifies the mortification of the flesh, wherewith the spirit is well anointed, while by that saving continence which is wrought [1272D] through fastings, through abstinence, through watchings, and other things of this kind, the flesh is called back from the allurements of luxury, and is adorned with the blessed end of continence: she declares that she has dropped this with her own hands, that is, has shown it forth in all her works, saying, « My hands dropped with myrrh. » And therefore she adds « the first, » because that which flows first from the tree, this the more effectually keeps bodies from corruption. But she shows that she has dropped this, that she may show that she has watched very greatly in the pursuits of frugality. There follows:

*And my fingers were full of the most approved myrrh.*

In which place she opens the virtue of « tearing apart » [cj: *discerptionis*; read *discretionis*, "of discretion"]: for by the fingers discretion is shown: [1273A] because after it is touched by the touch of the finger, that is understood by a certain sure reasoning of the mind. Whence, lest in this guardianship of chastity the measure be exceeded, it is added, « My fingers full of the most approved myrrh. » Myrrh indeed is approved with a wife, more approved with a continent widow, most approved with a holy virgin abiding so. There follows:

6. *I opened the bolt of my door to my beloved.*

Lest the approach to the beloved should be shown to lie in the continence of chastity alone, this is added: « Of my door I opened the bolt, » that is, I removed the silence of the mouth, wherewith until now I was held back, and I opened my mouth, and drew in the spirit, and because I desired thy commandments, those things which thou hast commanded me to keep, and which thou hast willed to be commanded and kept by all thine, these [1273B] I opened, these I admonished men to keep, that so they might approach the Bridegroom, and be pleasing to my beloved. There follows:

*But he had turned aside, and was gone.*

It has been said that she arose that she might open, and fitted her hands, that is, her works, that she might preach; that she admonished all to continence, that so they might approach the Bridegroom, and thus removed the *possulum* of the door, that all might cleave to Christ, and so possess him. « But he had turned aside, » lest, being laid hold on, he should be held; « and was gone, » that is, far surpassing what can be said or promised of him, he had withdrawn himself into that delightfulness which neither eye hath seen, nor ear heard, nor hath it entered into the heart of man, which he himself prepares for them that love him. Which indeed is so done, that [1273C] neither may a sluggish soul cease to seek him, nor may one that labours reckon that she has fully comprehended him. There follows:

*My soul melted when he spoke.*

This beloved had gone; but behold, after his manner, he now returns. He had knocked, he was asking that it be opened to him, and when the Bride rose up to open to him, he himself passed by, and did not suffer himself to be found even by her that sought him. This is the act of the inward man; for he sometimes seeks God, and does not find him. He is found also when the slothful man has now ceased to seek him. For behold this Bride has now risen, she has now held the bolt that she might open, and, not finding him who was knocking, she stood amazed within herself; but God, [1273D] beholding her amazed and marvelling, lest his absence long afflict her, returns to console her, that she may be gladdened by his speech. These therefore are the signs of his coming. As wax melts at the fire, and white snow at the sun, so at the coming of her Bridegroom this Bride was melted, and was changed from that sloth and hardness which she had already gathered in herself. For thus she says, « My soul melted, » that is, from hard and impenitent, penitent; from impatient, compassionate; from unmerciful, merciful; passing from every state of the vices, which I had already begun to feel in myself, into the contrary state of virtue, I melt, and, being now joined to God, I rejoice together with him. Thus indeed, when she bewails her own sins, when [1274A] she laments the hurts of others, or their misdeeds, when she bewails the state of this misery, to which she is subjected of necessity, thus when she bewails the delay of the glory to come, into which she is not received according to her desire, she is indeed melted, saying, « My soul melted. » These indeed are the four rivers of Paradise, wherein the faithful souls wash themselves, wherein they cleanse themselves, wherein they wash away sins, nourish virtues, and stand in the sight of the divine majesty, without spot and wrinkle, worthily and purely. But by her soul she means all who from Adam even unto the end shall have believed that Christ, the Son of God, was to be born, or was born, of the Virgin, and would redeem the world by his death; all of whom, for the unity of faith, naming them as it were one, she says, « My soul melted. » But she subjoins, « When [1274B] he spoke. »

That this may be the more diligently considered, that saying of blessed Paul is to be weighed; for he says, « In manifold wise and in many manners God of old speaking to the Fathers in the prophets. Last of all in these days he hath spoken to us in the Son [n: (Hebr. I, 1, 2)]. » Here indeed he spoke « in manifold wise » in the prophets, that is, through many words; for the great manifoldness of the sentences which the Lord spoke through them could not be unfolded in few: for a contracted brevity begets obscurity, and that obscurity indeed does not admit of understanding. « And in many manners, » he spoke through them; now indeed historically, now allegorically, now tropologically, now anagogically, mingling his words. « But last of all he hath spoken to us in the Son *(Matth. [1274C] IV, 17)*, » whom, sent forth from the bosom of the Father, brought forth through the womb of his mother, the world heard teaching and saying: « Do penance, for the kingdom of heaven is at hand [n: (Matth. V, 3)]. » And likewise that, « Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven. » Blessed are the meek, blessed are they that mourn, blessed are they that hunger and thirst after justice, blessed are the merciful, blessed are the clean of heart, blessed are the peacemakers, blessed are they that suffer persecution for justice, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven. They saw also works wonderful to tell: a leper coming to him, to whom Jesus said: « Be thou made clean [n: (Matth. VIII, 3)], » and forthwith his leprosy was cleansed [n: (ibid., 13)]: saying also to the centurion: « Go, and as thou hast believed, so be it done to thee [1274D] [n: (ibid., 14)]: » and his servant was healed in that hour; touching Simon's mother-in-law sick of a fever, who was straightway healed. They saw him also casting out all malignant spirits by his word, and healing all the blind, the dumb, the deaf, and the lame, and all that were ill at ease; and speaking thus, and confirming his words with signs following, and, that he might be believed the Lord of death and of life, raising the dead, and raising himself, who had been given over to death and laid in the sepulchre, and promising this resurrection to his own in himself in life eternal: what could they do but marvel, be astonished, and believe, and adore? And hearing from him that on the last day he will bring all things to judgment, [1275A] and will say to the good, « Come, ye blessed of my Father, possess the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world [n: (Matth. XXV, 34)]. » For I was hungry, and I thirsted, I was naked, and a stranger, sick, and in prison, and in all these things you helped me. What could they do but be melted, so that they sold their own goods, and bestowed all upon the poor, and gave their very selves to them, and made themselves subject, that so they might fulfil the Gospel. Rightly therefore has every such soul melted, when the Beloved spoke. For there is nothing that the Father loves more than the Son, to whom he bears witness by his own voice at his Baptism, saying, « This is my beloved Son [n: (Matth. III, 17)], » whom above all that are in the world I love, in that I find him in every thought, word, and work, just in all things. [1275B] There follows:

*I sought him, and found him not.*

This soul, or the Bride of God, has testified that she was melted at the voice of the Beloved; whence it might be understood that she had comprehended him, and had from this friendly speech found an end to her labours. Lest anyone conceive this in his heart, she adds, saying, « I sought him, and found him not. » For all the elect souls seek him; they desire, being raised again in their bodies, to stand by his presence; and because this is not yet, but is most certainly promised as to come, she says, « I sought, » that is, I asked the raising again of them all, and I desired that faith [cj: *fidem*; read *finem*, "an end"] be set to all the labours wherein we seek God, and pant after him. Him indeed I longed for, yet I did not find him, [1275C] because until the day appointed by the Father there shall be the waiting for him, and the certain reward of all. Yet it is written, « Every one that asketh, receiveth: and he that seeketh, findeth: and to him that knocketh, it shall be opened [n: (Matth. VII, 8)]. » Which indeed is thus: All the time wherein we live, wherein we run, is present to God; for eternity is the present state of all those things which have been, which are, or which are to come, since therefore the Lord, sitting in the midst of the circle, beholds with one glance the things which have been, which are, and which are to come, all these are judged present to him, by whom in one glance they are clearly beheld. He says therefore, « Every one that asketh, receiveth, » that is, every one who in truth asks that eternal life be given him, and who seeks that the incorruption [1275D] of the Saints be given him, and who knocks for this with multiplied prayers, that it may be granted him to be received into that glory of the saints; he indeed, when that day shall be at hand, is to receive this, to find this, and to be truly brought into that glory, of which it is said, « I will, Father, that where I am, there also may my minister be [n: (Joan. XVII, 24)]. » [var: Vulg. *ut ubi sum ego, et illi sint mecum*, "that they also may be with me"] There follows:

*I called, and he did not answer me.*

Assuredly every elect soul cries to God, saying, « O God, come to my assistance [n: (Psal. LXIX, 2)], » that if at any time he that is malignant tempts, your grace may withstand him, lest he bring to the effect of sin that which was conceived at his instigation. He adds [1276A] also, « O Lord, make haste to help me [n: (ibid.)], » as if he should say, Thou, O God, who protectest us by helping while we are tempted, make haste to bring us to that state wherein neither his suggestion may be able to move us, nor any temptation of his to bend us to evil: which indeed shall then come to pass, when all the multitude of the elect shall be joined to the angels of God, and they shall neither marry, nor be given in marriage, but shall be as the angels of God. There follows:

7. *The keepers that go about the city found me; they struck me, and wounded me, the keepers of the walls took away my cloak from me. 8. I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my Beloved, that you tell him that I languish with love.*



## 5. (cont.)

She has said that he whom she sought was not found, nor did he whom she called answer her. Whence it might rightly be said [1276B] and reckoned that the Bridegroom had left her to her own counsel, and had by right suffered her to be seized by those passing by; which she removes, saying, « The keepers of the city found me. » The city of God is holy Church; he who reigns in her, who disposes all her rights, himself sets keepers over her, who may forbid her both to sin and to enter by the broad way which leads many to death, and may diligently teach her to lay hold of the narrow way of the virtues, which few know, and to enter by the strait gate, until she come to eternal life. These are the keepers of the Church, whom the Lord teaches by his Spirit, whom he fills more abundantly with the spirit of wisdom and understanding, the spirit of counsel and of fortitude, of knowledge and of piety, and with the spirit of fear; and he bids them pour this abroad throughout the Church, that they may securely lay hold of God, whose [1276C] Spirit this is. But these, to whom this guardianship of souls has been committed, marking this soul, or this multitude of souls, seeking God, and labouring in this very thing, that she may find him, receive her mercifully, receive her devoutly. To these they preach the words of life, in these they mortify whatever sins remain, and in him whom they have received they now kindle them more fully and more keenly with the love of the Godhead. This therefore is what is said, « The keepers of the city found me: they struck me, and wounded me. » For those words strike well which slay sins; and those wound well which make the love of God already conceived to burn more fully, and magnify it. « The keepers of the walls took away my cloak [1276D] from me. » We have already said that the keepers of the Churches are their Prelates. But who are the keepers of her walls, must now be said. For it has been said, Gird us about with thy impregnable wall, O Lord, and protect us always with the arms of thy power. For this city is girt with a wall, and lest it be undermined anywhere, there is need of a guard, lest, if an entrance lie open anywhere to the enemy, he who is within sleep the less securely. This wall of the Church, therefore, is faith, and if it anywhere lie open where it has been broken through, there will now be the less rest for every citizen dwelling therein. But the heavenly citizens, namely, the Angels of God, keep this, bestowing their labour upon this, that Satan pierce it not, that they suffer not the Church to be scandalized or troubled in any [1277A] part of the faith. These therefore are the keepers of the Church, namely, the angels of God, going about her indeed and encompassing her with a rampart, and bestowing their labour that faith be not broken in any part of itself, and that hope be not lessened in anything, and that the charity of God, which is the highest virtue, be not corrupted by growing cold. But these keepers take away the cloak of the Church, establishing faith in her, strengthening hope, rooting charity in her, so that it is now nothing to despise all these earthly things, while she is able to cleave to Christ alone. And this indeed is what she says, « The keepers of the walls took away my cloak. » The cloak indeed is an outward covering, that is, all this earthly substance, which was created for the Church's sake: with this the Church protects herself, fortifies herself. [1277B] But she forsakes and despises the whole of it, when she now sees herself given to God and drawing near to him. She prays therefore, saying, « I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, » that is, ye heavenly Virtues, who are in nothing partakers of this misery, who owe wholly to the heavenly Jerusalem alone what you are, what you savour, that you protect us; « If you find my Beloved » (which indeed it is certain that you will find), « that you tell him, that I languish with love, » that is, for love of him I rest from all earthly things. There follows:

9. *What manner of one is thy Beloved from the Beloved, O most beautiful of women? What manner of one is thy Beloved from the Beloved, that thou hast so adjured us? 10. My Beloved is white and ruddy, chosen out of thousands.*

[1277C]The daughters of Jerusalem, being adjured to announce to the Beloved the love of her that languishes, lest they err, lest they present to one the love perchance given to another, inquire by what sign they may know her Beloved, lest the message of love be borne by them into uncertainty. They say therefore, « O most beautiful of women! » And they call her most beautiful, because there is nothing of spiritual beauty whereby a soul in this life may please God, which the Church has not in herself, which she does not represent out of herself. She cares not for that beauty of which Solomon says, « Favour is deceitful, and beauty is vain [n: (Prov. XXXI, 30)]: » but that which follows, « The woman that feareth the Lord, she shall be praised [n: (ibid.)]: » this she takes up, this she doubts not to offer to the Lord. Innumerable indeed are the virtues which spring from fear, [1277D] and many which spring from love; and if thou shouldst number them all, thou wilt find these in the Church a noble gift indeed, such as is worthy to be presented to God. Worthily therefore she thus names her, « O most beautiful of women! What manner of one is thy Beloved? » By what sign dost thou make it clear to us, that we may know him whom thou lovest, and may know his Father by whom he was sent, whom thou also lovest. And because this is exceedingly divine, and most capacious of both natures in Christ, she doubles her question, saying again, « What manner of one is thy Beloved from the Beloved, that thou hast so adjured us? My Beloved is white and ruddy. » This is the description of the Beloved, which she comprehends under the number twelve, [1278A] saying, « My Beloved is white and ruddy; » wherein she comprehends the twofold nature in Christ Jesus our Lord: the one, which is divine; the other, which is human. The divine indeed she comprehends, saying, « He is white: » but the human, adding, « And ruddy. » In his divine nature indeed he is white, retaining nothing mingled with himself; nothing black, nor red, nor anything coloured with a middle colour: but he himself is white, nay rather whiteness itself, spreading himself abroad into all his creatures: and though he shine in all, he diminishes nothing of himself, but he himself remains whole and entire in himself. Thus therefore he is white, because he is truly God, is truly light, is truly the simple good, which is diffused through all things that are his, and, remaining in itself, is one simple good. But he, having long [1278B] pitied human nature, after many thousands of years came down into the Virgin, and taking flesh from her, and creating for himself a soul, being made man he came into the world, choosing out of the world certain men for himself, to whom he himself said, « You have not chosen me: but I have chosen you [n: (Joan. XV, 16)], teaching the world first by himself, afterwards by them, and thereafter working mighty works; and since the world could not withstand them, it contrived the cross for his death, and laid his body in the sepulchre, from which, when he willed, he rose again, and thereafter remained on earth forty days to console his disciples, and thereafter, while all that were present beheld, he mightily ascended into heaven. He, being set upon the cross, because he redeemed the world by his blood, whose colour is red, [1278C] is deservedly called ruddy; so that in his memory and praise the Bride says, « My Beloved is white, and ruddy, » and adds also, « Chosen out of thousands. » For to this he alone out of the thousands of all men ought and was able to be chosen, who should wipe away the faults of men, who should not have the faults of men, but should be as an innocent lamb, who should take away the sins of the world, and by his innocence do away the guilt of all that believe in him; of whom David says, « Comely in form above the sons of men: grace is poured abroad in thy lips [n: (Psal. XLIV, 3)]; » of whom Isaias says, « Who is this that cometh from Edom, with dyed garments from Bozra? » [n: (Isa. LXIII, 1.)] There follows:

11. *His head is the finest gold. His locks are as [1278D] the topmost boughs of palm trees, black as a raven.*

The commendation begins from his head, which is compared to gold. For as the finest gold is more precious than all things desirable; so also God is found more precious than all things which can be coveted and desired. « His locks are as the topmost boughs of palm trees. » The topmost boughs of palm trees are the higher parts in the palm; and these bore before them the sign of victory; for the victors carrying them declared that they had obtained, as it were in war, the uppermost part, that is, the victory. His locks therefore, cleaving to the head and affording adornment to the head, are the faithful, obedient to God in all things, keeping his commandments, and fulfilling in deed what they [1279A] preach in word, and thus rejoicing that in the battle of this life they have obtained the victory of the palm. There follows, « Black as a raven, » because these, although they advance in virtue, protest that they are black as ravens. There follows:

12. *His eyes as doves upon brooks of waters, which are washed with milk, and sit beside the fullest streams.*

That is, spiritual men, who are clear-sighted and keen to see his mysteries, are « as *colombae* which are washed with milk. » The milk indeed wherewith these must be washed is the reasonable sense without guile; whence it is said, « As newborn babes, desire the rational milk without guile. » [n: (I Petr. II, 2.)] « And [1279B] they sit beside the fullest streams, » that is, beside the fullest writings of the ancient Fathers, wherein nothing but truth is taught, and simplicity is commended in all things. There follows:

13. *His cheeks are as beds of aromatical spices set by the perfumers.*

The use of the cheeks indeed is manifold; for these receive food, grind it, and pass it into the body. This also is the place of shamefastness, to which the blood comes forth, when a man is ashamed that he has said or done something less justly. By these are formed the words which teach Christ, and show beforehand that we ought to believe in him, and to follow him in heart and in work. But the « beds » which « by the perfumers, » namely, by those who discern the [1279C] powers of the perfumes, « are set, » are the Scriptures of the Prophets and of the doctors, which when we take up, when we pass them into our hearts, when we utter them to eager hearers, what are we made but the cheeks of the body of Christ, taking heed in all things to this, that we say or do nothing at which shamefastness should blush, and that we fulfil these things in faith and in work, whereby the care of the hearers toward Christ may grow, and their understanding. There follows:

*His lips are lilies dropping the first myrrh.*

Which is, These are rightly to be called his lips, who are as lilies dropping the first myrrh. The heavenly lips indeed rightly dropping to God the sweetness of odour, these are the hosts of Angels, which were created with the first hosts [1279D] by the supernal Power, which is God, and, when those sinned and willed to reign with power equal to God's, they *expreverunt* it, and, devoting themselves to God, by whom they were created, in all humility and obedience, they trampled under the feet of their mind pride and disobedience, which were the first sins. This indeed was the first myrrh, because that was the first mortification of sin; and all who in that manner persuade men to mortify sins are rightly called the lips of God, and lips persuading that which is dear to his heart. There follows:

14. *His hands are turned and golden, full of hyacinths.*



## 5. (cont.)

[1280A]That by his hands works ought rightly to be understood, is manifest. For since nothing is understood to be more precious than gold, nothing dearer, rightly are his hands golden, that is, understood to be more precious than every work. For what works could be compared with these? He made the deaf to hear, the dumb to speak, he restored sight to the blind, he cured the paralytic, he cleansed the lepers, he raised the dead, he commanded the sea and the winds, he put the demons to flight, he raised also himself when already dead, he showed himself to his own after death, and thus, a manifest victor, ascended into heaven. Nothing is dearer than these, nothing more precious; and these indeed are rightly called turned as well. Among workings no turning is easier: for he who works by the lathe sits, and whatever unshaped matter there is, he so composes it by the lathe, so unfolds it, that what now [1280B] was rough and unshaped, this, being now smoothed, he renders into a most fitting form: to him a leper was offered, and by one word was cured; a dead man was offered, and, cured by one word, went away alive. Rightly therefore are his hands understood « turned, » that is, curing lightly, which so work all things by a word alone, that they are now perceived to be better than they had been. Whence also it is added, « full of hyacinths. » For the hyacinth is a stone whose colour is that of the sky when it is clear. Therefore, that thou mayest understand all these things to be of heaven, and mayest not doubt of the power of heaven upon earth, there is joined, « full of hyacinths, » that is, full of heavenly good works. With this agrees that which follows:

*His belly is of ivory, set with sapphires.*

The belly is that part of our body which more lightly [1280C] receives injury; whence here it signifies that nature of the Lord, the human, namely, wherein he could be hurt, wherein he could be made weak, wherein he could also be afflicted with the cross, as it was said of him, « Truly he hath borne our infirmities, and carried our sorrows [n: (Isa. LIII, 4)]. » Which belly nevertheless is here called « of ivory, » that the firmness of it, which was taken up out of the passion, might be shown; for ivory is most solid, most white, and, as time comes on, from white becomes ruddy. All which things indeed befit Christ; for his whiteness of chastity stood forth incomparable, to which none among the sons of men could be made equal, the Psalmist bearing witness, who says, « Fair in form above the sons of men [n: (Psal. XLIV, 3)]. » There is in it also a firmness which could not [1280D] be corrupted; whence, when his soul descended to the lower regions, it could not itself be hindered, because there was in it no sin whereby it might be held: his flesh also in the sepulchre after death suffered nothing, because on either side sin was absent, whereby both corruption and a charge against the soul might be added. Whence the Psalmist, « Thou wilt not leave my soul in hell; nor wilt thou give thy holy one to see corruption [n: (Psal. XV, 10)]. » But that this is made ruddy after a long time notes the martyrdom of the passion, since they who imitate him have after a long time delivered up their bodies to punishment, and have now deserved to live with him for ever. Which indeed is set with sapphires, that is, adorned [1281A] with the heavenly martyrdoms of these. There follows:

15. *His legs are pillars of marble, which are founded upon bases of gold.*

This is rightly fitted to the preachers, who run through the world, who lift up the name of Christ, being rightly upright and solid, and in all their preaching confirmed upon bases of gold, that is, upon the sayings of the prophets. As it begins from gold, so it is consummated in gold, that thou, prudent reader, mayest have what thou shouldst truly understand concerning Christ. There follows:

*His fairness is as of Libanus, chosen as the cedars.*

Libanus is a great wood, beginning near the Jordan, and stretching itself on high, in length also as far as the Eastern Ocean: its chief [1281B] trees are the cypress and the cedar, whose whitening leaves furnish the name of the wood, so that it is called Libanus; for Libanus is, « a whitening. » In it the dwellings are fair and delightful, so that if any come into them, he has no care of returning, nor is delighted with dwelling among men. This fairness is attributed to God, who is the whiteness of souls, and the delight of all the virtues, unto whom he that has come has no will of returning to human things, no remembrance in his soul of things past. Wherefore? because he himself is « chosen as the cedars. » For the cedars are trees of Libanus, raised upward in length, wonderfully and nobly tressed with foliage, incorruptible, odour-bearing, not fearing the gnawings of worms. He that has come to this glory, it is enough for him to be honoured [1281C] by this glory itself. There follows:

16. *His throat most sweet, and he wholly desirable: such is my Beloved, and he is my friend, ye daughters of Jerusalem.*

What is designated by this throat, unless the sending forth of the New Testament? For the Old went before, wherein it was said: « A man shall render soul for soul, eye for eye, tooth for tooth [n: (Exod. XXI, 24)], » and the rest which follow. [var: Vulg. *reddet animam pro anima*, without a subject "man"] But in the New what has been said? « If any man shall strike thee on the one cheek, offer him the other cheek: and to him that will contend with thee in judgment, and take away thy coat, let go to him thy cloak also. And whosoever shall force thee one mile, go with him other two. Give to him that asketh of thee [n: (Matth. V, 39, 40, 41)]. » [var: Vulg. *in dexteram maxillam tuam, praebe illi et alteram*, "on thy right cheek"] What is sweeter than this? what [1281D] can be more delightful? But if thou consider the whole New Testament, thou wilt find nothing at all but what is sweet and desirable. These things being said, there follows, « Such is my Beloved, and he is my friend, ye daughters of Jerusalem. » There follows:

17. *Whither is thy Beloved gone, O most beautiful of women? whither has thy Beloved turned aside, and we will seek him with thee?*

The proclamation of this Beloved being heard, wherein his Bride commends him through his several members, the daughters of Jerusalem also account him worthy of commendation, and esteem that it will be worthy that a message of love be presented to him. [1282A] Whence, lest they should run in uncertainty, they inquire of the Bride in this manner, « Whither is thy Beloved gone? » and because « to go away » can be understood as to withdraw far off (which indeed would seem a wonder, since she is this most beautiful of women), therefore, correcting themselves, they redouble it, « Whither has thy Beloved turned aside, » that is, whither has he departed for a time? This is perchance thy business, that he may perchance convert some and join them to thee, and cause thee to be enlarged and to be known there where thou art not yet. And when we have learned this, we will seek him with thee, and will take care to offer him the gift of thy love. There follows:



## CHAPTER VI.

1. *My Beloved is gone down into his garden, to the bed of aromatical spices, to feed in the gardens, and to gather lilies.*

[1282B]This garden of the Beloved is the blessed multitude of the faithful, opening to him the whole bosom of its mind, loving and contemplating him with its whole strength, embracing him with a fuller affection of the heart, so that it accounts it gain to die even for him, and does not scorn to endure torments for his sake. In this number thou shalt reckon Abel, who first received the Lord by his own death, being alike virgin and martyr, confessing in himself the priest and the victim, himself alike priest and victim, alike virgin and martyr, he fell, victim and priest. Thou shalt number also Enoch, whom God took from the world; and because he walked with him, he deserved to have life in himself. Noah also, whom, the world being destroyed, God saved in the wood. Abraham also, and his noble offspring. Moses himself also, who, manifestly [1282C] confessing the Lord, says, « I will raise up to you a prophet from the midst of your brethren, like unto thee; and I will put my words in his mouth, and he shall speak to them all the words which I shall command him. But he that will not hear his words, which he shall speak in my name, I will be the avenger [n: (Deut. XVIII, 18, 19)]. » [var: Vulg. *suscitabo eis de medio fratrum suorum*, "unto them, from the midst of their brethren"] In the midst of these thou shalt behold the blessed and highest of virgins, the hope of the ages, Mary, in the bed of aromatical spices, trusting with John her holy guardian, having on the right of this aromatic bed, standing by her, Peter and Paul, Andrew and James, and all the rest, who, bearing witness to him by their own blood, gave forth to the world a most sweet odour, and by their own death brought back the dead to life. This [1282D] indeed is the bed of aromatical spices, which thou understandest with the mind more than thou couldst be taught by word, which by its own blood, and by pains that cannot be told out, deserved to confess Christ. Here the balsams grow fragrant, the pigments give forth their scent, and all the incenses sprinkle abroad a most sweet odour. Hither at times the Beloved turns aside, that he may feed in these gardens, that is, may be refreshed by the glorious testimony of all these, and may gather these true lilies, truly worthy of his fellowship, that is, may establish them in the highest peace and blessedness. Which Church assuredly the Lord is said sometimes to visit apart, that the palm of martyrdom in men of either sex may be understood to have deserved of him something far beyond [1283A] the rest; but that the remainder, the universal Church, scattered far and wide through the world, may be observed to be far greater in number, and less in merit. There follows:

2. *I to my Beloved, and my Beloved to me, who feedeth among the lilies.*

This is the voice of the Bride, who says that she and her Beloved exchange soliloquies, she with him and he with her; which, as they are hidden, so we let them go, nor do we in any manner communicate them to others. There follows:

3. *Thou art beautiful, my friend, sweet and comely as Jerusalem: terrible as an ordered line of battle of the camp.*

These things indeed have already been said in part: for what occurs concerning the beauty of this Bride, that has been recorded. [1283B] It remains that we say also of her sweetness that which the Lord shall teach. That sweetness is not bodily, delighting and alluring some by touch, but rather of the spirit; whence it was said to the Lord also, « Thou, O Lord, art sweet and mild [n: (Psal. LXXXV, 5)]. » So also is she sweet, who, being hurt, murmurs not again, and patiently bears what is brought upon her; whence she obtains the comeliness of patience, like that heavenly Jerusalem, to whom on earth no peace was given, and who, being now taken up into heaven, rests in a great fulness of peace; because « she is terrible as an ordered line of battle of the camp. » For an ordered line of battle of the camp is when they who are about to fight, their whole strength being gathered, stand armed in a fitting place, and have on this side and on that, on the right and on the left, the massed lines of fighters, [1283C] which withstand those that come, and by the strength of arms break their onrushing assaults before it comes to the battle; which indeed strikes terror into the enemy, and therefore rests in the highest peace. There follows:

4. *Turn away thy eyes from me, for they have made me fly away.*

Behold, the Bridegroom has recorded that his Bride is beautiful, sweet and comely, as one who is pleasing to the Bridegroom; her also terrible, so that she fears no encounter of an opposing enemy, so that she stands secure; her therefore, contemplating him in great part, desiring now and now to enter into the secret places of the Godhead, and hoping now to have the fulness of glory which the Lord promises to his own, he beautifully rebukes, [1283D] saying, « Turn away thy eyes from me, » that is, seek not to lay hold of the beauty of my sublime glory; « for they have made me fly away, » that is, to ascend beyond the sense of men, and to show myself as yet incomprehensible to man, because neither hath eye seen, nor ear heard, nor hath it ascended into the heart of man, to will [cj: *velle*; read *nolle*, "not to will"] to show it to any one before the common resurrection of men, and their conjoining with their own spirits. There follows:

*Thy hair is as a flock of goats, which have appeared from Galaad. 5. Thy teeth as a flock of sheep which have come up from the washing, all with twin fruits, and there is none barren among them. 6. As the rind of a pomegranate, so are thy cheeks, besides that which lieth hid within thee.*

These same things have been said above, and expounded; which [1284A] to say again is superfluous and burdensome. There follows:

*There are sixty queens, and eighty concubines, and of young maidens there is no number.*

This also, because, the Lord teaching us, we have explained above, we forbear to explain again, and turn our attention to those things which follow. There follows:

8. *One is my dove, my perfect one, one is she of her mother, the chosen of her that bore her.*

Which indeed is a reciprocal praise. For the Bridegroom commends with worthy praise the Bride who praises him, saying, « One is my dove. » In what manner she is called a dove, by what reason she is understood to be perfect, thou shalt observe from those things which have been said above. But she is here called one, because, as out of many grains one heap, out of many berries one bunch, [1284B] out of a multitude of cattle one flock is fitly called one; so also this one Church, one Bride of God, is understood out of the multitude of all the souls which have been called, or shall be called, to God in the unity of faith, which are strengthened in the unity of hope, which are already established, or shall be established, unto God in charity not feigned. And though she be one, yet is she « one » « of her mother, » that is, one after the likeness of her mother, namely the heavenly Jerusalem, which the Bridegroom asserts to be the mother of this one; and as that consists of a multitude of heavenly spirits, and, joined to God in one charity, is one, so he understands this one to be gathered out of an infinite multitude of souls and to be one, and in this to be like and conformed to her mother, in that she in no wise separates herself from God, [1284C] but binds herself with firm charity to him whom she loves. Whence there follows, « The chosen of her that bore her, » that is, chosen after the manner of her that bore her. But this bearer is the same as is also her mother: yet by the diversity of the words he denotes diverse things; naming her mother, because she consists of a diversity of spirits, and is one, and this one, gathered out of a diversity of souls, likewise stands fast in unity: but her that bore her, because God, the reprobate part of the spirits being condemned, established and confirmed her chosen unto himself; and in like manner the Church, the reprobate multitude of men being condemned, he chose unto himself, and appointed her to be a partaker of his own glory with him. There follows:

*The daughters saw her, and declared her most blessed: the queens and the concubines, and they praised her.*

[1284D]It has been said that the heavenly Jerusalem, the Church, namely, which consists of blessed spirits, is the mother of the Church which consists of souls, and is from the earth; and that this one has been chosen by God unto glory after the likeness of that one. Whence, if it be asked by what testimony this is true, or by what reason it is proved, this answer is added, Because the daughters of Sion saw her, that heavenly Jerusalem, namely, which is the mother of this Church which is from the earth, and which has been taken up by God, and chosen unto glory; the queens saw her, the concubines also saw her, and declared her most blessed, contemplating her with the eyes of the heart, those most blessed spirits, some indeed standing by the majesty and privy to its secrets, others most dutifully ministering, others laboriously running through the regions of the world, [1285A] and announcing the will of God in all things and through all things, and fulfilling it in effect. Her the « queens, » which is discerned to be the highest order in the Church, and the « concubines, » which is next after them, beholding together with the daughters of Sion, truly declared most blessed, and commended as worthy of all praise. And this testimony of the whole Church which is on earth, there is none who denies it to be true, none who rightly contradicts it. There follows:

9. *Who is she that goeth forth as the dawn rising, beautiful as the moon, chosen as the sun, terrible as an ordered line of battle of the camp?*

By whatever love a man is drawn, to that he is quickly turned, to that he is called back in mind. The Bridegroom therefore, conscious [1285B] of his own love, and not ignorant how great admiration the Bride whom he has chosen is worthy of, that he may show her admirable to all, turning to her, says, « Who is she that goeth forth [sic: *progeditur*] as the dawn rising? » It is called *aurora*, as it were *hora aurea*, the golden hour, which, going before the Sun, announces that the sun is about to come, and, glowing red after the manner of gold, shows beforehand that an end is about to come to the darkness: and who knows not that this existed in the primitive Church; who, scripture revealing it, is not ignorant that those first fathers went before the rising of Christ, who is the true Sun of justice, and went before him by their own testimony? For who would doubt that just Abel fell by his brother's sword, and by his own passion showed forth the passion of Christ; that Enoch was translated by the Lord; that Noah, when the deluge was washing the world away, was saved by wood; that Abraham also [1285C] prefigured the Lord in many things? These assuredly, and all the Fathers of that testament, stood forth as the dawn, in whom the rising Church showed beforehand that the true Sun, who is Christ, was to be born, and that she was to be more fully illumined by him. But when the Lord was born, that came to pass which is written, « He came unto his own, and his own received him not. But as many as received him, he gave them power to be made the sons of God [n: (Joan. I, 11, 12)]. » Who, being made sons of God, and illumined by his light, shone indeed as the moon, not with their own light, but with a light not their own: whence it is added, « Beautiful as the moon. » For the moon receives from the sun that whence it shines, and floods the world with a fuller light than the dawn: so the rising Church, [1285D] first as the dawn, then as the moon, but thirdly as the sun, being chosen, ministered full light to the world.



## CHAPTER VI. (cont.)

For, the wonders having been run through which came to pass at the birth of Christ, and the miracles being finished which bore witness to Christ preaching, and the signs being seen which appeared in his death and after his death, the Church powerfully arose: in the first fathers, the dawn; in the second, as the moon; but now, refreshed with full light, she appeared as the sun, chosen, radiant with full light, and furnishing light to all by her own brightness; wherein it is said, « Chosen as the sun. » For of all the creatures which were bodily created by God, none has been chosen for this, that it should be the eye of the world, and furnish light and illumination to the world on every side, save the substance of the sun alone, which alone [1286A] goes about us, warms us, and increases and fortifies us with benefits. In this manner also has this Bride been chosen, that she might furnish spiritual light to the world, and, the darkness being taken away wherein the world was wrapped, might bestow and confer upon it the light of faith through the apostles, and the holy martyrs, and all the doctors of the faith. Who, when she shall come together whole after the resurrection, will appear terrible as an ordered line of battle of the camp. For when the Lord sits in that day, and pronounces the sentence of judgment: « Come, ye blessed of my Father; possess the kingdom which has been prepared for you from the origin of the world [n: (Matth. XXV, 34)], » [var: Vulg. *possidete paratum vobis regnum a constitutione mundi*, "from the foundation of the world"] so terrible and admirable will she appear, that she gives no entrance to the enemy that approaches her; but, like a line of battle ordered by the hand of the Lord, keeps her own to herself, and by the power of her Lord drives off all strangers. [1286B] Yet blessed Gregory says upon this, « The dawn announces that the night has perished, nor yet does it display the brightness of the day; but while it drives away the one, it takes up the other, and holds a light mingled with darkness. » Therefore he who in this life follows the truth is the dawn, because he does certain things which are of the light, yet is not without the leavings of darkness. When therefore the law of sin contends with the law of the mind, it is still the dawn, because the light which has shone forth has not yet wholly pressed down the darkness, and because the light of the flesh [cj: *lux carnis*; read *lex carnis*, "the law of the flesh"] strikes the law of the mind, and on the contrary light and shadow strive with one another by turns: it will be day when the shadow of sin shall not remain, when the inward light shall have shone perfectly. Therefore the Church whom the Bridegroom admires is deservedly compared to the dawn, because, forsaking the [1286C] darkness of sin, she breaks forth into the light of eternity, and by the knowledge of faith is changed into the light of justice, as the dawn is turned from darkness into light; faith is light, unbelief is night. Therefore the Church, seeking eternal things, glows with the supernal brightness and with the light of justice, and rises as the dawn, because, having left behind the depravity of the world, she turns herself into the splendour of a new light. There follows:

10. *I went down into the garden of nuts, to see the apples of the valleys, and to look whether the vineyard had flourished, and the pomegranates had budded.*

The Bridegroom, having above addressed the whole Church, now turns to that part of her which is here, which is subject to temptation; which, while the rain descends, the floods come, the winds arise and beat upon her house, [1286D] needs help so much the stronger as it is shaken by the more frequent whirlwind. Coming down therefore to her, he says, « I went down into the garden of nuts, » and so forth. But a garden (as has been said) is a place set apart, planted with health-bearing herbs and wholesome trees, which after labours it is sweet to visit and to be refreshed in spirit. Which place indeed designates the Church that is set here, and how she is set apart Isaias designates, saying, « As the heavens are exalted above the earth, so are my ways exalted above your ways, and my thoughts above your thoughts [n: (Isa. LV, 9)]. » By these ways and thoughts he designates the thoughts and works of the Church, which is as far removed from every intention of the wicked as the heaven is distant from the earth. But this garden is planted [1287A] with trees of nuts; which indeed are diverse. For one nut is the hazel, another the filbert, another the chestnut, another the almond, another the pine-nut, another the Libyan, which is called the nutmeg, another the Gallic, which in the common tongue is wont to be called simply the nut. What do these nuts signify? The hearts, assuredly, of the saints, which indeed within display the sweetness of the kernel, that is, the profit of the spirit; but without they show the profit of the flesh, which is so hard that of it it was said, « We have this treasure hidden in earthen vessels [n: (II Cor. IV, 7)]. » [var: Vulg. *habemus autem thesaurum istum*, without "hidden"] And again of it it was said, « I know that neither death, nor life, nor angels, nor principalities, nor any other creature shall be able to separate us from the charity of God, which is in Christ Jesus our Lord [n: (Rom. VIII, 38)]. » [var: Vulg. *Certus sum enim quia*, "I am sure that"] To [1287B] this garden of nuts therefore he goes down, that he may strengthen their kernels within, and subject the bodies of the same without to suffering, laying up for it the rewards of life, which shall be lacking to none that dies for him. He goes down also that he may see the apples of the valleys; for that garden is not planted with nuts alone, but rather with trees which bear apples. For all fruits which are covered with a soft rind are called apples, and these signify the confessors, who, burning within with the love of God, and confessing him without, receive their reward from God in eternal blessedness, although they are not made equal to the martyrs. To these therefore he goes down, who follow after him in the sublimity of martyrdom, and to these who, abiding in the valley of humility, are likewise not slow to follow after him by death: but the Lord spares them, [1287C] willing that they receive eternal blessedness by their will alone, and not also by a death of their own procuring. He goes down also to the newly planted vineyards, that he may look whether they flourish, whether they give of themselves the hope of fruit, whether they persevere in faith, and, an example of approaching, of believing, and of persevering being set before others, enlarge his name: which same thing he desires concerning the pomegranates, namely, that they bud, and by their budding afford to others examples of bravely undergoing suffering for Christ, and pledge the rewards of that recompense which they look for. There follows:

11. *I knew not: my soul troubled me because of the chariots of Aminadab.*

The Lord in the foregoing words admonished [1287D] his Bride, made known to her what she should seek, and set himself before her as the end of her course; for in vain do we run, if we do not lay hold, if we do not find him whom we seek. But hard things have been set before her, at which the flesh is afraid, at which it trembles; but for these the spirit is strengthened and girded by the gift of God, accounting light all that it suffers, while it glories in the gift of its own recompense. Which indeed the Synagogue hearing, stands, is amazed, and trembles, and, returning at length to herself, says, « I knew not, » that is, I was ignorant of so great grace and of the gifts of the spirit conferred upon thee; and therefore, « my soul troubled me, » because I wandered astray in darkness, « because of the chariots of Aminadab. » This Aminadab is read to have been the great-great-grandson of Juda [n: (I Par.
