II, 10)], who by the interpretation of his own name signifies Christ; for Aminadab [1288A] is interpreted, « The willing one of my people; » for he himself stood forth as the head of the whole people, at whose birth the Angel said to the shepherds: « Fear not; for behold I bring you good tidings of great joy, that shall be to all the people: for this day is born to you a Saviour, who is Christ the Lord [n: (Luc. II, 10, 11)]. » But he stood forth as the willing one of that people; for willingly was he born, willingly shown forth by angels and by great powers, willingly adorned with miracles, willingly betrayed, willingly given unto death; and since he bore all these things willingly, rightly is he named the willing one both of the people and of him. Whose chariots indeed are the four Gospels, which are drawn through the world, while they are preached to the peoples, and are believed, and are received as worthy of all veneration. Beholding which [1288B] the Synagogue is troubled, despairing that that can be false which is thus received in the heart by all, is magnificently honoured, and is crushed out neither by exquisite torments nor by any kind of death. But Saint Ambrose notes two chariots. For anger, cupidity, pleasure, and fear he makes the wheels of one chariot; but to the other he assigns prudence, temperance, fortitude, and justice: in these wheels there is then the spirit of life, when a good driver guides them, restraining these, admonishing these, recalling these, urging these on. These horses indeed are subject to the yoke of faith, bound fast with the bond of charity, with the bridles of justice, with the reins of sobriety. There follows:

12. *Return, return, O Sulamitess: return, return, that we may behold thee.*

[1288C]The Synagogue confessing her slowness, the Church answers by way of consolation, « Return, » and so forth. It is said to the Sulamitess that she return, because at the end of the world faith will be offered by the Church to the Synagogue, which was held captive under unbelief. And she is admonished four times to return, because the Jews, scattered through the four parts of the world, will be gathered together at the end; whence it is said to her, « Return, return, O Sulamitess, » thou who wast held captive or despised under the prophets, « return, return, » that in that liberty wherein we are we may « behold thee. » There follows:



## CHAPTER VII.

1. *What shalt thou see in the Sulamitess, but the companies of the camps?*

This is the voice of the Bridegroom. Behold, the Church admonishing the Synagogue, [1288D] the Bridegroom, rejoicing, promises that he will give the effect, saying, « What shalt thou see in the Sulamitess, but the companies of the camps? » For the companies of the camps are they who, set as it were in camps in the array of the virtues, fight against the devil, and, God fortifying them and bringing help by his own power, overcome. For then shall the camps of them that war be seen in the Sulamitess, when she shall do battle for the faith which now she assails, and, when Henoch and Elias preach the same, asserting it to be true and to be that wherein one must be saved, she shall bear witness to it by her own death. There follows:

2. *How beautiful are thy steps in shoes, O daughter of the Prince!*

Behold, the Bridegroom praises the Bride, beginning from her steps: whence also, commending her, he says: « How [1289A] beautiful are thy steps in shoes, O daughter of the Prince! » Her indeed, who in her first fathers stood forth as the « daughter of the Prince, » that is, of God who has princedom over all things, he commends with a twelvefold praise, beginning from her steps, advancing through the middle parts, and consummating her praise with the fruitfulness of her breasts; and so he says, O thou who now in thy conversation art worthy to be called the daughter of the Prince, who also in thy first fathers didst worthily deserve to be called his daughter, « how beautiful » now « are thy steps! » For now thou drawest near to God by those same steps by which the apostles drew near; confessing that the Son of God was sent by the Father into flesh, and that he in that same flesh underwent death for the salvation of all, and after the third day rose again, and thus, the victory being won, ascended into heaven, thou standest for him, thou bearest [1289B] witness to him, thou dost not refuse to die for him. Exceedingly beautiful therefore are thy steps, wherewith thou walkest unto God; and this, « in shoes. » For shoes are made of the hides of dead animals; whence by these are understood the examples of the saints: of the Fathers, namely, of the apostles, and of all the martyrs, because, in confessing Christ, they endured that their hides be drawn off them, and their bodies be withdrawn from their souls. To these therefore thou art made equal, to these thou art made a peer, who, having received a sure faith, dost fight for it, and dost labour to be crowned with the glory of the apostles by contending for it. There follows:

*The joints of thy thighs are as* monilia, *which are wrought by the hand of a craftsman.*

[1289C]The thighs, whether called *femina* or *femora*, wherewith we walk and frame our goings forth, are these two, namely the Old Testament and the New: the joining of these is the concord of the Testaments, whereof he says, « The joining of thy thighs is as *monilia*. » *Monilia* indeed are so called from *monere*, to admonish, in that, when they are hung upon the neck and set upon the breast, they admonish her that wears them not to let a stranger's hand come to her bosom, but to keep her faith undefiled to him to whom she is betrothed. They admonish any man also, that where he sees these hung, he be not drawn thither by love, nor by any chance be carried thither by love, lest perchance, being taken in this, he be held guilty of adultery. Whence the two Testaments are deservedly named *monilia*, which admonish us to fear God, and to venerate him, and to love him with the whole [1289D] heart, the whole soul, and all our strength, and to meditate upon him with a firm mind. They admonish also that we love our neighbours, and contrive not nor do to them anything which ought rightly to displease. In these the two Testaments are, and are in concord, which also so strengthen faith as it truly is; and they confirm it to be even as we believe. These also « are wrought by the hand of a craftsman, » that is, through the Son, who is the hand of the Father, and the highest craftsman of all, they were declared to men and written down. There follows:

3. *Thy navel is a turned bowl, never wanting cups.*

The navel indeed is in the middle of the belly, having a certain [1290A] part of it beneath itself, and a certain part above. But this navel is the infirmity of our mortality, one part whereof is below, and the other above. That part is below whereof it is said: « All flesh is grass, and all the glory thereof as the flower of the field. The grass is dried up, and the flower is fallen [n: (Isa. XL, 6, 7)]. » Whereof David says: « In the morning let him pass away as grass; in the morning let him flourish, and pass away: in the evening let him fall, grow hard, and wither [n: (Psal. LXXXIX, 6)]. » The morning also of our infancy, of our childhood, of our youth passes away; the flower of our young manhood passes away; the evening also of our old age passes away: wherein indeed a man falls down in death, grows hard in the corpse, withers in the dust. But that part is above whereof blessed Paul makes mention, saying: « It is sown in [1290B] corruption, it shall rise in incorruption: it is sown in dishonour, it shall rise in glory: it is sown in weakness, it shall rise in power: it is sown a natural body, it shall rise spiritual [n: (I Cor. XV, 42, 43)]. » This navel therefore is as a turned bowl: for that working is lightest which is done by the lathe; and lightest is the working of God, which shall be wrought in the resurrection of all. For as an angel most lightly lifted up Habacuc, and set him in Babylon; so, carrying food to Daniel, he set him back again in Judea [n: (Dan. XIV, 35)]: so shall it be in the resurrection, when at the voice of the angel, at the sound of the trumpet blowing, the dead shall rise again forthwith incorrupt, who indeed were worthy of corruption. This therefore is that part of our infirmity which shall be lightly wrought by the lathe of the divine working, which is called a turned bowl, which [1290C] neither here wants cups, nor in the time to come shall in any measure want them. Here therefore their glory is said to be about to be great, which shall then also be seen bodily, and shall appear. There follows:

*Thy belly is as a heap of wheat, set about with lilies.*

The belly is that part of the body which receives whatever is put into it; and whatever in it can work together for its substance, that it retains, and therewith cherishes the body, increases and advances it, but drives off and spits out the rest. Whence by this is understood the holy memory of this synagogue, which comprehends all things, and these only which are holy, and which work together unto eternal life, and direct the steps thither, these she loves, these she keeps, and in these she goes forward: the rest, as vain and unprofitable, she spits out and [1290D] sends away. Whence also it is said: « Thy belly is as a heap of wheat. » For in a heap of wheat some things work together unto life, and some are otherwise: but of those which work together unto life it is subjoined, that this heap « is set about with lilies, » that is, is trained by the examples of the Fathers, and is taught by the instructions of the heavenly spirits, who are truly lilies. There follows:

4. *Thy two breasts, as two twin fawns of a roe. 5. Thy neck as a tower of ivory.*

This is understood of the breasts of the converted synagogue, as it was said above also of the breasts of the Church; as also that which follows concerning the neck, « Thy neck as a tower of ivory: » yet here is added, « of ivory, » [1291A] that both the brightness of their preaching may be shown, and the firmness wherewith they truly preach Christ, and confirm this by their own death. There follows:

*Thy eyes as the fishpools in Hesebon, which are in the gate of the daughter of the multitude.*

The neck being shown, we come to the eyes; of which it is here said, that they are « as the fishpools in Hesebon. » But Hesebon is interpreted, « a girdle of mourning. » But the « eyes, » that is, they who contemplate the secret things of faith, and show them to the synagogue now converted, to be seen and to be believed, these are « as the fishpools in Hesebon: » for Hesebon, whether it be a fortress or a city, had at its very entrance fishpools, wherein the bodies of those entering could be washed and cleansed, so that trust might thus be had in those who had gone in, [1291B] that they would contrive no evil which should be against the place, or in any measure hurtful to it. So also these, who prepare and show the ways of life to this converted synagogue, provide beforehand and show the way of life to others who are to be converted to her, and that they enter not feignedly, that they receive not Baptism in vain, nor contrive in guile to set themselves against those in whom Christ now reigns, and now holds his own princedom. This therefore is that which is said, « which are in the gate of the daughter of the multitude, » that is, at the entrance of the fortress or the city, where there is a multitude of nations, to whom the word of God is now preached, and they receive it unto ages of ages. For that Simon Magus did this feignedly, feignedly received Baptism, feignedly came to know the sacraments of faith, with all which he afterward sharply [1291C] assailed Peter, the doctor of the Church appointed by Christ. Whence, lest a like assault disquiet her in her conversion, when it shall be given, it is said to her that she shall have eyes providing beforehand for herself, as at the gates of Hesebon provision was made beforehand for the city. But this multitude is rightly called « a girdle of mourning, » because on every side it is girded with mourning. On this side she mourns that she has sinned against God; on that side, lest she sin, she likewise has tears of fear; on this side she complains that he whom she loves is lacking to her, and that the rewards which she seeks and desires are lacking to her. Girded therefore with this mourning, she is rightly called Hesebon, who, surrounded with these and with other tears which compunction gives, rightly makes her complaint. There follows:

*Thy nose is as the tower of Libanus, which looks toward Damascus.*

[1291D]To the nose are assigned those of the Church who perceive near at hand the fragrance of the virtues in the Church, and who know the stench of the vices in her by discerning it from afar. These stand as a tower in Libanus, which designates the whiteness of the Church, who in the Church of God receive before others the name and office of prelacy, and who look toward Damascus. For Damascus is the mother city of Syria, where Cain is read to have slain Abel; whence many evils were brought upon Israel, where unbelief reigns, and sin rages exceedingly. Against this the Church of God looks earnestly, attentively considering whether there be any whom she may snatch away for herself, and, when he has been snatched from the devil, may be able to turn to Christ. There follows:

5. *Thy head as Carmel: and the hairs of thy head as the purple of the King bound in the channels.*



## CHAPTER VII. (cont.)

[1292A]Carmel is a mountain, going up into which Elias obtained that the rain, shut up for seven years, should be given him abundantly by God, signifying assuredly Christ, whom confessing, and asserting the things which are said of him to be true, we pray to the Father that he send forth upon us the gifts of his graces, whereby he may make us pleasing to his will. But this Carmel is interpreted « a mountain, » or, « the knowledge of circumcision; » which rightly befits Christ, for he himself stood forth as the beginning and the end of circumcision. For he himself enjoined this upon Abraham (who saw his day and was glad) and upon his house, and by the austere cutting of this member forbade the intercourse unlawful to him, and all other dishonourable and superfluous things of every member. He also took this upon himself, and foretold that it was consummated [1292B] in himself: for the shedding of his blood, as well upon the cross as in this circumcision, is a sufficient correction for the people of grace, that they abstain from vices, and correct and amend their depraved motions, not by that austerity of the sacrament, but by the receiving of faith alone and of holy baptism. But the hairs of this head are they of whom it is subjoined: « The hairs of thy head as the purple of the King bound in the channels. » The hairs of the head indeed are those which adorn the head, make the face comely, and, ever cleaving to the head, by the great fairness of themselves render it honourable to whomsoever meets it, and most worthy of all veneration. By these indeed we understand those who, cleaving with the whole heart to Christ the highest King in an indissoluble bond of love, could never be divided from him, neither by princedom, nor by any power that wrought it; [1292C] but through all the torments that could be devised deserved truly to cleave to him, and to be called inseparable hairs. And the hairs of the head indeed are as the purple of the King bound in the channels. The channels indeed are the sendings forth of the graces of God, which in many manners he [sends] into his own whom he chooses, and he himself both makes them worthy of the grace of martyrdom and takes them up. And so these hairs are in these channels, and are indeed joined to them, not knowing how to be sundered from them, but rather, some heavenly liquor being received in these, they become ruddy as the purple of the King bound in the channels, conformed indeed to Christ who suffered upon the cross, and now conformed to him in glory. There follows:

6. *How beautiful art thou, and how comely, dearest, [1292D] in delights!*

After the commendation of those things which have been said, he subjoins the praise of the synagogue, which has now been made the Church, saying, Since the things which have now been said stand thus, therefore thou art exceedingly « beautiful » in the work of virtue, and « comely » in the contemplation of the same; dear to us through thy conversion, dearer through the carrying out of virtue, « dearest » to God at thy arrival; whence he subjoins, « in delights, » that is, in those things which have been said, which God declares to be delights, and in these, as in delights, he takes his pleasure. There follows:

7. *Thy stature is likened to a palm tree, and thy breasts to clusters.*

The commendation of her began from her steps; the joining of her thighs also, her navel, and her belly have been commended; [1293A] her breasts, and her neck, her nose, and her eyes, together with her head and her hairs, have been described. Lest this should seem ill-sounding, and out of tune with her stature as too much drawn together into itself, he adds concerning her stature, saying that it is likened to a palm tree, stretched upward, namely, narrow and rough below, spread out and widened toward heaven with its arms, bearing a most sweet fruit, which, joined to the clusters which are the fruit of the vine, renders the supper rich, and the supper itself most delightful by the fruit of the vine. There follows:

8. *I said: I will go up into the palm tree, and will take hold of the fruits thereof: and thy breasts shall be as the clusters of the vine, and the odour of thy mouth as of apples.*

This indeed the Bridegroom says, that he himself will go up into the palm tree; which indeed we believe to have been fulfilled, [1293B] when he went up upon the wood of the cross. To which indeed this also was granted, that as he himself, by going up upon it, stood forth victor over all things, so it also justly obtained princedom over all the trees of the woods, and deserved rightly to be called a palm; whence also it is sung to it: above all the trees of the woods, thou alone wast worthy to bear the King of the ages and the Lord. « And I will take hold of the fruits thereof. » This was the fruit of this palm: immortality, impassibility, and eternal delight also: these fruits of it Christ received upon the cross. For because he humbled himself, being made obedient upon the cross to the Father unto death, even the death of the cross, the Father exalted him, that in his name every knee should bow, of those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth, [1293C] and every tongue confess that he himself is in the glory of the Father, whereunto none comes save through him. « And thy breasts shall be as the clusters of the vine. » The breasts are those wherewith the infancy of little ones is fed and nourished; and these are the sayings of the prophets, whereby long before those times the passion of Christ was prophesied, and announced by many proclamations of the same. Which breasts indeed are now made the clusters of the vine, now showing the very fruit which they prophesied, and only then, when Christ suffered upon the cross, showing forth the effect which they sounded out. « And the odour of thy mouth as of apples. » For the odour of the cross, before Christ ascended it, was horrible; but after Christ the giver of life ascended, all its odours were made most sweet, and, as the odours of apples, most pleasing; [1293D] whence Paul also says: « We are the good odour of Christ unto God in them that are saved, and in them that perish [n: (II Cor. II, 15)]. » There follows:

9. *Thy throat as the best wine, worthy for my Beloved to drink, and for his lips and his teeth to chew upon. I to my Beloved, and his turning is toward me.*

The Bridegroom has now long commended her as worthy of praise, beginning from the lowest and advancing to the highest; which the Bride, holding it acceptable, answers: « Thy throat as the best wine, worthy for my Beloved to drink, and for his lips and his teeth to chew upon. » And so she shows that the praise which the Bridegroom himself has sounded out concerning her is such as it is worthy that it be drunk by the Bridegroom himself, and, passed over into his body, [1294A] be held in memory; and this is that which is said, « for the lips and the teeth to chew upon. » For in the law [n: (Levit. XI, 3)], an animal which does not chew the cud is held unclean, and that which chews the cud is beyond doubt accounted clean: for he who calls back to memory the good things which he hears or reads, and declares them by chewing them with the teeth, or by uttering them with the lips, is affirmed to be a clean animal. Whence also, affirming that this cleanness is in the Bridegroom, she exhorts him to call this praise back to memory, and to account and adjudge her worthy of this praise. And that this shall indeed come to pass she declares, saying, « I to my beloved, and his turning is toward me; » as if she should say, I am worthy of the memory of my Beloved, and he most worthy to be held in memory by me: for I love the Father, I venerate and adore his [1294B] only-begotten Son, I judge the Holy Spirit equal to these in all things. I understand the works of the Father which he wrought for my redeeming; in these I acknowledge the obedience of the Son, and through his Spirit coming into me I receive him turned toward me, and his gifts. And so she says, I am worthy of my Beloved and dear to him: for he emptied himself for me, making himself a child and a little one; to whom the Church sings that saying of Isaias: « A little one is born to us, and a Son is given to us [n: (Isai. IX, 6)]. » He emptied himself also, being made obedient to the Father unto death; and what death? that which was of the cross, which was accounted most unworthy, whereby heaven was denied to a man, the earth was withdrawn from him, the remedy of water was not afforded, [1294C] and, being dead, he was committed in the air to be torn by the birds:

*By these deservedly dear, by these merits to be held dear.*

« His turning » also is « toward me: » for what kind of death should I shudder at, when I understand that the Son of God suffered such things for me? None indeed. For in Stephen I endured the stone, in James the sword, at Rome in blessed Peter the cross, in his partner blessed Paul the troubles of the sword; the boiling oil in John, in Andrew the bitter things of the cross, I bore; and in the apostles, and in all the martyrs far and wide throughout the world, I presented the torments that could be devised. There follows:

10. *Come, my Beloved, let us go forth into the field.*

The charity being shown wherewith the Church is united to Christ, [1294D] she confidently calls upon him, desiring that he himself also come to her who loves him, and behold her loving him, and look through whether the things which have been said of him are true. And so she says, « Come, my Beloved, » whom I love even unto death, who at thy departing didst promise to the Church, saying: « Behold I am with you even to the consummation of the world. » [n: (Matth. XXVIII, 20.)] « Come, let us go forth into the field. » A plain is a place lying open far and wide, containing woods and places lacking woods, on this side beset with thorns, on that showing places not thorny yet untilled, designating this world, such as it was when Adam was cast over into it after his sin, and the sentence was spoken to him, « Because thou hast eaten of the tree whereof I had commanded thee that thou shouldst not eat, cursed is the earth in thy work: in labours shalt thou eat of it all the [1295A] days of thy life. Thorns and thistles shall it bring forth to thee, and thou shalt eat the herb of the earth [n: (Gen. III, 17)]. » [var: Vulg. *quia audisti vocem uxoris tuae*, "because thou hast hearkened to the voice of thy wife"] But this was called a field, after that, being assailed by the ploughshare and the coulter of the earth, it laid aside this horrible face, answered to husbandry, and gladdened its husbandmen with good fruits. And it is called *ager* from this, that it began to be driven, *agi*, by the ploughshare, and to be cast down by the coulter, and (as is the custom of husbandmen) so to be turned over in the same place, that it may be fit for receiving the seed, and be made fruitful for the propagating of the same. To this field therefore the Bride invites her Bridegroom, saying, « Come, » that is, consent to this, that I may have access to thee. « Let us go forth: » Do thou go forth, the flesh being assumed, out of the unspeakable hidden place of thy majesty, that is, appear manifestly; and I, having gone forth wholly with thee out of my hidden places, out of my uncleannesses, out of unbelief, [1295B] believing thee with the heart, confessing thee, humbly adoring thee, cleaving to thee inseparably, am ready to come with thee into this field, that we may consider whether it be well tilled, whether the seeds of the word are to be entrusted to it, and whether any hope of good is to be looked for from it. There follows:

*Let us abide in the villages.*

Dwellings indeed are made in fields, as was said of the plain of Sennaar, wherein the city of Babylon was built by the sons of Noe [n: (Gen. XI, 2)]; towns are made; villages also are made: in the city the rich dwell, in the towns the powerful; but in the villages is the abode of the lesser and the more humble, which is so much the more pleasing to God as it is more humble, and so much the more acceptable as it is the more capable of virtue and of doctrine. Here she desires that the Bridegroom make his stay, [1295C] and that she abide with the Bridegroom, where there is the consecration of his holy body and blood, where the washing away of sins is made in baptism, where there is the daily confession of offences, and the frequent oblation of the faithful peoples is made to the Lord: hence the frequent bestowal of tithes, hence of other gifts also, and at their going forth the holy acknowledgment of the Lord, and their passing over to eternal blessedness. There follows:

11. *In the morning let us rise up to the vineyards: let us see if the vineyard has flourished, if the flowers bring forth fruits.*



## CHAPTER VII. (cont.)

He is blessed whose vineyard is visited in the morning by the Lord. For the vineyard of each one is this present life; and the morning is for each one the beginning of the present life. But there are certain ones whom in the morning, that is, from the very [1295D] outset of life, God visits, directs, and magnifies, and whom he shews even by manifestation to be such that from them great fruits of miracles are to come; « Let us see if the vineyard has flourished. » To these the Bride invites the Beloved, that he may abide with them, and make these to flourish, and himself keep the flowers that bring forth fruits. These indeed are our advances in God: for as a vineyard produces first leaves, then the flower, thence the fruit; so in the things that are God's, for those who are solicitous to see and to have him, a certain initiation is the turning away from evil; for a great advance is a happy conversion to the things that are God's. But the highest thing in these is the love, pleasing to God, of the things that are God's, and the blessed completion of them in work. Whence also it is subjoined:

*If the pomegranates shall have flourished.*

[1296A]In pomegranates indeed the love of martyrdom is signified. Greater love indeed hath no man, than that one lay down his soul for his friends. Him indeed, though she admonishes all her own to see, yet these especially, who burn with the love of martyrdom, who desire to lay down their life for Christ and through death to ascend upward, she admonishes by a certain special visitation to give heed. Whence also she says:

*There will I give thee my breasts.*

What are these breasts which the Bride is about to give to the Bridegroom when he shall so have come, and shall so have visited the Church? The breasts indeed are that part of the body wherewith the Church nourishes her sons, wherewith she draws out their infancy, and brings them forth into the strength of youth; and when they have grown up into this strength, she leaves them to themselves, and to the martyrdom which they desire, and [1296B] to him for whose sake they desire it. She says therefore, « There will I give thee my breasts, » that is, I will open all the folds of my mind, that I may understand from thee what is to be said to each order, in what manner each order is to be admonished, that it may come to thee and possess thee for ever. There follows:

*The mandrakes have given a smell. In our gates are all apples: the new and the old, my Beloved, have I kept for thee.*

Having called her Beloved to come to the Church, to visit her, and she who desires to see him, she adds this, saying: For that indeed which I long for, I doubt not that I shall find thee difficult [cj: *difficilem*; read *non difficilem*, "not difficult"], because I understand that the mandrakes in our gates have already given thy odour. The mandrakes indeed she names the Prophets, which keep the form of a human body, and which heal whatever member [1296C] of the human body a disease occupies, from a like member, by powder scraped for the purpose. This also makes the barren woman fruitful, and profits those who suffer by much art of medicine: whence this expresses the Prophets, who express the humanity of Christ, and the things which he wrought in man; and this in our gates, that is, in the Scriptures which have now been made ours, they cease not to cry out and to prophesy. These indeed are all the apples, which the new and recent prophets foretold; « and the old, » the ancient, namely, which have been spoken from Adam until now, have I kept for thee, because they are to be fulfilled in thee, and to be shewn by the very accomplishment of the work. There follows:



## CHAPTER VIII.

1. *Who shall give me thee for my brother, sucking the breasts [1296D] of my mother, that I may find thee without, and kiss thee, and that now no man despise me. 2. I will take hold of thee, and lead thee into my mother's house: there thou shalt teach me, and I will give thee a cup of spiced wine, and new wine of my pomegranates.*

This Bride, solicitous concerning the coming of her Bridegroom, has long since in prayer greatly desired it, saying, « Come, my Beloved; Let us go forth into the field; » and what she prayed for she foretold would assuredly be at hand, because the mandrakes have spoken this beforehand, this all the prophets have foretold. Therefore concerning this which shall certainly come to pass, she now asks, saying: « Who shall give me thee for my brother, » that is, [who] will be so worthy as to shew thee to me visibly as my brother, to demonstrate thee to me in bodily form as [1297A] my brother. For it is ancient, and known by hearing, that God the Father of all created me among the rest, and stood forth as a father to me among the rest, and that I among the creatures have always called upon him as a Father: but that he himself should be a brother to me is new and unheard of from the ages, and yet is to be spread abroad to the world. For the angel Gabriel was sent to Mary, saying, « Hail, full of grace; the Lord is with thee. The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee, and the power of the Most High shall overshadow thee. And therefore that holy thing which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of God [n: (Luc. I, 28, 35)]. » Behold that the Son of God, born of the holy Virgin, born again by holy baptism, was made a son of the true Church, and began to be a brother to us all, who before all things stood forth to us as Father. Whence after the resurrection [1297B] he says, « Go, tell my brethren that they go into Galilee; there they shall see me [n: (Matth. XXVIII, 10)]. » Whence it is that him whom hitherto I had called God the Father, I now name my brother; and therefore brother, because sucking the breasts of my mother, namely of Mary; who, from this that she was made his mother, is worthily the mother of all who call upon him, and implore his help for themselves in whatsoever necessity. Through her indeed the Son of God was found without, that is, in this bodily form, which he newly took, and in it appeared to the world, and in this he received to himself from him (as has been said) the kiss of reconciliation; whence she says, « And now let no man despise me. » For before God had held out to man the kiss of reconciliation, [1297C] man was held despicable both to the angels and to the demons, and even to his own self, who was being dragged wholly into Hell to be possessed. But after he was reconciled by the death of the Only-begotten, forsaking Hell, he began to possess the region of Paradise and of heaven, and from thenceforth he was held in contempt neither by the possessors of heaven, nor by those of Hell. For she herself says, « I will take hold of thee, » that is, I will hold thee with a most firm embrace: not by threats, not by stripes, not by every kind of death shall I be removed or torn away from thee: and thus will I bring thee « into my mother's house, » namely of the heavenly Jerusalem, which instructs me, and shews me the example of that love whereby I may always cleave to thee, and may not be able to be removed from thee; and I will bring thee into « the chamber of her that bore me, » that is, that ancient dwelling-place of love, wherein [1297D] she that bore me has ever held thee, that is, that most ancient gathering sprung from the patriarchs and the prophets and the other faithful, and that holy congregation which could never be torn away from thee. « There thou shalt teach me, » because then shall we all be taught of God. And what shall we give thee? « A cup of spiced wine, » namely everlasting love; which is formed in so many ways, as wines are wont to be formed from divers kinds; « And new wine of my pomegranates, » that is, the passion of my martyrs, who are so joined to one another in the sweetness of love, as apples are joined to one another among apples, and by no evils could they be turned away from thee. There follows:

3. *His left hand is under my head; and his right hand shall embrace me. 4. I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, that you stir not up, [1298A] nor make the Beloved to awake, until she herself will.*

Because these things have already been said, we do not repeat them, but where they were said, we commit them to the studious to be read. There follows:

5. *Who is she that goeth up from the desert, flowing with delights, leaning upon her Beloved?*

One that loves easily looks back upon one that loves him, and the words which love forms it easily pours over to itself. Whence, because from the things already said the affection of the Bride that loves him is easily marked, the Bridegroom straightway affords her a response of love, asking and inquiring who she is: not that he is ignorant of her, but that he may permit no one to be ignorant. Of what sort is she, and how great, and how most worthy of wonder, who thus goeth up from the desert? Naming this world [1298B] a desert, which very many strive to inhabit as a fatherland, and, cheated of their endeavour, descend from hence to the places below, and thus come to know what fatherland is destined for them. This desert is that place which the Lord deserted by ascending, when, in the sight of many, he was lifted up and ascended into heaven. Wherefore it follows, that this same one is « mighty in delights, » flowing with which, and understanding that they please the Lord in the highest degree, she seeks to stand by him with right faith, strong hope, fervent charity, and to present to him these things given to her by himself. But this is what he says: Who, and of how great merit, is she who, this world being deserted, namely which God deserted by ascending from hence, which for others has been wont to be a place of descent, making for herself a place of ascent, leaps forth on high, presenting to God the delights [1298C] of the virtues, which she knows to have been conferred upon her by him? But blessed Gregory, looking more deeply upon these things, says: The Church, flowing with the delights of the words of God, ascends from the present desert to things above. To flow with delights is, in the love of God, to be filled with the banquets of the Scriptures. Flowing therefore with delights she ascends, because daily she lifts us up, fed with mystical understandings, to things on high; in whose words there are as many delights as there are diversities of spiritual understanding. Now history feeds us, moral allegory refreshes us, sometimes contemplation suspends us toward higher things. The soul flowing with these delights is refreshed with mystical understanding, in which the present obscurity is illuminated by the brightness of the day that follows, so that even in this corruption it may break forth into the understanding of that [1298D] true light, and, fed with the delights of the words, may learn by foretasting what it hungers for of the food of truth. There follows:

*Under the apple tree I raised thee up: there thy mother was corrupted: there she that bore thee was violated.*

It has been said that she, flowing with delights, is leaning upon her Beloved: now it is consequently shewn that she could and ought to lean upon him. For he says, « Under the apple tree I raised thee up, » that is, I called thee back from death; thee set indeed under the apple tree, that is, liable to the sin which was contracted by the apple tree. For they say that that forbidden tree, from which man in Paradise was bidden to abstain, was an apple tree, and that that wood, which was good for food, and beautiful to the eyes, and delightful to behold, [1299A] came to be a concupiscence to the woman; who took of it, and held it out to the man, and made herself, and her posterity, guilty of disobedience. From this evil of disobedience indeed this Bridegroom, bearing the cross, snatched all; and therefore he confesses that he raised up and redeemed her, placed under the apple, that is, under the sin which was contracted from the apple. And that this be not esteemed a small thing, he adds, « There thy mother was corrupted; there she that bore thee was violated; » calling mother the whole generation which was drawn out from Adam even to Abraham: but naming her that bore thee that generation which, from Abraham even to Christ, was bound by a certain stricter law of commandments, teaching by these that all begotten by human generation are liable to this offence; unless either the Spirit sanctified them before [1299B] they were born, as the holy Virgin who was made the dwelling-place of God, John the Baptist, and Jeremiah, whom we know to have been sanctified from the womb, or God succoured them by his sacrament. But if anyone cares to look more deeply, let him read again the treatise which we discoursed above concerning the kiss of reconciliation, and wherein we set forth the things which were granted to us, the Lord dictating. There follows.

6. *Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a seal upon thy arm.*

Because I freed thee from the death which thou didst deserve, through the death of my cross; therefore I consequently enjoin upon thee, that me, that is, my love, whereby I was made man [1299C] for thee, whereby I was crucified for thee, whereby I humbled myself that I might rescue thee from the death which thou hadst deserved, thou set always as a seal, that is, as a certain memorial, upon thy heart, that is, in thy heart; that in everything which thou shalt have purposed to do thou attend always to this love, that thou presume not to fulfil anything in act, unless thou see it to cleave to this love. The same also he subjoins concerning the arm, « As a seal upon thy arm. » For if these are things cleaving together, the heart deliberating, and the arm executing that which is deliberated by the heart. And if both of these hold the memorial of this love, they will restrain themselves from every work which suits not this love. Thus fortifying the heart and the arms, he urges that which has been said elsewhere: for elsewhere it is said, « Six things there are which [1299D] the Lord hateth, and the seventh his soul detesteth: Haughty eyes, a lying tongue, hands that shed innocent blood, a heart that deviseth most wicked thoughts, feet that are swift to run into evil, a deceitful witness that uttereth lies, and him that soweth discord among brethren [n: (Prov. VI, 16, 17, 18, 19)]. » All which things indeed, if thou hast the love of God, if thou fix it in thy heart and in thy arm as a memorial, shall be made far from thee, nor is there anything wherein they may hinder thee in the least.

*For love is strong as death*

Death indeed is strong, because it destroys kings, casts down tyrants, despoils the rich, and finally snatches to itself everything [1300A] that lives in the world, ending all things by one common end, and shutting up the universe: so also the true love of Christ is indeed strong, rejecting whatever things are adverse to Christ, not shuddering at the sword, not fearing the prison, not dreading chains, willingly taking upon itself every torment that can be devised, reckoning these things not as torments, but venerating and adoring them as helps to its own salvation. The world devised wild beasts, devised burning plates, devised wheels and screws; yet charity, abiding in the hearts of those whom malignity was assailing, overcame and conquered all these things. Whence this love is not strong as death, but is stronger than death itself, which remains whole while death rushes upon it, and through it deserves to obtain eternal rewards. There follows:

[1300B] *And emulation is hard as hell.*

As love is commended, so emulation is disapproved; for emulation is said to be an envious imitation of charity. For certain men, seeing the martyrs honoured, and the holy confessors venerated by all, envying those honours, and desiring the same, put themselves forward as though followers of the martyrs and imitators of them, and thus esteemed themselves martyrs, or as it were martyrs: whose zeal the Bridegroom himself thus disapproves, saying, « Emulation is hard as hell. » For as Hell drags those whom it snatches, nor permits any of them to be drawn out from it; so hard is the condition of these men, which is thus made one with hell, feigning good for itself only with evil guile. There follows:



## CHAPTER VIII. (cont.)

[1300C] *His lamps are lamps of fire and of flames. 7. Many waters could not quench charity, neither shall the floods overwhelm it.*

This is the commendation of charity, which is the true love of God; for its lamps of fire are the hearts, namely, of the saints, formed after the manner of lamps, which are narrow here below, but widened above, and prepared for a dwelling-place of the spiritual fire. Which fire indeed is always fire, always warming, and always kindling the heart in the love of God; sometimes fire with flame, when it not only burns and kindles, but rather shines in work, and manifests in deed what it does within; in such wise indeed, that many waters cannot quench this charity: for no tribulations, no devices [1300D] of vexations can quench this love; because, the more evils are multiplied, the more it is certain that it grows up hence into God, because « the floods shall not overwhelm it, » that is, evils heaped upon evils shall in nothing prevail against the grace of God. There follows:

*If a man shall give all the substance of his house for love, he shall despise it as nothing.*

This is the consummate praise of it: for if a man shall give the whole of that which is temporally acquired, that he may acquire this charity for himself, he will despise that whole substance as nothing, and will seem to himself to have given nothing, because that which is acquired is dear and lovely. There follows:

[1301A]

## 8.

*Our sister is little, and hath no breasts. What shall we do to our sister in the day when she is to be spoken to.*

The Bride has been commended by the Bridegroom, and he has admonished her that she was redeemed by him from death, and she has been taught that she should have this memorial of the heart; taught also that charity conquers all things; she indeed holds all things pleasing and receives them; and now, admonished concerning charity, she adds a question, saying, « Our sister is little, and hath no breasts. » There comes to her mind what the Apostle says, « When the fulness of the Gentiles shall have come in, then all Israel shall be saved [n: (Rom. XI, 25, 26)]. She calls to mind therefore this synagogue, which for the present walks in darkness, denies Christ, and blasphemes the things which are said and believed concerning him. [1301B] Foreknowing therefore that she is to be converted in the last times [by] the holy apostle, and that Elias and Henoch have been reserved by Christ for the converting of these, and naming her sister on account of the conversion to come, she thus says out of charity, « Our sister is little, and hath no breasts: » little indeed in faith, least in hope, and now none at all in charity. She hath no breasts, she contains in herself no bands of doctrine; the things which were done for her sake, these she denies and keeps silent about. Foreknowing therefore that she shall come to the faith at the last, she thus asks of the Bridegroom, « What shall we do to our sister, in the day when she is to be spoken to? » What shall we say? How shall we admonish her? What words of salvation shall we give her? To these questions the Bridegroom thus answers:

*[1301C]*

## 9.

*If she be a wall, let us build upon it bulwarks of silver: if she be a door, let us join it together with boards of cedar.*

For some of those who are to be converted are as a wall, having in themselves great knowledge, and also acquaintance with letters, and on this account also the hardness of a wall against those that assail them; to whom the Bridegroom says that bulwarks of silver be built upon them, that is, that the testimonies of the divine Scriptures be given them, and of the authorities wherewith we are fortified; that the ensigns be granted to these, whereby these, now fortified, are the defenders and the safeguard of the true Church. « But if she be a door, let us join it together with boards of cedar. » But the door are those through whom some enter, and some go out: who indeed are lesser in knowledge, [1301D] less instructed in the Scriptures, to whom the highest things are not to be set forth, nor things difficult of understanding, but boards of cedar are to be joined together, that is, light Scriptures are to be set forth; as is that board which the Lord painted, saying, « Blessed are the poor in spirit: for theirs is the kingdom of heaven. Blessed are the meek: for they shall possess the land. Blessed are they that mourn: for they shall be comforted. Blessed are they that hunger and thirst after justice; for they shall have their fill [n: (Matth. V, 3 et seq.)]. » Which board indeed is of cedar, that is, incorruptible, and bringing forth sentences undefiled, true and unbreakable. And other boards like to these, which, set before them, will be able to help and to confirm them in the truth received. There follows

*[1302A]*



## 10.

*I am a wall: and my breasts are as a tower; since I am become in his presence as one finding peace.*

The answer of the Beloved being heard, wherein he admonishes the Bride both to set forth bulwarks of silver for the converted synagogue, and to send fit defenders for the defence of the Church, she gratefully says, « I am a wall, » I too will set myself against the blows; and my breasts, namely the prelates of the Churches, whom I name my breasts for this cause, that I nourish them with my milk, these shall be with me the firm and unassailable defence of the Church, and with me the incontestable acquiring of eternal life; in that, my sister who had perished being now restored to me, « I am become in his presence as one finding peace. » For then shall peace truly be found, when none of those who were foreknown unto life shall any longer be lacking, [1302B] but the Church, now holding all her own within herself, shall rejoice in the highest prosperity. There follows:

11. *There was a vineyard to the peaceable one in that which hath peoples: he delivered it to keepers.*

The praise of this canticle being now consummated, and the fulness of this Bride being consummated, when the synagogue, which she names her sister, shall have been converted to the faith for her, and the fulness of the Gentiles shall have entered into the faith, the Bride now speaks of herself entire, saying, « There was a vineyard to the peaceable one. » I indeed, now called with the fulness of the Gentiles, with the synagogue now converted to the faith, have stood forth a vineyard to the peaceable one, that is, to Jesus Christ our Lord, who has made peace with all things that are in earth, and that are in heaven. « In that which hath peoples, » that is, in the multitude of the Gentiles, which was wholly created by God, but [1302C] not wholly foreordained unto life; « For many are called, but few chosen [n: (Matth. XX, 16)]. » But I, with the multitude of all the elect, of Jews and of Gentiles, was a vineyard to the peaceable one, making for him chosen fruits, worthy to be received by him, worthy to be presented to God the Father. Which vineyard indeed she says the peaceable one delivered to keepers: for that part of the Church which stood forth from Adam even to Christ was committed to the Patriarchs, and the Prophets, and the Doctors of the holy Law, to be kept. But that which shall be from Christ even to the end of the world was granted to the holy apostles, and to the martyrs, and to others excelling in honour, to be instructed. There follows:

[1302D] *A man bringeth for the fruit thereof a thousand pieces of silver. 12. My vineyard is before me. A thousand are thine, O peaceable one, and two hundred for them that keep the fruits thereof.*

This vineyard indeed the peaceable one tended, planted, and pruned, and brought through to flower and to fruit; which was not turned into bitterness, nor brought forth wild grapes, but choice grapes, and most worthy to be presented to God the Father. Whence here it is said, « A man bringeth for the fruit thereof a thousand pieces of silver. » Who is this man? That good man whom David calls to mind, saying, « The man who hath not walked in the counsel of the ungodly, and hath not stood in the way of sinners, and hath not sat in the chair of pestilence. But his will is in the law of the Lord, and on his law he shall meditate day and night [n: (Psal. I, 1, 2)]. » [var: Vulg. opens the verse with a beatitude, "Blessed is the man who hath not walked…"] He alone, most worthy to be called a man, receives the fruits of the Church, [1303A] and offers them to God the Father, from whom, for the rewarding of the Church, he receives a thousand pieces of silver, which he brings, that the Church may be enriched, and may rejoice together that she has borne fruit well. But by the thousand pieces of silver he denotes eternal life, and its infinite beatitude, wherewith the Lord shall reward his own, when he shall have presented them to the Father, and shall have set seats for them in the heavenly places. But he signifies that this beatitude is comprehended by the number of a thousand, which is the fourth unity: for the first unity is unity itself; the second, the ten; the third, the hundred; the fourth, the thousand; after this the number is extended to infinity, having always the thousand for its root and unity. Well therefore by this is infinity shewn, and by the silver, the excellence of beatitude; for concerning this it is said, « The words [1303B] of the Lord are chaste words: silver tried by the fire, proved of the earth, purified seven times [n: (Psal. XI, 7)]. » Because the words which preach the Lord contain chastity in themselves, admitting in themselves no heresy, no incontinence at all, but are in themselves as silver tried by the fire of the Holy Spirit, than which no utterance rings sweeter, nothing is more savoury to the soul; than which nothing is found more proved from the earth, nothing more purified by the fire of the sevenfold Spirit: concerning which she receives this answer of the Father, « My vineyard is before me, » that is, All this multitude of the elect is pleasing to me, it is with me in every thought and work, which thou signifiest to me by the thousand peaceable ones, and by the two hundred, who keep the fruits thereof. For these are those perfect ones who from Adam even to [1303C] Christ instructed this whole body with words, kept it with examples, and adorned it with merits. But the hundred that remain are those who from Christ even to the end of the world presided over that elect multitude: these are presented to God the Father, and are crowned in eternal beatitude with those over whom they had presided. There follows:

13. *Thou that dwellest in the gardens, the friends hearken: make me hear thy voice.*

This is the last admonition of the Bridegroom, wherewith he admonishes the Bride concerning his praise, saying, « Thou that dwellest in the gardens, make me hear thy voice. » She indeed, now holding the primacy in the gardens of Judaea, now holding her converted in the faith, instructing her with the precepts of life, [1303D] fully possessing also the multitude of the Gentiles, plainly residing in the gardens of the virtues, containing in herself all the virtues which smell sweetly to the Lord, is well said to dwell in the gardens of the Churches, and, that praise may be brought forth by her to the Lord, is entreated with heavenly admonitions, when it is said, Thou that dwellest in the gardens of the Churches, [1304A] make me hear thy voice, that my praise may resound from all the Churches, and that from every one my praise may ring back again with thanksgiving. For « the friends » « hearken; » for the Angels, and all the angelic powers with them, joined to Christ together with these from the beginning by the virtue of faithful friendship, await and hearken to the glory of my rewarding, which they already hope for in the glory of thy praise. There follows:

14. *Flee my Beloved, and be thou like to the roe and to the fawn of the harts upon the mountains of spices.*

This is the last answer of the Church, wherewith the Bride answers her Beloved, saying, « Flee, my Beloved. » The Bridegroom admonishes her concerning praise, and exhorts her to sound forth praise, not that he wills that she should now begin the canticles of praise, but the common resurrection of all being awaited, [1304B] when the Bridegroom shall have come in a cloud, when an angel with a clear voice shall have sounded forth his coming, saying, « Behold the Bridegroom cometh, Go ye forth to meet him [n: (Matth. XXV, 6)]; » when the mournful trumpet shall have sounded for all to rise again, and the Judge, thronged about with Angels, shall have sat on the seat of his majesty, and the world, none excepted, shall have run to meet him, and he shall have borne a dreadful sentence upon the reprobate, and shall have given to all his elect the sentence which he is to speak, « Come, ye blessed of my Father, possess the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world. » Then indeed shall the praise and thanksgiving of the Church with all the saints sound forth, such as has not been heard from the beginning of the world, and all that partake of her praise shall be blessed by the Lord unto the ages. And because this was shewn beforehand to the Bride that loves him, therefore, not [1304C] with indignation, but bearing this delay patiently, she thus answers, « Flee, my Beloved, » which is: withdraw thee for the present from our sight; in this standing like to the roe and to the fawn of the harts, which are indeed clean animals, and keep themselves from the sight of men upon the ridges of the mountains. Which mountains indeed, where this fawn of the harts dwells, that he may distinguish them from the other mountains, he adds, « of spices. » For the mountains of spices are the holy hosts of the angels, and the dwellings of all the heavenly powers, from which breathe forth all the aromatic graces, which it is certain are pleasing to the Lord. Upon these the Lord pours forth from himself the balsam of life, that, anointed therewith, they may now live for ever: of which anointing assuredly the Church, desiring to be [1304D] made partaker, and always sighing after it in the things which have been said, has sought that the kiss of most holy delight be given to her; and now, made certain by the most sure promise of her Bridegroom that she shall receive it after a little while with the whole of her fulness, she rests.
